# Our Civic Duty — Patch Notes

Newest release first.

## UNRELEASED — A petition signature follows shared choices

Signing a candidate's petition no longer adds a separate personality note to
the choice. The shared trait system now handles that influence through the
same option labels used by other decisions.

## UNRELEASED — A speech reaction uses shared trait pulls

People who hear a speech still react according to their recorded personality,
but the reaction now comes from the shared option labels instead of a separate
inline lean.

## UNRELEASED — Starting law estimates use recorded state medians

When a place has no recorded starting-law amount, the Observer view now uses the
median of matching recorded state terms. D.C. and territories use that same
median, labeled as an estimate from recorded states. Results no longer depend
on the world seed.

## UNRELEASED — Tomorrow is actually a new day

Getting on with the day used to move the date and leave the clock alone. If you
had spent your evening, tomorrow opened at a quarter past eight at night, and so
did the day after that. Campaign work never books anything that would run past
nine, so after five or six afternoons on the phones or the doors every campaign
button went gray and stayed gray — and pressing on through a week, or twenty
days, could not bring one back, because every one of those days opened at the
same late hour.

Moving to tomorrow now starts tomorrow, in the morning. The campaign is there
again when you get up. Nothing else was loosened to do it: an appointment you
have not kept yet still stops the day being skipped over, the money still has to
be raised before it can be spent, election day still arrives when it arrives,
and a session still will not be booked to run past nine.

## UNRELEASED — It reads like your life now

The game talks to you. "You're ten, and you live in Lexington. Most weeks were
built around school. You spent most evenings at home with your dad." — instead
of a narrator reciting your name and age at you. Your name and age sit together
in a small badge at the top of the moment rather than being repeated in every
line.

Starting a life only asks for your gender now — male, female, or non-binary —
and never for pronouns. Searching for where you're from stays put on the screen:
a long list of matching towns scrolls on its own instead of shoving the Begin
button off the bottom of the page.

Opening People, the day, or the office now dims the room and floats a panel with
a clear way out — a close button, the Escape key, or a click back on the room —
and closing it drops you right back where you were.

And a ten-year-old is no longer handed the household's errands to sort out with a
parent. That is a grown-up's conversation, and the game now knows the difference.

## UNRELEASED — The life happens in the room

Playing a life used to be a big card of text in the middle of the screen with
the room behind it like wallpaper, and your family nowhere to be seen. Now the
room is the game. It fills the screen, the moment you're in is a small panel
over it, and the people the game generated for you — your mom, your dad, a
brother or sister — are a standing list down the right side that you can open,
keep pinned, or fold away. Where you are, what the date is, and the way to
everything else sit quietly in the corner.

The people in the room are shown as placed figures for now, because the finished
character art is still being made; the names and who they are to you are real,
and the real figures will fill those places as the art arrives. The controls
have been redrawn too, in ink, navy and brass rather than the old flat green
buttons.

## UNRELEASED — The front door, and who's at home

The title screen is a room, not a room with a caption. The line that used to
tell you it was "a hall set out for a community meeting with nobody in it" is
gone; the picture speaks for itself. The menu sits on the left so the room keeps
the open side of the frame, and when the background changes it crossfades from
one room into the next instead of flashing white between them. The slow drift is
a touch stronger.

Starting a life is no longer a form. You say who you are and where you're from,
one step at a time, and each step you finish folds up into a line you can reopen
— so the whole thing fits on the screen without scrolling. You pick a first
name, a last name (leave either blank and you're given one), an age and a
gender; the game uses the pronouns that follow, and you can change them from a
small control if they don't fit. Where you're from is now a search over the
whole country — any town, city or place — with a short, true note about the one
you pick rather than a status label. A few states also have a legislature you
can work in; everywhere else is an ordinary life, which the game says plainly
instead of pretending otherwise.

Who your family is, whether there's a brother or sister in the house, and the
years behind you are not questions any more. The game builds them when you press
Begin, from the world's own seed — some lives have a sibling, some don't. If you
want to set that yourself, Custom Start still lets you.

"Who are you?" is optional. A few questions help the game understand what
matters to you, so the situations it puts in front of you land closer to home —
but nothing there locks a path or decides who you become, and you can skip it
and let the game learn from how you play.

And a formative moment is grounded in who is actually there. A ten-year-old is
no longer told that their younger sister has been coming home late from a
different place each night; a scene about somebody out on their own is only
offered when there's somebody at home old enough for it to be true.

## UNRELEASED — One life, told once

The game has a name on its title screen: Our Civic Duty. New Game, Continue,
Saved Games, Options and Quit, with Quit sitting there grayed out rather than
pretending to be something it is not yet.

The biggest change is one you feel rather than see a control for. Playing used
to go: a card, a click, a jump in the date, an unrelated card. Now the years
between the moments are told. Time passing says what happened in it — school
carried on, the same person was in the house every evening, the group kept
meeting — read off the record rather than made up, and a quiet stretch says what
the life actually contained instead of "nothing this year that anyone would tell
a story about". Time passes unevenly, the way it does.

People come back. Somebody you had a conversation with at ten can be the reason
a moment at thirty is possible, and what you did the first time decides whether
the second time happens at all — including quiet choices that gave no sign at
the time that they were choices. Some threads simply end: a job finishes,
somebody moves out, an argument stops mattering. The game will not promise you
a payoff for every decision, and most decisions do not have one.

What you remember is now a journal you open, in chapters, with the people in it
listed as people — instead of a list growing down the side of the screen with
every beat.

The opening set of situations has been rewritten. It starts in a kitchen with
somebody you know rather than with a policy initiative, the choices are things
you do rather than paragraphs explaining what you believe, and the longer set no
longer says "3 of 26" because it does not have a fixed length any more. It stops
when it has enough, which is a different number for different people. If you
would rather not answer, you start the life; there is no per-question refusal to
click twenty times.

Smaller things: you can search for a place to start in, and somebody who lives
in Lexington is told they live in Lexington. An ordinary adult is no longer
informed that this character does not work in a legislature. Seeds and replay
addresses have moved behind an Advanced disclosure, where they belong.

## UNRELEASED — Groundwork

The game has a chamber to legislate in.

A legislative chamber floor — tiered member desks, a raised rostrum with a
lectern, public galleries above — is now in the game's own art library at full
size, drawn from a picture four times sharper than it needs. Nothing in the room
names a state or carries a seal, and the monitor and the papers on the near desk
are deliberately blank: those say something only when the game genuinely knows
what to say. Nobody is in the room yet.

The first adult bodies, heads and shoes also arrived and were taken apart into
32 separate pieces. Twelve faces came through clean. The eight bodies and the
twelve pairs of shoes did not, for reasons that are written down rather than
guessed at, and none of them is in the game yet. The short version: the bodies
have a green line around them that the game's own art style does not use, and
the shoes are drawn at an angle the bodies' feet are not standing at.

The game has a picture on its front door.

Opening the game used to show a pale page with three buttons on it. It now
opens on a community meeting hall — a real room, drawn at full size, the same
one whatever machine you open it on. If you have played before, the title shows
you a room that fits the life you left: an ordinary adult gets their own living
room, with their name on it. Nobody is drawn into those rooms yet, because the
people have not been drawn, and the game says so in a sentence rather than
putting a stranger in your living room.

A child never gets an office. Neither does an adult who does not work in one.
Which room the title shows comes from what the game actually knows about your
character, and what it does not know it will not guess: the municipal office
with the county map on its wall now appears only where somebody really works
there, and it can no longer turn up as the default room for anybody with a
saved game.

Three more rooms came in with it — a second living room, a public hearing room
with a bench and a lectern, and a courtroom that nothing uses yet. All of them
are the full-size approved pictures, shrunk to fit rather than blown up, and
none of them has a single readable word painted into it. Anything a room needs
to say — a date, a bill number, a name — stays blank until the game genuinely
knows it.

Nothing here changes what you can do in the game yet. It is the plumbing that
future rooms and future people are built on, and it is worth one paragraph
because of what it makes possible next.

Backgrounds can now come in four sizes of the same picture, and the game picks
the sharpest one your screen can actually use — so a big or a Retina display
stops being served a small picture blown up. Nothing is ever enlarged to fill a
gap: where a picture is too small for a screen, the game says so instead of
pretending. Rooms and people are now described by data rather than by
hand-placed numbers, which means a person put in a chair sits in it, a person
put on a floor stands on it, and swapping one person for another does not knock
either of them out of place. None of that is visible yet, because the art it is
waiting for has not been made.

Postures are now described the same way. Standing, sitting at a desk, sitting
in a guest chair and standing while talking are each written down once — where
the feet meet the floor, where the hips meet the seat, where the shoulders and
hands sit — so a person can be asked for a posture and the game either draws
them properly in it or says plainly that the picture does not exist yet. It
will not put a desk-work drawing in a guest chair and hope you do not notice.
The same descriptions produce the drawing guides the art itself is made from,
so the picture and the maths come from one source.

The game now has a real office. A high-resolution shared staff workroom — the
approved master, not a development stand-in — is in the game's own art library
and drawn from a picture four times sharper than the old one, with the room's
own floor, furniture and sight-lines measured rather than borrowed from the
placeholder room it replaces. Nobody is standing in it yet, and the game says so
plainly instead of filling it with development mannequins: the people who belong
in that room have not been drawn.

The old placeholder office is still there, still working, and now clearly
labeled as the development room it always was.

The two people in the development office now sit in their chairs rather than
near them. The woman at the desk was hovering above her seat and the man in the
guest chair was cutting through his, because the game had been told the chairs
were somewhere they are not — the numbers describing the furniture had been
worked out from the drawings of the people instead of being read off the room.
They are read off the room now, and a person is placed by the part of them that
actually rests on a seat.

A batch of real character art is banked here but deliberately switched off: the
bodies are untextured gray shapes and the clothes are design drawings rather
than clothes drawn onto a person, so nobody can wear any of it until a person
looks at it and says it is good enough. It is now recorded as belonging to no
release at all, rather than to a release it was hidden inside — a small
bookkeeping difference that keeps "we have this art" and "you can see this art"
from ever being the same sentence.

## UNRELEASED — "What's In It For My District"

_Open for review. Not merged._

Bills now have text in them, and the people around you have opinions about that
text. A colleague can tell you what their district is missing, another can tell
you what the session can afford, and the two of them will not be satisfied by
the same answer.

### Added

- **Bills you can read.** A measure now prints as numbered sections with the
  actual operative language. A section written for everybody and a section
  written for one named project look different on the page and are different in
  the record.
- **Bargaining that changes the bill.** Ask a colleague what they want, hear a
  concrete answer, counter with a smaller version, and put it to the chamber as
  an amendment. If the chamber adopts it, the language is in the bill. If it
  does not, nothing anybody said changed a word.
- **Promises that behave like promises.** A colleague can say "I'm with you if
  Section 4 is adopted" or "I'm a no unless it is." The game records exactly
  what was said, to whom, and who was in earshot — and later works out for
  itself whether the promise was kept, was never triggered, or was broken.
  Nothing is guaranteed by having been said.
- **Colleagues who are not the same person.** One member argues about their
  district, another about the money. They open differently, object differently,
  and refuse differently.
- **A fiscal note you have to actually read.** It is filed with the bill. You
  do not know what is in it until you open it.
- **A line the game will not let you cross quietly.** You can offer someone
  something for themselves, in private. It is refused, it is recorded as what
  it is, it costs you, and it never buys a vote. Asking for a project in your
  district is not the same act and is never described as one.

### Notes

- Two members of the House are drawn from this world's own people and decide
  for themselves. The other ninety-eight seats vote as the scenario records.
  There is no whip count and no support meter, by design.
- The colleagues in the members' room are named and clickable but not yet
  illustrated; the approved character art covers the two office figures.

Repaired after an independent audit, before review:

- A colleague who says "I'm with you if you write my section in" is no longer
  read as being against the bill while your section is still missing. They said
  a conditional yes; the game treats it as one, and as nothing more.
- Delivering what somebody asked for answers their objection. It does not turn
  their objection into a promise of support they never gave.
- Nobody is reported as having kept a promise the room never called in. If the
  thing they asked for never happened, the promise was never owed, however the
  vote happened to land.
- What somebody said about passing the bill and what they said about overriding
  a veto of it are two different positions, and holding both is not changing
  your mind. Each is tested by its own vote.
- A promise can only be conditioned on something the game can actually settle.
  One condition on the list could never come true and is gone.

## PRE-ALPHA 0.5.0

_Released October 7, 2026._

### Added

- **People serving a sentence can ask for clemency, and the law of their state
  decides who answers** Someone the game sentences to jail or probation can now
  ask for a pardon or a shorter sentence. Whether they ask depends on their
  situation and their own temperament, and they ask again when a new governor
  takes office. Each state, D.C. and territory answers under its own real rule.
  In Oregon the governor decides alone. In Texas the board must recommend it
  first. In Alabama the board decides without the governor. In Massachusetts the
  Governor's Council must also agree. In Arizona the board's yes is needed only
  for crimes committed before 1994. A governor the game plays answers from their
  own principles, how they know the person, whether the case was in the papers
  and how close the next election is. When you are governor, the request comes
  to your desk. A granted pardon or commutation ends the sentence that day, so a
  jailed person is free to campaign and hold office again.

- **A neighbor who loses a job holds it against the governor** When a neighbor
  is laid off or their workplace closes, they think it over a few days later and
  come away holding it against the governor of their state or territory. That
  view counts in the town's support for the governor, and when you ask them
  about the people in office, they tell you why.

- **The member record now shows cutoff-aware historical legislative actions and
  law effects** The member record reader now reports dated, cutoff-aware
  legislative actions, lived outcomes, and recorded law effects. Historical
  attributions require saved official reflections or documented member actions
  with saved knowledge links. Law effects retain their recorded amount, cadence,
  dates, and source record IDs.

- **A parent whose child leaves school mid-year holds it against who answers**
  When a family moves in the middle of the school year and a child has to leave
  school, each parent who moved with them thinks it over a few days later. They
  come away holding it against the head of their local government, or the
  governor where no local government is recorded. When asked about the people in
  office, they name the child and say so.

- **People act on private goals of their own** Before, every generated person in
  the world was given a private goal when the world was built — to learn
  something, to keep up with people, or to keep some time for themselves — and
  no simulated person ever acted on one. The goals were read only to vary what
  the player could say in a scene, so the player was the only character in the
  world with an agenda. Now, when somebody decides on their own whether to get
  back in touch after a long gap, their own goal is weighed alongside their
  temperament. A person who has been meaning to keep up with people is likelier
  to be the one who rings. It leans on the decision rather than making it: the
  same person may still leave it, and a person with no such goal decides exactly
  as they did before. When they do ring, that is recorded as a step toward the
  goal, against the message they actually sent. The goal stays live, because
  ringing one person is not finishing it, and a goal nobody acted on records
  nothing at all. Nothing about this is shown to the player. An agenda is
  something to notice in what somebody keeps doing, not to read off a card.

- **Each territory has its own Governor, and a life there can run for the
  office** A life in Puerto Rico, Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands, American Samoa
  or the Northern Mariana Islands now opens with that territory's own Governor
  in office, and the player can put their name in for Governor through the same
  campaign route a state resident uses. Before, the Campaigns screen told every
  territory life that it was "not set in one of the fifty states, so there is no
  state executive office to stand for," and no territory had a Governor. None of
  the territories' election laws has been read yet, so each office runs on the
  game's own national range for a term and a calendar, exactly as an unread
  state does. That will be replaced by each territory's real rules when the
  territory research comes back. The government screen now treats a territory as
  a territory. The scope button and heading say "Territory" instead of "State."
  The Represented by list shows the territory's Delegate to the U.S. House (for
  Puerto Rico, the Resident Commissioner) in place of a congressional district,
  and it no longer shows a U.S. Senate row the territory does not have. Empty
  branches read "No record of Guam's legislature is kept in this save." instead
  of "The game has not established Guam's legislature." Still missing: each
  territory's legislature, its local governments, and the person in the
  Delegate's seat. The government map also does not show the territories yet.

- **A tied presidential election goes to the House and Senate** When neither
  ticket wins a majority of the electoral votes, a 269-269 tie, the presidency
  no longer stays empty. After the count, the House chooses the President with
  one vote per state delegation, and the Senate chooses the Vice President, as
  the Twelfth Amendment requires. The news says how many delegations or senators
  each nominee carried, and the winners take the oath at noon on January 20. For
  now each member of Congress votes for their own party's nominee, and an evenly
  divided delegation casts no vote. If the House or Senate cannot reach a
  majority, that office stays unfilled.

- **The Constitution can be amended on any national question, by Congress or by
  a convention the states call** Congress now proposes an amendment to the U.S.
  Constitution when two-thirds of each house believe in it: every member votes
  by their own principles, weighed against the higher bar of changing the
  Constitution. State legislatures can apply for a convention on a question, and
  when 34 of 50 have applied, Congress calls one; the convention votes one
  state, one vote. Either way, 38 of 50 state legislatures must ratify. A
  ratified amendment that writes a policy into the Constitution becomes the law
  in force everywhere, above every statute, so whatever the game reads from the
  law follows it. A state amendment that writes a policy into a state
  constitution now does the same within that state. In a save that already holds
  such a state amendment, the law in force changes the first time the save is
  loaded, and the next monthly outcome for that state follows it. A question a
  constitution settles is closed to statute: legislators no longer file bills
  that could not change it.

- **State amendments can now take up policy and town recall** A state's
  constitution can now be amended on more than the governor's term limit. An
  amendment can write a policy into the constitution or take it out, the way
  Prohibition was adopted and later repealed. It can use any policy that states
  decide, such as legalizing cannabis sales or restricting abortion. An
  amendment or an ordinary law can also change whether a state's towns may
  recall their officials. If recall is taken away, a town's Recall section says
  which law did it. In a world you watch, states now and then propose one of
  these on their own. Each goes to the legislature and then to the voters. The
  world record marks it "No recorded cause", because the game does not yet know
  why such amendments come up. A policy written into a constitution is recorded
  but does not yet change anything else in the game. What it should do is part
  of the research this depends on.

- **An officeholder can answer for a complaint, or resign, from the press desk**
  When a complaint or question about you is public and you hold an office, the
  press desk now offers five answers under it: explain it yourself, stand behind
  your account, cooperate with the inquiry, decline to cooperate, or resign the
  office. Resigning asks you to confirm first. Whatever you choose is recorded
  in public, and the desk says what it did to the office, including the date the
  office becomes vacant if you resign.

- **Ask people you know for campaign contributions** You can now ask the people
  you know, and active members of your party chapter, for campaign money one at
  a time. Each person answers from what they think of you, how they are related
  to you, what they can afford and how much more the law lets them give. Gifts
  go straight to the campaign committee, and the campaign screen lists who gave
  and why they said yes or no.

- **Ask people you know to help your campaign** You can now ask people you know
  to help your campaign. Each person answers from what they think of you, how
  they are related to you, the hours they have free and their own temperament.
  Those who agree join your campaign staff and add to your reach when they are
  on shift. A person who says no is remembered as having said no.

- **Ask somebody out, and become a couple** Before, nobody in the game could
  become a couple. Having a child with a partner and the scenes about a
  partner's plans each looked for a partner and never found one. Now the People
  screen lets the player ask an adult they know, who is not family, out on a
  date. The other person answers from their own side: whether they are already
  with somebody, whether they want people in their life, and how the two of them
  already get on. After two dates the player can ask them to be a couple and
  hears the answer on the spot. A couple is recorded as a partnership, so the
  person card says "your partner" and every system that asks after a partner now
  finds one. Either of them can end it. How many dates come before asking, and
  what weighs on the answer, are marked placeholders until ChatGPT answers the
  two questions filed on how couples form. Moving in together, marriage and
  one-night stands come next.

- **Corruption acts leave a record of who was involved** When a corruption act
  is recorded, the game now keeps the people directly involved and the records
  the act leaves. Campaign money used personally and a payment for an official's
  promised action use the same record, so later discovery can follow what people
  actually knew. There is no automatic chance of being caught.

- **Executive authority profiles cover every state and territory** Every state,
  the District of Columbia and U.S. territories now have an executive authority
  profile. Read packs remain read; other jurisdictions use clearly marked game
  estimates. Municipal mayor powers use registry readings where available and
  otherwise follow the government's form.

- **Executive orders use the shared office desk** State and federal executive
  orders open and resolve through the existing governing matter inbox.
  Management orders write to the shared law measure and enactment records, while
  requests that exceed recorded authority are refused with the reason.
  Computer-run executives can evaluate a recorded condition against their
  priority and recorded principles before acting. Recorded priority orders
  delegated-rule matters and sets an enforcement ranking for the office's top
  and remaining topics.

- **Executive orders and regulations enter the shared law record** Executive
  orders and regulations can be recorded as government measures with filing,
  publication, effective and expiry dates. The shared law reader ranks them
  below their government's statutes and stops applying them after expiry. Orders
  are limited to branch management or terms a current statute delegates;
  out-of-range and independent-policy clauses receive a plain refusal reason.

- **Choose how to answer an endorsement request** When a candidate asks for your
  endorsement, choose whether to endorse, decline, or return an earlier favor
  when one is owed. The game records who gave and received each favor.

- **Cash bail, mandatory minimums and the juvenile court age change what happens
  to a defendant** When someone is charged, the law in force where the case is
  tried now decides what happens before trial. Where the court sets money bail,
  bail follows the typical amount for the charge, and the defendant goes home
  only if they have the tenth of it that it takes; a defendant who cannot pay
  waits in jail, and every job they hold goes on leave until the case ends.
  Where a law has ended money bail, a judge decides, weighing the presumption of
  release, and may hold someone only for a violent offense. Each defendant's
  answer is on record with its reasons. Where a law sets mandatory minimum
  sentences, a judge can no longer give probation for a violent offense, or to
  someone sentenced before. A law passed in the game takes effect on its
  effective date, and a repeal lifts it on the repeal's date. The juvenile court
  age now decides who the police charge as an adult. Where the law keeps
  17-year-olds in juvenile court, only adults 18 and over are charged; where it
  does not, a 17-year-old is charged and tried as an adult.

- **A member you bargain with has a manner** Members you bargain with now have a
  manner, read from the commitments they have actually made rather than from
  anything invented about them. A member who has answered plainly before has a
  reason to answer plainly again, and one who has kept things open has a reason
  to keep them open. A member the game has not seen answer often enough has no
  manner at all, and the sitting weighs what it always did.

- **A bill now says which policy question it bears on** Before, no bill in play
  was about anything in the game's list of policy questions. A bill could record
  the question it concerned, and nothing that files one ever said. Now the
  Transit Access Pilot a Kentucky staffer opens on, the bill the Kentucky
  legislature files on its own, and a player's own draft of fare relief for
  assistance enrollees all bear on "Should local transit be free to ride?", and
  a drafted bridge repair program bears on funding upkeep before new
  construction. The bill's page and the docket show the question under the
  sponsor. Most bills still bear on nothing the list asks — signage, ferry
  notice, dredging schedules — and are left unlinked rather than pinned to the
  nearest-sounding question. A save made before the questions shipped keeps
  filing bills; they are simply not linked.

- **Budgets hire and lay off the town's police officers and teachers** A town's
  police officers and teachers are now the positions its budgets fund. Each
  quarter, when the police line of the government that serves the town rises in
  real terms, the town hires officers from its own job seekers; when it is cut,
  officers are laid off, the newest first. Teachers follow the state's school
  line the same way. Those laid off are recalled first when funding returns. A
  teacher needs a bachelor's degree wherever the person's schooling is on
  record.

- **You can buy a news outlet whose owner is selling** The press desk now shows,
  for each outlet whose owner will sell, what it would cost. If your savings
  cover the price, you can buy it: the money goes to the seller and the outlet
  is yours. If you cannot pay, the desk tells you why. An outlet you own is not
  for sale to anyone else. Prices are set by outlet size for now and are not
  based on the outlet's finances.

- **The campaign screen shows what the committee must file** The campaign screen
  has a Filings section. After you file for office, it shows when the
  committee's first statement is due with the state and lets you file it on the
  spot. A filed statement becomes a public record, and one not filed by its
  deadline is shown as missed. Kentucky uses its own statement of spending
  intent, due within 5 days. Every other state asks for a statement of
  organization within 5 to 15 days, a placeholder drawn once per state until the
  real rules are researched. In Minnesota, trying to run a second time for the
  office you are already running for is now refused in the words of the state's
  one-committee rule.

- **Campaign hours are a standing routine** A candidate can now set standing
  campaign hours on the campaign screen: which days and hours go to knocking on
  doors and which to fundraising calls. The hours repeat every week until the
  candidate changes or stops them. As the day passes, the game books and does
  each session when its time comes, the same way it keeps a job's hours, and
  each session is written up like one the candidate chose by hand. The hours are
  a limited resource, not something saved up. When a job, a class, a scene or
  another promise already holds the time, that session is lost and the next one
  goes ahead. No sessions are set for election day or after it.

- **Campaigns pay ordinary bills, and spending reports show what the money went
  to** Campaign committees now pay the ordinary costs of running: office rent,
  signs and printing, postage, travel, events, phones and software, food and
  bank fees. Each bill is paid on its own day, two to four days a week, to a
  local business, and appears on the committee's monthly spending report with
  what it was for and a running "Spent to date" total. Your rivals' committees
  pay bills every week; yours pays them in the week after you did campaign work,
  and never touches money set aside for an advertising buy you already approved.
  The kinds of cost and how much each takes are placeholders until the research
  on real campaign spending comes back.

- **Candidates can put their own money into their campaign** The campaign screen
  now offers to put $500, $1,000 or $5,000 of your own money into your
  committee, up to what you actually have. The committee receives it the same
  day, and the contribution is on the public record. If the game is not tracking
  your money yet, the screen says so instead of showing $0. There is no limit
  beyond what you have for now; the real rules on a candidate's own money,
  loans, party money and public financing are waiting on research, and those
  other sources are not offered yet.

- **Campaign purchases are priced by the size of the place** Yard signs, palm
  cards, postage and print ads now have a fixed unit price, and the number you
  need grows with the households in your town or district, so a big-city race
  costs more than a small-town one because it has more doors and mailboxes, not
  because anyone set a bigger budget. Prices that have not been researched yet
  are marked as estimates.

- **Campaigns file monthly spending reports you can read on the campaign
  screen** Every campaign committee, yours and your opponents', now files a
  public spending report once a month listing each payment it made: the date,
  who was paid, what it was for, and the amount. The campaign screen shows them
  under "Spending reports". A payment a candidate made to themselves appears as
  "Paid to the candidate", so a careful reader can spot it. The monthly schedule
  and the categories are placeholders until the real filing rules are
  researched.

- **Campaign weeks offer dated work with named hosts** Once the campaign has
  staff or a chapter that has agreed to help, Campaign shows dated work with its
  host, place and time. Until then, the page points to the chapter support
  request. After attendance, Campaign shows recorded contacts and only
  contributions the committee actually received. Existing committed week plans
  remain finishable, while the old count boxes and duplicate immediate actions
  are no longer offered for a new active week. The existing paid advertising buy
  stays reachable with its geography and spending ceiling, and the candidate can
  still transfer their own money to the committee.

- **Candidates for Congress and the state legislature win their party's primary
  first** Each state now holds its primaries on its real 2026 date, using its
  own method: a primary for each party, or one primary for all parties that
  sends the top two or four on, as in California, Washington and Alaska. Runoffs
  follow where the state holds them, such as Texas in May and North Carolina
  when the runner-up asks. Only the winners reach the November ballot, so a
  sitting member can lose their own party's nomination, and a seat a party is
  favored to win can draw candidates nobody recruited. A state law passed in
  play can move its primary or change its method. The new rule governs the next
  election whose filing opens after it takes effect.

- **Your state's capitol at dawn, at night, in the rain and in winter** Each
  state's own capitol building now changes with the day and the season: a
  sunrise morning, lit windows at night, a gray rainy day with wet pavement, and
  a winter look with bare trees and snow where the state really gets it. Warm
  places like Hawaii, Guam and American Samoa keep their green in winter. The
  building, its grounds and the view stay exactly as in the midday painting, so
  it is always the same place.

- **Parents can request funded pre-K and after-school spots for their
  children.** Recorded parents can request a funded service for an eligible
  child in their household. The child enrolls with the paid provider, and
  attended hours reach the existing delivered-service record. Service forms
  supply ages, enrollment exclusions, and session hours for both pre-K and
  after-school programs.

- **Every child now has a childhood record built from what happened to them**
  Each person now keeps a childhood record. It starts with where and when they
  were born, and it notes any move that pulls them out of school in the middle
  of a school year, along with the grade they were in. Families with children in
  grade school can now move away. Before, a child's school held the whole
  household in place. The child leaves their school as a transfer, and the
  school's record says whether they left mid-year or over the summer. The record
  can also tell how long a child was eligible for public health coverage, though
  today's coverage rules reach only adults. Saved games load with an empty
  record.

- **A city council can set its own wage floor, fairness rules, term limits and
  wards** A town council can now take up four questions that belong to the city
  itself: a minimum wage above the state's, an ordinance barring discrimination
  in housing and work on grounds state law does not cover, term limits for its
  own members, and an independent commission to draw its own council districts.
  Whether the state lets it is the state's answer. A city can't set its own
  minimum wage where the state bars cities from doing so, and a fairness
  ordinance can't stand where the state has barred local ones. If a state later
  bars its cities, their ordinances stay on the books but stop counting as the
  law from that day. State legislatures can now also pass the two housing
  questions that states have taken up in real life: allowing small apartment
  buildings where only single-family homes were allowed, and approving housing
  that meets the code without a drawn-out review.

- **A city or county council can pass a property tax, and the bills land on
  named households** A city or county council can now take up a property, sales,
  payroll or corporate income tax, if the state lets that level of government
  levy it. A passed property tax takes effect on its own date, then assesses
  every household's home or rent once a year and sends each bill to a named
  person. Where a household's rent is not recorded, the bill starts from the
  average rent for a home its size and says so.

- **A city minimum wage ordinance now sets the wage where the state lets cities
  set one.** A city ordinance that answers yes to "should the city set its own
  minimum wage above the state's?" now raises the wage in that city, by the
  average premium of real city minimums over their state's rate (9.2 percent,
  from the 40 California localities posted by the UC Berkeley Labor Center),
  from the ordinance's effective date. Town jobs paid below it are raised on the
  first pay period after. It counts only where the state's law lets cities set a
  wage, so a state law on that question turns city ordinances on and off, and a
  later ordinance that answers no takes the city back to the state's rate.
  Counties are not modeled, and the answer to the research question on real city
  and county rates replaces the average.

- **A city or county payroll tax taxes paychecks paid by local employers** A
  passed city or county payroll tax, where the state lets that level levy one,
  now taxes each paycheck paid by an employer inside that government, on payday,
  to the worker who earned it. A paycheck that was never paid is never taxed.

- **Your county's clinics, roads and fair run on what its board voted** Before,
  a county board voted a budget but nothing it voted for reached a person. Now
  the health, highways and parks lines of a voted county budget become the
  county's clinics, road repair and fair. The county manager commits the money
  to a department already working in the county, it is paid in installments, and
  residents who are unwell ask for the clinic and others spend a day at the
  fair. Each visit is recorded on the person. A county with no such department
  commits nothing, and no provider is made up.

- **Colleges in every state, painted from their real landmarks** Fifty-two
  college campuses now have paintings, each drawn from a photo of its best-known
  landmark: the flagship university of nearly every state and territory, plus
  Harvard, Princeton, Penn, Cornell, Georgetown and Montana State. A college
  without its own painting borrows one that fits: a campus from the same state
  first, otherwise one from the same region with the same climate and ground, so
  a Kentucky college never shows a desert. The game can now pick these pictures;
  showing them where your character studies comes next.

- **Congress and the states can amend the U.S. Constitution** The U.S.
  Constitution can now be amended without the player, through Article V. Once a
  year Congress is reviewed. When there is a reason on record (a President
  barred from another term, or one who has served three or more), Congress may
  propose changing the presidential term limit. It happens rarely. Two-thirds of
  each house must vote for it, then each state legislature acts on it within
  seven years. It becomes part of the Constitution on the day the thirty-eighth
  state ratifies. The District and the territories do not ratify, and the
  President has no part in it. The presidential election now reads the term
  limit the Constitution holds in that world, so a ratified amendment decides
  who may run. The reasons, how often Congress acts, and how the votes divide
  are the game's own until research answers them. Only the presidential term
  limit can be amended so far. Not modeled yet, and marked so in the code: other
  subjects, a convention called by the states, ratification by state
  conventions, and a state withdrawing its ratification.

- **Congressional candidates enter the race before election day** Background
  congressional races now have fictional candidates recorded before the general
  election. A sitting member can seek another term or step down, and the
  election names a winner from its saved candidates. These entries use a
  disclosed game timing rule; they do not represent state filing dates or
  primaries.

- **See how each chamber of Congress divides, and who sits against their own
  party** The Government screen now shows how the Senate and the House divide,
  by party and by caucus, and names the members whose caucus is not the party
  they belong to — an independent who counts with one of the two, or a member of
  a party too small to have a caucus of its own. Each name opens that person. A
  chamber whose save records no caucus for anyone still shows its parties and
  says so plainly, rather than disappearing.

- **Congress files, debates and passes federal bills** Congress now makes law
  while you live your life. On the first of each month a member of each House
  files a bill on one of the federal issues they have taken up, and members who
  work on the same issue sign on as cosponsors. The bill goes to the committee
  for its field, then to a vote in each House. In the Senate it first needs
  sixty votes to end debate, so a bill backed by only one party usually dies at
  the filibuster. A bill that passes both Houses goes to the President, who
  signs it or vetoes it, and two-thirds of each House can override a veto. A tax
  bill has to start in the House. Every member casts their own vote and each
  ballot records why. A federal law takes effect the day it is enacted. What a
  federal law changes in the world is not built yet; each bill says so plainly.
  You can read any of Congress's recent bills from Politics, Government, United
  States. Each one is printed the way Congress prints a bill: the Congress and
  session, who introduced it and with whom, the committee it went to, the
  enacting clause, its sections, and a record of each House's vote and the
  President's decision underneath. A bill's votes and history now show dates the
  way you would write them, and a bill sent to the President says so rather than
  naming a governor.

- **State constitutions can change without the player** Every state can now
  amend its constitution through its own legislature and voters, not only
  California. Once a year each governorship in the world is reviewed, and when
  there is a reason on record (a governor barred from another term, or one who
  has served three or more), the legislature may propose changing the governor's
  term limit. It happens rarely. A proposal goes through each chamber and then
  to the voters at a November general election. If it is ratified, the next
  governor election follows the new limit. Law now ranks by level: the federal
  Constitution, federal law, a state's constitution, state law, then local
  charters and ordinances. A higher level governs a lower one on the same rule.
  What the game does not model yet (home rule, compacts, how often amendments
  really happen and why) is marked as a default and has gone to research.

- **A council member's voting workflow now covers the quiet items on the
  agenda** A seated council member's own ballot used to be ignored on the
  council floor, so a player on a town council or county board was always
  recorded absent. The council vote now reads the ballot the member saved. A
  council seat can also hold the same voting workflow every other office has.
  With prior instructions, a standing instruction that still matches an
  ordinance's text casts the vote on any item nothing in the member's life makes
  matter, before the roll call. With a review batch the quiet items wait in one
  list for the member to decide, and handling each vote individually leaves
  every item to them. An item the member never decides is still recorded absent,
  and the item that matters to them is never cast for them.

- **Your county board holds a budget hearing and votes the property tax levy**
  Every year, about three months before its fiscal year opens, your county board
  holds a budget hearing on one of its sitting days. The hearing sets the levy
  the county's books call for, as a property tax rate on homes, and puts it on
  the board's agenda. The members vote it at their next reading, each from their
  own reasons, including how close the county's offices are to closing without a
  budget. A levy that passes becomes the property tax your household's home or
  rent is assessed for, on the day it takes effect, and the county's budget for
  the year expects exactly that levy. A levy the board turns down leaves the
  county collecting at its current rate.

- **Towns in a county have the county clerk's office** Before, a town had a city
  clerk but nobody from the county clerk's office, the office that keeps a
  county's records and takes filings. Now a town inside a county with a county
  government, such as Belzoni in Humphreys County, has a county clerk who works
  the clerk counter on weekdays. A town whose county has no separate government,
  such as Lexington, keeps its city clerk and has no county clerk.

- **The county courtroom shows the day the court sat on your case** On a day a
  court tries or sentences your case, the screen now shows the county courtroom.
  The prosecutor who reviewed the referral stands at the counsel spot, the
  jurors who cast ballots sit in the jury box, and the judge who chose the
  sentence takes the bench. Nobody is added who the saved records do not name:
  defense counsel are not recorded yet, so no second counsel spot is filled, and
  a day with no sentencing carries no judge. The next day the room clears.

- **A county's elected executive is read from its state's structure and can be
  run for** Counties now read their form of government from the record. Where a
  county elects an executive, such as a county judge-executive or county mayor,
  that office is seated at the opening and a resident can file for it.

- **County sheriffs, prosecutors, clerks and other row officers are held and
  electable** Each county's state decides which row offices it elects. Those
  offices now have generated holders, and a resident can file for one.

- **A law can change how many judges sit on a court** A bill can now set the
  number of seats on a court: Congress for the federal courts, including the
  Supreme Court, and a state for its own courts. A court whose size is written
  into its constitution changes only by constitutional amendment. On the day the
  law takes effect, a larger court gets new, empty seats, and on the Supreme
  Court the President nominates for each one and the Senate votes. A smaller
  court removes nobody: each sitting judge keeps the seat until leaving it, and
  that seat is then retired instead of refilled. The news records the change.

- **Crisis response keeps its actual state spending authority** A new
  government's budget opening records the state's reported adopted 988
  appropriation for its actual funding period. Spending authority grants no cash
  or service. Payments still require the existing commitment and cash checks.
  Federal grant ceilings and all-source funding totals create no appropriation.

- **Saved crisis authority can enter the existing service contract** The crisis
  funding adapter exposes actual saved appropriation authority to the shared
  service contract. It preserves the government, account, source basis and
  availability dates. An operating payment remains a payment; delivery requires
  the existing service consumer's completed recipient activity.

- **The D.C. Council passes acts, and the Mayor signs or returns them** A life
  in Washington now opens with the whole Council of the District of Columbia in
  its seats: the Chairman, four at-large members and a member for each of the
  eight wards. The Council meets on its own every two weeks. A member brings in
  an act on a question the District decides, such as consumer data privacy, and
  the act comes back for a second reading at least two weeks later. If it
  carries both times, it goes to the Mayor, who signs it or sends it back. A
  signed act takes effect after Congress's review period. In a year of play, 25
  acts came in and 10 became law. A councilmember can do the same from the city
  screen. You bring in an act and vote at each reading. The screen shows which
  reading comes next and the earliest day it can be held. Once the act passes,
  it shows the day the Mayor must act by. If you are the Mayor, you sign or
  return each act the Council sends you, and an act you leave alone becomes law
  without your signature once ten weekdays pass. If the Mayor returns an act,
  the Council has thirty days to reenact it, and that takes two-thirds of the
  members voting. The Council's own rules are not read yet, so for now any
  member can bring in an act, there is no committee stage, and both readings are
  decided by a simple majority. How often the Council meets, how each member
  votes, and when a Mayor you do not play returns an act are marked stand-ins
  until they are researched.

- **A statehood law for D.C. adds two Senators and a voting Representative**
  When Congress passes statehood for the District of Columbia and the law takes
  effect, the District gets two Senate seats and a voting House seat. The Senate
  grows to 102 and the House to 436, and every vote count and majority reads the
  new seat count. The Delegate in office becomes the Representative, and each
  Senate seat goes to the party the District's own 2024 result favors. Before,
  Congress could pass statehood and nothing changed. The seats come from a data
  file, not from code.

- **Laws on the debt limit and foreign aid change the federal deficit, what
  states pay to borrow and the aid in local budgets.** When Congress makes
  itself cut spending before it raises the debt limit, federal spending falls by
  about 2% a year, the size of the last deal that did this. Every state, county
  and city budget then gets that much less federal aid, and because the federal
  deficit is smaller, every state pays less to borrow. When Congress spends more
  on foreign aid, the deficit grows and what states pay to borrow rises by a
  very small amount, because aid abroad has almost no measured effect on places
  at home. A later law answering no ends either change the day it takes effect.

- **A federal law that grows defense spending raises earnings in the states that
  draw the contracts.** When Congress answers yes to "should defense spending
  grow faster than inflation?", defense contracts grow each year at the pace
  they grew in the years they beat inflation, for as long as the law stands and
  for up to five years. Each state gets extra contracts in proportion to what it
  draws today, and its earnings rise by about one and a half times the share of
  its output those contracts add. A state with big bases and shipyards gains
  most; a state with few gains little. A later law that answers no ends the
  build-up the day it takes effect and earnings go back.

- **A lawmaker's record lists the laws they wrote and what each is doing** A
  person's full record now has a "Laws they wrote" section for anyone who
  sponsored a bill that became law. It lists their five most recent laws, newest
  first, with the date each became law. Under each law it says what the law is
  doing to the conditions the world keeps: how far it moved a condition, the
  month a delayed effect starts, or that a later law replaced it. Each title
  opens the law's own page.

- **Every state now has a graduation rate and a math proficiency rate that laws,
  money and life move.** Each state starts at its real on-time high school
  graduation rate and share of eighth graders proficient in math, and both
  change over the years. Laws that equalize school funding raise achievement
  over the following years; universal preschool shows up in graduation more than
  a decade later; vouchers help some students graduate while costing others
  ground in math; and poverty, health coverage and the economy push schools up
  or down. More graduates, in turn, mean less poverty a decade on.

- **Every state now tracks hunger, reading, degrees, broadband, asthma,
  overdoses, earnings, transit, migration and borrowing costs.** Each state
  starts at its real recent share of households that go hungry, eighth graders
  who read at grade level, adults with a college degree, homes with broadband
  and children with asthma, and at its real overdose death rate, typical
  earnings, amount of bus and train service, number of people moving in or out,
  and what it pays to borrow. Each drifts over the years, sometimes with the
  whole country, and is never pulled back to where it started. Laws now reach
  them too: taking the sales tax off groceries eases hunger, naloxone programs
  may cut overdose deaths, and letting towns build broadband brings more homes
  online. A state that requires a balanced budget, funds its pensions on
  schedule or keeps a minimum reserve pays less to borrow after a year or more.
  Places with no published figure record nothing rather than a guess.

- **Elected officials can hire office staff** A new state legislator now has an
  office to staff. "Look for staff" brings three applicants each for a
  Legislative Aide and a Constituent Caseworker, with their working lives on
  record, and a Hire button for each. Your staff briefing then reads the people
  you hired. A governor's Legislative Director and Caseworker posts, which
  nothing ever filled, open for hiring on the governor's desk the same way. A
  legislator's two positions are a marked placeholder until research says how
  many staff each legislature really gives its members.

- **Election night is held at the venue, and a school's town hall is in the
  gym** When you give your victory speech or concede, the screen for that day
  shows the election night venue, with the count on the wall screens. The next
  day it goes back to where you were. A town hall that a school or a civic group
  hosts is held in the school gym, with its own spot to stand in. A town hall
  hosted by a party chapter or a campaign stays in the community room. The four
  other unused pictures (convention hall, debate stage, rally stage and radio
  booth) stay unused until something in the game holds those events.

- **Election night has victory and concession speeches** When your race is
  decided, the other candidates give their speeches at once: a victory speech
  from the winner, a concession to the winner from everyone else. Yours is your
  choice. The result screen offers "Give your victory speech" or "Concede to"
  the person who beat you, and what you said stays on the record.

- **Everyday talk is measured from real recorded conversation** How long
  everyday turns run, how often they are three words or fewer, how often they
  ask a question, and which reactions, openers and hedges people use are now
  counted from hours of recorded American conversation, as numbers only, for the
  dialogue engine to match.

- **The dialogue engine knows how families talk among themselves** Counts from
  20 recorded family conversations, such as sisters talking late at night, a
  birthday party and a Christmas morning, now sit beside the everyday counts:
  10,620 turns with a median of 5 words, and slightly more questions than talk
  in general. No line from a recording ships.

- **The dialogue engine knows how friends talk** Counts from 10 recorded
  conversations among friends, roommates and neighbors, such as an after-dinner
  talk, a block party and three retired women before lunch, now sit beside the
  family and work counts: 5,518 turns with a median of 5 words. No line from a
  recording ships.

- **Hearings sound like real congressional hearings** A bank of 76 short turns
  from congressional hearings, such as "Thank you for the question.", "That is
  correct, sir." and "The gentleman's time has expired.", is now in the game's
  data for the dialogue engine to run a hearing with.

- **Judges can rule the way real courts write** A bank of 34 shapes from the
  Supreme Court's opinions, such as "It is so ordered.", "We hold that" and "I
  respectfully dissent.", is now in the game's data for the dialogue engine to
  word rulings and dissents with.

- **Bills can be worded the way Congress words them** A bank of 35 drafting
  shapes from real bills, such as the enacting clause, "This Act may be cited
  as", section headings and "is amended by striking", is now in the game's data
  for the dialogue engine to word bills with.

- **Floor speeches can open the way real ones do** A bank of 119 ways a speaker
  opens, manages and closes a turn in a formal meeting, each taken from the
  Congressional Record and kept with its date and page, is now in the game's
  data for the dialogue engine to draw from.

- **Meeting minutes read the way official minutes do** A bank of 37 shapes from
  the Federal Open Market Committee's minutes, such as "Several participants
  noted that", "By unanimous vote" and "Voting against this action:", is now in
  the game's data for the dialogue engine to write a body's minutes with.

- **The dialogue engine knows how news stories are written** Counts from 51
  Voice of America staff stories now sit in the game's data: a median sentence
  of 15 words, a lead of 24, most paragraphs one sentence long, about one
  sentence in nine quoting someone, and "said" as the usual attribution word. No
  sentence from a story ships; the counts shape the game's own news writing.

- **Press secretaries talk the way the briefing room does** A bank of 62 short
  turns from White House press briefings, such as "Go ahead.", "I'm not going to
  get into hypotheticals." and a reporter's "Two questions.", is now in the
  game's data for the dialogue engine to run a press briefing with.

- **Officials can honor and remember people the way Congress does** A bank of 40
  shapes from the Congressional Record's Extensions of Remarks, such as "I rise
  today to recognize", "is survived by" and "I ask my colleagues to join me in",
  is now in the game's data for the dialogue engine to word tributes,
  congratulations and remembrances.

- **Votes are won and lost in the words the floor uses** A bank of 35
  announcements from the Congressional Record, such as "On this vote, the yeas
  are 212, the nays are 205." and "The motion was rejected.", is now in the
  game's data for the dialogue engine to report a vote's result with.

- **The dialogue engine knows how people talk at work** Counts from 14 recorded
  work conversations, such as loan officers in a bank, a veterinary office and
  an air traffic tower, now sit beside the everyday counts: 5,099 turns, more
  one-word turns than talk in general, and fewer questions. No line from a
  recording ships.

- **Every state now has its own air, drinking water, violent crime and
  homelessness levels, and laws move them.** Each state starts at its real
  recent levels of fine particles in the air, drinking-water violations, violent
  crime and homelessness, and each changes month by month, sometimes along with
  the whole country. Nothing pulls a state back to where it began. Laws act on
  top: dropping a concealed-carry permit raises violent crime over the following
  years, clean electricity and carbon pricing slowly clear the air, and federal
  housing vouchers for every eligible family cut homelessness. A town's crime
  now rises and falls with its state's violent crime. Places with no measured
  level record nothing rather than a guess.

- **A fiscal note accompanies each draft and filed bill** The docket now shows a
  fiscal note for a draft before filing and for its saved bill afterward. It
  lists each section's scope and stated amount and identifies whether the amount
  is an appropriation, an authorization ceiling, or a charge per covered event.
  When a total cost or receipt needs an activity count or a payment record the
  world does not have, the note names that missing input instead of showing
  zero. Reading the note does not move money or enact the bill.

- **Every state, county and city government keeps a budget, and budget laws act
  on it** Each state, D.C., territory, county and city in the world now keeps
  its own books month by month: taxes collected by source, spending by program,
  a balance, a reserve, debt and a pension record. A state opens at its real
  Census finances per resident and NASBO balances; a county or city opens at a
  share of its state's local figures that research will refine. A new budget is
  adopted automatically each fiscal year. A law requiring a balanced budget now
  forces mid-year cuts and reserve draws when collections fall short, and
  without one the deficit is borrowed. A minimum-reserve law sends surpluses to
  the reserve. A pension law makes the government pay its full contribution;
  without one it pays its own share, starting from the measured spread of what
  real pension plans are paid and drifting year to year, and any shortfall grows
  its unfunded liability. Each budget law binds the government that passed it.
  American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands, which the national budget
  survey leaves out, open from Guam's and the U.S. Virgin Islands' spending per
  resident.

- **Every state legislature has members from the first day** A new game now
  seats a full legislature in every state, not only your own, and a council for
  your city and county. Each member is a person with a district where one can be
  named, a party, and principles of their own, so their votes follow what they
  believe. The News lists the nation's officeholders and your own state's, not
  every state's governor. Starting a new life takes a few seconds longer while
  the legislatures are prepared, and the loading screen shows the progress.

- **Every state has a legislature now, not only the nine that were researched**
  Nine states had a legislature. The other forty-one and Puerto Rico had none at
  all — no chambers, no seats, no floor vote — so a life lived in Texas or
  California could reach a seat and find nothing to sit in and no bill to carry.
  That is fixed: a state without a researched legislature now has one anyway. A
  generated legislature has two chambers, a real number of seats in each, a
  quorum, a passage vote, a governor with a veto and a threshold to override it.
  The figures differ state to state, so crossing a state line changes what a
  chamber looks like instead of finding the same one everywhere, and a given
  state keeps its own forever, in every session and every save. Seat counts fall
  anywhere in the range the researched states span; veto windows and override
  thresholds are only ever ones a state actually enacted, because those are
  choices a legislature writes rather than numbers that could be anything. A
  senate is always smaller than its own house, which is the one thing American
  bicameralism never gets wrong. Where a state's own constitution has been read,
  that is what applies and nothing here is consulted. Reading a state's real law
  replaces its generated legislature entirely. The District of Columbia is the
  exception and keeps none: it is legislated for by a single Council rather than
  by a House and a Senate, and a generated bicameral legislature would be a
  shape the District has never had. Where a state's own constitution has been
  read for its veto override, that is the threshold the game uses, even in a
  state whose legislature is otherwise the game's own. Tennessee overrides on a
  simple majority, not the two thirds the game would otherwise have picked;
  North Carolina on three fifths of those present and voting; West Virginia on a
  simple majority for an ordinary bill. Real law always wins over a generated
  figure.

- **Reading a published story saves what you learned** The News story reader can
  save a player's explicit read of an actual published story. The saved
  knowledge names that publication. When its recorded basis contains a supported
  law effect, the existing news-exposure writer records what law the player
  heard about. Repeated reads preserve one record, including after Save and
  Continue. Reading does not change money or opinion weights. The existing News
  page forwards its world update callback to that story action.

- **A federal law that cuts farm subsidies lowers farmland values.** When
  Congress answers yes to "should federal payments to farmers be cut?", every
  state's farm payments fall by about a fifth, the usual size of a cut in the
  years payments have fallen since 1996. Payments are built into what farmland
  sells for, so a year later the value of farmland drops in each state, most
  where payments are large next to what the land is worth. A later law that
  answers no restores the payments, and land values recover as land trades.
  Farmland value per acre is now tracked for all 56 places, starting from the
  2025 land values survey.

- **Three more federal laws change the world** A federal tariff law now raises
  what households pay, Medicare drug price negotiation lowers what enrollees pay
  out of pocket, and a higher retirement age keeps more people 62 to 66 at work.
  Before, Congress could pass each of these and nothing in any place changed.
  Each place now keeps that outcome from the start of the game, and the law
  moves it after its lag. The delegates' 2024 results now come from a data file,
  not from numbers typed into the code.

- **The federal government has its own policy questions** A new world now knows
  what the federal government is about: twenty fields and sixty questions, from
  appropriations and the debt to Medicare, trade, immigration and federal
  courts, each with a matching subject a person can come to know. They sit
  beside the state and local questions rather than replacing them, so a federal
  education grant and a local school budget stay two different questions.
  Registering them gives no one knowledge of them, and no federal positions are
  authored yet.

- **The federal government now keeps its own books, and a top-rate tax law
  reaches them** The United States government now keeps a monthly account beside
  the state and local budgets. It opens from the government's real 2025 receipts
  and outlays and its real debt at the end of 2025, borrows each month's
  shortfall, and pays interest on what it owes. A law raising the top income tax
  rate now brings in about 1.5% more income tax from the next tax year, and the
  government borrows that much less.

- **Ethics findings, laws and a governor's record now have consequences** A
  public ethics finding against a candidate now costs them support in any race
  they are running, and a finding about campaign money they took for themselves
  orders that money repaid to the committee. Their family, household, colleagues
  and party organizers hear about it and decide for themselves whether to stand
  by them or keep their distance, and a party chapter weighs it before agreeing
  to help their campaign. A finding is remembered when they run again. A
  candidate who pays themselves from campaign money can now be caught. The
  payments appear on the committee's monthly public report, where newspapers can
  see them, a rival can complain, and the state's campaign-finance regulator can
  open a case from its own review. A campaign bookkeeper who has already raised
  one such payment privately may report the next outside the campaign. A finding
  orders every reported payment repaid, and the regulator fines the candidate
  for each one. Every state now has a regulator. Where the real one has not been
  researched yet, its name, timeline and fines are generated. Laws now reach the
  economy through the money they actually move. When a government pays out under
  an appropriation or a program, that state's own economy picks up; when it
  collects a tax, demand comes out. Passing a law does nothing to the economy
  until money moves. A sitting governor who runs again starts ahead if
  unemployment fell on their watch, and behind if it rose. How large each of
  these effects should be has not been researched yet. The game uses cautious
  placeholder sizes until it has.

- **Civic words are underlined and explain themselves when you rest on them**
  Words like primary, filing deadline, cloture, veto override, quorum and
  appropriation now carry a quiet ink underline wherever they appear: in
  conversations, the Journal, the news, bills, dossiers and the Guide. Resting
  the pointer on one, or moving keyboard focus to it, opens a short plain
  definition. "Got it" removes that word's underline everywhere; the Guide keeps
  the entry, and it can take the mark back. Each word is marked once per
  paragraph, and never inside a button or a field. Ten terms were added,
  including committee assignment, germane amendment and the three interview
  ground rules.

- **Government rules record their effects on residents** State laws can now
  record whether a photo-identification requirement applies to voting, whether
  same-day registration is available, and whether a former public official is
  subject to a lobbying restriction. Each permission keeps the law, date, person
  and saved records that support it. The current law data does not specify a
  lobbying restriction's duration, so no duration is invented.

- **A governor's term limit and term length follow the law, and a law can change
  them** A governor's term limit now bars a candidate who has served the terms
  their state allows, and a law passed in the game can change it. If a
  legislature raises the limit from two terms to three, a two-term governor can
  stand again. If it changes the term from four years to six, the office's
  elections move to the new cycle from the first term the law reaches. Missouri,
  Nebraska and Ohio use the limit read from their own constitutions. A state
  whose law the game has not read has no limit: an office is term-limited only
  where the law says so. A consecutive limit resets after a term sat out; a
  lifetime limit does not. A law that is silent on terms already served counts
  them. Refusals are plain sentences with no citations. The governor turnover no
  longer retires every incumbent after two terms; it asks the same limit. Not
  modeled, and marked so in the code: a law that shortens or lengthens the
  sitting term is applied from the next term instead, and records that it was. A
  partial term served after a vacancy counts as a whole term.

- **People a law costs band together, and residents contact officials and attend
  meetings** When a law costs at least six residents of a town a tenth of a
  month's pay or more, a group forms there against it. Each person decides
  whether to join from how hard the law hit them, their temperament, and whether
  they already know a member through family or work. The group presses
  legislators to change the law and works against the candidates its members
  blame in the next town election. Each year, about a fifth of a town's adults
  contact an elected official and about a quarter attend a meeting of the town's
  government. Older and longer-settled residents, people with a job, people a
  law cost money, group members, quick reactors and people with strong feelings
  about an official do it more often. Where no local government sits, residents
  write to their governor instead.

- **People remember hearing about a law in the news** Reading a published story
  about an enacted law's effects now records that the reader heard about the law
  from the news. The record keeps the story and its supporting evidence
  together, survives saving, and is written only once for the same reading and
  reported effect. Hearing the story carries no money or opinion weight.

- **Hire a campaign manager with real experience** When your campaign can afford
  a salary through election day, people you know, and people the party chapter
  organizer knows, who have worked on campaigns before can be offered as your
  manager. Once hired, the manager draws up your weekly plan from their own
  experience and temperament, and the salary comes out of the campaign treasury.

- **Choosing a hometown shows its state's housing and income benchmarks** When
  you pick where your life starts, the game now shows published figures about
  that state: the federal fair market rent for a two-bedroom unit, area median
  family income, how housing prices compare with the nation, and the
  unemployment rate. Each line names its measure, area and year, and only
  figures public by the day your life begins appear. For a January 2026 start
  that is the fiscal year 2025 rent benchmark. Each of the fifty-six places also
  has a record of the issues that dominate its politics, waiting on research.
  None is filled in yet.

- **A passed city, county or state sales tax taxes what a household really
  spends** A passed sales tax, where the state lets that level levy one, now
  taxes the food and bills a household actually pays each month, to the person
  who paid, on the day the month is settled. A month the household could not pay
  for is not taxed, and spending from before the tax took effect is never taxed.

- **Illness goes around town, from person to person, and officials close schools
  and cancel meetings** An illness now reaches a town from outside, more often
  in winter, and moves through the people the world knows by name: the people
  someone lives with, their family, the people they work beside, the people at
  their school, and the people they know. Your journal says who came down sick
  and who they caught it from when it happens to you or someone in your home.
  Nobody catches it by chance: a person falls ill when the week's exposure from
  the sick people they spent it with, weighed by their age, their health and how
  careful they are, is high enough. A case is an ordinary illness that raises
  the person's risk of dying while it lasts, more for a serious case: an
  infant's, a person of 80 or older, or someone already fighting another
  illness. A school's principal decides each week whether to close for a week,
  from how many students and staff are out sick and how cautious they are, and
  reopens it when fewer are sick. A closed school passes nothing on. The town
  council's chair cancels a meeting when too few members are well enough for a
  quorum, or when enough of the town is sick. A worker out sick, or home with a
  sick child, misses those workdays, and in a part-time job, which carries no
  paid sick leave, the missed days come off the next paycheck. The adult who
  stays home is the one in the household with the fewest paid hours. When enough
  new people fall sick in a week, the illness becomes public news that the local
  paper can pick up. Every rate is a placeholder until it is researched.

- **Independent people counter a proposed meeting time** A person with a marked
  independent manner now leans toward offering another day when someone proposes
  a meeting time, because they prefer to arrange their own time. They still want
  to meet, so the lean argues for a different day and not for saying no. The
  same person answers differently with and without the trait.

- **Jobs lists real openings in your town, and employers can say no** Jobs now
  shows openings at your town's own employers instead of the same three jobs
  everywhere. Each listing gives the job, the employer, where the work is, the
  pay by the hour or as a yearly salary, the usual weekly hours and the last day
  applications are taken. Your county and city governments are employers in
  every state and D.C., and a business hires for the work its staff already do,
  at about what it pays them. You can apply, or ask someone you know who works
  there to put in a word. The employer answers a few days later and can turn you
  down. An offer waits a few days for your answer and then lapses. Once you
