  accept, you start on a set day. If you don't come in, the employer may call
  with a new date or withdraw the offer. A job you start pays weekly. Every date
  is fixed when it is set, so saving and reloading never moves it. The old three
  jobs are still under Other work for anyone already doing one. How often
  openings appear, how often employers hire and the public-office pay are
  placeholders until research comes back.

- **The Jobs screen says the lowest legal pay where you live and which law set
  it** The Jobs screen now opens with the lowest pay the law allows where you
  live. It says whether federal or state law sets the rate. After a raise is
  enacted, it names the new rate, the rate it replaced and the day it took
  effect. It reads the same rate paychecks use, so the line and the pay agree.
  Where a state's rate is not known, the line is left out instead of showing
  zero.

- **The Journal says when a law reached you** The Journal now says when a law
  changed your money. It shows a raise that a wage law gave your pay, with the
  old and new amounts. It shows a rent change that a housing law set, and income
  tax or a leave premium that a new law took from a paycheck or stopped taking.
  Any other time a law's record names you shows up there too. In a watched
  Vicksburg, Mississippi world, a federal minimum wage law raised 39 workers'
  pay on July 1, 2028, and each of their Journals now says so.

- **Court rosters show named judges** New games seat fictional, named judges in
  the saved federal and state court catalog. Each generated judge has a stable
  person identity, a dated prior legal career, a qualification record, and a
  seat tenure. A court roster can show the judge's portrait and the date the
  recorded tenure began. Looking at the roster spends no game time. The new
  opening is versioned. Earlier replay descriptors rebuild the same opening they
  recorded, and older saves gain no invented judges. The opening biographies and
  age ranges are game-authored placeholders; judicial philosophy, selection
  proceedings, and knowledge-limited trait disclosure remain later work. A
  separate UI change mounts the roster.

- **Court rosters open from Government** The State and Federal levels of
  Politics > Who governs now show the saved court roster. A judge opens the
  existing person card. Older saves with no judiciary record continue without a
  roster.

- **A law's page says what it did to the state, and Conditions shows how the
  state is doing** The world tracks how each state is doing: the share without
  health insurance, poverty, test scores, turnout, homelessness, violent crime
  and more. It also records every law that moves them. Until now only the
  developer's world report read those records, so a law that lowered
  homelessness changed nothing a player could see. A law's own page now says
  what it is doing to each condition it moves, for example: "People homeless per
  10,000 people in Derby Line, Vermont: 57 per 10,000 people when the law took
  effect, 38.1 per 10,000 people now. This law lowers it by about 37.8%." A law
  that another law has replaced says so, and a law whose effect comes later
  names the month it starts. Politics, then Issues and budget, now has a
  Conditions page. It shows each condition in the player's state as it stands
  now and where it began. It names what is moving each one, and puts the ones a
  law is moving first. Reading it passes no time.

- **A law passed in the game can change the rules of office** A bill can now
  carry a clause that changes a rule of government: how many seats a chamber
  has, how long a term runs, how old someone must be to serve, or how long they
  must have lived in the state or district. A state constitutional amendment can
  carry the same kind of change. Nothing changes while the bill is only a bill.
  Once it becomes law, the new rule applies from the day the act takes effect,
  and the old rule still governs every day before that. Where the game does not
  yet know a state's own rule for when its acts take effect, the change takes
  effect ninety days after the act is recorded, and the record says that this is
  the game's default. Rules that no law can change yet (term start dates,
  election calendars, the form of a legislature, local charters) are refused
  with the reason, not recorded as if they had acted. The constitutional
  workspace now says in plain words which rule a ratified amendment changes.

- **A law that changes something in town makes the local paper** When a law
  raises paychecks, sets rents, collects a tax or pays a benefit to people in a
  town, the town's paper and the state's papers can now report it the week it
  lands: "LB 900, 2026 raised pay for 17 workers." A year after a law takes
  effect, the papers in each place it governs report where each outcome it moves
  stands against the month before it took effect, such as the homelessness count
  after a housing-voucher law, and they do the same a year after a repeal. The
  people the law reached, its sponsor and the members who voted on it and live
  there learn of the story. Before, a law made the news only when it passed.

- **A law that places a duty on a body now does something** A passed law that
  requires a class of body to act, such as a utility keeping a plan for
  continuing service or a state agency publishing how its posts are classified,
  now records that duty. On the date the law sets, the law's page says how many
  of the bodies on record met it. Where no one is on record as working at a
  body, or the law covers only bodies above a size the world does not record,
  the page says that part is not known rather than guessing. Before this change,
  the law's page said nothing acted on these sections.

- **Laws on policy questions now change life in the towns they govern.** What
  the law in force says on a policy question, from voter ID and Medicaid to rent
  limits and preschool, now feeds the world's causes and effects. The first to
  reach people: a state law restricting abortion raises births in its towns by
  about 2.3%, starting about seven months after it takes effect, as births rose
  in states that enforced bans. More outcomes follow as the world starts
  tracking them.

- **A passed law's page says who it applies to** The section of a law that says
  who qualifies, or who must follow it, now shows on the law's page in the words
  the law was passed with. Where the town has bodies of that kind on record, the
  page counts them. Where the answer turns on something the world does not
  track, such as a household's income or a bridge's inspection rating, the page
  says who meets it is not known yet, rather than showing zero. A section that
  only states why the law was written is no longer listed as a part nothing acts
  on.

- **A legislature ends its session when its leaders decide** Before, a
  legislature in play sat until the last day its law allowed, and only
  Kentucky's sessions ever ended. A new law that waits a number of days after
  the session counted from that legal limit. Now each chamber's majority caucus
  adjourns the regular session once the budget has passed both chambers and none
  of its own members' bills is still pending. Bills other members carry die with
  the session where the state's rules say pending bills do. The legal limit
  still ends a session the leaders have not closed, and it now comes from each
  state's own limit wherever the game has read one. A new law's waiting period
  counts from the day the leaders adjourned. A bill the governor vetoes after
  the session has closed waits for the legislature's next sitting, where the
  members vote on the veto, unless the state's rules say pending bills die at
  adjournment.

- **The player's court cases, sentences and clemency requests in one record**
  The player's own legal record is ready for the Legal tab of their dossier. It
  lists each case once the player is charged, with where it stands: the hearing
  date, a plea entered, a retrial after a hung jury, or how it ended. It lists
  each sentence with its term and whether it is being served today, and each
  clemency request with every answer given so far. It offers a plea or a request
  only when the court or the clemency authority would take one, and otherwise
  gives the reason in the same words the action would. Reading the record spends
  no game time and records nothing.

- **Your own record has a Legal tab** Open your own record and choose Legal to
  see where you stand with the law: every charge filed against you and where it
  stands, the hearing date, each sentence and its term, and every request for
  clemency with the answers so far. While a charge is waiting for your plea you
  can plead guilty or not guilty there, and while a sentence is running you can
  ask for clemency where the law of your place allows it. Other people's records
  have no Legal tab.

- **Members of Congress see the committees they sit on** A member of Congress's
  office card now names the committees they sit on, for example "You sit on the
  Committee on Agriculture and the Committee on Armed Services." These are the
  same committees whose votes count the member when a bill comes out of
  committee. A state legislator's office record says why they have no committee
  seat. Either the chamber has no committees in the game yet, or its seats went
  to the members who were already serving. It no longer says "not appointed"
  when the chamber has no committees at all.

- **Carry a life into Begin** A character present during the years before Begin
  keeps their recorded family, work, money, and memories when play starts.
  Creator choices about work, keeping in touch, and learning become recorded
  aims that guide the character's decisions during those years. A character who
  dies before Begin cannot enter play.

- **The TV and the newspaper at home change every day** The television in your
  room now shows a broadcast from a real station in your world: its logo in a
  corner, the day's program or its own top story along the bottom, and a ticker
  of the day's headlines, each naming the paper that ran it. The newspaper on
  the table is a small front page with your town's paper's masthead, the day's
  date and its lead story when it has one. Both change with each day, and
  clicking either still opens the story to read.

- **A life can start on Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands, American Samoa or the
  Northern Mariana Islands** The new-game place list now offers all fifty-six
  places: the fifty states, D.C., Puerto Rico, Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands,
  American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands. Before, only Puerto Rico of
  the five territories could be chosen, because the Census list the game's towns
  come from covers the states, D.C. and Puerto Rico and nothing else. Each of
  the four new territories offers a short list of its well-known villages and
  towns for now, such as Dededo on Guam, Charlotte Amalie on St. Thomas, Pago
  Pago on Tutuila and Garapan on Saipan. The full lists are filed as research,
  and the placeholder towns will be replaced when the answer arrives. Each
  territory is its own government. None of them is given a legislature drawn
  from the states, and the introduction to a new life now says what a territory
  is: not a state, with its own Governor and legislature, a Delegate (or, for
  Puerto Rico, a Resident Commissioner) in the U.S. House, and no seat in the
  Senate. It used to call Puerto Rico "Your state government" and say no
  governor was recorded there.

- **A cap on loan rates closes storefront lenders** A state law capping consumer
  loan rates now lowers the number of payday, title and installment lenders in
  that state by about a third, a year after it takes effect, and repealing a cap
  brings them back. An Act of Congress capping consumer loan interest does the
  same in every state that did not already cap rates. Each state starts with its
  real count of consumer lenders per 100,000 residents; Alaska, Vermont and the
  territories are unknown until their counts are read.

- **Towns now have ordinary crime** Towns now have crime. Assaults, robberies,
  home burglaries and vandalism happen in the background each month. A reported
  crime goes into the town's police log, where the local paper can pick it up,
  and the victim is never named. An unreported one stays private to the people
  it happened to. The month after a report, the police either make an arrest or
  they don't. Every rate is a placeholder and is the same in every town, so a
  village is as busy as a city for now. Crime does not yet change anyone's vote,
  and nobody is charged or tried yet.

- **A research question asks which cities and counties set their own minimum
  wage.** The research queue now holds a question about the cities and counties
  that set their own minimum wage: which ones, at what rate today, and on what
  schedule the rate rises. Until it is answered, a job's pay floor is the higher
  of the federal and state rate, and the game starts from the most common real
  rule, no local minimum, and marks it as estimated from the average.

- **Local service funding can lead to repairs** In local governments using the
  game's shared rules, a manager can carry out approved service funding.
  Payments use the government's cash, and completed repairs can return a service
  unit to operation later.

- **A recorded term-limit bar now reaches the legislator it affects.** The state
  legislative term-limit path now has a person-level landing for an incumbent
  whose saved candidacy-intent event records that the law barred them from
  seeking another term. The landing uses the existing law-exposure history,
  names the legislator and source event, records a nonmoney cost, and does not
  create a new eligibility or candidacy decision. Replaying the writer is
  idempotent. Independent redistricting, automatic voter registration, and
  broadened local authority still lack a saved individual exposure record that
  this batch can consume. No aggregate turnout estimate is converted into an
  individual voter decision.

- **A town's term-limit bar now reaches the council member it affects.** When a
  town's council term-limit ordinance bars a sitting member from another term,
  the saved retirement event now names that member and the law exposure history
  records the cost for them, as the state legislative path already does. The
  landing reads only the saved bar and the law in force on that date; it does
  not decide who may run. Replaying it is idempotent.

- **A district map drawn under the commission law now reaches the members it
  pairs.** When a town's independent district commission law is in force and the
  map it draws puts two sitting council members in one district, each of those
  members now carries a recorded exposure to the law in their history. It
  records that their eligibility to seek another term changed; it does not
  decide who runs or wins. The relationship-record cost with people left outside
  the new lines is not built yet.

- **People with a substance use disorder ask for the funded harm reduction
  service** Before, a funded harm reduction law moved money to an operator, but
  no resident had a reason to ask for a visit. Now a resident who holds the
  substance use record on their private health record asks the service for a
  visit; full-time work holds a person back, and people without the record have
  no reason to ask. The shared funded-service test world also keeps its own wage
  questions, so the service tests run again.

- **Ending cash bail now reaches the defendant it releases or holds.** Where the
  end-cash-bail law is in force, the saved decision about a named defendant
  (released without bail, or held in jail by a judge) now writes a recorded
  exposure to the law in their history, with Journal and news wording for the
  new court-rule channel. The judge's decision still decides release or
  detention; the landing only records what it meant for the person. Where money
  bail stands, nothing is written. The other three LW-17 laws are not landed
  yet.

- **A mandatory minimum now reaches the defendant it binds.** Where the
  mandatory-minimum law is in force and the judge's saved decision says it
  required a jail term, the sentence now writes a recorded exposure to that law
  in the named defendant's history, shown through the court-rule channel. The
  judge's decision and the court's term range still set the sentence; the
  landing only records what the law meant for the person. Where the law is not
  in force, or the judge was not bound, nothing is written.

- **A felony sentence now suspends your vote, and the state law decides whether
  it returns** Before, a felony jail sentence never touched a person's vote, so
  a state's law on restoring the vote after a sentence had no one it applied to.
  Now a jail term over a year suspends the vote while it runs. When the sentence
  ends the vote returns where the state's law says so and stays withheld where
  it does not, a pardon returns it, and the Journal says which law did it.

- **Medicaid expansion covers named people, and a work requirement takes it
  away** Each month the World reads who holds Medicaid expansion coverage:
  adults aged 19 to 64 in a state whose law expands Medicaid, with household pay
  at or under 138% of the poverty line. Where a work requirement is in force,
  the state's own or the federal one from 1/1/2027, an adult who works fewer
  than 80 hours a month and is not exempt loses it. Covered people aged 55 to 64
  face 9.4% lower death risk, from a year after the expansion took effect, and a
  repeal ends coverage at the next monthly pass.

- **New people come into a life, and the player can go and meet them** Before, a
  new character knew the people they started with and nobody else. A 24-year-old
  in Houma or Reno knew the same two people after six months as on their first
  day. Now new people come into a life as time passes, and the People screen has
  a "Meet somebody new" choice: at work, on the same program, through a group
  the player belongs to, or through a friend. Only settings with somebody real
  in them are offered. Nobody is invented to be met, and living in the same town
  is not a way of meeting somebody. Meeting somebody makes the two of you
  acquainted; asking them to meet and then spending the evening together is what
  makes a friend. Played through those routes, a new character in Houma or Reno
  who joins the walking group has a friend they did not start with inside their
  first month. How often people meet on their own, and how much one evening
  together counts for, are labeled calibrations: ChatGPT found no rate to use
  for either. Also fixed: asking two people for the same evening could crash
  passing time when both said yes. The second person now offers a different day.
  A meeting the player asked for is also named after the other person on their
  calendar, not after the player.

- **The game can now read a state's minimum wage against what its workers
  typically earn** Before: researchers find that a minimum wage set well above
  what a typical worker earns costs low-wage jobs, but the game had no way to
  read how high a state's minimum sits against its typical pay, so that effect
  had no cause to react to. After: the game reads each state's minimum wage in
  force, including any change a state law makes in play, against the state's own
  recorded median earnings spread over a full-time year. The effect on low-wage
  jobs is now ready on its cause side. It does not move any number yet, because
  low-wage jobs are not yet one of the numbers the game keeps for a place; that
  is the next piece. Nothing a player sees changes today.

- **A state law raising the minimum wage raises your neighbors' pay** Before, a
  state's minimum wage was fixed at its 2026 rate for the whole game, whatever
  its legislature passed. Now, a state law can set a new minimum wage from the
  day it takes effect. Everyone in town paid less than the new rate gets a
  raise, starting with their first pay period that begins on or after that day,
  and the paycheck names the law that raised it. In a test game in Omaha, a
  Nebraska law raising the minimum wage to $18.00 an hour raised 14 of 79 town
  jobs, and 52 paychecks were paid at the new rate within 100 days.

- **A minimum-wage law now raises the pay of every job below the new floor, and
  a first job starts at the state minimum.** When a federal minimum-wage raise
  takes effect, every job in town paid below the new floor is raised to it from
  the first pay period that begins on or after that day, and each raise is
  written down as a change of pay terms that names the law. The floor where a
  job is stands at the higher of the federal rate, the state's own rate and any
  state law passed in play, so a Tennessee job below the federal floor rises
  with the raise while a Washington job that already pays more does not. A
  teenager's first job now starts at the state's minimum wage for that date,
  where it used to start at $7.25 everywhere. Jobs found through the job market
  are raised the same way, and listings never start below the floor. A law that
  lowers the floor cuts no one's pay. City and county minimums are not on file
  yet, so none is claimed. Until the level of a passed federal raise is
  researched, it takes the rate the outcome links are calibrated for, $15.00 an
  hour (marked PLACEHOLDER in the code).

- **Floor amendments record their messaging purpose** Player floor amendments
  offered through the legislative session now record the messaging motive,
  including when the chamber rejects the amendment.

- **Chamber votes can read recorded leadership strain** The shared chamber vote
  evaluator accepts recorded relationship-strain reasons from a cross-party
  bargain and weighs them with the member's other reasons.

- **Senators decide cloture from their own reasons** Cloture uses the chamber's
  unlimited-debate rule and cloture bar, and a member may hold the floor for
  another sitting day when their reasons support it. The roll call and floor
  extension are recorded with the member and the return date.

- **Member absences can deny a legislative quorum** Members decide whether to
  attend or stay away using their own view, recorded leader and home reasons,
  personal traits, and the chamber's attendance rules. The shared floor writer
  records a failed quorum and its roll call; when enough members remain,
  business proceeds under the same quorum rule.

- **Chambers can record a sine-die motion vote** The existing procedural-motion
  path records a chamber's sine-die vote and links the carried-motion action to
  its ordinary roll-call record. Session-completion recording remains on the
  dated legislative queue.

- **Money and property says what new laws did to money** Money and property now
  has a section called "What new laws did to money". It lists the five most
  recent times a new law reached the player's own pay, taxes, benefits or rent,
  or a family member's, with the date and the amount. It also says what each law
  did across the player's town, such as a minimum wage raise that added a total
  each month to the pay of a number of residents. Each law's name opens its
  page. Before any new law has reached anyone's money, the section says so.

- **People move for more reasons than floods and taxes** People in your town now
  leave for more reasons. Someone who lost a job this year and hasn't found
  another is three times as likely to move away. When the town's unemployment
  runs above the nation's, more people leave. An unusually bad quarter of
  assaults and robberies pushes people out. Someone leaving leans toward a state
  where a relative lives, and the move says so. Moves between states also read a
  state's unemployment against the nation's when the economy records that state
  separately. Schools, housing costs, climate, politics, age and military
  service don't move anyone yet. Every strength is a placeholder until research
  comes back.

- **Police arrest a named resident for a reported crime, and prosecutors take
  the case** A reported crime in town is now laid at the door of the resident
  whose own circumstances point to it: being young, being out of work (more so
  for a crime that takes money or goods), a past record, knowing the victim, and
  a taste for risk. Nobody is picked by chance, and when nobody the world names
  fits, the case stays open. Police arrest that person when the victim knows
  them, when they have a record, or when their circumstances point to them
  plainly. The arrest is public and names them, and it goes to prosecutors,
  where the case is charged, pleaded or tried, and sentenced. A resident already
  answering for a recent case is not blamed for a new one. Every weight is a
  placeholder until it is researched.

- **The District of Columbia has its own mayor, and short-term states keep short
  terms** A life in the District of Columbia now opens under the District's own
  chief executive — a mayor, under the District's own Code, not a fifty-first
  governor. The District is one government, so everything district-wide and
  everything citywide names the same jurisdiction: one office, one public
  account, no second mayor beside the first. A mayor elected in November takes
  office on January 2nd, the day the District's Code names, rather than on the
  game's usual first Monday. New Hampshire and Vermont run two-year terms and
  elect every two years, instead of inheriting a four-year default nobody had
  read. Vermont's and the District's terms come from their own instruments where
  those fix a value, and from the game's disclosed rule where they do not —
  Vermont's constitution sets a two-year term but no calendar start date, so the
  term length is the state's and the start day is the game's. Every office now
  tells the player, plainly, how long the term runs, when it begins and how
  often it is elected — and nothing else. Where a rule was read from a law,
  which law it was is a record the game keeps, not a line on the screen a
  candidate files from.

- **Twelve new places to be in the world** The world has twelve new painted
  places: a polling place set up in a school gym, a school board meeting room,
  the motor vehicle office, a local newsroom, a courthouse hallway, a hotel
  ballroom set for a fundraiser, the public library, a police station lobby, a
  fire station bay, a county party office, a small-town diner and a neighborhood
  barbershop. Most come in morning, midday, night and rain light. They appear as
  the game gives people reasons to go there.

- **A new life opens with a close family and a readable earlier life** A new
  ordinary life can begin with parents, a sibling and grandparents in the
  record. Older adults may also have a partner and grown child. Shared moments
  appear in the Journal across childhood and adult years, and the people in
  those moments are the same people the world knows as family. Newly generated
  close relatives have distinct first names. Earlier shared moments, moves and
  jobs are spread through the year; school starts in late summer and school
  completion falls in spring. A birthday remains a birthday. An adult with
  recorded work and active pay terms starts with a personal balance shown in
  Personal. That opening balance is a seeded game savings profile, not a claim
  that every past wage was paid. The local employer and monthly wage are still
  the game's stated business placeholders. Pay actually earned after the life
  begins is recorded separately, month by month. A life without known pay keeps
  its starting money unknown.

- **News and economy screens follow the offices you hold and the outlets you
  read** The economy and budget screens show what the offices a player holds
  make public, and the front page lists only the outlets the reader's own habits
  include, with press coverage that follows an office holder's travel and
  nationally significant state events.

- **News outlets have owners, and an owner decides for every outlet it holds at
  once** Every newsroom in a new life now belongs to somebody: a private equity
  chain, a family newspaper group, a broadcast group, a nonprofit trust, or its
  own independent publisher. The press desk shows who owns each outlet. Owners
  review their holdings on their own schedule and act across everything they own
  in one decision. A chain can eliminate newsroom jobs at all of its outlets in
  one round, and the reporters who lose them are gone from the desk, so there
  are fewer people to cover the places those papers served. A chain can also buy
  an independent outlet, which then falls under its next decision. Owners can
  already decide to share stories across their outlets, send them all the same
  commentary to run, or combine newsrooms into one. Those decisions go on the
  record, but they do not yet change what the outlets publish. The owners and
  what each may do are data that a mod can replace. All of them are fictional.

- **The news page lists the laws that reach you and what each one did** Before,
  a law that passed showed up only on its own bill page. The news page for your
  town said who held office and what happened lately, but nothing about the laws
  in force where you live. Now the news page has a "Laws that reach you"
  section: national law, your state's, and your town's, newest first. Each one
  says when it was enacted and what it changed in the world, such as a tax and
  what it has collected, or money it provided and how much has been paid out. A
  law with no part the game acts on yet says so plainly. Laws from other states
  never appear.

- **The News overview says what the laws in force changed where you live** The
  News overview now has a "What the laws changed" section. When a law in force
  answers a policy question differently from where the place began and that
  moved a condition where you live, News names the law, says which condition it
  moved, and gives the value with and without it. Before, those effects were
  worked out and never shown. A place where no law has moved anything shows
  nothing, and no line names a source.

- **A news story about a bill opens the bill's page** A story on the News front
  page that reports on a bill now names the bill under the story, and the name
  opens the bill's own page, where what the bill did once it became law is
  written. A law's page that another law has replaced now names the law that
  decides the question instead of saying "another law".

- **Watch the world with nobody played** The title screen has a new button,
  Watch the world. One press opens a real town in a state picked at random, with
  nobody played in it, and the world goes on by itself. Its people work, die and
  hold elections, its parties meet, and its papers print the news. While you
  watch, you can let a day, a week, a month or a year pass, or press Run and let
  it go on week after week until you press Pause. The World record lists every
  bill and what became of it, every constitutional amendment, every election and
  who won it, who holds office, and every living person, with the news and what
  has happened lately. Choosing a person shows their whole record: their work,
  their party and everything that has happened to them, including things nobody
  else in the world knows. You can save a watched world, and it comes back as a
  watched world. The save list shows it as "Watching the world", not under the
  name of the resident the town was opened around. What a watched world does not
  have yet: nobody in it introduces bills, ordinances or amendments without a
  player, so the laws section stays empty for now. That work is under way
  elsewhere, and the record will fill in as it lands.

- **An official who dies in office has a funeral, and may lie in state first** A
  public official who dies in office now has a funeral a week later, attended by
  family and the colleagues who served beside them. A president who dies in
  office first lies in state in the U.S. Capitol Rotunda, and a governor in the
  state capitol.

- **A favor you actually did can come back, larger** If you agree to help
  somebody with something and then actually carry it out, that person may come
  back to you later asking for something considerably bigger. The scene says how
  many times you have helped them before, because the whole question it puts to
  you is what your earlier help is now being read as meaning. You can help
  again, help and say plainly that this is not the same favor, or say that the
  first one did not buy this one. It only happens where you really did the
  thing. Agreeing and never getting round to it does not count, and refusing
  certainly does not — the game will not have somebody turn up on the strength
  of help you did not give. It happens once, and it waits for a quiet stretch
  rather than arriving on top of everything else.

- **Nine more real numbers for every state, and two cause-and-effect links now
  run** Before: the game tracked what researchers say police staffing, speed
  limits, taxes, heat and air conditioning do to a state, but it had no starting
  number for any of them, so none of those effects could happen. After: every
  state, D.C. and territory now starts from the latest published figure for
  sworn police officers per 1,000 people, people in state prison per 100,000,
  babies born at low birth weight, days at or above 90 degrees, homes with air
  conditioning, the highest speed limit on rural interstates, traffic deaths per
  100 million miles, the top state corporate income tax rate and the effective
  property tax rate on homes. Where no source reports a place, it starts at the
  national average and says so. From there each number drifts with the times, as
  the game's other measures do. Two effects now act. A state that hires more
  officers than it started with sees less violent crime, about 3.4% less for
  each 10% more. A state that raises its rural interstate speed limit sees more
  traffic deaths, about 3.8% more for each 5 miles an hour.

- **Name plates for the President and Vice President** On the opening White
  House card, a small plate over each leader's head shows their name and office.
  On a phone, where the picture is cropped, each plate stays inside the part of
  the picture that shows.

- **A state paid leave program pays part of the wages a serious illness takes**
  Where a state's paid family and medical leave program is in force, a part-time
  worker home with a serious illness, or with a seriously ill child, now
  receives a benefit from the program for the unpaid days. Days of the worker's
  own illness count after a 7-day waiting period, and days caring for a child
  count from the first day. The benefit replaces the program's lower-wage share
  of the pay lost, up to its weekly maximum, and it is paid out of the state
  account the premiums went into. Where no program is in force, or a law ends
  one, nothing is paid.

- **Screens, boards and posters in the places you go show what is happening**
  The screens, boards, posters and papers painted into the places you visit now
  show what is going on in your world that day, instead of sitting blank. - A
  county commission's screen shows the county's own latest votes and the bills
  filed there. - In a campaign office or a party office, the cork board carries
  posters for the people running in your races, and the whiteboard lists your
  own appointments for the week. - At an office desk, a clipboard holds your
  appointments and a sheet on the board lists the bills filed in your town. -
  The brochures at a clerk's counter and the notice board in the park describe
  how your town's public services are doing. - The screens at a convention hall
  or an election-night party show a candidate or the evening news, and for a few
  days after a race is decided they show the count. - A television in a living
  room shows your local station. When there is nothing to show, the surface
  stays as it was painted. Nothing is made up to fill it. At night, the paper
  and card on the walls dim with the room while the screens stay lit.

- **Council-president parishes have a parish president** Louisiana's 21 parishes
  governed by a council and an elected president now seat a parish president
  beside the parish council, as Jefferson and St. Tammany do. Police jury
  parishes still have no chief executive of their own.

- **Talk with the party organizer about running** Going to a planned talk about
  running for office now opens the community room with its organizer present.
  You can ask about qualifications or filing, stay through the conversation, or
  leave and return home. The visit and the words you heard remain in your
  history. A time advance that stops at a calendar commitment now names that
  commitment.

- **A law you pass changes what it governs, and its page says what changed**
  Before this change, whether a law did anything depended on the route it came
  through. When you passed a bill, its tax took effect, but its spending never
  became money the state could spend. When a legislature passed a bill without
  you, it was the other way around. A city ordinance did neither. Every passed
  law now goes through the same step, whoever passed it. A tax becomes a tax
  that collects. An appropriation becomes money the governor can commit and pay
  out. A law's page now has a "What the law changed" list with a line for each
  effect. It says what the law made available to spend, and how much of that has
  been committed and paid out. It says when a tax took effect and what it has
  collected so far. It also names each part of the law that nothing in the world
  acts on yet, instead of letting you assume it worked. What those parts should
  do, and who really bears each one, has been asked of the research desk.

- **A paycheck owes the taxes that apply where you live** Take a shift at the
  shop in Ely, Nevada, and $5.50 of the $72.00 is withheld: $4.46 for Social
  Security and $1.04 for Medicare. Nevada has no income tax, so the game records
  a lawful $0 for it. The employer owes its own matching share. Each tax records
  what was owed, withheld and still unpaid, and anything unpaid stays owed in
  the save. Where the research cannot price a tax, the game marks it unknown
  rather than guessing: federal income tax withholding, state income tax in the
  40 states and D.C. that have one, and city and county wage taxes.

- **Paychecks now withhold federal and state income tax** Before, a paycheck
  took out Social Security and Medicare and nothing else, so nobody in the game
  paid income tax. Now each paycheck also withholds federal income tax at the
  2026 rates, for a single filer or a married couple filing jointly. It also
  withholds state income tax for single filers in the 32 states whose 2026 rates
  are known, and that money goes to the state rather than to Washington. States
  with no wage tax withhold nothing. Montana is now counted among the states
  that tax wages. Where a rate isn't known yet, the tax is left unknown instead
  of zero. That covers head-of-household filers, married filers' state tax, the
  10 states that use personal exemptions, the territories and local wage taxes.
  A worker's filing status comes from their own family: married, a single parent
  living with their child, or single.

- **People are known for qualities of their own** Beyond the five temperament
  traits, the adults you meet are now each known for one or two named qualities
  drawn from a catalog of ninety-eight: somebody can be industrious, cocky,
  blunt or forgiving, and those show on their card. A quality that is a marked
  habit rather than one end of a scale, such as cocky, is never given an
  invented opposite: somebody who is not cocky is not thereby humble. What
  happens to a person can move these qualities the same way it moves their
  temperament. They do not yet change any decision a person makes. Your own
  character is not given any; children receive theirs once they are adults.

- **People who helped you may call to ask for help back** Somebody who once
  helped you, still expects something back and now needs help, whether with a
  race they are running or with finding work, may call to ask. Whether they call
  is their own decision, from what they expect, what they need and who they are;
  somebody who helped out of kindness never does. You can say you will help,
  offer something smaller, say not now, or say no. A yes is a promise the game
  remembers and judges by what you do next; a no strains things between you. A
  person who still feels they owe you is also likelier to agree to meet you, or
  to back your campaign when you ask. More of what people say now counts as a
  promise. A neighbor who agrees to go to the public meeting for you, or a
  coworker who offers to check a case, now owes you that. At your own campaign
  town hall, somebody may ask where you stand on a bill still in play in the
  body you are running for. Saying you would vote for it or against it is a
  public pledge, and your first vote on that question once you are seated says
  whether you kept it.

- **People in your life go after their own goals while time passes** The people
  around a life now act on their own private goals each week, whether or not the
  player looks at them. Somebody in town whose job ends starts looking for work:
  they apply to openings the town's employers actually listed, hear back on the
  day the employer answers, and take the job and start it, or keep looking.
  Family, housemates and the people the player works with are given the same
  ordinary goals every generated person already had, and act on them: a person
  keeping up with people calls somebody they actually know, and the person
  called decides for themselves whether to talk. A step is never taken twice,
  and opening a screen changes nothing. When a step cannot be taken, the reason
  is recorded once, as a fact: no opening is listed, or nobody they know
  teaches. A learning goal with no route at all is set aside after a long
  stretch, and the person turns to the people around them instead. The earlier
  goal and its history stay. The player learns about this the way anybody would.
  A housemate or relative who applies somewhere mentions it, and the Journal
  records what they said. Nothing shows another person's private goal on a card.
  Somebody keeping time for themselves is also a little more likely to say no to
  an optional plan, including one the player asks them to.

- **People move in and out of your town** Your town no longer stands still. Four
  times a year the world checks who is leaving and who is arriving. A household
  with nothing holding it in place may pack up for somewhere else in the state
  or for another state. Newcomers arrive from elsewhere in the country, and each
  has a name and a record of where they came from. Nobody you live with moves on
  their own, and nobody with a job, a school place, a lease, a party membership
  or a campaign is moved either. Moving would strand those, and closing them
  properly is not built yet. This also adds the first society-wide movements: an
  exodus from the city, an exodus after the jobs leave, and a religious revival.
  The exodus after the jobs leave can begin when unemployment runs high, and
  while it lasts more people leave. Each movement begins and ends as a public
  event the newspapers can report. The other two movements are defined but begin
  only when a scenario starts them, because their real causes are not modeled
  yet. The pace is a placeholder until the historical rates come back from
  research. It applies to lives started from now on; an older save does not
  change.

- **People in a room pose by what they are doing** When you talk with someone,
  the person answering you now gestures as they explain, and the others in the
  room listen with their arms folded or a hand on the hip, turned toward whoever
  is speaking. Someone at a lectern stands at the podium, and someone at a desk
  or a table leans in or sits with their legs crossed. Each person keeps the
  same habits every time you see them, and a reserved person folds their arms
  more often. The new poses show as their paintings arrive. Until then, everyone
  stands or sits as before.

- **People feel the laws that reach them, and it shows in votes and talk** When
  an enacted law takes money from someone, the game now records it, and their
  spouse feels it too. A few days later they blame or credit whoever signed the
  law and the legislators whose votes they know of. Close followers of local
  news are likelier to know a vote, and people tell up to three others they live
  or work with, who think it over as something a friend went through. Town
  elections now weigh what residents think of each candidate's part in the laws
  they felt, with the half year before the vote counting more. A legislator who
  was blamed for a law leans toward a bill that changes it. Ask an adult what
  they think of the people in office, and they will say who voted for or signed
  the law that reached them and how it hit their household.

- **People sit, lean and speak where the picture has room for them** Before,
  only 11 place pictures marked where people could stand, and everyone stood
  facing you in a row, like models. A courtroom, the Senate floor or a state
  capitol had nowhere to put anyone at all. Now every place picture (all 50
  rooms and street scenes and all 56 state capitols) marks where people can be:
  open floor at several distances, the chairs at a desk or table, a bench or a
  sofa, the lectern, and a wall or doorway to lean on. Each spot knows which way
  a person there faces and how large they are at that distance, and a person on
  a stage, a dais or the capitol steps is sized for that raised floor. The
  people on shift in a place now take those spots: the city clerk sits behind
  the counter, office staff sit at their desks, others wait on the floor or by a
  wall, and nearer people are drawn in front of farther ones. Waiting people are
  set to fold their arms or rest a hand on a hip, and people at the side to turn
  toward the room; they show standing and facing you until those poses are
  painted. Each place also marks one spot for your own character, for the title
  screen.

- **Choose how personal events are worded in headlines and the Journal** Add a
  saved personal-life depiction preference with full, softened and summary-only
  wording for personal-event headlines and the Journal. It changes presentation
  only; recorded events remain unchanged. Older saves use the full wording by
  default.

- **The Journal records how your petition closed** When a petition you filed
  closes on its recorded deadline, your Journal now carries the closing, with
  the count of valid signatures against the number the place required. Before,
  the closing was recorded but never reached your Journal, because the filer was
  not listed as having taken part.

- **Fifty painted places now stand behind the opening and the places you go**
  Fifty painted places, 180 pictures in all, are now in the game. The opening
  tour shows your state capitol (a tower in Florida, Louisiana, Nebraska and
  North Dakota), the U.S. Capitol behind Congress, city hall behind your town,
  and your town's main street behind your life so far. During play, places that
  had no picture (school, work, the campaign doors and phone bank, the
  governor's office, courtrooms and council rooms, and the street on the way to
  a meeting) now show one, matched to the time of day and to rainy days. Your
  job decides your workplace, and your home decides the street you canvass. Home
  and the meeting room keep their current rooms.

- **Every state now has a real uninsured rate and poverty rate that laws and the
  economy move.** Each state, D.C. and Puerto Rico starts at its real 2024 share
  of people without health insurance and share of people in poverty, and both
  move month by month. Expanding Medicaid cuts a state's uninsured about 15%.
  The federal Medicaid work requirement, starting in 2027, raises the uninsured
  in states that cover low-income adults by about a third. Hard times raise both
  the uninsured and poverty, and a higher state minimum wage lowers poverty over
  the following years. Medicaid's work requirement is now its own policy
  question, separate from work rules for food and income assistance.

- **A charged player enters their own plea** Once prosecutors charge the player,
  the player can enter a plea before the hearing. A guilty plea is taken at the
  hearing and ends the case there, and the judge's sentence follows. A plea of
  not guilty, or no plea at all, sends the case to a jury. The player's case
  appears once they are charged, with the date of the hearing, any plea entered
  and how the case ended; a case that prosecutors are still weighing stays out
  of sight.

- **Read what a term means without leaving the screen** Institutional words like
  committee referral, appropriation and quorum now carry their explanation where
  you meet them. Open one and a short definition appears beside the word, with a
  way through to its full entry in the Guide. It works from the keyboard and
  with a screen reader, and it never covers the sentence you were reading. Every
  definition is the Guide's own, so a term reads the same everywhere it appears.

- **The game ships sixty-six policy questions a person can take a side on**
  Before, the catalog knew what politics is about — thirteen domains and a
  hundred and twenty-seven issues — and held no question anyone could be for or
  against. Every player-facing reader of that catalog reads propositions, and
  the game shipped none, so those screens had nothing to show on an ordinary
  first day. This adds sixty-six authored positions in their own pack,
  `us-policy-positions`, each naming a specific thing that could be done about
  one of the shipped issues and asking it as a question. Direction lives in the
  answer, not in the question; magnitude, where a position carries one, lives in
  its parameters. They are declared authored fiction, not law. The issues they
  point at are sourced, and the pack keeps its own provenance so the two are
  never confused: a stance is not a finding, and nothing here is a claim about
  what any real jurisdiction has done. Coverage is deliberately partial.

- **Every policy question now says which principles it engages** Before, a
  character could have a recorded conviction and the game had no way to work out
  what that conviction implied about any particular bill. A screen showing what
  somebody thinks of a question had to guess the connection, and inside a save a
  guess looks exactly like a grounded answer. Each of the sixty-six positions
  now declares the principles it engages and which way agreeing with it cuts —
  consistent with the principle, or against it. Fourteen principles come with
  them, chosen so that each is engaged from several domains rather than
  restating one question. Two values and not a scale, deliberately: an author
  can honestly say which way a question cuts, and could not honestly say how
  much. A position that engages no principle is written with none rather than
  with an empty list, so a consumer can tell "nobody has said" apart from
  "engages nothing".

- **A planned press exchange now shows the reporter's own newsroom, studio or
  booth** When you arrange an exchange with a reporter, the screen shows where
  it takes place instead of staying blank. A spoken exchange with a broadcaster
  shows its studio, a spoken exchange with an audio-only outlet shows its booth,
  and every other exchange shows the outlet's newsroom. A reporter with no
  recorded outlet still meets you in a newsroom. A check draws a generated world
  and confirms each outlet type picks its own place and an unarranged key picks
  none.

- **An ethics complaint now reaches your own state's ethics body in twenty-four
  states** An ethics complaint against a legislator or legislative candidate
  used to reach a real commission only in Kentucky. Everywhere else it went to a
  simulated oversight review that said on its face it could not discipline
  anyone. In twenty-three more states and the District of Columbia the complaint
  now goes to that state's own body, named as the state names it — the Ohio
  Joint Legislative Ethics Committee, the Nevada Commission on Ethics, the Texas
  Ethics Commission — and the proceeding is called what that state calls it. The
  steps run from the complaint through notice, an answer period, an inquiry and
  findings, and the body stops there: whether anyone is disciplined is decided
  separately, and the game says so rather than inventing a punishment. States
  nobody has read yet still reach the simulated review, which is the honest
  answer, not another state's commission.

- **A law that ends, extends or repeals a program now changes what can be
  funded** When a law set an end date for a program, extended it or repealed it,
  the program stayed open for new spending bills as if nothing had passed. Once
  the program's last day under the law has gone by, a new spending bill can no
  longer be written against it, and the list of programs says which law ended
  it. A later extension replaces an earlier end date, but nothing brings back a
  repealed program, and a law ends a program only in the place that passed it.
  The law's page says when the program ends or ended.

- **A protest you organized or attended shows the rally stage on the day it is
  held** On the day a protest is held, the screen shows the rally stage for the
  person who organized it and for each resident whose attendance was recorded.
  The organizer and the recorded attendees stand in the picture; nobody is added
  to fill the stage, and a resident who did not go sees no stage. The next day
  the stage clears.

- **Public employers can retain their source-backed government identity** An
  organization's dated profile can retain a source-backed public government
  identity. Pay and service readers can use the identity of the actual employer
  at the saved occurrence date and sequence. A workplace, budget funder or
  classification never supplies a missing employer identity, and older profiles
  remain unchanged.

- **Join the public meeting in person** The posted public meeting now opens in
  its room when you arrive. You can read the agenda, hear the people present,
  make a public comment, stay through the discussion, or leave early. Your visit
  and words remain in your history; a short visit does not count as full
  attendance. The discussion ends without an official vote.

- **More state chambers can consider federal amendments** Six more states can
  record their members' federal amendment votes under cited chamber rules.
  Elected-member and voting-member majorities remain distinct, and explicit vote
  floors remain required. States without an admitted rule or an implemented
  legal condition continue to await that evidence. Direct rules and cited
  bridges both require valid source quotes, HTTPS links, calendar dates and vote
  fractions before a chamber can use them.

- **Residents can petition to recall a town official** Your town's government
  screen now has a Recall section. Where your state's law lets towns recall
  their officials, you can start a petition against anyone sitting on the
  council. It circulates for as long as that state's law allows (thirty days in
  Nebraska, for example). When the window closes, it either falls short of
  signatures or goes to a recall election about two and a half months later. If
  the voters remove the official, the seat stays empty until the town's next
  regular election. If the official leaves office first, the recall lapses.
  Where the law gives towns no recall, as in Indiana, the section says so and
  offers nothing to press. Where a state's law on it has not been settled, the
  game gives that state a realistic rule drawn from the range other states use,
  the same every time. How many signatures a petition gathers and how the vote
  goes are placeholders until the research on recall returns: the game does not
  yet count a town's voters or measure how they feel about an official.
  Recalling governors, legislators and judges is not built yet.

- **Open the inspector from the settled Observer world** Observer can open the
  developer inspector using its paused current world. Back and Close restore the
  previous record view without advancing the clock or changing who is
  controlled.

- **Saved election results and meeting choices reach play** The watched World's
  record now displays saved congressional and state legislative result summaries
  in pages, with named winners available on demand. Attending a posted public
  meeting opens its recorded room at the start; the player can read the agenda,
  choose exact words for one comment, stay, make a brief visit, or leave. The
  new result labels and speech remain marked for overnight copy review.

- **Permit applications preserve the applicant's recorded choice** An
  application can be saved after the person's evidence-backed decision when its
  operative law and recorded issuing authority are available. Applications
  round-trip with saved worlds and retain their identity on repeat. Missing
  authority or undecided choice creates no application; submitting an
  application does not issue a permit or change permission.

- **The world introduction shows the countryside around where you live.** When a
  new life begins, the panel about your own town now opens on an illustration of
  the landscape that surrounds it, drawn for a region rather than for a state,
  so a place in west Texas and a place in eastern Oklahoma can share a picture
  that looks right for both. The picture also matches the time of year: a life
  begun in January never opens on a summer canopy, and one begun in July never
  opens on bare branches. Where more than one illustration genuinely fits, your
  town keeps the same one every time you look. If nothing in the collection
  genuinely fits where you live, the panel shows no picture at all. A familiar
  view is the whole point, and an unfamiliar one would be worse than none.

- **Renters pay rent to a real landlord** A rented home in town had no rent
  behind it, and the only rent in the game was one flat $900 inside your own
  monthly costs. Now every renting household pays rent on the first of each
  month to a landlord on record: a neighbor who owns their home, a realty or
  property-management firm, or the town's housing authority. The rent comes from
  the county's federal Fair Market Rent for a home with that many bedrooms,
  carried forward with prices, and a public housing rent is 30% of the
  household's income. When your household rents, your month's costs no longer
  include the flat $900; the real rent is paid to your landlord. A household
  that falls two months behind can be taken to court and evicted. Rent
  stabilization holds down yearly increases and makes covered renters move less,
  an inclusionary housing law sets aside affordable homes in new buildings, and
  a right to counsel gives tenants facing eviction a lawyer, so fewer lose their
  homes.

- **Taking campaign money can lead to prosecution, and jail takes people out of
  office and off the campaign trail** A finding that somebody took campaign
  money for themselves can now go to prosecutors, and that gets more likely with
  every finding against the person. Prosecutors may charge or decline, depending
  on how strong the evidence is. A charge can be dismissed, end in acquittal at
  trial, end in a guilty plea, or end in a conviction. A plea or conviction
  brings probation or jail, and jail is the less common of the two. The papers
  cover the charge, the outcome and the sentence. Someone sentenced to jail
  loses every office they hold. A jailed candidate stays on the ballot but can't
  campaign. The screen says so, and a jailed opponent stops campaigning too.
  Every chance, length and effect is a placeholder until the real rules come
  back from research.

- **You repeat the oath of office phrase by phrase, and choose what to swear
  on** Swearing-in is now a ceremony you take part in. A new legislator or
  governor chooses what to place a hand on: a Bible, a Hebrew Bible, a Quran, a
  copy of the state constitution, a copy of the Constitution of the United
  States, or nothing but a raised right hand. They also choose whether to swear,
  ending "So help me God", or to affirm. The officiant then says "Raise your
  right hand and repeat after me", and the player clicks each phrase of the oath
  to repeat it in their own name. The oath is recorded, with the choice, only
  when the last phrase is spoken. The oath opens with the words federal law
  requires of every state officeholder, to support the Constitution of the
  United States. The rest is a general wording shared by every state until each
  state's own oath is researched. Nothing in the world reacts yet to what the
  oath was taken on.

- **Neighbors now use the parks, libraries and transit your laws pay for.** When
  a parks, public land, library or transit law is funded and its operator is
  paid, people who live there decide for themselves whether to use it. A parent
  with a child at home or someone who wants time to themselves signs up for a
  park program. A worker whose job is in town asks for a ride, while one who
  works elsewhere does not. Someone whose reasons for and against weigh the same
  stays undecided. Whoever goes is counted as served once the visit is over, and
  only then.

- **Residents contact officials about recorded reasons** A resident's contact
  now carries the law effect, lived outcome, official view, or organized
  opposition that prompted it. A council resident with a recorded ward
  representative contacts that member. Contacts without a recorded reason remain
  general opinion calls.

- **Restoring the vote after a sentence changes a state's turnout** Where a
  state bars people from voting after their sentence, a law that restores the
  vote at the end of a sentence now changes the state's turnout. Restored voters
  join the eligible population but vote less often than others, so the share of
  eligible people who vote falls while the number of ballots rises. In Tennessee
  the turnout rate falls about 4 percent. The size depends on how many people
  each state bars after their sentence. Where the vote already returns by the
  end of a sentence, the law changes nothing.

- **Run for the U.S. House or Senate** Campaigns now offers a seat in Congress
  from the state a character lives in: every House district in the state, since
  a Representative need only live in the state, and whichever Senate seat is
  elected next. Filing opens a campaign in the seat's own November election. The
  winner sees the transition before January 3, takes the seat that day, and
  appears as its member in the Congress overview. Their office names the seat
  and the term. The background model that fills Congress no longer decides a
  seat the player is running for, and a player who holds a seat keeps it only by
  filing again and winning. Winning creates no separate legislature job. Floor
  votes, committees and a member's staff are not playable yet, and the office
  says so.

- **A life can run for mayor where the town's voters elect one** Towns whose
  voters elect their mayor now offer a mayor's race beside the council race. It
  falls on the same election day and is won the same way. The winner takes
  office as mayor, and the sitting mayor's term ends that day. The office screen
  and the day's summary then read "Mayor", with the length of the term. Where
  the game has read a city's own government, that decides whether the mayor is
  elected and for how long. Duluth and Boise elect theirs; in Iowa City, Bangor
  and Charlottesville the council chooses, so there is no race. Every other town
  draws from national survey shares: about three towns in four elect their mayor
  directly, and a four-year term is the most common. That draw is labeled as
  typical on the screen. It is a marked placeholder until the rules by town
  size, and those of the larger cities the game has not read, have been
  researched.

- **You can run for your own town's governing body** Every town with a
  government of its own now has a seat you can stand for. In Paducah, Boise or
  any of about nineteen thousand others, the list of offices under Campaigns now
  shows the town's governing body beside the state's seats, and filing for it
  runs the same campaign to the same election day. Win, and you sit on that
  body. Your office says so and says since when. Where the game has read the
  town's government in depth, as in Bowling Green, the seat also shows under
  that city's local meetings and records. Where it has not, the screen says
  plainly that the town's meetings, votes and powers are not established yet,
  rather than telling a winner they hold nothing. The game does not invent what
  it has not read. The number of seats, wards, term length and who may stand are
  left unknown, and a separately elected mayor is not offered, because which
  towns have one is exactly what has not been read.

- **Show recorded gross pay, withholding and net pay after payday** Payday
  notices now show a pay stub from the actual saved paycheck, employee tax
  liabilities and withholding payments. Starting taxes are included, unknown
  rules remain unpriced, partial pay is distinguished from promised pay, and
  enacted laws are named only when saved attribution identifies them.

- **Seated people show what they are doing** Seated people now show what they
  are doing: reading or writing at a desk, on the phone or with legs crossed
  while they wait, leaning in or with hands folded while they listen, hands
  folded or leaning in at a meeting, and plainly or leaning back when they just
  sit. Men also stand with their hands in their pockets and sit with an ankle on
  the knee. Six outfits for each presentation are painted in the new seated
  poses; anyone in another outfit sits plainly.

- **The People screen says who kept away from you, and a new count says how far
  your support moved** When somebody keeps their distance after a case against
  you becomes public, the People list and their card now say so from your side:
  "Carol has kept away from you since September 3, after the case against you
  became public." Before, it showed only in the case record and, in other words,
  deep in their card. Any other falling-out that is the latest thing between you
  shows as "Things have been strained with ... since ...". On the campaign
  screen, a new count now says how far it moved since the one before, for
  example "Down 3.0 points since the count on May 2, 2027." Before, a finding's
  cost to your support never showed anywhere, and in the Alaska race the
  candidate still won 99 to 1.

- **How senators are chosen is now a rule an amendment can change** Each state's
  U.S. senators are elected by its voters, as the Seventeenth Amendment
  requires, and that is now a rule the game reads rather than an assumption. An
  amendment to the U.S. Constitution can return the choice to the state
  legislatures, as before 1913. Then no one files with the voters for a Senate
  seat: at the regular election each legislature's majority chooses the senator,
  and an empty seat is filled by the legislature on the second Tuesday after the
  vacancy, as the 1866 federal law had it. No state bill can change the rule.

- **An empty Senate seat is filled the way that state's law says** An empty U.S.
  Senate seat is now filled the way that state's law says. In states where the
  governor may not appoint, like Wisconsin and Kentucky, the seat stays empty
  until a special election. In states that require it, like Arizona and Oregon,
  the appointee shares the departed senator's party; elsewhere the governor
  appoints someone of the governor's own party. Texas, Massachusetts and eight
  other states hold a prompt special election instead of waiting for November.

- **Medicaid eligibility is checked from opening** The first Medicaid expansion
  eligibility review is now scheduled when a world opens, then continues
  monthly. Coverage changes are recorded for the residents affected by the law.

- **A held protest names who came, and the local reporter covers it** A protest
  that is planned and held now records which named residents were invited, who
  chose to come (people on shift or with a view against it do not), and a single
  turnout count for the public event. The local-government reporter picks the
  held protest up as a news lead and can file it, so a town hears about its own
  protest. Scheduling the protest from a played action, what officials decide
  about it, and its effect on statewide pressure are not built yet.

- **Unemployed residents can begin looking for work** Unemployed working-age
  residents can form a recorded goal to find paid work and open the town's
  recorded job listings when their weekly goal review runs.

- **Executive budget requests name dollars by program family** Executives can
  send a dated dollar request from their shared office desk and compare it with
  the legislature's enacted program authorizations. Requests survive saving and
  do not create spending authority or treasury money. Current modeled budget
  categories remain separate from individual program allocations. Preparing a
  dollar request is optional and does not impose an action deadline or block
  time when the player changes roles.

- **A shared governing desk for mayors** Mayors can set their first governing
  priority and budget request on the same office desk as governors and the
  President. Leaving office removes the authority to decide unfinished matters.

- **Seven more laws can be passed, from stand your ground to a clean-air plan**
  Seven laws that research had already sized, and that nobody could pass because
  no question asked about them, are now real questions in every state: stand
  your ground, responsibility for unsecured guns near children, a handgun buying
  age of 21, same-day voter registration, ballots mailed to every voter, local
  police partnering with federal immigration enforcement (287(g)), and a
  clean-air plan for counties that fail the federal soot and dust standards.
  Each state, D.C. and territory starts with its real 2026 law on each, from
  Giffords Law Center, NCSL, ICE, news reports of 2026 state laws and the EPA's
  list of failing counties. A state that adopts stand your ground now sees
  firearm homicide rise about 8%, a state that mails every voter a ballot sees
  turnout rise about 3.5%, and a state that adopts a clean-air plan sees its air
  pollution fall about 2% a year later. The other four are asked and recorded,
  and their effects switch on as soon as the game measures what they change:
  young people's voting, youth gun deaths, school enrollment and immigrant
  employment.

- **Three additional states have official taxing-power foundations.** Captured
  constitutional text supplies the existing state funded-service reader with
  three additional taxing-power foundations. Each record keeps its legislative
  process, public-purpose and spending conditions, source date, and artifact
  hash. Missing states stay evidence gaps. These records do not enact a tax,
  supply a rate or taxable base, collect money, or authorize spending without an
  appropriation.

- **Six more states have sourced taxing-power foundations.** Captured
  constitutional evidence supplies the existing state funded-service reader with
  six more taxing-power foundations. The records retain the state's legislative
  requirements, tax restrictions, revenue limits, receipt dedications, source
  date, and artifact hash. Missing states remain evidence gaps. These records do
  not enact a tax, supply its rate or taxable base, collect money, or authorize
  spending without an appropriation.

- **Eleven more states have sourced taxing-power foundations.** The existing
  funded-service capability reader now has captured constitutional taxing-power
  evidence for eleven additional states. Each row retains its own official
  source, legal excerpts, capture date, and constitutional limits. The remaining
  states remain explicit evidence gaps. These foundations do not enact a tax,
  supply a tax rate, collect money, or replace the required legislative process
  and appropriation.

- **Four additional states have sourced taxing-power foundations.** Captured
  constitutional text supplies the existing state funded-service reader with
  four more taxing-power foundations. Each record keeps its legislative process,
  subject restrictions, receipt and spending conditions, source date, and
  artifact hash. Missing states stay evidence gaps. These records do not enact a
  tax, supply a rate or taxable base, collect money, or authorize spending
  without an appropriation.

- **Three more states have captured taxing-power foundations.** Captured
  constitutional text supplies the existing state funded-service reader with
  three more taxing-power foundations. Each record keeps its legislative
  process, subject and spending conditions, source date, and artifact hash.
  Missing states stay evidence gaps. These records do not enact a tax, supply a
  rate or taxable base, collect money, or authorize spending without an
  appropriation.

- **Six additional states have official taxing-power foundations.** Captured
  constitutional evidence supplies the existing state funded-service reader with
  six additional taxing-power foundations. The records retain each state's
  legislative requirements, restrictions, receipt dedications, source date, and
  artifact hash. Missing states remain evidence gaps. These records do not enact
  a tax, supply its rate or taxable base, collect money, or authorize spending
  without an appropriation.

- **A state's 988 crisis money now reaches people who need it.** A state's
  standing crisis-response money is now carried out by a health department,
  clinic or hospital already working there, never by an unrelated company. Once
  the provider is paid, a resident who is acutely or seriously unwell decides
  whether to call the crisis team. A family member at home with a recorded duty
  to look after them counts against calling. If no state provider can run a
  crisis team, the money is not committed and no provider is invented. The visit
  counts as service only after it has taken place. Money paid to an organization
  that cannot run a crisis team buys no calls.

- **Every state starts with its real laws on eleven policy questions.** A new
  game now knows what each state's law already says on eleven policy questions:
  whether it has expanded Medicaid, is a right-to-work state, runs paid family
  leave, sells cannabis to adults, requires a permit to carry a concealed gun,
  limits rent increases statewide, offers universal preschool, sets a minimum
  wage above the federal one, taxes wages, has ended cash bail, and the federal
  work requirements for food and health assistance. Lawmakers now argue from
  that starting point, filing bills to change the law rather than to enact what
  is already in force, and a law passed in play replaces the old one once it
  takes effect.

- **A state's highest court can strike down a challenged law before it takes
  effect** A state or local law that answers a question the way laws that were
  really challenged in court did is now reviewed the day before it takes effect.
  The questions are a ban on new gas hookups, age checks for social media,
  public money for private schooling, photo identification to vote, ending cash
  bail, a permit to carry concealed, right to work, rent stabilization and
  independent redistricting commissions. Each justice of the state's highest
  court votes for their own reasons: the real rulings decided by that day, what
  they themselves hold right, and the presumption that a statute is valid. A law
  most of them vote to strike stays on the record but never takes effect, so a
  bill that passes can still fail in court. Before, every law a legislature
  passed took effect.

- **State legislative candidates enter races before election day** Background
  state legislative races now record fictional candidates and their choices
  before the general election. New prospects receive seeded prior residence
  histories, and supported known age and residence requirements are checked
  before they enter a slate. Sitting members with incomplete older residence
  records retain a labeled provisional game-profile route. Results name living
  candidates from saved slates, and a seat with no survivor records a vacancy
  when its term begins. Candidate timing, generated seat views, and unresolved
  qualifications remain labeled game profiles; state filing deadlines and
  primaries are not represented.

- **The home state's legislature is elected again at every regular election**
  The members seated in the home state's legislature when a life began used to
  serve until they died, and nobody replaced them. Now every seat is decided at
  the state's regular legislative election. Most sitting members win again.
  Members who retire or lose are replaced by new members who take their seats
  when the new term begins, and a seat left empty by a death is filled at the
  next election. The news reports how many members returned and how many seats
  changed hands. For now, who runs again and who wins follows the same game
  profile Congress uses. Primaries, campaigns and vote counts for these seats
  are not modeled yet.

- **Your state's legislature is full of real people from the first day** A new
  life now opens with every seat in its home state's legislature held by a
  person: a name, an age, a district where the state's map names one, a party
  and the years they have served. Before, only Congress had members; a state
  chamber was empty until somebody won a campaign. A chamber takes its size from
  the state's rules where the game has them and otherwise one member per
  legislative district. Where the game has not researched a state's legislature
  and its profile drew a chamber size, the state's own Census legislative
  districts set the size instead. Party balance follows the state's own
  politics, so Illinois opens mostly Democratic and Missouri mostly Republican.
  Puerto Rico's chambers are seated without a national party, since its parties
  are not the Democrats and Republicans.

- **A state law that raises the minimum wage now raises it, even when its bill
  names no dollar figure.** A state law that answers yes to raising the minimum
  wage now adds the average real state raise to the state's rate: $0.90 the day
  it takes effect, another $0.90 each year after, up to $2.00, measured from the
  Department of Labor's table of state rates from 2013 to 2024. A bill that
  names its own dollar figure still uses it. Every town job paid below the new
  rate is raised on the first pay period after each step, and the state's
  poverty rate follows after its lag. A later law that answers no takes the rate
  back to where it was, and the effect on poverty ends with it.

- **A state's own property, sales and payroll tax can be filed, passed and
