  felt** A seated state legislator can now file a state property, sales or
  payroll tax, and the state legislature can pass it. Once it is in force, a
  state property tax assesses every household home in the state each year, and a
  state payroll tax taxes each paycheck paid by an employer in the state. The
  tax lands on the named person who owns, rents or earns, and repealing it stops
  the bills.

- **Disasters and taxes push people out of a state and out of town** Each state
  now carries a pressure to leave and a pull to arrive, and they change as
  things happen. A major flood or storm pushes people out of the state it hits,
  and the push fades over the following year. A state tax the game enacts or
  raises pushes people out; a cut draws them in. Once a year the game records
  how many of each state's people moved to other states and where most of them
  went. When one state is pushing people out, the newspapers can report it.
  Households leaving your town lean toward states that are drawing people, and
  newcomers more often come from states that are pushing them out. When a
  disaster destroys or damages homes in your town, some of those households
  leave for good at the next quarterly review; the rest stay and rebuild. People
  who move to your town now have a household of their own there, so a disaster
  can reach them. Someone whose job, school or membership had already ended is
  no longer kept in town by it. Many real reasons are not read yet: each state's
  starting taxes, living costs, jobs, climate and how people feel about its
  laws. Until those come back from research, every state starts alike and the
  pace is a placeholder. It applies to lives started from now on; an older save
  does not change.

- **Supported service bills carry spending terms into government accounts**
  Lawmakers can file supported service bills with a recorded amount and a named
  government, and their chambers record the vote. An enacted bill records what
  the government may spend. The current officeholder can decide how to use that
  authority; actual payments draw from the saved public account. The game keeps
  the bill, spending decision, payment, and service result as separate dated
  records, so a bill passing does not itself claim a service was delivered.

- **Every Supreme Court seat is filled by the President's nominee and a Senate
  vote** When a justice dies, the seat now opens and the President names a
  sitting federal appeals judge or state supreme court justice. About two months
  later every senator in the world votes, each for their own reasons: whether
  the nominee is the choice of their own party's President, whether the nominee
  already serves as a judge, and whether the nominee is from their state. A
  majority confirms; a tie goes to the Vice President. A nominee the Senate
  rejects sends the choice back to the President. The Chief Justice is now
  chosen the same way, from the sitting associate justices and the other judges,
  instead of from any adult in the world, and the Senate no longer confirms
  without a vote. A justice promoted to Chief Justice leaves an associate seat
  that is filled in turn, and a judge who joins the Court leaves the court they
  sat on.

- **Questions about tax terms** Add questions for changing government tax terms
  to the loaded policy catalog. Existing legal authority still controls which
  government can answer each question.

- **Record actual state ratification ballots** Routes state ratification through
  the shared member vote and records each actual chamber's rollcall using
  explicit researched ratification rules. States with inferred rules or
  unimplemented conditions remain pending; no federal delegation or pooled state
  majority substitutes for their legislature.

- **Tell the people at home you are thinking of running** Once you mean to run
  for office, you can tell whoever is at home with you. The talk runs three to
  eight exchanges: when the election is, the news, something the two of you
  shared before, whether they would help and what worries them. Their answers
  come from their own temperament and your history together, and a date that has
  already passed is never spoken of as ahead.

- **The District and the territories each have a Delegate you can name** The
  District of Columbia, Puerto Rico, Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands, American
  Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands each send one member to the U.S. House
  who speaks and sits on committees but casts no final vote. Before, a life in
  any of those places read "No current record names who holds the seat." Now
  every new game seats a fictional Delegate (or Puerto Rico's Resident
  Commissioner) for each of them, with a party and a term. The terms follow the
  law: two years, and four for Puerto Rico's Resident Commissioner. When a term
  ends on January 3, a living member stands again and returns if the place's
  leaning still favors their party; otherwise a new person takes the seat. Each
  place's leaning comes from its own 2024 result, read one way for all six.
  American Samoa's race is nonpartisan, and Puerto Rico's and the Northern
  Marianas' parties or independents do not map cleanly to the two House
  caucuses, so those three are marked ESTIMATED FROM AVERAGE in the code with
  the source. The six members are not among the 435 voting seats.

- **Every town can say how the place is doing** "The place you live" used to be
  blank almost everywhere. Outside one city, the panel that reports how a town's
  area is doing was simply missing from Money and property, with nothing said
  about why. It now appears wherever you live. It finds the county areas your
  town sits in and reports on those, and it is careful about the difference: a
  town inside a county is described as sitting in it, not as being it. Living in
  a county itself still reads as your own jurisdiction. Where there is genuinely
  nothing to show, the screen says so and says why, instead of leaving a gap.
  That includes the case you will see on an early date: the figures for your
  area exist and are being held back until the game can show that they had been
  published, and it now tells you how many are waiting rather than claiming
  nothing has been published at all.

- **Businesses, churches and clubs in your town open and close** Before, every
  store, diner, farm and garage in town lasted forever, and so did every church.
  Now, each season, a few of the town's businesses close and their workers lose
  their jobs and look for new ones. About as many new businesses open, of
  whatever kind the town is shortest of, each started by a neighbor who was out
  of work, or who quits a job to start it when nobody is. When a business's
  manager is 65 or older, its closing is the owner retiring. Now and then a
  church disbands, its members and staff leaving with it, and neighbors who
  belong to no church found a new one. Clubs, such as a garden club or a bowling
  league, are founded and disband the same way. Over five years in Columbus,
  Ohio, 13 businesses opened, 12 closed and 20 jobs ended with them.

- **Towns have businesses with owners, staff and monthly revenue** Every town
  now opens with eight businesses, such as a market, a diner, a law office and a
  construction firm. Each is named for its owner and has staff who work there.
  Money and property lists them. Each business takes in revenue and pays its
  owner and staff on the first of every month. Nobody can start a business yet.
  The kinds, staff counts and dollar figures are placeholders until research on
  real towns' businesses comes back. A child is no longer offered a house, and
  the offer only appears once the game holds your money.

- **Your neighbors fall in love, split up and have children** Before, the
  families around you never changed. Nobody in town started seeing anybody,
  married, divorced or had a baby unless you were one of the two people
  involved. Now the town's families change with time. Every few months single
  neighbors may start dating somebody in town close to their age, and a couple
  who has been dating for a while may move in together and later marry. Couples
  also break up, more often early on, after a layoff or when money is tight, and
  less often when they have a small child at home or many years together. When a
  couple living together splits, one of them moves to a new home in town.
  Couples who live together have children, most often in their late twenties and
  early thirties, and less often when they already have several children or when
  nobody at home is working. Your own relationships and children stay yours to
  decide.

- **A town's rent, income and jobs keep moving, and every town council has a
  size and a term** Once the last published local figures are behind a life, the
  "How the place is doing" panel now shows where the world's own economy has
  taken them: two-bedroom rent follows prices, income per person follows prices
  and output, and the unemployment rate moves by as much as the world's own rate
  has. Each line gives the last published figure beside it and says these are
  the world's conditions, not an agency's release. Before this, a life in 2031
  still read 2025 rents. Every town with a government of its own now has a
  council size and a term. Where the game has read the town's own rules, the
  office screen says so. Where it has not, the town is given a size and a term
  drawn from the councils the game has read, the same every time, and the screen
  says that too. A record of which towns have read rules sits behind this, so
  other features can tell a town's own rule from a typical one.

- **Every household in your town has a home, and people move** Before, nobody in
  town had a home on record, so every home in the game looked like the same
  suburban house, and nobody ever bought, sold or moved within town unless you
  did. Now every household in your town, yours included, lives in one of six
  kinds of home: a small apartment, a rowhouse, a suburban house, a large house,
  a mobile home or a farmhouse. Each home is rented or owned. A city has more
  apartments and rowhouses, and a small farm town has more farmhouses and mobile
  homes. Young people living alone more often rent an apartment, and families
  with children more often live in a house. Your own home now shows the picture
  of its kind. Every few months some neighbors buy a house, a family with
  children moves out of an apartment, an older couple sells a house that has
  grown too big, and renters move. Newcomers and people who move out after a
  breakup find a home, often one somebody else left. A household that leaves
  town gives up its home on the way out.

- **The grown-ups in your town go to work** Before, most of the adults living
  around you had no job at all. In a new game in Lexington, 17 of about 90
  working-age neighbors worked anywhere, mostly at the market and the schools.
  Now nearly everyone of working age in your town has a real job, with a title,
  a workplace in town and weekly hours. The mix of work follows your own county:
  a farm town has farmhands and truck drivers, a city has more accountants and
  nurses. Every town also has the people a political life runs through: a city
  clerk and a planner at City Hall, a principal at each school, a pastor at each
  church, police officers and firefighters, a nurse at the hospital, a community
  organizer, a union representative, staff at each party office and a few
  campaign workers. A few neighbors are still in school, retired, at home with
  small children or looking for work. The town's jobs also change hands as time
  passes. Every few months some neighbors quit, most of them for another job, a
  few are laid off, and people retire when they reach 67. Somebody looking for
  work, a newcomer or a young person who has just turned 18 can be hired.
  Layoffs come more often and hiring more slowly when unemployment is high. A
  neighbor with a job can now move away; the move ends their job instead of
  keeping them in town.

- **Councils elected by ward have wards** A town council elected by ward now has
  a map of its wards. Each ward seat is filled from its ward's residents. Only
  that ward's residents can run for the seat, and only they vote for it. A
  vacancy is filled by someone who lives in the ward. A player's council
  campaign runs for the seat of their own ward. How many seats are ward seats
  comes from the town's charter where it has been read. Elsewhere it is
  estimated from the national shares of councils elected all at large, all by
  ward, or both, and the larger towns in each state are the ones that elect by
  ward. The map is redrawn after each census. A council draws it to keep each of
  its members in a ward of their own, within the population deviation the courts
  allow. An independent commission draws equal wards without regard to where
  members live. A member whose home falls outside their seat's ward cannot run
  for that seat again.

- **Everyone with a job in your town is paid** Before, only you and
  officeholders were ever paid. Your neighbors' jobs had titles and hours but no
  pay. Now, everyone with a job in town is paid by their employer on the
  employer's own payday: weekly, every two weeks, twice a month or monthly.
  Governments pay every two weeks. Each worker's pay comes from what their
  occupation really pays in the town's area, higher the longer they have held
  the job, and never below the state's minimum wage. Social Security and
  Medicare are taken out of every paycheck, just as they are from yours. In the
  first 200 days in Columbus, Ohio, 84 neighbors were paid $2.38 million.

- **Your hometown now has people in it** A new game's town is now sized from its
  real population. Every home in town exists in outline, and the game writes out
  the people you are likely to meet: the neighbors on your street, the staff at
  the market and the schools, and four congregations with their members. Their
  children go to the town's schools. Everyone else is written out only when you
  meet them. Household shapes, the number of congregations and the starting
  drift from the Census figure are placeholders until they have been researched.
  Existing saves are unchanged; new games open with a different world than
  before.

- **A content pack can add a personality trait** A content pack can now carry
  personality traits as well as encounters. A trait it adds is given to the
  people of that life the way the built-in five are, and once somebody's
  temperament on it has been written down it shows on their card beside the
  others. It can also give a reason in the decisions the game already lets
  traits argue about, such as how somebody answers when you ask to meet. A trait
  row that is malformed is left out, and the content pack screen lists what was
  left out and why; the rest of the pack still loads. A member of a legislature
  whose manner the game has read now shows it on their card too, as the built-in
  five already did. Nobody else's card changes in a life with no content packs.
  A life in which a legislator's manner had been recorded could no longer be
  saved; it can now.

- **A tuition freeze now shows up in the Journal of the student it saved money**
  When a state's tuition freeze holds a student's college charge below the price
  the school billed, the student now gets that law and the amount it saved in
  their own Journal, and their partner hears of it at home. A charge the freeze
  left unchanged writes nothing.

- **Every state now has a turnout rate that drifts with the times and rises with
  its graduates.** Each state starts at its real share of eligible voters who
  voted in 2024, and that share now changes over the years, partly with the
  country and partly on its own. A state that graduates more of its students
  sees more of them vote once they come of age. Strict voter ID laws, as the
  research finds, change turnout about not at all.

- **Every state has rules for standing for office, and they differ the way real
  ones do** Standing for office now asks something of you in every state, not
  only in the handful whose law the game has read. The rules differ from state
  to state, so crossing a state line changes what is asked of you instead of
  finding the same numbers everywhere, and a given state always asks the same
  thing, in every session and every save, so nothing shifts under a life already
  being played. Where a state's real law has been read, that is what applies.
  Everywhere else the game holds a rule of its own, drawn from the range real
  states actually set, so the figures stay recognizable rather than inventing
  something no legislature would write. Reading a state's real law replaces it.

- **Anger in a state can turn into unrest, threats and attacks** Anger now
  builds in a state when a governor or President fails a disaster there, when
  national unemployment rises, and when a political figure is attacked. It fades
  when nothing feeds it. When a state's anger runs high, unrest can break out
  there. If the unrest lasts, a prominent political person in that state can be
  threatened: the governor, a member of Congress or a local party organizer, and
  that can be you. If the anger holds, an attack can follow. It can injure or
  kill, and a death leaves the office empty by the usual rules. Nothing happens
  on a timer, and nothing happens where anger never ran high. An international
  dispute the news has reported, such as shipping delays or fishing-rights
  talks, can now become a crisis the President has to handle if it stays open
  while unemployment rises and anger builds at home. One dispute starts one
  crisis at most. How high anger must run, how fast it fades and how likely each
  step is are placeholders until research comes back. It applies to lives
  started from now on; an older save does not change.

- **Time controls include Until needed beside Day and Week** The play screen now
  offers Until needed with Day and Week at the bottom right. Before a click, it
  shows the next known stopping date from the existing quiet-stretch command.
  The same command runs the player's ordinary routine and returns at a protected
  commitment or new need; the result says where time actually stopped. A work
  offer reply or accepted work start already due leaves Until needed unavailable
  with a Work-directed explanation, while Day and Week remain deliberate
  choices.

- **What someone thinks of an official now reaches their relatives** When a
  resident forms a view of an official from what happened to them, such as a
  lost job the official answers for, they now tell the people they talk politics
  with. Each of them learns it as told by that person, and a relative or
  housemate weighs it, through their own temperament and party, with the view
  they already held. People further out know what was said and decide nothing
  from it alone. What a relative was told is not passed on again.

- **Amendments change what a vote means** A vote used to count only for what a
  bill was filed to do. Now the game keeps the bill exactly as it read at every
  recorded vote, so a work requirement added on the floor is on the record of
  every member who voted for the bill afterward, and on the law once it passes.
  Members of a seated chamber now offer floor amendments of their own, each for
  a reason the game records: to pass something they care about, to split a
  bill's coalition and sink it, to put colleagues on the record, or to attach
  what they want to a money bill that must pass. They weigh a part by their own
  principles and judge the count from what colleagues are known to stand for, so
  a private objection can surprise them. Each chamber starts under its real 2026
  amendment rules: germane amendments only in almost every chamber, the U.S.
  House floor open only to amendments a special rule allows, and each state's
  single-subject rule. A chamber can change its own rules in play, but not what
  its constitution holds. A governor with an item veto can strike a rider from a
  budget they sign; the rider stays on every vote's record and never becomes
  law. Each part of a bill is known by the plain question it answers, such as
  "Work requirement for assistance".

- **Who may sign a petition now follows the place's own terms** Petition
  circulation and the clerk's count used one national voting-age check for every
  place. A signer must now be an eligible registered voter under the place's own
  terms: old enough, alive and living in the area the petition covers. Maryland
  lets a 17-year-old sign who will be 18 by the next general election, so a
  petition circulated there now reaches them and one anywhere else does not.

- **Between winning and taking office, a winner has a transition** A player who
  won a Kentucky House or Senate seat read "You hold no office in this life yet"
  under Your office for the eleven months until the term began. A governor-elect
  saw nothing between the result and the first day but the Qualify button on
  Campaigns. Your office now opens on "Before you take office" for anyone who
  has won and not yet begun: what you are called until then (Member-elect of the
  House of Representatives, Governor-elect), the day the term begins and how
  many days away it is, and that the office and its powers are not yours until
  then. A governor-elect is reminded to qualify under Campaigns. Below that are
  the services open before the term: for a legislator, new legislator
  orientation, the caucus meeting and committee requests, in the weeks before
  the session; for a governor, assembling a transition team and a briefing from
  the outgoing administration. Each shows when it opens and closes, can be
  attended while it is open, and reads as missed if its window closes without
  you. The game also carries the House's and Senate's own November orientation,
  office lottery and party organizing meetings, ready for the day a player can
  run for Congress. Every state and every locality uses one general transition
  until its own is researched.

- **Turning down someone who helped you gets around** When you say no to someone
  who once helped you, they may tell the people they confide in: their family
  and household, the people they are warm toward (an outgoing person tells more
  of them, a reserved one only the closest), and anyone who saw the favor done.
  Whether they tell is up to them, from how much the favor meant and who they
  are. People who heard about it are a little less willing to meet you, or to
  back your campaign, when you ask. A person caught up in a news story is now
  reacted to by the same circle of confidants, instead of the first six
  relatives and housemates.

- **People in your town keep working hours** Before, a neighbor with a job had a
  title and an employer, but no working days or hours, so nobody in town was
  ever at work at any particular time. Now every job has its own working week.
  Clerks and city staff keep office hours on weekdays. Nurses work 12-hour day
  or night shifts, police officers rotate through 10-hour shifts, and
  firefighters are on for 24 hours and off for 48. Diner and store staff work
  morning or evening shifts through the week, and coworkers take turns so the
  place stays open. Some new hires, mostly at diners, stores and recreation
  centers, work part-time. Each workplace also knows which picture it is: the
  city clerk works at the clerk counter, a nurse in the hospital hallway, a
  union representative at the union hall. On a weekday morning the clerk is at
  the counter; at 9 p.m. the counter is empty and the clerk is at home.

- **Each state now tracks firearm homicides, and concealed-carry permit laws
  move them** Every state and D.C. now keeps a firearm homicide rate, estimated
  from its 2024 homicide rate and the national share of homicides committed with
  a gun. Requiring a permit to carry a concealed gun lowers it about 8% once the
  law is in force, and dropping a permit requirement a state began with raises
  it by about the same.

- **Each state now tracks infant deaths, and abortion laws and dirty air move
  them** Every state and D.C. now keeps an infant death rate, starting from its
  real 2024 figure. A new abortion restriction raises it about 6% seven months
  after it takes effect, and lifting one lowers it by the same amount. Cleaner
  or dirtier air moves it too: each 1% less fine-particle pollution than the
  state started with means about 0.5% fewer infant deaths.

- **Each state now tracks how many married women work, and home broadband moves
  it** Every state, D.C. and Puerto Rico now keeps the share of married women
  who are working or looking for work, starting from the 2024 Census survey.
  Each point more of homes with broadband than a state began with raises it
  about 0.07%, and losing broadband lowers it the same way.

- **Each state now tracks medical debt in collections, and expanding Medicaid
  lowers it** Every state and D.C. now keeps the share of adults with medical
  debt in collections on their credit reports, starting from the Urban
  Institute's August 2025 credit data. Seven states whose own laws keep medical
  debt off credit reports start at zero. A state that expands Medicaid sees that
  share fall about a third six months after the expansion takes effect, and a
  state that ends its expansion sees it rise.

- **Each state now tracks how many mothers of young children work, and pre-K and
  paid leave move it** Every state, D.C. and Puerto Rico now keeps the share of
  mothers of children under 6 who are working or looking for work, starting from
  the 2024 Census survey. Universal pre-K raises it about 15% a year after the
  program starts. Paid family leave raises it about 2%, and repealing a leave
  program a state began with lowers it by the same.

- **Each state now tracks public school spending per student, and equal-funding
  laws raise it** Every state and D.C. now keeps its public schools' spending
  per student, starting from the Census Bureau's fiscal 2024 figure. A law that
  equalizes school funding raises it about 7% a year after the law takes effect.

- **Each state now tracks teen marijuana use, and legal cannabis sales move it**
  Every state and D.C. now keeps the share of 12- to 17-year-olds who used
  marijuana in the past month, starting from the 2023-2024 national drug use
  survey. Legalizing cannabis sales lowers it about 4% once the law is in force,
  and ending legal sales a state began with raises it by the same amount. The
  studies disagree, so the effect is marked contested, and the range from a 13%
  drop to a 10% rise checks it.

- **You know the grown-ups on your street** A new game now starts with the
  player acquainted with the adults in the six households on their street.
  Neighbors are people the game can have ask you over or ask for a hand; the
  rest of town is met the ordinary way.

- **You can be taken to court over your own rent** Your household was never
  evicted, and everyone else's case was decided by a roll. Now, when you hold
  the lease and fall two months behind, your landlord takes you to court, and
  you learn what you owe and the day the case is decided. Before then you can
  pay it all, offer a payment plan, take the lawyer a right-to-counsel law
  provides, or move out first. Every case is decided by how far behind the
  tenant is, whether the landlord follows through, what the tenant does, and the
  state's trial judge.

- **Vote on the bills put to your own chamber** A player who holds a legislative
  seat was recorded absent on every vote the chamber took, with no way to cast a
  ballot. Now each question you sit on, whether a committee report, a floor
  vote, the other chamber's changes or a veto override, goes on your calendar
  the day before it is put, and time stops there so you can decide. The Work
  screen lists the votes coming up with Yes, No and Present, not voting. Your
  ballot is what the roll call records. If you have not decided by the day of
  the vote, you are recorded absent; nobody votes for you.

- **Your town's council and mayor are your neighbors, and they are elected and
  vote** A life now begins with the town council full and a mayor in office
  where the town elects one, all people who live in town. The town holds its own
  elections: members retire, neighbors file, a field of more than two meets in a
  primary, and a sitting member can lose. A member may resign, and the council
  appoints someone until the next election. The public meeting on your calendar
  is a council meeting and ends with a vote on its agenda item. After that the
  council meets every two weeks and votes on ordinances. If you win a seat, you
  replace a sitting member, and the government screen lists the council's
  members.

### Improved

- **Deciding bail stays quick as the world ages** Each week, deciding bail asks
  every defendant how much money they have on hand. When a defendant had no
  money account yet, that question read every transfer the world had ever
  recorded and searched every payment arrangement for each one. It now reads
  only the defendant's own payment arrangements and transfers, from indexes that
  grow with those records, and gives the same answers. The world after each game
  year is exactly the same as before.

- **Open official person cards from their figures** Opening officials no longer
  have floating name plates or a separate name box. Their figures open the
  ordinary person card when clicked, and remain reachable by keyboard. Other
  in-game room nameplates are unchanged.

- **Receive artwork updates while keeping your current life** The private
  desktop app can receive artwork separately from game updates, keep an open
  life unchanged, and apply verified updates at the title screen. Art Desk shows
  incoming art-team messages alongside replies and retains your unfinished
  notes.

- **A county sheriff makes the arrests and the county prosecutor decides the
  charges** When a county elects a sheriff and a prosecutor, the sheriff is now
  the arresting officer named on the county's arrests, and the prosecutor makes
  the charging decision on each referred arrest. A county with no sitting
  prosecutor charges nobody. Portraits and business scenes show a sheriff in the
  city police uniform until the sheriff's own uniform is drawn.

- **College directories retain campus and control evidence** College directory
  records retain the source's campus coordinates, public-control level, academic
  calendar and controlling-system fields. College applications use the recorded
  county location when that county already exists in the saved world. A
  public-control level does not establish an owning government or tuition price.

- **Town councils make law for their members' own reasons** A town council and
  the Council of the District of Columbia used to take up a random question with
  a coin-flip answer, and each member voted yes two times in three. Now a member
  files an ordinance on the question their own principles press hardest, where
  the town's law does not already say what they want, and every member votes
  through the same engine as the legislatures. A member with no view of their
  own no longer votes yes by default: they follow a trusted colleague, the
  mayor's known position, the committee, or a clear lean among the town's
  voters, and otherwise answer present. A council elected without party labels,
  like the Nebraska Legislature, gives no party cue. An adopted ordinance takes
  effect 30 days later where the charter's own rule is unread. Sixty-six
  councils now have their real seat counts, read from their charters and codes.

- **County boards and Puerto Rico's municipal legislatures are seated** A life
  now opens with its county's board seated, as well as its town's council. The
  board has the number of members its state's law sets: California seats five
  supervisors, and Arizona and Nevada seat three, five or seven by the county's
  population. Nine other states set a size of their own. A state whose law has
  not been read yet seats seven members, the national average. In Puerto Rico,
  each municipio is now a government, with a mayor and a municipal legislature
  of 12, 14 or 16 members by population, 17 in San Juan and 5 in Culebra, as the
  Municipal Code sets. A municipio also hires, as any local government does.

- **Faster days in long lives** Days and weeks no longer wait while the game
  prepares each autosave. Starting a new life shows progress while its world is
  prepared.

- **A friendlier way to turn down a game** Someone who wants a change now says
  "I feel like doing something else."

- **A game day takes about a third less time while the world fills in its
  candidates** Before, every batch of new people the world added in a day made
  the game read every household in the country again to learn about families,
  about 445 times in ten days. A day cost about 2.2 seconds on an ordinary life,
  and a month cost 131 seconds. Now, when a day only adds new people in
  households of their own, the family reading carries forward and adds those
  households. Anything that touches an existing person or household still reads
  everything again. A month on the same life costs 84 seconds, and the world it
  ends in is identical.

- **Panels move and resize freely, the opening fills the screen, and Attend
  holds a meeting you arranged** A meeting you arranged with somebody through
  Contact can now be attended. Pressing Attend used to do nothing, because the
  game looked for a route to a place called "Arranged in person" and found none.
  Panels move by their title bar and resize from any edge or corner. After a
  change, "Keep this size" saves it; otherwise the panel opens at its saved or
  default size next time, and double-clicking the title bar resets it. The
  Maximize and Reset layout buttons are gone, and panels now fade out when they
  close. The opening cards fill the screen on each place's approved picture,
  without the camera drift or its Pause motion button, and one title fades out
  before the next fades in. Politics opens on Government. Contact opens as its
  own screen over whatever is open, and "Talk to somebody here" lists only
  people who are actually in the room. Scrollbars are thin and styled. The table
  papers in the room and the minutes notice after a day passes are gone, and the
  desktop app opens maximized, with the hub's toolbar stepping aside in full
  screen.

- **State legislatures weigh floor amendments much faster** Before offering a
  floor amendment, each legislator counts how the chamber would vote. Every
  member's count used to decide the whole chamber again, two or three times for
  each question they might raise, so the work grew with the square of the
  chamber's size. A colleague's vote on the same reading of a bill is the same
  whoever counts it, so each reading is now decided once, and only the counting
  member is decided again. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, the first game year
  takes 14.4 seconds instead of 36.6. Nothing that happens in the world has
  changed: the world after each of five years is exactly the same as before.

- **Weekly pardon reviews stay quick in later game years** Each week, every
  sentence up for a pardon review asks which governor holds the pardon power.
  Answering that read every state's records and every event again for each
  sentence, so the cost grew with the number of people in prison. Each world's
  governors are now read once. The illness lookups that find who is already ill,
  and a crisis record by its id, also keep indexes that grow with their lists.
  In South Fork, Pennsylvania, game years six to ten take 7.9, 12.6, 8.2, 12.8
  and 14.0 seconds less. The world after each of ten years is exactly the same
  as before.

- **Home play script checks rent and nonhousing payments together** The records
  play script checks that a household pays recorded rent and the sourced
  nonhousing estimate as separate flows on the same due date. It preserves each
  payment on reload and refuses duplicate settlement. Missing HUD rent data
  still creates no lease. Actual personal bill contracts and ordinary screen
  follow-through remain unfinished play-script steps.

- **The Journal says which month things happened in** Each year in the Journal
  now reads in order with its months: "In March, you started at the plant. In
  July, you joined the county party." A sentence that already says its own date
  is left alone, and a long year breaks into paragraphs where the month changes.
  Nothing is added that the record does not hold; the storytelling voice itself
  is waiting on a style ruling.

- **Official funerals stay quick in later game years** When an official dies,
  the funeral looks for the colleagues who served with them, and asks every
  person in the world which offices they hold. Each of those questions read
  every Supreme Court seat again. The justices are now read once for each world,
  with the other office holders. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, the tenth game
  year takes 13.3 seconds less. The world after each of ten years is exactly the
  same as before.

- **A civic title screen in glass and brass** The title screen displays the
  ballot-box emblem and gold wordmark. The title and character creator use dark
  glass and brass, with matching Back and Next controls. The approved typefaces
  are included for offline play, and the ballot box appears as the game icon.

- **Clear identity summary in the character creator** The creator uses the
  shared fine glass frame. Completed identity shows the recorded name, age,
  gender, and birth date on separate lines. Actions use the shared letterpress
  controls, and completed-step summaries remain flat. State and town searches
  stay unframed, with clear and return-to-results controls.

- **Keep the title screen in Kit 13 glass without artwork** The title and its
  menu keep the dark glass frame when no room artwork is available. On short
  screens, the page scrolls naturally instead of trapping the menu inside its
  frame.

- **Glass navigation and workspace frames** Navigation and workspaces use glass
  with a fine brass edge. Radial menu entries stay flat. Search has clear and
  return controls, and selected filters no longer look raised.

- **Fine glass frame on the title menu** The title menu uses a fine brass and
  iron frame, Cinzel headings, and flat choices with a subtle hover and solid
  pressed state.

- **Bravery bears on whether officeholders seek another term** Brave
  officeholders now lean toward seeking another term, and timid ones lean toward
  stepping down, when they weigh whether to run again.

- **Loyalty bears on whether couples stay together** People who hold their bonds
  firmly now lean toward staying in a relationship, and people who let bonds go
  readily lean toward ending one, when a couple decides where it stands.

- **Compassion bears on clemency petitions** Compassionate people now lean
  toward petitioning for clemency when they understand someone's suffering, and
  callous people lean toward waiting.

- **T9 facet-acquisitive** Acquisitive officeholders lean toward another term.

- **Analytical people counter instead of just agreeing** Analytical people lean
  toward countering a request after weighing its terms.

- **Assertiveness bears on how people answer requests** Assertive people now
  lean toward countering with their own terms when someone contacts them.

- **Calm workers weigh staying through a rough stretch** Calm workers lean
  toward staying in a job through a rough stretch rather than quitting.

- **Competitiveness bears on seeking another term** Competitive officeholders
  now lean toward seeking another term.

- **Curiosity bears on how people answer requests** Curious people now lean
  toward accepting a request when someone contacts them.

- **T9 facet-deferential** Deferential people lean toward agreeing.

- **Duty bears on whether workers quit** Duty-bound workers now lean toward
  staying in the job instead of quitting.

- **Enterprise bears on seeking another term** Enterprising officeholders now
  lean toward seeking another term.

- **Forgiveness bears on how people answer requests** Forgiving people now lean
  toward accepting a request, even from someone who wronged them.

- **Friendliness bears on how people answer requests** Friendly people now lean
  toward accepting a request when someone contacts them.

- **Guardedness bears on how people answer requests** Guarded people now lean
  toward declining a request from someone they do not know well.

- **T9 facet-informal** Informal people answer requests casually and counter.

- **Inventive people offer a different way instead of just agreeing** Inventive
  people lean toward countering a request with an alternative they thought up.

- **Persistence bears on whether workers quit** Persistent workers now lean
  toward staying through setbacks instead of quitting.

- **Philanthropy bears on clemency petitions** Philanthropic people now lean
  toward petitioning for clemency on someone's behalf.

- **Politeness bears on how people answer requests** Polite people now lean
  toward accepting a request when someone contacts them.

- **Practical people accept workable requests** Practical people lean toward
  accepting a plain, workable request.

- **Restless workers lean toward quitting** Restless workers lean toward
  quitting a job that keeps them in one place.

- **Work focus bears on whether workers quit** Work-centered people now lean
  toward staying in the job instead of quitting.

- **Trust bears on how people answer requests** Trusting people now lean toward
  accepting an ordinary request, and suspicious people lean toward asking for
  different terms, when someone contacts them.

- **Trait readers register themselves from their own files** The list of
  personality trait readers is now generated from the trait files, so adding a
  trait is one new file and two trait pull requests no longer conflict on the
  shared index.

- **Flexibility bears on how people answer requests** Flexible people now lean
  toward accepting a request, and stubborn people lean toward declining, when
  someone contacts them.

- **Emotional display bears on answering reporters** People who show what they
  feel now lean toward answering a reporter's request, and people who hold
  feelings in lean toward declining.

- **Patience bears on whether workers quit** Patient workers now lean toward
  staying through a slow stretch, and impatient workers lean toward quitting,
  when a worker decides whether to leave a job.

- **Outlook bears on whether officeholders seek another term** Optimistic
  officeholders now lean toward seeking another term, and pessimistic ones lean
  toward stepping down, when they weigh whether to run again.

- **Effort bears on whether workers quit** Workers who give voluntary effort now
  lean toward continuing work, and those who give only what is required lean
  toward quitting.

- **Later years of a long world run faster** A world still got slower each year
  it ran, even after paydays were fixed: year 10 took about seven times as long
  as year 1. Paydays copied the whole tax list once per paycheck, and the
  election turnover step, the scheduler and Congress's votes each read records
  from the whole of the world's history to find the few they needed. They now
  read only what they need. On the same world, year 10 takes about four times as
  long as year 1, and less than two-thirds of the time it took before. Nothing
  that happens in the world has changed: the world after each of ten years is
  exactly the same as before.

- **Paydays and the daily record check stay quick in later years** After each
  payday, the game looks for pay a law changed so the worker can notice it. That
  look listed every payment arrangement and pay term in the world first, even
  when no law had changed anyone's pay; it now looks up only the records a law
  names. The check that each day's new records can be saved also stops
  remembering every value inside every record, which had made its memory, and
  the work of clearing it, grow every year. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, each of
  the first ten game years runs 0.4 to 5.5 seconds faster, and the world after
  each year is exactly the same as before.

- **Passing time during state legislative filing season is faster** Passing two
  months of a new life during the spring filing season took about a quarter less
  time in a measured Palm Beach, Florida life: 69 seconds instead of 94. A year
  of a watched world took 498 seconds instead of 564. The same people file,
  decide and win as before; only the reading of where candidates live and how
  each state's chambers are drawn got cheaper.

- **Conversation replies use the selected scales control.** The scales in the
  conversation's upper right switch between truthful and knowingly false
  replies. False replies use soft red words and numerals; unavailable scales
  stay dim and level. Speakers retain their own portraits, and spoken turns keep
  the existing save and reload behavior.

- **A world that has run for years no longer slows to a crawl** Each day in a
  world got slower the longer the world ran, and by the fifth year a single day
  could take a couple of seconds. Most of that time went to paydays: every
  paycheck was checked against every paycheck the town had ever paid, and every
  money record was looked up by reading all of them. Days in later years are now
  many times faster. Five years of a watched world that took most of an hour to
  run now take under three minutes, and opening a save from a world's fifth year
  is about three times faster. Nothing that happens in the world has changed:
  the same world plays out exactly as it did before, only faster.

- **Ambitious officeholders lean toward another term** Officeholders who seek
  advancement and responsibility now lean toward running again when they weigh
  another term. People with no marked ambition are not pushed either way.

- **Contented officeholders lean toward stepping down** Officeholders who place
  less weight on further status once their aims are met now lean toward stepping
  down when they weigh another term. People with no marked contentment are not
  pushed either way.

- **Generous people are quicker to give a campaign the backing it asks for**
  People who readily share what they have now lean toward granting a campaign's
  request for support. People with no marked generosity are not pushed either
  way.

- **A rent renewal explains its market change** A market-rent renewal now
  records how the local housing-market level changed the rent over the year.
  Estimated rent rows keep their estimate basis in the renewal record.

- **Bill records show requests when someone has made one.** The bill record
  omits the empty negotiation helper and its heading. Recorded requests and
  floor actions remain available.

- **State legislatures weigh floor amendments faster again** Before offering a
  floor amendment, a legislator asks how two members would vote. Since town
  councils joined the same vote engine, answering that worked out every member's
  own view first, so each question cost a whole chamber's thought. A member's
  view is now worked out only when that member votes, or when a colleague who
  trusts them asks how they voted. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, game years one
  to five take 0.9, 3.8, 4.7, 6.0 and 3.7 seconds less. Every vote comes out the
  same: the world after each of five years is exactly the same as before.

- **Appointments and emergencies look up office holders once** Choosing someone
  for an appointment, such as a new Chief Justice, weighs the public offices
  each candidate holds. Each candidate's offices were found by reading the whole
  of Congress and every state's executive offices again, so an appointment cost
  more with every year a world ran. The offices a world seats are now read once
  for each moment of the world and kept by holder. Every choice comes out the
  same.

- **Legislatures count their votes faster in later years** Every chamber vote
  asks two things about the bill and its members: who signed on to the bill, and
  which party each member belongs to. Finding the signers meant reading every
  event the world had ever recorded, and a member's party was worked out again
  for every question the chamber weighed that day. The signers are now kept by
  bill as events are written, and a member's party is worked out once for each
  moment of the world. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, game years one to five take
  1.3, 4.7, 2.8, 6.2 and 5.5 seconds less. Every vote comes out the same: the
  world after each of five years is exactly the same as before.

- **Minimum wage and teacher pay floors stay quick on paydays** On each payday,
  the minimum wage and the teacher salary floor check which jobs have ended and
  when each job was last paid. Each check read every job status and every
  paycheck the world had ever recorded. Both now read indexes that grow with
  those records, and the check for legislative seats recorded before terms were
  dated reads only legislative seats. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, each of the
  first ten game years runs 0.2 to 2.3 seconds faster, and the world after each
  year is exactly the same as before.

- **Worlds in states without their own place data run faster** In a state the
  game generates, such as Pennsylvania, each step of each bill in the state
  legislature searched every place in the state for the same first town. It now
  finds that town once. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, the first game year takes
  8.8 seconds instead of 20.0, and nothing that happens in the world has
  changed: the world after each of ten years is exactly the same as before.

- **The people around you know people besides you** Your parents, housemates,
  old classmates and teachers now have a few neighbors and friends of their own
  in their town. When they want to keep up with people, they call them, not only
  you, and they may introduce you to someone they know. Nobody new is made up
  for this: their friends are people who already live there.

- **People stand, sit and gesture like real people, men and women each their own
  way** People in scenes now take real poses instead of standing stiffly with
  their arms at their sides: explaining with their hands, arms folded, speaking
  at a podium, and sitting leaning in, in every body type and every outfit. Men
  and women have their own body language. A woman explains with open palms,
  rests a hand on her hip and crosses her legs when she sits. A man explains
  with a low, firm hand, stands with his hands in his pockets and rests an ankle
  on his knee. Every pose is also painted turned three quarters toward the
  viewer, ready for when faces and hair are turned too. Faces show feeling.
  Every face, at every age, can smile, laugh, look concerned, angry, skeptical,
  sad or surprised, and the expression follows what is being said.

- **Edit and return exact artwork revisions in the private Art Bench** See the
  latest image on each artwork card. Requests, questions, rejected images, and
  approved images waiting to be added now have separate places. Ask a question
  and read replies alongside every revision. Old preparation work stays in
  Library. Download an exact revision, edit it externally, and return the result
  without losing its original or history. The private client checks GitHub for
  game updates while preserving the previous version and saved lives.

- **Introduce the home region with government and population cards** The home
  state now follows the White House, with Government, Population and Voting
  cards over a suitable regional illustration when one is available. Population
  figures identify their historical year and source. The voting card shows the
  November 2024 Census survey with reported counts, group rates, rounding and
  uncertainty; it does not present survey estimates as current voter rolls. D.C.
  keeps its distinct government and Congress sequence. Compatible alternate
  regional views can be browsed without changing the information card or saved
  game. Unsupported places and seasons keep the information cards without an
  illustration. The childhood questionnaire introduces its imagined caregiver
  and sister before asking about them. Existing answers and saved lives retain
  their identities.

- **Explore your life through clearer creation, records and navigation** Explore
  appearance choices with immediate reversible previews, then meet the public
  world and read your life so far. Keep map selections when reopening,
  distinguish Here from Home, and move or resize reading windows. Calendar keeps
  events beside its grid, News opens complete articles, and person records bring
  known history together. Save and Quit returns to the title after saving your
  life. Art Desk requests show a ready-to-copy prompt and full-size reference,
  with clear review queues and preserved revision history. The private desktop
  app adds guarded native Save and Quit and standard editing shortcuts.

- **State capitols look like the real buildings** Forty state capitols are now
  painted from the real buildings and their real surroundings, from Montgomery
  to Madison. Night and rainy days show the daytime picture until those lights
  are painted.

- **Every state, D.C. and the territories show their real capitol** The last
  sixteen seats of government are painted from the real buildings: Washington,
  D.C.'s city hall, Delaware's Legislative Hall, the capitols of Puerto Rico,
  Guam, the Virgin Islands, American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands, and
  nine repainted state capitols.

- **Checking each day's new records costs less** After each stretch of days, the
  game checks that every new record can be saved. That check wrote out the full
  location of every nested value it looked at, whether or not anything was
  wrong. It now writes a location only for a value that fails, with the same
  message as before. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, each of the first ten game
  years runs 0.3 to 6.4 seconds faster, and the world after each year is exactly
  the same as before.

- **Later game years run faster as legislative and news records grow** The game
  checks each new legislative record and each news story against lookups built
  from every earlier record. Whenever any of those lists grew, the lookups were
  rebuilt from the start, so each year cost more than the one before. Each
  lookup now grows with its list. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, game years one to
  five take 1.0, 1.4, 2.7, 9.6 and 3.6 seconds less. Nothing that happens in the
  world has changed: the world after each of five years is exactly the same as
  before.

- **Paychecks stay quick as a worker's years add up** Withholding a paycheck's
  taxes checks what the worker holds, and so does recording the payment. Each
  check added up every payment the worker had ever made or received, so every
  paycheck cost a little more than the one before. Each account now keeps
  running totals as payments are recorded, and a balance is read from them. A
  balance at an earlier moment, or for an account opened after some of its
  payments, is still added up payment by payment. Every balance comes out the
  same.

- **The developer inspector explains saved decisions.** Decision inspection
  shows the recorded options, constraints, considerations, source snapshots,
  results, ranks and peer estimates. A separately labeled current-code
  calculation shows component weights, contributions, sums and ties without
  presenting them as historically stored totals or changing the world.

- **Petition forms have explicit legal terms** A shared petition rule gate reads
  local initiative and protest referendum terms from the compiled municipal
  packs and state initiative, referendum and constitutional initiative terms
  from per-place rows. Read constitutional provisions retain their signature
  bases, deadlines, distribution and refusal reasons; unread terms carry
  explicit median estimates. Proposition petition lifecycle dispatch remains
  pending the signer and ballot integrations.

- **Petition signatures follow recorded requests and answers** Petitions close
  on named, eligible signed records from recorded requests and signer decisions.
  Circulators reach people through the existing campaign field model. A saved
  human answer follows the canonical life action and its player-choice
  appraisal; an ignored or generated answer stays unsigned. Duplicate,
  eligibility and clerk review records explain invalid signatures. Proposition
  petitions share the signature counter. Required geographic certification and
  ballot referral remain explicit next steps.

- **Education and city records use their locked source details.** Education
  choices now use published institution control, calendar, and location data.
  Charlottesville's government record includes its locked Virginia budget
  procedure.

- **Humility contributes to public-life choices** People who downplay their own
  status and share credit now weigh those concerns when voting, organizing
  outreach, considering endorsements, and responding to press requests.

- **Pride bears on public-life decisions** People who place importance on
  dignity and recognition weigh it alongside their existing reasons when voting,
  organizing campaign outreach, considering endorsements, or responding to press
  requests.

- **Self-consciousness bears on public-life choices** People who pay close
  attention to being observed and evaluated now weigh that concern when voting,
  organizing outreach, considering endorsements, and responding to press
  requests.

- **Dramatic characters can put a response on the record** Press response
  decisions now weigh recorded personality tendencies alongside the person's own
  knowledge.

- **Observant characters can answer live press requests directly** Press
  response decisions now weigh recorded personality tendencies alongside the
  person's own knowledge.

- **Vindictive characters can dispute live press accounts** Press response
  decisions now weigh recorded personality tendencies alongside the person's own
  knowledge.

- **Clear Calendar and News screens** Calendar dates and News sections use fine
  borders and square edges.

- **Clear Campaign cards** Campaign cards use fine borders, square edges and the
  shared glass surface.

- **Readable People names and Contact frames** People names are easier to read.
  Contact uses a fine brass edge and the shared glass frame.

- **Clear Money and Governing screens** Money and Governing use fine borders and
  square edges. Office, Issues, Budget and Parties appear when there is an
  office or a record to read.

- **Clear Places and Transit screens** Places offers and Transit fields use fine
  borders and square edges. Transit keeps its filing instructions without the
  developer explanation.

- **People greet you again and hear your news in their own words** Someone you
  already talked with no longer answers every greeting with "Hi again." They may
  use your name, mention that you talked earlier the same day, sound glad to see
  you when you get along, or a little short when things are tense, and children
  talk like children. Telling someone news they had not heard gets one of
  several plain answers and often a question back, instead of the same line each
  time. A person avoids repeating the words they used with you lately. News you
  mention no longer ends with two periods.

- **Places weigh their month's outcomes faster** Each time the game worked out
  which laws are in force in a place, it searched the list of places once for
  every state to find that state's identity. The list never changes, so each
  state is now found once and kept. In South Fork, Pennsylvania, weighing each
  month's outcomes takes about 1.4 seconds less of every game year. Nothing that
  happens in the world has changed: the world after each of five years is
  exactly the same as before.

- **State legislatures find their seats once a day** Each game day, the state
  legislature's turnover checks which seats are up in each election year it
  looks at. Every check read the chamber's seats again, and each reading walked
  every member the chamber has ever had, so the check cost more with every
  election. A chamber's members and seats are now read once for each moment of
  the world. Every seat and member comes out the same.

- **Choose what stops passing time from beside the Day and Week buttons** A
  Stops button now sits beside Day and Week. It opens a checklist of what
  interrupts passing time: work shifts and invitations you have not answered can
  be switched on or off, and the stops that always apply are shown as checked.
  It is the same list as the Calendar's Interruptions tab, so a change in one
  shows in the other.

- **The Stops checklist shows your own election day** The Stops checklist beside
  Day and Week, and the Calendar's Interruptions tab, now list your own election
  day. Passing time always stops the morning after an election you are running
  in and says how it came out, so the row is shown checked and cannot be
  switched off.

- **Jobs describes an empty list without guessing about employers** When no
  current job listings are available, Jobs says, “No job openings are listed
  here right now.” The sentence comes from the existing English engine and the
  same saved listings that the screen displays. It makes no claim about every
  employer in town. The wording check also catches several specific awkward
  constructions and suggests plain American English repairs.

- **Reporting assignments reserve their estimated effort against actual staff
  hours** Newsrooms use their reporters' recorded job hours and open work
  reservations instead of a fixed small, standard, or major story limit.
  Assignments save an estimated reporting task, and publication waits for its
  actual work progress. Effort and experience factors are labeled CTO-admitted
  estimates, not measured durations for an individual story.

- **Faster state legislative intake** State legislative intake reuses existing
  history and candidate lookups and prepares incumbent traits together, reducing
  repeated work while preserving the people and decisions in the watched month.

### Fixed

- **A seat in a big chamber no longer makes every member someone you know**
  Before, taking a seat in a state House made every member somebody you knew on
  the first day: People showed "Work 181", and the web drew all of them in one
  ring of overlapping faces with names printed on top of each other. Now, in a
  workplace or group with more than 20 other people, a colleague is somebody you
  know once there is something between you on the record. The rest are listed
  under Work or Politics as "Not met yet", and you can open their card to reach
  them. A small workplace works as before: you know everybody there. The web
  also fits its drawing. It uses up to three rings sized to the space and prints
  names only when they have room; a name that doesn't fit shows when you hover
  or pick the face. Anybody who doesn't fit is counted, with a button to see
  everyone in the list. The size of 20 is a placeholder and is filed as a
  research question.

- **A child who starts in school attends a school with a real name** A new life
  that starts at school age put the child in "Ely, Nevada public school", or the
  same phrase with their own town's name, which is not what anybody calls a
  school. New games now give it a generated name for the level the child is at:
  an elementary school below eleven, a middle school from eleven, a high school
  from fourteen. The names are made the same way as the schools in a grown
  character's summarized past. A child past elementary school also has the
  schools they already finished on their record. A seventeen-year-old started at
  17 went to elementary school from five, completed it at eleven, completed
  middle school at fourteen, and has been at the high school since then, where
  before they had one school attended since the age of five and no earlier life
  at all. Saved games keep the school they started with.

- **A child is no longer offered an adult relationship as a private aim** The
  private aims panel showed a child the adult-relationship aim, with a note that
  there was nobody to have it with. A character under eighteen is no longer
  offered that aim at all.

- **A child moves on through school as the years pass** A character who started
  a new game in school stayed at the school they began at. A five-year-old was
  still at the elementary school at sixteen, and a grown character still had it
  on their record at twenty-four. Now a child finishes elementary school in late
  spring and starts middle school that fall, then moves on to high school, and
  graduates at the end of their last year. The classmates they started with move
  on with them. Nothing already on the record is rewritten: the elementary
  school stays where it was, marked finished. This applies to new games. A game
  already in progress keeps the school it has. When school years begin and end
  is a placeholder for now, filed as a research question.

- **A new character's two parents are a couple** A new character's two parents
  had nothing to do with each other on the record, and each was drawn a man or a
  woman on their own, so about half of all parent pairs were two men or two
  women. Now two parents raising the character together are a couple, and a
  parent who died before the game began was the other's partner until then. A
  parent who lives elsewhere is not claimed to be anything to the other. Most
  parent couples are a man and a woman. This applies to new games. A game
  already in progress keeps the family it has.

- **A dead senator's seat is filled by appointment, then by a special election**
  When a U.S. senator dies, the seat no longer stays empty until the end of the
  term. The governor appoints a senator of the same party, and a special
  election at the next November election chooses who serves the rest of the
  term. If the term ends at that election anyway, the appointee serves until the
  regular winner takes office. A member of Congress who resigns or is removed
  after a jail sentence now leaves a seat that is filled the same way: a Senate
  seat by appointment and a special election, and a House seat by a special
  election.

- **A death has a cause, the family is told, and a long wait stops on the day
  your character dies** When somebody dies of ordinary causes, the death now has
  a cause: an illness that ran its course, a sudden illness, or an accident. The
  screen that tells you your character has died says which, for example "died
  after a serious illness" or "died in an accident". A death recorded in an
  older save still reads plainly as "died"; no cause is made up for it
  afterward. A death from an illness no longer comes out of nowhere. Some weeks
  or months beforehand the person falls seriously ill, and their household and
  close family are told. You can see a relative's illness under Health and
  emergencies. If the illness is your own character's, you see it there too and
  decide whom to tell. Who dies, and on what day, is unchanged. When a relative
  or someone in your household dies, you are now told. It reads like "Your
  younger sibling, Dana Keller, died after a serious illness on May 10, 2026."
  The line appears once in your Journal. After you let time run on, it is also
  the first thing you're told about the stretch you skipped. The cause is
  included only if their illness had been shared with you; otherwise the notice
  says only that they died and when. Nobody outside the family and household is
  told. Deaths that were already in a save before this change are not announced
  after the fact. Letting the weeks run on now stops on the day your character
  dies. Before, the ending screen could appear with the date already weeks past
  the death.

- **A governor who dies is succeeded, and a vacant Chief Justice seat is
  filled** When a governor dies in office, the next officer in line takes over
  and serves the rest of the term, instead of the office sitting empty until the
  next election. When the Chief Justice dies, the President nominates a
  successor and the Senate confirms them. A nominee who sat in Congress leaves
  the seat. Candidates for the Maine House now need to be 21, the age the state
  constitution sets, instead of the 23 the game had drawn.

- **A governor can arrange how the office handles casework, and "What came of
  it" shows only their own term** A governor's office said "The office's
  workflow is set up when you take the office" for the whole term, because the
  choice was only ever offered alongside a legislator's voting workflow, which a
  governor does not have. The office desk now lets a governor choose how
  constituent requests are handled from the first day in office. The governor's
  "What came of it" list also showed the predecessor's budgets and bills. It now
  lists only what came of this governor's own term.

- **A grown character starts with their schooling and working life on the record
  again** Starting a new life at thirty gave you somebody with no school behind
  them and no job they had ever held. The reasoning was sound — the game should
  not invent a biography you never chose — but a blank past is not the absence
  of a claim about your life, it is a claim, and a stranger one. It cost more
  than a line on a page. The people you had worked with were part of that
  history, so they did not exist either, and they are the people most likely to
  get in touch with you. An ordinary forty-year-old was about half as likely to
  have anybody reach out to them at all. A new character again begins with a
  school, a working life and the people who came with it. The past it writes is
  a placeholder, not research, and it will be replaced by histories built from
  real schools — but a generated past is nearer to your life than none. Saves
  made under the previous behavior are untouched and still rebuild exactly as
  they were.

- **Somebody who starts as a child can apply to college when they are grown** A
  life begun in childhood reached eighteen to find every college application
  closed. Colleges and schools now stay open to applicants in the years after
  the game's school listing.

- **The Study tab opens on real colleges, and a degree can be applied for once**
  The Study tab under Jobs and study now opens on the college finder, so someone
  out of high school sees real colleges first instead of a long scroll of the
  game's own programs. Study terms, tuition notices and the journal speak
  plainly, with dates written out and amounts like "$3,840", and the directory's
  "Certifiicate" misspelling is corrected. Applying again for a degree you are
  already studying, or already hold an offer for, is refused with the reason
  shown.

- **An afternoon you said yes to always has a way there** In Elko, Noah said yes
  when Gia invited him over, and going still said "You have no way to get from
  Home to" her house. Saying yes is meant to book the short trip there, but some
  route left the afternoon with no trip, and then nothing could book one. Going
  to an afternoon you said yes to now books the trip itself when it is missing.
  The afternoon is then kept as usual.

- **A seat whose member-elect dies before taking office is filled** When someone
  elected to Congress dies before the new term begins, the seat no longer stays
  empty for the whole term. A Senate seat is filled by the governor's
  appointment and then a special election, and a House seat by a special
  election.

- **A party organized out of a party body records the body it came from** When
  people organized a new party out of a body they belonged to, the history
  recorded the new party as having come from nowhere. The founders were walked
  out of their old organization during the same step, and then the record wrote
  an empty list of parent organizations, so the one moment that knew the
  parentage discarded it. The record now keeps it. A founding proposed from
  nothing still records no parent, because it genuinely has none.

- **A parent who lives at home at the start is alive** A grown-up start could
  seat a parent in their home in town even though the family history said that
  parent had died years before. A relative who lives in a home at the start is
  now alive, and only relatives with no home today can have died before it.
  Birthday invitations from a living parent come back with it.

- **A party request past its deadline no longer offers Accept** When a party or
  campaign asked for your help with something like a door canvass, the request
  kept its Accept button after the time to answer had passed. Pressing it only
  said it was too late. A request now stops offering Accept once you would have
  had to set out for it. It says who needed the answer and by when, for example
  "Monroe County Democrats needed an answer by January 7, 2026, 6:10 PM; that
  has passed." The same line shows on the Calendar. When the day passes, the
  request is recorded as having lapsed, not as something you turned down, and it
  reads the same after you save and load.

- **A person you live with no longer reads as someone you haven't spoken to**
  Before, the card for a sister in the same home said "You haven't spoken," and
  a father's said "You last spoke" more than a year back. Now the card for
  anyone you live with says you live together, and adds that you spoke today
  when you did.

- **A page opened while another is closing stays open** Closing a page, such as
  People, fades it out before the room returns. A pin pressed during that fade
  opened the person's page, and then the old close went through a moment later
  and shut the new page. The page now stays open, and Back from it returns to
  the page underneath. Three browser tests that were failing on main now pass:
  the People web test at both widths, the docket test, and the calendar test,
  which failed about two runs in three.

- **A player who wins a state legislative seat holds it alone** When the player
  won a seat in the home state's legislature, the game also chose its own winner
  for the same district at the same election, so the chamber listed two members
  for one seat. The state election now leaves the player's district to the
  player's race. The member who held the seat stops serving when the winner's
  term begins, and the chamber lists the winner in that district's seat. A
  member who wins through a campaign serves out their term. If they don't run
  again, the seat opens at the next election. A vacant seat on the State screen
  used to say that state legislative elections weren't held yet. It now gives
  the date of the election that fills the seat, or, once that election is over,
  the day the new member takes it.

- **A public program's note to the books no longer prints as news** "In the
  news" showed a public program's own bookkeeping sentence, program key and all,
  such as "$750,000 may be committed for a program-key from one date through
  another. Authority to spend is not cash." The press desk no longer publishes
  those notes, and saves that already printed one no longer show it in the news,
  the opening year or the recap. The program's record keeps its fields; no news
  line is made from them yet.

- **When a reporter asks you to respond before a story runs, a long skip stops
  so you can answer.** A reporter gives you two days to respond, and the
  newsroom decides what to cover once a week. A skip of a week or more used to
  carry you straight past the question, and the story then ran saying you did
  not respond. A longer skip now pauses at each weekly newsroom review and ends
  there when a reporter has just asked you something.

- **A requirement the game reports without deciding is written in words again**
  Reading whether you could stand for office in Ohio said the game "requires
  true". Nevada's Attorney General required "MEMBER_STATE_BAR_OF_NEVADA". These
  are how the requirements are stored, not what they are, and neither sentence
  told a player anything. Ohio asks that a candidate be a qualified elector, and
  Nevada asks that its Attorney General belong to the state bar; that is what
  each now says. The game still declines to decide either one, because it
  records neither voter registration nor bar admission, and saying somebody met
  a requirement it never checked would be an eligibility it invented.

- **A life saved before school stages moves on to the right school** A life
  saved before children moved on through school stayed at the first school it
  started in, so a teenager, or a grown adult, was still at elementary school.
  The first time such a save moves forward, it catches up. A child still at the
  right school stays there and moves on at the end of the year. A child who
  should be further along moves to the middle or high school for their age that
  day, with their class. Someone past the end of high school leaves school.
  Nothing earlier is filled in.

- **A territory life saved before territories had a Governor gets one** A life
  in Puerto Rico, Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands, American Samoa or the Northern
  Mariana Islands saved before territories had their own Governor went on with
  nobody in the office. The first time such a save moves forward, the
  territory's Governor is seated for the term in progress that day, the same way
  a new game seats one. Nothing earlier is filled in: no past Governors and no
  past election. The government screen's "Territory" label, Delegate row and
  missing Senate row already applied to old saves, because they are read fresh.

- **A state chamber's roster keeps the seats of members who have died** The
  State screen listed only the living members of the home state's legislature,
  so after years of play a chamber looked smaller than it is: Maine's House
  showed 103 seats instead of 151. Every seat is now listed. A seat whose member
  has died reads "Vacant since" the date of death. The game does not hold state
  legislative elections yet, so no one has filled it.

- **Playing on as someone gives them a past of their own** When play passes to
  someone who was only ever a figure in another person's story, such as the
  teacher who once mentored the character, they now arrive with an earlier life
  of their own: a parent, the schools they went to in their home town, a
  classmate, a teacher of their own and a first job. Before, they knew only the
  person whose life had just ended and had nobody at home. Someone with no home
  on record now has one of their own. Everything already recorded about them,
  including their job and the people they already knew, stays as it was. Nothing
  is added for someone whose childhood is already on record, such as the
  character's own children, for anyone under eighteen, or for anyone older than
  the oldest age a new character can start at. A new character born on the 29th
  of February could not have an earlier life written for them, and starting such
  a life failed. It now works, with their parent's and teacher's birthdays
  falling on the 28th in years without a 29th.
