  with two partners, and was still offered more dates. Now nobody in a couple is
  offered a date or a couple with anyone else, nobody is offered one with a
  person who is with somebody, and the existing couple stands until one of them
  ends it.

- **A family's storm damage matches their house** Before, when a storm or flood
  struck a town, a family and the house they lived in were judged separately, so
  the record could say the house was destroyed while the family inside came
  through untouched, or the reverse. Now each home is judged once, and the
  family living in it shares that result. A destroyed house means a family that
  lost its home, with the injuries and deaths that follow from it.

- **One meeting is not a standing disagreement** A member of a party body
  considers leaving to organize when the body keeps deciding against something
  they hold most firmly. That "keeps" counted recorded decisions rather than
  separate occasions, and the player's own route through the party panel writes
  a fresh decision every time the button is pressed, with no date gate. So
  deciding the same question four times in one sitting read as four repeated
  defeats, and on a measured world it produced four people ready to found a
  breakaway party out of a single afternoon. Repetition is now counted in days
  on which the body actually decided. Four presses in one sitting are one
  occasion. Somebody who loses the same vote month after month reaches the same
  place they always did, and the strength of the finding still rises with the
  number of occasions rather than the number of rows. The test that covered this
  path asserted only that nobody was leaving, on a world where nobody could have
  been; it would have passed if the function had returned nothing at all. Two
  tests replace it: one holds the single sitting to no initiative, the other
  walks a member through months of losing the same vote and requires a real
  split or founding with named allies and cited decisions.

- **A congregation has one pastor** When a town's jobs were filled, a resident
  drawn to work at a church could be made its pastor even though the church
  already had one, so Frankfort, Kentucky opened with five pastors for four
  congregations. The pastor of each congregation, like the principal of each
  school, is still hired first, one to each, and the other church jobs no longer
  add a second.

- **Classmates start and finish the school year together** Each child used to
  get their own first and last day of school, so two children in the same class
  could start weeks apart. Now every school year starts on the first Monday on
  or after August 24 and ends on the Friday of its fortieth week, the same days
  for every child.

- **A big save opens in less than a third of the time and half the memory.**
  Opening a save used to read the whole world twice, copy it, and write it out
  again three times to check it. The check now reads the world once and hands
  that same world to the game, and it no longer copies it. It also remembers the
  world's identity instead of recomputing it. On Julia Griffith's 92 MB Casper
  save, opening went from about 20 seconds to 6, and the extra memory it needed
  from about 1.9 GB to 0.8 GB. That is the pressure that crashed browser tabs
  holding big saves.

- **People seated at an open table keep their legs** Someone seated behind a
  folding table in a painted room was cut off at the tabletop, so they looked
  legless. Open tables now hide only the tabletop's edge, and the person's legs
  show underneath. Counters and solid desk fronts still hide what is behind
  them.

- **The opening's state card and the rent figure never show a blank** The state
  card at the start of a new life now shows an estimated population and an
  estimated share of citizens who voted right away, and swaps in the exact
  figures when they arrive. Under "How the place is doing", the two-bedroom rent
  now shows its number on the card instead of only inside "Exact values", and a
  place with no rent figure of its own shows its state's average, marked as an
  estimate. A face that fails to draw is now tried again instead of staying as
  initials.

- **A new life opens several times faster.** Beginning a life checked the entire
  world after every record it wrote: each legislator seated, each reporter
  hired, each party committee formed. It now checks the finished world once.
  Opening a life in Concord, New Hampshire, whose House has 400 seats, went from
  3.3 seconds to 0.9 seconds in Node, and the opened world is byte-identical.

- **Keep opening figures clear of the right-side wash** The opening's right
  white gradient begins farther right and stays softer, leaving full figures
  visible. Existing text-card backing and bottom controls remain.

- **Opening records distinguish Independent from unaffiliated** Congress opening
  counts and member labels retain a declared Independent affiliation separately
  from no party. New initial tenures save that declaration; older generated
  saves read their existing starting record without rewriting history. Later
  recorded party affiliations retain precedence.

- **Show recorded officials' full figures beside their names** Members by state
  and your representatives reuse the saved full-body people-pack figure already
  used for the President and Vice President and Congress's cast. Congress's cast
  includes both recorded home-state Senators. Vacant seats have no figure; a
  missing House district does not select an arbitrary representative.

- **Show your recorded state lawmakers beside the governor** The state opening
  slide reuses the recorded home-district lawmakers and their saved full figures
  after the governor. A missing district or holder creates no invented
  representative.

- **A new life's first week no longer hands out errands that do not exist**
  Every new life opened with the same three errands: groceries, a clothing
  fitting and a visit to fix a kitchen cupboard hinge, followed by "Neither
  appointment has a time yet", which counts two of three. The fitting and the
  hinge were never anything in the world. The item now asks only for the week's
  groceries, which is a trip the player can actually make. The public-meeting
  item said "The agenda is posted" without saying which meeting. It now says
  what is true of it in any life: a public meeting is on the local calendar at
  6:30 in the evening, its agenda is posted, anyone may go and nobody has asked
  you to. The journeys to that meeting and to a party chapter's community room
  described themselves as "game-authored" and explained that no fare is charged.
  They now just say it takes about twenty minutes to get there. Lives already
  saved keep the wording they were written with.

- **Oregon rent renewals read the announced annual cap** Oregon's starting rent
  law now reads the official 9.5% annual cap for 2026 instead of the statutory
  10% ceiling. Its next dated phase reads the officially announced 10% cap for 2027. Both retain the sourced 15-year construction exemption. Coverage still
  depends on the existing recorded tenancy facts; this correction does not
  establish missing affordable-housing exemption facts.

- **Organization profile readings reuse the existing history index**
  Organization profile queries read the existing organization-keyed history
  index. Date and sequence cutoffs remain unchanged, and only the filtered
  result is sorted, preserving shared groups and earlier snapshots.

- **A chapter member sees one way to reach their own organizer** A member of a
  local party chapter used to see the same way to reach its organizer listed
  twice on the Parties page. It is now listed once.

- **The opening and filing screens no longer mislead about districts, elections
  or Puerto Rico's voting survey** The House roster on the opening screen lists
  every member from a state, and a player in San Antonio read the first row,
  Texas's 1st district, as their own representative. The roster now says it
  lists all of the state's members, and it points to Represented by under
  Government for the player's own. Filing for governor after the candidate field
  closes showed the election four years out as "the next regular election". The
  card now names the election that is still ahead, says its field has closed,
  and then gives the election a filing enters. The voting card on a Puerto Rico
  life explains that the survey does not report Puerto Rico, instead of saying
  no state matches.

- **Hard times now cut births about half as much as before, matching the
  research.** A rise in unemployment now lowers births by about three quarters
  of a percent per point, down from almost one and a half. Nine places' starting
  poverty rates were corrected by a tenth of a point to match the published
  figures.

- **The President and Vice President take their places in the Oval Office** On
  the opening's White House card, the President and Vice President used to stand
  stiffly in a row over the left sofa. They now take the room's measured places,
  the President at the desk first, drawn at the room's own scale and in a
  natural pose.

- **The Difficulty step is gone from setup** Setting up a life no longer stops
  at a Difficulty step, and Options no longer lists challenge, notebook, family
  money or press settings. The world runs one way for every life.

- **The "Your life so far" step is gone from setup** Setting up a life no longer
  stops at the "Your life so far" step with its choice cards. After the place,
  setup goes straight to Appearance and Begin.

- **Unapproved time controls removed; Also here matches the other panels** The
  "Until needed" and "Stops" buttons beside Day and Week are gone until they
  have approved names. What passing time stops for is still set in Options. The
  "Also here" list now uses the same glass panel as the rest of the screen.

- **Every setup step uses the same panel** Setup steps no longer change size
  from one to the next: each one fills the same panel, with Back and Continue in
  the same row at its bottom.

- **The red preview bar is gone** The red "Private preview" bar no longer
  appears over the game in any build.

- **Buttons and panel corners no longer cover text** The brass corner mark on
  main buttons shows only while the button is pressed, not all the time. The
  gold corner pieces on the opening panels now frame the text instead of sitting
  over the date and title.

- **The president, vice president and chief justice have the career that led to
  office** Before, the vice president's card showed one line: the day they took
  office. Now each of them arrives with the office they held before, written for
  their own home state or court, and their card lists it with the year it began.

- **The opening walks through the rooms of government instead of one building
  front after another** Before, the opening showed the outside of the Capitol
  twice, a landscape for your state, and the outside of city hall and a main
  street for your town and your life. Now each step stands inside a room: the
  Senate and House floors for the year and Congress, the governor's office for
  your state, the council chamber or the county commission room for your town
  (whichever your place's government records), and your own home for your life.

- **A person's card no longer stays open after the day moves on** Before, a
  person's card opened in a room stayed on screen after you passed the day. The
  person had left the room and the clock showed another morning, but the card
  still sat over the new scene. Now the card closes whenever the scene or the
  world's clock moves on, so it never follows you into a moment its person was
  not part of. Opening a card and then going to any screen from the menu already
  closed it; this covers the time controls, which did not.

- **The family screen shows your family standing in your own home** Before, the
  family screen showed a blank gradient, a parent could be missing from the
  picture, and a parent could wear a suit their job never calls for. Now your
  parents or guardians stand in the home your household lives in, dressed for
  their own work. Every kind of home has a picture, so the screen never falls
  back to the gradient, and each name is printed under its person on a brass
  plate in dark ink.

- **Your first job's store is named for your town** Before, the store where a
  generated past began work carried the town's postal form, such as "Dry Ridge,
  Kentucky Market", or the stand-in "Neighborhood Market". Now it carries the
  town's own name, such as "Dry Ridge Market", in every place.

- **The new-life screens show your chosen town's main street, not the White
  House.** Before, every new-life screen showed the White House behind it,
  whatever place you chose. Now the screens show your chosen town's main street,
  and your state's capitol until you pick a town.

- **A person's card and every scene now show the same outfit for the day.**
  Before, a person could wear one outfit on their card and another in the scene
  you were standing in. Now the card and every scene show the same outfit for
  the whole day.

- **Paid leave uses the adopted employee premium and wage cap** An operative
  paid-leave bill's final employee-premium rate (basis points of covered wages)
  and annual covered-wage cap (dollars per year) now reach the existing paycheck
  assessment and withholding writer. Missing or invalid terms retain the
  existing sourced or labeled fallback; filed or later unadopted text cannot
  change an assessment. Existing collection dates and completed payments remain
  intact. Save/Continue accepts adopted rates above the old typical-program 5%
  surrogate, with withholding bounded by the actual covered wages.

- **Paid leave uses the adopted replacement share** An operative paid-leave
  law's final adopted replacement share now sets the benefit paid through the
  existing claim and government-account writers. Filed terms alone do not apply.
  Wrong units and shares outside zero through one retain the existing sourced
  fallback. Weekly caps, eligibility, payments and provenance keep their current
  paths. Employee-premium terms remain a separate catalog contract gap.

- **A paper no longer reprints a story it has already run word for word.** Towns
  repeated the same few stories for years. The fishing-rights talks kept
  announcing an interim arrangement, shipping kept "returning to its usual
  pace", and the same road-repair proposal was adopted and withdrawn over and
  over, because each recurrence was a new record. A paper now passes over a
  development whose wording matches one it has already taken up. The recurrences
  themselves still happen; making them vary is a separate change.

- **Local party chapters are named the way people say the county** The Parties
  tab named a home chapter from the county's filing name in the Census listing,
  so a player in Catonsville, Maryland saw "County of Baltimore Democrats".
  Every county in the country read that way, and every parish and borough too.
  Chapters are now named for the place as its residents say it, such as
  "Baltimore County Democrats" or "Caddo Parish Republicans". A town whose
  county area has no separate government is named for that area too, because
  parties organize by county, parish and borough either way: a life in Houma,
  Louisiana now meets the Terrebonne Parish Democrats rather than the Houma
  Democrats, and Nashville's chapter is Davidson County's. Where the city is
  itself the county (Denver, Juneau, Anchorage, Richmond), in a New England
  town, in an Alaska Census Area and in a city spread over several counties, the
  chapter is named for the town, without its state attached. Chapters in saved
  games keep the names they were created with.

- **Party work that cannot happen is shown as unavailable** Asking a party
  organizer for something like a phone shift could offer a button that only
  answered that you and the organizer had no free evening in common in the next
  two weeks, or that nobody was available to host it. Saying yes to an offer
  could fail the same way. These choices are now shown as unavailable, with the
  reason, before you press them. A party chapter's meeting invitation also stops
  offering Accept once the meeting has begun.

- **Pay laws retain the recorded government payer** Payroll law checks now
  retain the recorded workplace while accepting its government's saved public
  account as the payer. The same dated ownership check applies to completed-work
  assessments. Wage controls use the canonical payroll path and retain their
  enacted-rate, payment and replay assertions.

- **Pay reads regional wage rules at the recorded workplace** The saved
  hourly-pay caller now supplies its recorded workplace's canonical place key to
  the existing regional wage reader. It does not use the worker's home as a
  workplace or infer missing regional boundaries.

- **Remove paycheck routine notices** Routine notices no longer surface paycheck
  transfers, pay details, or pending-pay reminders. Recorded pay, tax
  assessments, and balances remain in the saved simulation state.

- **A pension paid in full now pays down what it owes** A government that paid
  its whole required pension contribution still saw its unfunded pension debt
  grow, because the payment covered a thirtieth of that debt but not the
  interest on it. The required payment now pays the debt off over 30 years at
  the plans' assumed 7% return, so a government that pays in full owes less each
  year.

- **A small government paying its whole pension share is no longer recorded as
  underpaying** A town or small county that paid its whole pension share was
  recorded as underpaying by a dollar or a few, because each month's payment
  rounds to a whole dollar. The budget now counts a shortfall only when it is
  more than a dollar a month.

- **Hair sits properly on every head** Tall hairstyles such as buns, top knots
  and afros are no longer cut off at the top of the picture. On fuller bodies, a
  band of bare scalp no longer shows above the hair. Where hair falls on both
  sides of the face, the back of the hair now shows behind the neck instead of
  the room. Ears are no longer faded.

- **Asking somebody out says what happens next, and a meeting can be called
  off** Before, asking somebody out or asking to meet showed nothing, so it
  looked like the button did nothing. A meeting both people had agreed to could
  not be called off, and time stopped at it until it was attended. A child could
  also be offered party work, see a party organizer among their contacts, or ask
  an unrelated adult to meet. Now the People screen says who will answer and by
  what day. Decline on the Calendar calls off an agreed meeting, and the other
  person is told. Party work and party organizers are for adults, and a child
  meets an adult outside the family through a parent or guardian.

- **The People screen says how things stand with somebody** The People screen
  only ever said when the player was last in touch with somebody. A mother who
  had publicly distanced herself read exactly like one who had not, and a
  housemate read "Last in touch" with a date months old while the two of them
  still lived together. Now somebody the player lives with reads "You live
  together." and never "It has been a long while". Each person also carries the
  same sentence the person card uses for how things stand between them: that the
  player would not rely on what they say, that something between them was never
  settled, or that they were close once.

- **"Record a person's congregation membership"** The life record can now read
  each person's own congregation memberships over time. Family congregation
  context no longer has to stand in for an individual's affiliation.

- **Hair stays in front of collars and hoods** Front hair and face accessories
  now draw over collars and hoods while the garment still covers the neck. This
  removes the garment-colored strip that could cross the lower edge of a
  hairstyle.

- **Faint white person edges borrow their own opaque layer color** Garment
  assembly replaces faint near-white edge colors with the nearest opaque color
  within two native pixels of the same layer. Original source rasters, opaque
  artwork and every alpha value remain unchanged. Isolated edges without an
  opaque neighbor retain their source color. This candidate addresses the
  white-outline defect only. Actual same-person intro comparisons and human art
  acceptance remain separate requirements.

- **The town you typed is the town you get** Searching for a town offered a
  longer name containing it ahead of the name you actually typed, so asking for
  Columbus, Ohio and taking the first result started your life in Columbus Grove
  — a village of a couple of thousand instead of the state capital — and
  everything that follows from where you live, your city, your representatives
  and the offices you could stand for, quietly belonged to the wrong place for
  the rest of that life. The town whose name you typed now comes first.

- **A place with no government of its own is served by its county's budget** A
  census-designated place, such as Bear, Delaware, or Palmas, Puerto Rico, has
  no government of its own, yet it was given its own budget. Now its residents
  are served by the county that holds most of them, or in Puerto Rico by the
  municipio, and that government keeps the budget. Where the county area has no
  county government (New England towns, Alaska's unorganized borough and
  consolidated city-counties), the place is listed with that reason until the
  government that serves it is linked.

- **A place with no government of its own is served by its town, consolidated
  government or state** A census-designated place whose county area has no
  county government now keeps the budget of the government that really serves
  it. In Connecticut, Massachusetts and Rhode Island that is the New England
  town holding most of its residents; around Honolulu, Baton Rouge, Lafayette,
  Houma, Louisville, Nashville and Athens it is the consolidated city-county
  government; in Alaska's unorganized borough it is the state. Before, 490 such
  places kept no local budget at all, and their police were funded by no one.

- **Pending cases resume when a played save opens** Opening a played save
  reviews its existing overdue cases on the current date. Read-only and retired
  lives keep their saved records, and cases still require a sitting judge. A
  completed full opening performs the same existing review.

- **A bill the player carries through the legislature now goes to the state's
  governor, who decides it.** When a player's bill passed both chambers, the
  governor never decided it. In Kentucky, Nebraska and Alaska, the bill replayed
  the answer written into the developer scenario, which vetoed eight of its nine
  bills. In any other state the step was refused, so the bill could never be
  signed or vetoed. Now the bill lands on the seated governor's desk like every
  other bill the legislature passes. The player waits while the governor
  decides, and a veto goes back to the legislature for an override vote. A world
  with no seated governor, such as an older save, keeps its written answer.

- **Repeated floor requests preserve recorded votes** Requesting a floor vote
  again after a bill has left the floor preserves its recorded votes and shows
  that no floor vote remains.

- **Player bills use the institution's committee choice** Requesting a referral
  for a player bill now uses the same committee choice as an automatic referral.
  A congressional bill follows the existing policy-field mapping; other bills
  retain the first compiled committee as their fallback. A chamber without
  compiled committees refuses the referral. Both routes keep the existing
  session-end rules and record the referral through the same writer.

- **When a law lifts the minimum wage above what your shift pays, your shift
  pays the new wage.** A completed shift at a job you took through the career
  paths, such as shop assistant, is now paid at least the minimum wage in force
  where you live on the day you worked it, when a law passed in play set that
  wage and it is above what the shift paid. The rise is recorded as a change to
  your pay terms that names the law, so your Journal shows it and later shifts
  pay the same. A law that lowers or repeals the wage cuts nobody's pay, and a
  wage that is already below what your shift pays changes nothing.

- **You stay where you last arrived until you walk home** A meeting you finished
  no longer stops placing you: the corner and the places list name where you
  last arrived until you walk home, as the game intends. This restores that
  after an earlier change made a finished meeting's room disappear from where
  you are.

- **Played town workers receive the same payroll as their neighbors** Playing a
  person with an existing town job no longer excludes them from town paydays.
  Their employer's saved pay terms now produce pay transfers and the same
  federal and state withholding records as an NPC's pay.

- **Restore home after walks and finish meeting journeys** Walking home restores
  the household room. Taking the public-meeting journey separately from Calendar
  now records arrival so the meeting can be attended. A saved life stopped
  immediately after that journey can attend without repeating the travel time.
  Attendance remains a separate choice. New lives generate legislative members'
  given names from their already-recorded identity using the existing name
  pools. Previous replay links and saved people retain their original names and
  identities. Additional regional classifications prepare distinct location
  requests without approving or enabling new artwork.

- **Clearer art review and reliable return to title** Art requests have clearer
  categories and stable labels, with current versions and review decisions kept
  together. Removed revisions stay removed without bringing their earlier images
  or older alternatives back into review. Incoming revisions and clothing
  preparation stay with the art team; only an explicit handoff puts a finished
  revision in your review queue. Verified artwork uses follow the selected game
  version; artwork outside the checked consumers remains unconfirmed. Return to
  title waits for saving and preserves earlier saves when unsaved changes are
  discarded. New lives use contextual household history, clearer optional
  questions, readable dates and correctly owned invitations. State information
  appears together, hometown lists scroll continuously, and windows fit smaller
  screens.

- **Policy laws honor their saved effective dates** Policy law readers use the
  shared enactment date and preserve a saved game profile's date basis.
  Enactments missing a required source date remain inactive and do not enter
  judicial review before that date is known.

- **Politics opens in one press again, and the budget names your town** A Map
  entry had been added beside Politics in the menu, which quietly turned
  Politics into a folder you had to open first. The map was already a tab inside
  Politics, so the extra entry is gone and Politics opens directly again. The
  Issues and budget tab said it was showing public finances for "Here: Home"
  when your character was at home. It now names the place, such as "Here: Alamo,
  Nevada".

- **The Politics menu entry no longer describes itself as jobs and study** On a
  new character the menu read "Politics — Jobs and study", one press away from
  the actual "Jobs and study" entry, while Politics is the only way to reach
  parties, government, campaigns, local records, running for office and the
  public budget. The line under it was assembled from your office, your campaign
  and your job, and a new character has none of the first two, so only the last
  one was left. It now says what is behind the entry whatever you have reached
  so far, and still names your office and your campaign once you have them.

- **Show a state's population when a new game starts** A new game no longer says
  "Population unavailable" for your state or map areas. When the newest figures
  on file came out after the game's date, the count for a year that was already
  public is shown, rounded, as an estimate ("About 7,582,000 people, 2024
  estimate"). The voting share on the same card now reads in plain words: "About
  62% of citizen adults said they voted (give or take 3 points)."

- **Portraits show initials while a face loads, and the time previews are
  readable** A portrait drawn by the people engine was a blank circle until its
  face finished loading, so the People web opened as rows of empty rings. It now
  shows the person's initials until the face appears. The previews beside Day,
  Week and Until needed (where each jump stops) were small brown text straight
  on the room picture; they now sit on the same paper as the buttons beside
  them.

- **A week press on a long save takes about a quarter less time.** At the end of
  every press, the game checks the whole saved world. Three parts of that check
  compared each record with every other record of its kind: each paid or missed
  wage against every earlier one, each news source against every press record,
  and each death against every event. About 110 other places found an event by
  going through every event of the life. They now use an index. On Parker
  Brooks's 84 MB Woonsocket save, a week press went from about 3.4 seconds to
  2.4 in Node, and the world it produced was byte-for-byte the same.

- **Private aims say what they can and cannot offer** An aim you set for your
  character now tells you the truth about itself. "Run for office" is offered
  where the game actually knows of an elected office you could stand for, and
  when it has nothing to put under it, it says why in the same words the
  candidacy rules use — that it has not recorded how long you have lived here,
  or that it has not read this state's offices yet — instead of leaving an empty
  space. Where the aim is unavailable it no longer blames your age when your age
  has nothing to do with it. Someone you have not spoken to in over a year is no
  longer offered as both somebody to get back in touch with and somebody to
  pursue a relationship with. Getting back in touch comes first, and the
  relationship aim becomes available once you have actually been in contact
  again.

- **Profile and territory legislatures keep their hearing calendar** A saved
  state legislature profile remains recognizable when the same state also has a
  researched rule pack. Hearing requests in that profile and in a territory can
  use the existing shared state-session calendar. An explicit calendar and a
  hearing already scheduled keep their supplied dates. The change does not
  supply missing executive decisions or admit unregistered rule packs.

- **Program funding news reads in plain words** News of money set aside for a
  public program now names the program and dates in words instead of an internal
  key.

- **A promise someone brings up again is no longer counted as kept** When a
  neighbor brought up something you had promised, the game counted the promise
  as kept, whatever you had done. It now stays open until something on record
  answers it. Saying you will go to the posted public meeting is kept when you
  are there and broken when the meeting ends without you, and a canceled meeting
  leaves nothing to keep. A promise to vote is judged by the roll call. The
  neighbor who heard you weighs a broken promise when deciding whether to raise
  it with you, and has less reason to raise one you kept. Help between people is
  now recorded as a favor: who helped whom, what was done, and how much it
  mattered. How much the person helped still feels they owe fades with time,
  faster for small favors and for people who let things slip, while a giver who
  wanted something back expects more, and a favor returned settles it. No list
  of who owes whom is shown anywhere.

- **The prose tools load the simulation again** Nothing changes for the player.
  The development prose tools had stopped starting: reading how long two people
  have been apart pulled in the whole contact engine, which made a loop of files
  that the tools' loader could not untangle. The names of the contact records
  now live in a file of their own, and a test loads the whole simulation the way
  the tools do.

- **Governments start with money in their accounts** A new game now opens every
  government's account with a starting balance instead of nothing: the federal
  government, each of the fifty states, and each local government the game runs.
  Each state also starts with its own generated tax and service settings, so a
  funded program has money to draw on from the first day.

- **The posted public meeting shows its room instead of a blank screen** The
  posted public meeting and neighborhood meetings in a community room now show
  their painted rooms. Any screen whose room has no picture of its own now shows
  the place's picture instead of a blank background.

- **Public payment effects use recorded monthly personal income** Public
  spending and tax collection effects compare completed payments with recorded
  aggregate personal income for the same jurisdiction and calendar month. A
  fixed dollar amount, labor-income proxy, or divided annual total cannot supply
  that denominator. A compatible positive income record must be available at the
  read date. This consumer change does not create ordinary monthly income
  records; the existing canonical metric writer and its producer coverage remain
  separate.

- **A life in Puerto Rico is named the way the island names people** A new life
  in Mayagüez drew Douglas Pope from the random name button and grew up with
  Alexander Rosado, Frances Patton and Waylon Rosado. Names came from the
  national tables, which do not cover Puerto Rico. Once Puerto Rico is chosen,
  the random name button now draws from the island's own names, and so does the
  new game for the character, their family, and the neighbors, classmates and
  meeting-goers they meet. Each person has a given name and two surnames, the
  father's first surname and then the mother's, and each parent carries the
  surname they passed on. The names come from the people the island's public
  schools are named for, so they lean toward an older generation until a
  birth-year source is found. Guam, the Northern Mariana Islands, the U.S.
  Virgin Islands and American Samoa have too few such schools to measure, so
  they keep the national names for now. Saved games keep their names, and so
  does everywhere else.

- **Puerto Rico's legislature has its real 51 Representatives and 27 Senators**
  A life in Puerto Rico used to start with a legislature whose size was drawn
  from the states' range. It now starts with the Legislative Assembly the
  Constitution of Puerto Rico sets: 51 Representatives (one from each of 40
  districts and 11 at large) and 27 Senators (two from each of 8 districts and
  11 at large). Represented by, under Government, names the home's district
  members along with the 11 elected at large in each chamber.

- **Quiet conversations stay with the people who hear them** Speaking quietly to
  someone nearby no longer gives other people in the room direct knowledge of
  the exchange. The conversation's witnesses and remembered knowledge now follow
  who could hear it, including after saving and reloading.

- **Time stops when a work decision needs you** A quiet stretch now shows a
  known job-offer reply date or accepted work start as its next stop. Once it
  reaches that day, another quiet stretch waits for your Work decision. You can
  still choose Day or Week to pass time.

- **The quiet-stretch checks open the posted meeting the way Begin does** The
  quiet-stretch checks now seat the town's council before the first day, as
  Begin does, and stay to the end of the posted meeting with the finish-meeting
  command. Nothing changes in play.

- **Letting the weeks run on stops for meetings you were invited to** When the
  story has nothing to ask you, it offers "Let the weeks run on". That button
  used to jump one to four months without looking at your calendar. It walked
  past every posted public meeting, party meeting and campaign shift you had
  been invited to, and each one was recorded as having passed without an answer.
  A Nevada life took the button about forty times and lost five years that way.
  The button now runs only as far as the morning of the next thing on your
  calendar: a public meeting, a party meeting, a campaign shift, a commitment or
  a dated matter. Its description says which one and on what date. On the day
  itself, the choices include "Attend" for that meeting next to letting the
  weeks run on, so going or not going is your decision. An unanswered social
  invitation still lapses during the stretch unless you asked to be stopped for
  invitations.

- **Recall counts use residents' recorded views** Existing qualified recalls
  count living adult residents' saved support and opposition views without
  drawing or extrapolating turnout. Counts retain their source records and exact
  zeros. An election with no recorded views waits for evidence. Petition
  qualification waits for valid signature records and the concrete electorate
  base required by its existing legal threshold; a private opinion is not
  treated as a signature.

- **Keep rendered scene clothing when inspecting a person** Inspecting a person
  from a scene retains the rendered clothing in their portrait and expanded
  figure.

- **Campaign memos label district estimates and show their recorded spread**
  Campaign memos label district estimates when the campaign has no poll of its
  own. They list compared districts and their recorded spread instead of
  exposing hidden candidate support. Action effects and election-night shares
  use recorded support without added random noise.

- **Government cash books retain actual payments** Governments with a recorded
  public account keep their balance and monthly cash receipts and payments from
  that account, including partial payments. Federal cash uses its existing
  receipt and outlay categories; the old federal forecast remains available
  while its callers are converted.

- **Childhood history follows recorded evidence** Opening characters' childhood
  histories use recorded family and childhood facts. Unrecorded care, illness,
  school, legal and first-job experiences are no longer assigned by chance.
  Household money retains its sourced estimate when historical pay is
  unavailable. Opening histories now carry each person's recorded parents,
  household, income band, place and congregation context. Historical household
  pay uses a labeled proxy from the recorded family household when available.
  Missing family counts reuse saved family patterns matching place, household
  type and income band first, then same-place families, then the game's recorded
  family spread. These estimates preserve their contributing person IDs and do
  not create unrecorded treatment, illness or other events. Caregiver
  availability is estimated from recorded parents or household adults, otherwise
  the saved pattern's parent count per child; its continuous value contributes
  to the existing reliability reader. Emotional warmth or harshness is never
  inferred. Opening childhood money now uses the family's pay and household
  size. Where pay is absent, the existing place and occupation wage reader
  supplies a labeled estimate from recorded jobs, tenure and hours. A comparable
  paid family in the same place, then state, then observed game pool supplies
  missing income; source people remain attached to the estimate. When no
  parent-child sample exists, actual household adults and children supply the
  caregiver proxy. Childhood estimates display plain prose, rounded ratios and
  correct singular forms.

- **Tuition retries reuse one saved charge** Tuition charges are saved before
  payment, and retrying an unpaid period reuses the same bill. Repayment records
  distinguish fees, interest and principal. Balance reductions are recorded
  separately from cash payments.

- **Recruits weigh the cost of a campaign** A person a party asks to run for the
  state legislature now weighs what a campaign takes from their life, a year and
  their money, next to the odds. The recorded reasons for declining include that
  cost, so a recruit's age and circumstances can tip the answer instead of every
  district drawing the same number of candidates.

- **People form political views from what they hold, not from a coin flip** When
  someone in the world thinks over a question they have met, their view now
  comes only from what their history records about them. Two close options are
  no longer settled by chance, so the same person with the same history reaches
  the same view in every new game.

- **Earned pay queries use the recorded workplace region** Final enacted pay
  terms and earned-pay authority validation use the workplace recorded at the
  earned cutoff. An unbound workplace does not acquire a home or default region.
  Existing authority, payer and amount checks remain intact.

- **The version number and patch notes advance again** New releases no longer
  stop because their generated notes need formatting.

- **Remove the aimless short-walk choices** Places and the opening no longer
  offer “Take a short walk nearby” or “Walk home.” Trips connected to real
  activities and the conversations used by those activities remain available.

- **New market rents follow the home's bedroom count and market prices** New
  market leases use HUD's rent for the home's bedroom count, adjusted by the
  modeled market price level. Random lease and county price multipliers no
  longer change the amount.

- **A renter has a real rent bill from the start** New renters have a rent bill
  when their game opens. Places without a direct HUD rent row use a
  population-weighted average from published county rents, with the estimate and
  its source recorded in the simulation.

- **Campaign money ordered repaid now goes to the government, not back to the
  campaign** When an ethics or elections body finds that a candidate took
  campaign money for personal use, the repayment it orders is now paid to the
  state (or to the United States Treasury for a federal case). Before, the money
  went back into the campaign account, where the same candidate could take it
  again. Reactions to how a disaster was handled now arrive with the weekly
  news, which also fixes a loading-order fault that stopped part of the test
  suite from starting.

- **Repeated history counts occasions rather than records** Repeated formative
  history is what proposes a change to a character, and it was measured by
  counting interaction records. Several records can carry one event, so one
  thing happening twice and one thing recorded twice looked the same. It now
  counts distinct occasions; a record the history cannot tie to an event still
  counts as its own, because the record is then the only evidence that it
  happened. This does not change which change is proposed. That is still the
  first entry in the personality catalog whatever the occasions were about,
  which is recorded as a defect and filed as a question rather than guessed at.

- **State and local accounts open from budget estimates** New games use each
  state's estimated budget balance and reserves for its opening public cash.
  Local accounts use the average of the available local budget estimates.
  Existing saves keep their recorded amounts.

- **Bills in six researched states can reach the floor** In Minnesota, Illinois,
  Maryland, Missouri, Nevada and Ohio, no bill ever became law. The game has
  read these legislatures' constitutions but not their committee lists, and a
  bill that cannot be sent to a committee never moves. Until those lists are
  read, each chamber in these states has one standing committee, which hears
  every bill and reports it on a majority vote. This is the game's own stand-in,
  not the state's rules, and the real committees will replace it once they are
  read.

- **Nonhousing bills use sourced regional estimates** New nonhousing bill terms
  use the retained 2024 regional consumer spending categories. Territories use a
  marked national estimate. Existing terms and payments stay recorded; revised
  estimates apply prospectively. Actual rent and mortgage contracts remain
  separate.

- **Rival field work counts only completed effort** A rival's public appearance
  no longer earns campaign support from an assumed length or crew size. Field
  effects require recorded completed work, its duration, and the people who
  worked. An appearance without that record has no field effect.

- **Rival committees budget messages from their own money** Rival committees
  plan paid messages with the same weekly advertising budget as the player's
  campaign. Each buy comes from the rival's own treasury and goes to its own
  vendor, with spending limited to the money it actually holds.

- **Saves with years of Congress votes are much smaller.** Every recorded vote
  used to write each member's seat, name and reason in full, so after twenty
  game years the votes of Congress were a third of a save. A save now lists each
  set of members once and writes every vote as a short code against it. On the
  61 MB Ketchikan save that is 43 MB, and the world it loads is identical. Saves
  written before this still open, and each is rewritten in the smaller form the
  next time it is saved.

- **Time controls no longer offer a routine shift stop** The Calendar and corner
  Stops checklist now show the choices that can still change how a skip stops.
  Ordinary work shifts continue on their schedule, including when an older save
  contains the former work-shift setting. Confirmed commitments and decisions
  that need the player still stop time.

- **Running for office lists every office where you live, each with its own
  age** A character too young for every office nearby used to see one unlabeled
  sentence under "Running for office" ("This office asks for a candidate to be
  at least 24.") instead of the offices themselves. In Rapid City, South Dakota,
  an eighteen-year-old now sees the state House and Senate seats, the city
  council and the mayor, each with its own requirement beside its name, and
  choosing one shows why it cannot be filed for yet. Every minimum age now reads
  the same way: "You must be at least 21 to stand for this office." The
  governor's section no longer explains that the game had not read the state's
  rule. The same held in Louisiana and Indiana, where the state House's "at
  least 24" sat directly above the governor's "at least 21" with nothing to say
  which was which. Each age now stays with its own office, and the governor's
  section shows one age: the one a filing is held to. "Who governs where you
  live" now uses the state's own word. Lake Charles lists Calcasieu Parish and
  no longer mentions counties, and Anchorage speaks of boroughs. The note about
  places that span several of them appears only when yours does, and gives the
  actual number ("This place lies in two parishes, and each is listed above.").
  A place with no separate county, parish or borough government is told so
  plainly instead of citing a government listing. Ohio, Missouri and Nebraska's
  governors, and any seat whose age comes from the state's written rules, now
  use the same sentence too, in place of "Too young to stand: this office has a
  minimum age of 30, and this character is 18." A requirement a character cannot
  yet show, such as being a qualified elector or fifteen years a citizen, reads
  "This office requires a candidate who is a qualified elector, and nothing yet
  shows that you meet it." instead of explaining what the game does not record.

- **Big saves load and pass a week faster.** The check that runs on every save
  used to search whole history lists record by record, over and over, so its
  cost grew with the length of a life. It now builds each lookup once per check.
  On a 65 MB Seattle save, one weekly press went from about 17 seconds to about
  6, and loading went from about 10 seconds to about 5.5. The saved world is
  byte-identical.

- **Saving no longer rewinds a day pressed while the save is in progress.** If
  you press **Day** while **Save** is still in progress, the newer day stays in
  the game and reaches the following autosave. The existing browser save format
  is IndexedDB database version 4 and record version 4; large snapshots use
  chunk rows. This build still reads saved-world records from versions 1 through
  4, including version 3. An older build that requests database version 3 cannot
  open a database after it has been upgraded to version 4; IndexedDB rejects
  that open, but the saved data is left untouched. Reload with the current build
  to continue.

- **The title screen no longer says you have no saved lives while it is still
  opening them.** A long life can take a moment to open. The title screen and
  the saved games list now say your lives are being opened instead of "None
  yet". If they cannot be read, they say so, say that nothing was deleted, and
  offer to try again, instead of claiming the browser will not store anything.
  The list of saved lives no longer opens every whole life to show it, so a very
  long life can no longer stop the list from appearing; if one cannot be read at
  that moment, it is listed as kept and the rest still show.

- **Saved paycheck taxes retain their governing law** Saved wage-tax liabilities
  and payment allocations retain the enacted law that governed them. Attribution
  uses the existing recorded amount and does not assess or collect money again.
  Missing law bindings remain unavailable.

- **Enacted typed taxes collect without a catalog question** Declaring a taxable
  occurrence under an already enacted typed levy again records its assessment
  and scheduled collection. The shared handler uses the saved levy, policy and
  enactment without inventing a catalog question. Repeating the activity does
  not assess it twice; catalog-bound levies retain their adopted-term checks.

- **A long life's exported save can be imported again** Importing a save refused
  any file over 8 MiB with "That file is larger than a saved life is allowed to
  be." Long lives exported from play measure up to 91.6 MB, so they could be
  exported but never brought back. The limit is now 256 MiB, and the Save screen
  and the importer read it from the same place.

- **A school-age child goes to school again** A child old enough for school was
  starting life attending nothing: no school, no classmates and no enrollment on
  their record. Every opening scene set at school or with a classmate quietly
  stopped appearing, and the town the child lived in had no school in it.
  School-age children are enrolled again, and those scenes come back with them.
  An adult's summarized earlier life is unchanged: it still declines to assume a
  school, employer or credential the record does not actually hold.

- **A small town's high school is usually named for the town** Generated schools
  named one high school in five after a national figure, wherever it was, so a
  new life in Rugby, North Dakota, a town of about 2,500, went to "Booker T.
  Washington High School". New games now draw the kind of name from how schools
  are actually named. The measurement comes from the school directory the game
  already ships, and no real school's name is used. Where a town has only one
  school at a level, about two in three carry the town's name, and a national
  figure names about one high school in five hundred. In a city with many
  schools, most are named for a person, a neighborhood or the landscape. A
  national figure is picked by how often schools in that part of the country
  carry the name, so Cesar Chavez turns up mostly in the West. Saved games keep
  their schools, and so does a person someone continues as.

- **A child's School screen names their school and grade** A child opening
  School read only "You are a student," and over the summer not even that. It
  now names the school they go to and the grade they are in, or, over the
  summer, the school and grade they start in the fall. The Study tab shows a
  child still in school their school instead of a list of colleges to apply to,
  and the college list no longer prints where its listings came from.

- **A new character starts school on the first day of a school year** A new
  character's first school was recorded as starting on their fifth birthday. A
  child born after September 1 was a year ahead of the school calendar, spent
  seven years in elementary school and moved up a year late. Now each school a
  new character has attended starts on the first day of a school year and ends
  on the last. A five-year-old not due in kindergarten until the fall has a
  place waiting and starts that fall. This applies to new games. A game already
  in progress keeps its dates.

- **A new world seats all 100 senators, and the opening card names any empty
  seat** A new world could open with a Senate or House seat left vacant by a
  draw, and the opening card's "In the year" line counted only the parties'
  members, so it showed 99 senators with nothing about the hundredth. Now a new
  world seats a member in every seat of both chambers; a seat is vacant only
  when a later record, such as a death or a resignation, makes it so. When a
  seat is empty, the opening card names it ("a Hawaii seat is vacant"), so its
  counts always add up to the whole chamber.

- **Services read only their own recorded funding authority** Service delivery
  accepts its saved standing appropriation without treating an unrelated saved
  pay rule as service funding. Existing funded-service requirements remain in
  force.

- **Mortality starts at world opening** Newly created worlds now schedule
  mortality on their opening date, so age-based risk is represented during the
  first watched days.

- **Town job changes begin at opening** The town's recorded job needs receive
  their first review at opening.

- **Residents answer one another's meeting requests** Residents pursuing a
  connection now send a real contact proposal, and the other resident's
  personality helps shape and remains in the recorded answer.

- **Privacy does not force an existing couple to split** People keep their
  relationships unless their own relationship record gives them a reason to
  leave.

- **Decide session-work offers from recorded NPC intentions** Session-work
  offers use the shared decision engine and recorded intentions. An undecided
  answer creates no work commitment or offer history.

- **Medicaid eligibility changes reach covered people when a law takes effect**
  An enacted Medicaid eligibility change now evaluates recorded people on its
  effective date and saves any resulting health coverage change with the enacted
  law as its cause.

- **Voter ID and same-day registration laws reach recorded residents** The photo
  identification and same-day registration policy rows now use their existing
  per-person law consequences in generated policy catalogs, so a law in force
  can write the rule for each recorded voting-age resident.

- **Hair leaves the sides of the face visible** Front hair follows the visible
  sides of the selected face while preserving bangs.

- **Senate vacancy appointments use recorded or estimated legal windows** Senate
  vacancy appointments now use recorded legal windows rather than a universal
  ten-day delay. Missing timing is estimated from comparable legal windows and
  marked in the scheduled action's provenance; late notices do not restart the
  window.

- **Committee report commands use the clock's referral** When a saved committee
  is absent from the caller's compiled list, the report command now uses the
  legislative clock's referral. If no committee is compiled, it leaves the bill
  unchanged and explains why no report vote was taken.

- **A filed campaign spending report keeps the payees and purposes it was filed
  with** A spending report a committee already filed no longer changes when a
  payee is renamed or reclassified later, or when the payee is paid again after
  the filing.

- **Public-life trait readers join the shared effects index** Pride, humility
  and self-consciousness are now registered through the shared effects index,
  with each decision reason loaded once.

- **Recorded pay uses one annual cadence reader** Town layoff decisions and
  recorded job pay now annualize weekly, biweekly, semimonthly, and monthly
  compensation consistently.

- **Read the current place from its recorded state** Shared scenes read current
  placement, recorded people and pending requests. They compose offered words
  through the existing English engine and retain exchanges through canonical
  conversation, claim, knowledge and contact writers. The Day view uses
  supported placement records. Historical recognition retains recorded family,
  encounter and overlapping past-work reasons without creating attendance or
  friendship. Already-recorded opening beats remain readable.

- **Recorded shared mortgages count as household housing bills** Household
  housing bills recognize shared-loan payments when the linked debt's operative
  recorded terms identify a mortgage. Legacy mortgage flows remain recognized.
  Other loan kinds do not become housing bills, and reading a bill does not
  change the saved contract or move money.

- **A bill the legislature passed still becomes law after the session ends** A
  bill that passed both chambers of a state legislature used to freeze when the
  session ended, even after the governor had signed it, so it never became law.
  Now the clerks still send it on, the governor still signs or vetoes it, and a
  signed bill becomes law after the legislature has gone home, as it does in
  real life. A bill still waiting in a committee or on a floor when the session
  ends still stops there.

- **A sitting governor can file for re-election** A governor in office could
  only read when their term ended; there was no way to stand again. A governor
  can now put their name in for the next election. A governor at their state's
  term limit is told plainly why they cannot stand.

- **Two slow checks finish in a fraction of the time** Two internal checks that
  build a whole nationwide world now check the world once per batch of writes,
  and the opening-life checks have a time limit that matches how long a
  nationwide world really takes. Nothing changes in play.

- **Small towns no longer get big-city councils, and D.C.'s mayor shows as its
  mayor** A town of fewer than 2,500 people no longer draws a council of eight
  or more seats; its council size comes from the smaller councils real towns
  have. In a watched world's vital statistics, D.C.'s executive row now reads
  "Mayor of the District of Columbia's party," and the mayor row names the same
  person instead of saying no mayor is recorded.

- **Somebody who is not here is on the phone** Opening a conversation with
  somebody who was not in the room, such as an old contact asking to meet, drew
  them in the conversation as if they were standing there. The room draws only
  the people actually present, so the conversation now does the same: somebody
  who is not here is marked as on the phone, their face is drawn as a call
  rather than a presence, and nobody absent is added to the people around the
  player.

- **Unread state tax deductions use their sourced average** State tax deductions
  absent from the acquired table use the plain average of the read deductions
  for their tax structure. The world seed no longer changes that estimate or the
  withholding from the same paycheck. Read deductions and tax brackets keep
  their acquired values.

- **Member filing reads sourced chamber limits** The existing member filer
  checks saved bills against declared chamber limits before introducing a bill.
  A missing research row leaves filing uncapped. Sourced limits require bound
  periods, conditions and exemptions; an unread exemption leaves its whole limit
  unapplied instead of blocking a potentially exempt bill. The existing filer
  records “limit not applied: exemption unread” for that intake. The reader
  applies admitted subject and sponsor tokens and selects the sourced period
  condition from each bill’s recorded date. Quoted exceptions and unselected
  conditions stay labeled; no session window is invented.

- **With no Vice President, the Speaker acts as President** When the President
  dies while the vice presidency is empty, the office no longer stays vacant.
  The Speaker of the House resigns as Speaker and as a Representative and acts
  as President for the rest of the term, as federal law provides; failing a
  Speaker, the President pro tempore of the Senate does. The Speaker's House
  seat is then filled like any other vacancy.

- **American spelling holds after other work lands** Words that came back in
  British spelling after the sweep (catalog, neighbor, behavior, modeled,
  offense) are American again. `npm run spelling` now lists anything the
  spelling check would flag, and `npm run spelling -- --write` repairs it, so
  new writing can be put right in one step.

- **A spending bill's fiscal note states what it provides** When a spending bill
  came up for negotiation, its fiscal note said the bill appropriated nothing,
  even when it provided millions. The note now states the amount the bill
  provides. A bill that charges a fee states the charge for each payment and
  says its total is unknown until the number of payments is known.

- **A House member's own seat is no longer shown as their home district** When a
  home place lies in two House districts, the Government screen now keeps the
  home's district unresolved after the player wins one of them. Before, it
  showed the seat the player holds as the district that contains their home,
  which the game has not recorded. The note naming both districts stays.

- **Split-town district assignments follow recorded population shares**
  Split-town homes start in the crossing legislative district with the largest
  recorded share of the town's Census population. This estimated assignment no
  longer changes with the game seed. Existing assignments and the player's
  explicit district choice stay intact.

- **A life in a town split between districts can run for the legislature**
  Before this, someone who lived in a town that crosses several state House or
  Senate districts, such as Anchorage, could never run for either chamber. The
  campaign screen said the game could not tell which district they lived in. Now
  a new life in such a town starts out living in one of the districts that cross
  it, and can put their name in for that district's seat. The district seat
  panel on the campaign screen says which district the home is in and how long
  the character has lived there. It also lets the player pick another of the
  town's districts instead, without restarting that clock. An older save in such
  a town can pick its district the same way. For now a new life's starting
  district is picked by chance until the game knows how to place a home more
  precisely. The note about a home in a town split between U.S. House districts
  no longer names the file it draws on.

- **SQLite saves can use the existing chunked world format** SQLite world saves
  now preserve the existing chunked payload when a saved world exceeds one
  JavaScript string. Legacy saves remain readable, and replacing a save writes
  its metadata and chunks together.

- **Let saved worker intentions decide town job quits** Town workers weigh their
  saved wish to stop working against their livelihood goals instead of quitting
  because they are the youngest. A worker who chooses to stop working is not
  immediately assigned another job in the same review.

- **Scene slots distinguish eligibility from attendance** Scene staging now
  names every existing spot and distinguishes neutral furniture from reserved
  staff, member, witness and speaker positions. Actual chosen poses must fit the
  slot kind. The existing measured live surfaces are linked into staging without
  changing their geometry. Intro proof telemetry preserves the selected spot and
  the actor projection's saved selection records. A recorded absence interval
  now overrides computed work whereabouts. The workplace reader requires the
  exact employer and labels its result as computed shift presence, which remains
  separate from recorded room attendance. The absence writer does not change
  pay, employment status or invent an illness. This candidate remains held for
  the owner's joint six-place intro evidence. Unstaged portraits, unavailable
  turned art and missing leaning pose contracts are reported as gaps. Original
  raster artwork and its lineage are unchanged.

- **Funded services retain their standing appropriation authority** The shared
  consequence contract admits an existing sourced appropriation without
  inventing a bill. The saved authority keeps its government, account, source
  and availability window. Actual paid service and completed recipient activity
  remain required by the service consumer.

- **Things you have not got round to now say how long they have sat** The
  household errands and a posted meeting you have not decided about used to read
  word for word the same on your first day and three months later. If you let a
  season go by without doing them, the game still described your life as though
  it were the first morning of it. They now say how long they have been
  standing, once they have stood a week or more. Nothing is added in the first
  few days, because a thing written on Monday and still there on Wednesday is
  not a fact about your life. Nothing expires and nothing is done on your
  behalf; whether an errand nobody ever does should eventually lapse is a
  separate question.

- **Starting a new life no longer takes many minutes** A new life opens in well
  under a minute again. People's personalities are now worked out the first time
  something needs them instead of for everyone at once.

- **Start with sourced health program thresholds** Starting law now includes
  Medicaid eligibility limits and SNAP and Medicaid work-hour thresholds. The
  Virgin Islands value uses its local poverty measure and is kept distinct from
  the federal poverty measure.

- **State legislative campaign days use faster life history lookups.** When a
  Day prepares many state legislative candidates, the game now reuses life
  history lookups across new campaign records. Candidate identities,
  qualification dates and recorded decisions stay the same.

- **A state House winner can take the district seat they won** When a
  candidate's home district is saved but the filing screen did not ask them to
  name it, winning the election now connects that result to the district seat.
  The winner can appear as its member and vote during the term, including in
  older saves with enough recorded district information. The game leaves a seat
  unresolved when its district cannot be established from the save.

- **State laws keep their recorded default date** State laws now save the
  default date calculated from their jurisdiction's date rule and recorded
  passage or adjournment. Explicit dates remain in effect, and local councils
  retain their own date rules. Estimated defaults stay labeled as estimates.

- **A state legislative seat ends when its term does, and a re-elected member
  keeps one seat** A state legislative seat won in an older save outside
  Kentucky had no end date, so the member held it indefinitely. One Nevada
  Assembly seat won in 2032 was still held in 2039. When you continue a save
  like that, the seat now ends when its term does. If the term's end is still
  ahead, the seat ends on that day. If the date has already passed, the seat
  ends the next time a day passes, and your work line no longer lists it. A
  member who runs again for the seat they hold and wins now keeps one seat.
  Before, the old seat stayed open next to the new one. The result now says "won
  and keeps the seat. The new term begins January 1, 2028." It no longer says
  the office is not theirs until then. The list of offices you could run for now
  shows every office's next election as a date, for example "The next election
  is November 3, 2026." Before, most offices said the timing was not
  established. An office with a race already on record shows that race's date.
  An office you can run for now says "You can stand for this office." It no
  longer says you are eligible "under the represented rules." When the field for
  the nearest governor's race has already closed, the governor card now says so:
  "The field for the November 3, 2026 election has closed. The next is November
  5, 2030." Before, it named only the later election and gave no reason for
  skipping the nearer one. A lost race now says only that the candidate lost,
  without the added line about it not being the end of them. The office offer
  names the office to be filled and no longer adds that nobody has asked you to
  stand.

- **Reuse recorded state legislature wake dates** State legislature queue
  planning reuses an append-aware index of recorded fields instead of scanning
  unrelated history for every pack and election year. Recorded dates, event
  order, decisions and canonical dispatch remain unchanged.

- **State lawmakers can bring forward another eligible priority** When another
  member has already proposed their first-choice question, state lawmakers can
  propose another eligible question from their recorded principles and the full
  policy catalog. Existing filing limits, session rules, pending bills, and
  cooldowns still apply. Each intake admits at most one bill per member and
  question; the player's member remains under player control.

- **State intake files bills only from seated members' recorded reasons** State
  bill intake now uses the shared member agenda. It no longer picks an authored
  bill or creates a person merely to sponsor one. A seated member files from
  recorded principles; a legislature without a seated sponsor files nothing.

- **Race results say the vote shares** A race result used to say only who won:
  "Fatima Erickson won. The seat is theirs." It now gives the margin, "won,
  52.3% to 47.7%", on the campaign screen, in the governor's status on the
  Candidacy screen, and in the public record of the result. The papers write
  their story from that record, so a statehouse paper's report of a governor's
  race now carries the numbers too.

- **Statehood voting-seat records retain their governing law** New voting-seat
  tenure records retain the enacted statehood law and the actual person and
  chamber behind the consequence, including after record reload.

- **After a day passes the status card names the room you are in** The status
  card used yesterday's opening workplace as the live room after a day had
  passed, which left the old workplace on the card and an empty room. It now
  uses the opening placement only while the opening scene or today's work
  arrival is current, and otherwise shows the ordinary day's room and the people
  actually there.

- **The store cashier's feet sit behind the counter** In the store backdrop the
  cashier's floor contact moves a little deeper, so they stand behind the
  counter instead of on top of it. A check now draws a random place, starts a
  new game there and confirms the cashier takes the staff spot with the counter
  hiding the lower body.

- **Fewer, better-timed disasters: only larger storms strike a town, each on its
  own day.** Every storm and flood the world produced was dated the first of its
  month, so several could strike one town on the same day. Each one now arrives
  on the day its recorded source episode began. Only the larger recorded
  episodes now become a disaster for a town. The storm records count every wind
  report, and treating each one as a damaging event gave one county about
  fifteen disasters a year. Which reports a town really lives through is filed
  as a research question; until it is answered, only episodes the game rates
  major or catastrophic are declared.

- **The Study tab shows the programs you can enroll in** The Study tab under
  Education and work showed only the directory of real colleges, where nothing
  can be applied for. The programs a person can actually enroll in, including
  the certificates that open better-paid jobs, sat only inside the collapsed
  "Other paths and invitations" section, so a Casper life reached 21 without
  finding them. The Study tab now lists those programs first, each with its
  cost, length, credential and an Enroll button, above the college directory.
  Work, volunteering and invitations stay where they were.

- **People are named from your side, the same way on every screen** Before: a
  34-year-old in Reno who retired from play was offered her own father and the
  person she lived with as "no connection on record", and the middle-school
  teacher who had mentored her as "Rachel Roth's someone they taught, age 64".
  Elsewhere, people were introduced with clauses such as "Dean Campos, who you
  live with". After: people are named the way you would name them. On the
  successor list, in the room, on the People screen and in scenes you see "Dean
  Campos, your housemate", "Your former teacher, 64", "your grandfather" and
  "your coworker". Someone with no tie on record reads "Not someone close to
  you, 41." How: the successor list reads parents and the current household, and
  which side of a mentorship the finished life was on. The relationship reader
  every screen shares now returns a noun phrase from your side and knows mentors
  and grandparents. Who is told about an estate is unchanged.

- **Prove affectionate choices in a generated game** The generated-game proof
  exercises the affectionate tendency's relationship choice and verifies that
  its recorded reason reaches the decision trace.

- **Brooding people can reconsider an earlier hurt** A recorded brooding
  tendency can contribute a reason to decline an invitation while past
  unpleasant experiences remain on the person's mind.

- **Teasing people offer another time for a meeting** People with a recorded
  teasing tendency may suggest another time for a meeting, keeping the
  invitation open in a way that suits them.

- **You can talk to a coworker or housemate the room shows** Before, picking a
  coworker in the room and choosing Talk opened a blank box that said "This
  person is no longer here with you." Now Talk opens the conversation with that
  person: their face, what you can say, and their reply, through the same
  ordinary-talk writer the rest of the game uses.

- **Teacher salary floors read the saved law's annual amount** A teacher salary
  floor now comes from the law's recorded annual amount, including a starting
  law, rather than a drawn share of a wage survey. Missing or nonpositive
  adopted amounts do not become a legal floor. The starting-law records include
  the $50,000 amounts already cited for Arkansas and Iowa.

- **Teacher salary comparisons use recorded pay** Teacher salary comparisons now
  annualize recorded pay using its actual pay schedule, without scaling it to an
  assumed 40-hour week. Only teacher roles with positive, finite recorded hours
  contribute to the salary comparison.

- **Teacher salary bills record the sponsor's requested amount** State members
  supporting higher teacher pay write a salary floor from their state's recorded
  public-teacher compensation. The saved median amount reaches the adopted law
  and its salary-floor reader, with or without an existing numeric floor.
  Missing recorded teacher pay does not become an estimated floor.

- **Wage readers and payday settlement use adopted terms** Federal and city wage
  readers use adopted hourly amounts and operative dates through the existing
  final-law reader. The unused average state-raise helper is removed. Payday
  settlement lets the common pay writer apply the wage law before payment,
  preserving its attribution. Historical cutoff and state readers remain
  unchanged.

- **Office pay uses the recorded-tenure wage query** Office staff pay now calls
  the existing recorded-tenure wage query without its ignored draw argument. Pay
  amounts, hours, cadence and withholding continue through their existing
  writers.

- **Recorded job tenure sets pay without a person wage draw** Town job pay no
  longer varies independently by a person's seeded percentile adjustment. It
  retains the existing tenure-based occupational wage calculation, recorded
  hours, employer cadence and legal floors. Credential-based wage sizing remains
  unfinished.

- **Office staff pay uses recorded work and occupational wages** Civil-service,
  executive-staff and agency-director jobs with a mapped occupation now
  initialize pay from their recorded workplace, weekly hours and the existing
  occupational wage reader. An unmapped occupation no longer receives the
  generic office salary. Existing salary agreements and enacted office salaries
  remain intact.

- **Vacant public-body job offers read a sourced occupation median** Public-body
  vacancies read the recorded employer's pay for the same occupation when
  available. Otherwise the approved sourced occupation/workplace median supplies
  an explicitly estimated offer. The fixed national clerk rate is removed;
  existing openings retain their saved terms. County workplaces retain their
  sourced five-digit Census geographic binding when the shared wage reader
  selects the occupational area and state median.

- **Hiring preserves the job offer without an inferred relationship pay cut** A
  recorded male partnership no longer automatically reduces the job's offered
  pay when no nondiscrimination law covers the worker. Existing offer amounts,
  legal coverage records and wage-floor enforcement remain separate.

- **Funded staffing applies the same layoff rule to the player** A budget cut
  can end the player's funded job under the same seniority rule as other staff.
  Teaching vacancies require a completed degree on the person's record; missing
  or unfinished schooling no longer qualifies someone automatically.

- **Jobs and study starts with town jobs and study** Jobs and study no longer
  repeats the role, waiting list and jump buttons above Work and Study. Jobs
  shows town employer listings without the legal-pay sentence, national median
  paragraph or separate Other work catalog. Existing work, study and financial
  records remain saved.

- **Shift replay checks follow actual completed work** Shift-payroll
  verification now checks one unchanged payment per saved completed shift,
  including all due completions after advancing another day. It retains gross
  pay, withholding, net cash, employer records and Save/Continue checks. This
  changes verification only; the payment engine is unchanged.

- **Starting a public meeting opens the room before it ends** Starting a posted
  public meeting from Today, the calendar, or its trip now opens the meeting
