  request amounts from their sponsor's recorded principles and verified
  legislative references, scaled to the government's saved population. Service
  choices and declared categories come from the same reference. A missing
  numeric reference still lets a supported member file the policy answer without
  inventing an amount.

- **Every legislative chamber has explicit minority procedure rules** Every
  compiled and generated legislative chamber, Congress, and admitted municipal
  chamber now carries a complete minority procedure row. The row uses the
  chamber's existing quorum and cloture-stage rule, and marks shared motion,
  attendance, and threshold defaults as estimates with provenance.

- **A national data privacy law costs businesses** A national data privacy law
  now costs the businesses in every town a share of their yearly costs from the
  day it takes effect, and a repeal takes the cost off. The size is a world's
  own draw between 0.1% and 0.6%, the European compliance cost (marked as an
  estimate). The ban on stock trading by members of Congress carries a sourced
  about-zero row: the game keeps no member holdings, and the study finds
  members' trades matched or trailed the market.

- **Legislators bargaining with you talk about the bill, not made-up history**
  Before, a legislator in the bargaining room could bring up things that were
  never true in your game: a garage that had sat on a replacement list for
  years, a water bill of their own coming up, four votes they could hold, or a
  session with "days, not weeks" left. Now four kinds of remark speak only from
  the bill and the room: a member pressing for something for home, a member
  saying they'll back you, a member urging you to settle the bill before its
  next step, and a warning about the next step itself. They name the section,
  the place, the amount and the next step as the bill records them. A member
  says nothing in the bill is written for their place only when that is true,
  and thanks you for a section only once it is in.

- **A legislator holding out on your bill gives reasons from the bill** Before,
  a legislator who would not commit yet, doubted how a section would be run, or
  liked your goal but not your method could back it up with things that were
  never true in your game: three agencies each blaming the others, a department
  that missed a deadline with twice the staff, rulemaking stretching past March,
  or grants going to the places that need them least. Now those remarks speak
  only from the bill and the room: its number, the section and its heading, its
  next step, what it commits as it now reads, and the analyst who would write
  the fiscal note. Where the record says nothing, such as how many agencies are
  involved or how many staff a program would need, the member asks rather than
  claims.

- **Record the existing title hero fix** This documentation change records work
  already delivered on the title screen and has no player-facing effect.

- **Cities over 100,000 people now hold a mayor's race** Every town now knows
  its size, from the Census Bureau's 2025 estimates. Where the game has not read
  a city's own rules, a city of more than 100,000 people now elects its mayor,
  so Philadelphia, Dallas, Atlanta, Las Vegas, Saint Paul and 64 other large
  cities offer a mayor's race they did not before. A city whose own rules the
  game has read keeps them, whatever its size. This is an interim rule until the
  rules by town size have been researched.

- **Bills are numbered the way each legislature really numbers them** A bill's
  number now starts over at 1 when a new session opens, and the bill's name says
  which session it belongs to, such as "HB 1 (2027 Regular Session)" or "H.R. 1,
  120th Congress." The session a life begins in still starts partway up, because
  other bills were filed before you arrived. Each state uses its own prefixes
  and chamber names: California and Nevada file Assembly Bills, Minnesota and
  Iowa file House Files and Senate Files, New York and New Jersey file A and S
  bills, and West Virginia's lower chamber is the House of Delegates. States
  that sit in two-year sessions keep counting through the second year. The
  District of Columbia numbers its bills by council period. A bill you draft on
  the docket now shows the number it will actually be filed under. Bills already
  in a saved game keep the numbers they have.

- **Bills start where each state starts them, and Columbus has its real
  council** Where a state's own rules say where its bill numbers begin, a
  session's first bill now carries that number: Washington's Senate opens at SB
  5000, Arizona's House at HB 2001, Connecticut's House at HB 5001. Kansas,
  Michigan, South Carolina and Washington keep counting through both years of
  the legislature. Every other chamber still starts at 1. Columbus, Ohio, now
  seats the council its charter describes: nine members, each living in one of
  nine districts and elected citywide, on four-year terms.

- **A child is born when a couple decides on one** Children in town are now born
  when two people who share a home decide on it. On each quarterly town review,
  a couple weighs having a child against their own lives: their ages, the
  children they already have and how young the last one is, how long they have
  been together, what they earn and pay in rent, the room in their home, their
  health, their temperament and beliefs, the law where they live, and how many
  people in town are out of work. If one of them raises it and the other agrees,
  the baby arrives on the plan's own date. The draw that used to give town women
  a baby by chance is gone, so the player and everybody else follow one rule. A
  birth from a plan now also carries that plan, so the plan shows the child it
  led to.

- **Fewer babies are born in a town after hard times hit.** When unemployment
  rises, births in the town fall about 1.4% for each point, starting about nine
  months later, as they do in real American cities.

- **Budget openings read D.C.'s shape and the median reserve from the data** A
  state NASBO reports no rainy-day balance for, such as Georgia, now opens with
  the median share of the states NASBO does report, computed from the table and
  marked as estimated from the average. Before, the share was typed in by hand.
  A state whose Census state column is empty because one government serves as
  both state and local government now reads its local column by that rule.
  Before, the District of Columbia was named in the code.

- **Governments spend their unreserved balance, and a city's state aid follows
  its state** Each new budget now carries the balance a government holds above
  its reserve target into the year and spends it across the year's programs,
  once, instead of letting it pile up untouched. A county's or city's aid from
  its state now rises and falls with what the state actually spends on aid to
  local governments, so a state's mid-year cut reaches its cities the same month
  and a state's growing budget raises their aid. A town with no Census 2025
  population estimate (a census-designated place such as Bear, Delaware, or any
  town in Puerto Rico) now keeps a budget from its American Community Survey
  population, and a Puerto Rico town, for which the Census Bureau publishes no
  local finances, opens from the national average per resident, marked as
  estimated.

- **A new business's cash, a new group's founders and a fallback chair are
  decided, not drawn** A new business now opens with the cash its industry
  typically keeps: 16 days of spending for a restaurant, 19 for a shop and 47
  for a real estate office. Before, a random draw set it. A new congregation or
  club is started by people who belonged to one before, then by the eldest. When
  a President names nobody to the central bank, the longest-serving governor
  becomes chair, and an empty governor's seat stays open until the President
  knows someone who may serve.

- **Businesses open from recorded demand in comparable towns** Business openings
  compare recorded residents per business with the game's other towns that have
  an open business in the same line. A missing comparison no longer supplies an
  opening from the authored job mix. Existing founder and staffing rules still
  write the new employer. Quarterly business closures come from the existing
  books. An empty staff list no longer closes an employer by itself, and the
  player's workplace receives the same books review. Congregations and clubs
  keep their separate lifecycle rules.

- **Six business and bank sizes are written in their own units** Nothing changes
  in play. Six sizes in the town business books and the nation's banks are now
  written in their own units, such as days of revenue for a credit line and
  percent a month for loans that reprice, instead of as shares. Each is still
  marked as a placeholder until it is measured.

- **Calendar and travel times read in hours and days** Times on the Calendar,
  Places and meeting screens read as people say them: "1 day 9 hours" or "1 hour
  5 minutes" rather than a count of minutes. A travel button no longer runs into
  its time ("Travel · until Tuesday, January 6, 2026, 6:30 PM"). A Calendar
  entry of yours says "You're in charge of it. You're going." under "How it was
  arranged", where it used to say "On the record". An optional plan is marked
  "Maybe" rather than "Tentative hold", and says "You might go."

- **Plainer words on the Campaigns and Parties screens** The Parties screen no
  longer opens with "Coming to any of this is not joining, endorsing or voting."
  A trip to a meeting says "There is no fare." instead of a note about travel
  costs not being represented. An office you may run for says "You can run for
  this office." A campaign with no poll says "There is no poll yet.", and a
  candidate whose own money cannot be used reads "None of your own money can go
  into the campaign yet."

- **Campaign recognition reads actual recorded contacts** The old
  unknown-candidate, afternoon and prior-win return curve now reads the share of
  recorded adult residents who have actually met the candidate in saved
  campaign-contact events. Repeated encounters count a person once. Both people
  must be recorded as present or acting in the dated source event; untagged
  relationships, mentions, children, outsiders and future contacts do not count.
  Current household-location records govern residency; the saved person home is
  used when no household location is recorded. The reader exposes the actual
  resident, recognized-person and contact-record IDs. An action's own outcome
  does not supply its prior recognition. Completed afternoon and prior-win
  counts remain informational. No win, newcomer or attendance-count bonus is
  invented. This measures the recorded resident cohort, not the unrecorded
  population, and does not create contacts for unnamed door-knocking. The
  existing minutes/workers field-gain calculation is unchanged.

- **Who may run for the legislature follows laws passed in the game** When a law
  passed in the game changes the minimum age for a legislative seat, or how long
  a candidate must have lived in the state or district, running for that seat
  now follows the new rule from the day the law takes effect. The reason a
  character cannot run names the law. A law can also remove a residence period
  entirely.

- **Opening jobs select recorded employers through town businesses** Opening
  jobs use the town-business module's existing recorded employer selection. The
  same saved jobs, family connections, experience and sourced pay decide the
  employer. The old local-economy export remains a compatibility alias to the
  single canonical selector, and missing employers do not create fictional jobs.

- **Each capitol in morning light, at night and in the rain** Each state's,
  D.C.'s and each territory's capitol now has morning, night and rainy pictures,
  so a night visit shows it lit against a dark sky instead of in daylight.
  Kansas has no rainy picture yet and shows its daytime one on rainy days.

- **Each legislature seats as many members as the state's own** Before, a
  legislature the game builds took its size from the state's Census districts,
  and a chamber with none was sized by a draw. A state whose districts elect
  more than one member came out too small: Arizona's House had 30 members
  instead of 60, and New Jersey's Assembly 40 instead of 80. Idaho, Maryland,
  North Dakota, South Dakota, Vermont, Washington and the West Virginia Senate
  were short the same way. Now each chamber seats the count The Council of State
  Governments lists for 2023 (The Book of the States, Table 3.3). A chamber no
  table lists takes the middle of the compiled chambers' range, marked as an
  estimate. A legislature already seated in a saved game keeps the size it was
  seated with. A game saved before September 29, 2026 with a veto override vote
  in one of these legislatures loads again. Correcting each state's override bar
  had made such a save fail to open; the vote is now checked against the bar it
  was taken under.

- **GitHub checks run only the tests a change touches** Owner decision, October
  1, 2026. GitHub no longer runs the full unit suite on every push. The `unit`
  job runs only the test files a pull request adds or edits, the same rule the
  local gate uses before a change lands. Checks run on pull requests and on
  main, not on every team-branch push, and a newer push cancels the older run.
  The full browser suite runs only when started by hand. Lint, type checks,
  release checks and the build still run on every pull request.

- **A city's or county's own laws now change things in that place.** Once a city
  or county council passes a law, the world keeps that place's own crime,
  schooling, health and other outcomes, so its ordinances act there: a city that
  sets up civilian oversight of its police sees less crime in that city, and
  towns that passed nothing are untouched. A state's figures are now the average
  of its places, weighted by how many people live in each.

- **A state law on cannabis sales now moves its overdose deaths** Legalizing or
  ending adult cannabis sales used to change nothing a player could see. Now a
  state's overdose death rate moves about 11 months after the law takes effect,
  which is how long stores have taken to open in six states that legalized. The
  research disagrees on the direction, so each world draws its own size for each
  state, from a fall of 20% to a rise of 10%. The effect ends the day a repeal
  takes effect. In one world, Alabama legalized sales in 2026, and its overdose
  deaths ran 1.5% above the same world without the law until the repeal in 2029.
  Four more questions now say, with the research behind it, why their effect on
  everything the world keeps is about zero: a consumer data privacy law, age
  checks for social media, local control of library materials, and a city
  nondiscrimination ordinance.

- **A successor taking office lapses the predecessor’s clemency requests** An
  actual saved governor entry now reviews that place’s open clemency petitions.
  Requests addressed to the outgoing governor lapse without transferring to the
  successor. Dated entry and late qualification use the same targeted writer.
  The original petition remains unchanged, and the weekly fallback remains
  available.

- **Saved clemency petitions carry their own review dates** Saved clemency
  petitions schedule review through the existing world clock using existing
  board waits, advisory deadlines and sentence-end dates. A request whose
  sentence has ended can close even when no executive is seated. Undecided
  executives receive no new retry timer. The weekly caller remains until its
  replacement is integrated and proven. An actual saved clemency choice now
  advances its matching petition immediately through the same writer, for both
  player and NPC executive decisions.

- **Saved governor desk openings reach existing clemency decisions** An actual
  saved governor-desk opening reaches the existing request decisions for that
  office’s running cases. Petitions follow the defendant’s durable decision and
  preserve the original request’s holder. Repeated delivery and stale saved
  terms do not create another request. Weekly request creation remains while the
  complete court-clock replacement is proved.

- **Saved sentences reach defendants' existing clemency decisions** An NPC's
  actual saved sentence reaches their existing request decision immediately
  through the case writer. Their choice controls the petition, while repeat
  delivery and SaveContinue preserve the original records. Weekly fallback
  remains while complete court-clock recovery is proved.

- **A governor weighs a favor the person asking once did them** When someone
  asks for a pardon or a shorter sentence, the governor or board member who
  decides now weighs any help that person once gave them and that still feels
  owed, such as running their campaign office. It counts toward granting the
  request, alongside the decider's views on mercy, how well they know the
  person, the coming election and the board's advice. A decider who owes the
  person nothing weighs nothing of the kind.

- **Clock controls use selected action styling** Day, Week, Until needed, and
  stopping-condition controls use the selected action styling. Their existing
  clock behavior and accessibility attributes are retained.

- **The opening card shows your local government as a labeled value, not a news
  line** Before, card 1 of a new game listed "Mineral County is governed by
  County board." under In the news, and the government's own records described
  it as a disclosed game profile. Now the card shows "Local government" with the
  name from the government's record, and the news section carries only real
  headlines.

- **A cocky defendant weighs going to trial over taking a plea** A person who
  overestimates their own prospects now counts that confidence as one reason to
  test a charge at trial instead of taking the plea on offer. It weighs beside
  the evidence, the sentence, their principles and everything else they carry,
  so the same defendant with the same facts and no such streak still takes the
  plea.

- **Committee reports show the whole recorded attendance** Committee reports now
  show votes for and against, members present without voting, absent and excused
  members, and the recorded votes needed out of the applicable denominator. The
  same account appears in the bill's history and its public event. Voting rules
  and outcomes stay unchanged.

- **Condolences use only what was recorded** When someone talks with you about a
  death, their words no longer invent memories the two of you shared. How much
  the reply moves them depends on how close the records say you were, and they
  no longer describe your relative as their own.

- **Members of Congress file and vote by their own principles** A member of
  Congress now files a bill on the federal question their own principles press
  hardest, such as raising the minimum wage, forgiving student loans or making
  the District of Columbia a state, and colleagues who lean the same way sign
  on. Each House's roll call now divides: a bill that used to pass 432 to 0 now
  passes something like 341 to 91, or fails. Once a bill is law, its answer is
  federal law, which outranks a state's. There are twenty federal questions for
  now, one in each field of federal government. A bill's steps in the news are
  now dated the day each one happened. Before, a long stretch of time put a
  bill's House vote, its trip to the Senate and its arrival on the President's
  desk on one date.

- **Every race for Congress records its nominees and a vote count** Congress's
  elections chose each seat's winner from a national total, so no race for a
  House or Senate seat had a ballot, a named field or a count anyone could read.
  Now each seat whose nominees come from different parties gets a ballot 45 days
  before election day, and on election day it is counted from that seat's own
  voters: the seat's recorded 2024 ballots split by its starting partisan lean,
  with the incumbent's usual edge and a share for candidates in neither party.
  The winner of that count is the member who takes the seat. The world report
  lists the place's own state's races in full and sums up the rest of the
  country.

- **Congress files through the same member agenda as state legislatures**
  Members of Congress keep their recorded reasons and cosponsors when filing a
  bill through the shared member agenda. Federal bill titles, numbering and
  origination rules are preserved.

- **Congress and the states decide a presidential term-limit amendment member by
  member** When a President is barred from another term, or has served three,
  members of Congress now decide for themselves whether to amend the
  Constitution's term limit. Each weighs the President's party against their
  own, how they get along with the President, and the higher bar of changing the
  Constitution. Congress proposes only when two-thirds of each house would vote
  yes, and each state legislature ratifies when most of its members would.
  Before, a draw decided whether Congress proposed it, how each house divided,
  and whether each state ratified. A sitting President now decides for
  themselves whether to run again, from their age, health, temperament and
  family, as other officeholders do. Before, a President 78 or older always
  stepped down and a younger one ran four times in five by a draw.

- **A state constitution that settles the shape of its income tax holds against
  a statute** Six states write the shape of their income tax into their
  constitutions: Illinois, Michigan and Colorado require one rate. Pennsylvania
  and Washington bar graduated rates as their supreme courts read their
  uniformity clauses. Massachusetts writes its surtax on high incomes into its
  constitution. In those states a bill that changes the shape of the tax no
  longer takes effect, and paychecks stay as they were; only a ratified
  amendment changes it, as Illinois voters were asked in 2020 and Massachusetts
  voters in 2022. Before, an ordinary statute could graduate Michigan's tax or
  flatten Massachusetts's.

- **Recorded principle strength carries continuously into decisions** Recorded
  life pulls reinforce or contradict continuous principle strength. Agenda,
  agreement and spending decisions read that strength instead of an integer
  conviction label. Repeated evidence does not reinforce a principle, and
  rereading an unchanged life writes no new principle row.

- **People at home talk to you differently from people outside it** The words a
  person picks when they turn down your invitation now depend on whether they
  live in your household. A housemate or relative puts you off briefly; someone
  outside the home still thanks you for asking.

- **A legislator objecting to cost talks about the bill in front of them**
  Before, a legislator who balked at what a bill cost could mention things that
  were never true in your game, such as earlier votes against "smaller" bills or
  money that would be "baked in" by a third year. Now an objection to cost
  speaks only from the bill itself: its number, what it commits as it now reads,
  the person being spoken to and, for a member who answers to a district, the
  place they represent. A careful spender asks where the offset is; a district
  member asks how much of the money reaches home.

- **Council decisions use the shared bill driver** The council meeting caller
  sends its actual government context to the shared legislative driver. The
  driver reads saved members and preserves their existing decision reasons,
  quorum and reading rules. The existing passage handler remains in use. Valid
  town council profiles now open institutional work with their own local
  jurisdiction and clock. An unresolved pack or local place remains pending.

- **A mayor fills a council vacancy from names the council puts forward** When a
  council seat falls vacant, the mayor still names someone they know first. Now
  the other council members also put forward the people they know, and the mayor
  weighs those names too. Before, a mayor whose only acquaintances were the
  council itself had nobody eligible, and the council drew a resident from the
  town roster instead. In Tchula, Mississippi, the mayor now appoints a resident
  one of the council members knows.

- **Council filing reads the shared game-profile effective date** Council
  members read a law's admitted saved fictional effective-date profile through
  the shared operative-date reader. The existing integrity rule still refuses a
  saved profile with a missing or mismatched cached effective date.

- **Other councilors decide their own ballots, and one with no view goes along**
  When the player sits on a town council, the other councilors' ballots on the
  player's ordinance are now their own decisions, from their principles, their
  record, the ordinance's sponsor and the voters they answer to. Before, each
  was a coin flip. A councilor with no view and nothing else to weigh on an
  ordinance now votes for it, as most real council votes are unanimous, where
  before they sat it out. This holds for every council vote, not only the
  player's.

- **A place with no town government gets its local law from its county board** A
  life in a place with no town government of its own, such as a
  census-designated place or a Puerto Rico community, now has a local body that
  makes law. The county board, parish council or municipio legislature meets
  every two weeks, its members file ordinances on what their own principles
  press them to, and they vote on each one. An adopted county ordinance governs
  the place. Before, the board was seated but never met, so these places passed
  no local law at all. A member with no view on an ordinance that would undo the
  body's own law in force now votes to keep it, so a board no longer passes an
  ordinance and its repeal back and forth.

- **Every state now says which county row offices its counties elect** County
  sheriffs, prosecutors, clerks, treasurers, assessors and coroners are now
  decided by each of the fifty states' own rows. Where a state's law has not
  been read, the row says which regional pattern it takes.

- **Town relationships follow independently recorded actor choices** Town
  breakup, moving-in, marriage and first-date proposals use the existing shared
  decision evaluator and bilateral stage-consent contract, replacing quarterly
  chance rolls. Reviews persist each actual actor evaluation through the
  existing durable decision writer and replay saved results without duplicate
  traces. The current-game peer reader may supply labeled estimates from actual
  saved comparable decision traces when the actor's own reasons do not separate
  the options. Equal peer means remain undecided; missing personal faith or
  cause weights are not manufactured.

- **A criminal case is decided by the people in it, not by chance** A criminal
  case no longer goes one way or another because a number came up. A regulator
  sends a finding to prosecutors when the record shows the person knew the rule:
  they had been found at fault for the same thing before, or they denied what
  the finding showed. Prosecutors charge when the evidence would probably
  convict. The person charged then chooses between pleading guilty and going to
  trial, from the evidence, the lighter sentence a plea brings, what a guilty
  plea would do to a career in public life, and their own temperament. Nobody
  chooses for the player: until the player can enter a plea, the court enters
  not guilty for them and the case goes to trial. At a trial, twelve people from
  their town who do not know them hear the case. Each juror votes from the
  evidence, the reasonable-doubt rule and their own temperament, and a juror who
  disagrees with most of the room usually comes around on the second vote. A
  jury that still cannot agree brings a retrial, and after a second one the
  charge is dropped. After a plea or a conviction, one of the state's own trial
  judges chooses between probation and jail. The judge weighs the plea, any
  earlier record, a violent offense or an abuse of public trust, and their own
  view of firm sentences. Every one of these choices is recorded with its
  reasons. Asking for clemency and answering it no longer settle a close call by
  chance either.

- **Crime and births now rise and fall over the decades in each state.** A
  state's crime and birth rates are no longer tied to fixed national figures.
  Each drifts over the years, partly with the country and partly on its own,
  with now and then a nationwide wave, the way crime roughly halved from the
  1990s to the 2010s and births rose and fell across generations. Hard times and
  laws still push them on top of that.

- **Crime falls where its causes point, not on a roll** Ordinary crime in a town
  no longer comes from a monthly roll. A town's offenses fall on the people and
  homes that its residents' circumstances point at. That means a neighbor who
  knows someone out of work with a past record, or a person something has
  already happened to. Each offense comes on the day that pressure builds to it,
  and the police log fills at a steady pace that the town's conditions move up
  or down.

- **Crime now rises with unemployment and frightens the state** Crime in a town
  now rises when recorded unemployment goes up and falls when it drops. A town
  with more reported crime than an ordinary month also adds to the fear its
  state builds up, alongside disasters and tax changes. Both sizes are
  placeholders until research lands. Poverty, policing and new laws do not move
  crime yet; each is listed in the code with the reason.

- **Crime and party lookups read the history index** Looking up the crimes a
  person has suffered and the party a person belongs to now reads the game's
  existing history index instead of rescanning the whole record each time.
  Nothing you see in play changes.

- **Crime victims decide whether to call the police** Whether an ordinary local
  crime reaches the police is now each victim's own decision instead of a fixed
  share. A victim weighs what was done to them against the trouble of reporting,
  whether it has happened to them before, their own past with the police, their
  temperament and their age. The same victim in the same circumstances always
  decides the same way.

- **A cruel juror counts wanting the defendant to pay toward a guilty vote** A
  juror with a cruel streak, one who sometimes uses humiliation or suffering as
  a means or an end, now counts wanting the defendant made to pay as one reason
  to vote guilty. It weighs beside the evidence in the case, the burden of proof
  and what the rest of the room says, so on a thin case the same juror without
  that streak still votes not guilty.

- **Sentences follow recorded minimums for covered offenses** An enacted minimum
  sentence applies only when its final adopted terms explicitly cover the saved
  case's offense. Courts use the case's venue and date, preserve the enacted
  sources on the saved sentence, and leave completed sentences unchanged after
  repeal. Missing amounts or coverage do not supply a substitute rule.

- **Day, Week and Calendar move a life forward** A life now moves forward
  through the Day, Week and Calendar controls, from childhood on. The quiet
  reading, resting and drawing choices and the separate buttons for letting time
  pass are gone. A meeting or invitation due today can still be played from the
  story screen, and a birthday invitation shows its reply and time. A notice
  about somewhere else waits until the player is there.

- **A day opens with the day it is, in the town you live in** The line that
  opens an ordinary day now says what day it is, where you are, how much is
  waiting on you and who is home, and it does not read the same two days
  running. A different town, a longer list or a new housemate reads differently
  on the same day. When nobody in a conversation answers, the room no longer
  says the same thing two turns in a row, and a visit on another day opens
  differently.

- **Keep daily catch-up tied to the time you read it** The daily catch-up reads
  the same saved news, knowledge, and election results as While you were away.
  It keeps the exact interval and last read record in the saved interface so a
  reload does not repeat an acknowledged day. The optional morning thought reads
  today's actual plans and waiting decisions.

- **The D.C. Council's members are District residents** A life that starts in
  Washington, D.C. now opens with a Council whose thirteen members are residents
  of the District, drawn from the same households as the rest of the city, and
  never from the player's own household. Before, each member was made up for the
  seat with an age drawn between 25 and 80. The Mayor's part in the District's
  acts is now read from a data file that cites the Home Rule Act, not from a
  rule written for one place.

- **D.C. statehood seats and money follow admission, and Medicaid aid falls when
  the state certifies** Before, D.C. gained its two Senate seats and a voting
  House seat the day the statehood law took effect, and no money moved. Now the
  District is admitted 180 days after the law takes effect, the days the bill
  allows for the President's certification, the Mayor's proclamation and the
  President's proclamation of admission, and the seats are filled then. At
  admission no federal payment changes. Each year, when the new state adopts its
  budget, its government decides whether it can certify to the President that it
  covers five years of what ending the higher Medicaid match costs, and writes
  down why. A state that certifies loses about $694 million a year of federal
  aid from the next fiscal year: Medicaid falls from 70 to 50 percent of $3.42
  billion, and children's health insurance from 79 to 65 percent of $68.3
  million. A state that cannot cover it keeps the higher match and asks again.
  The numbers come from a data file.

- **A governor dying in office is now tested through a real saved game.** A new
  check starts an ordinary life, lets the sitting governor die, lets the next
  race open on the empty seat and then saves and reloads the game. The race has
  two challengers, the dead governor is not on the ballot, and reloading changes
  nobody's office. The governor election calendar used for most states has also
  been sent for research.

- **Natural deaths follow a serious illness that a person's own records bring
  on** Nobody's natural death is drawn anymore. Each person's health strain
  builds from their age, their recorded conditions and their coverage. When it
  crosses one level, the same for everyone, they fall seriously ill and their
  family is told. The illness runs for a number of days that shrinks with age,
  with the weight of their conditions and without coverage. They die on its last
  day, and the death names the illness. Two people with the same records fall
  ill on the same day. A recorded condition brings the illness sooner. Deaths
  that earlier saves wrote as sudden or as accidents still read the way they
  were written. People now start with the long-term conditions people of their
  age commonly have, such as heart disease, diabetes or COPD, in national survey
  shares. The conditions are written once on their health record, and the person
  sees them there by name. Later in life a condition begins on the day its own
  strain crosses the same level. A household under the poverty line on its
  coverage record reaches that day sooner, and so does someone who lost
  coverage. Each condition weighs on the strain toward a serious illness. A
  condition is held mildly, moderately or severely, and one found young weighs
  more. A few children live with a life-limiting condition. Across a town,
  deaths by age now come out near the national life table, from working age to
  the very old and in the first year of play.

- **A state law dedicating revenue to parks moves the parks budget line and park
  access** Before, a state law on dedicating a share of revenue to parks changed
  nothing and no place had a starting answer. Now every place starts with its
  real answer: fifteen states already dedicate a tax, the lottery or royalties
  to parks (Missouri, Minnesota, Oregon, Texas, Florida and Michigan read from
  their own law, nine more from the Trust for Public Land's records of passed
  measures), and the other 41 places start at no, marked as estimated. A state
  that adds a dedication puts about $9.26 a resident a year on its parks line,
  the average of Missouri's and Minnesota's dedicated sales taxes, and a repeal
  takes it off. A year later the share of residents near a park or recreation
  facility moves by about a tenth of a percent, either way, because the research
  found access about zero against spending. Cities and counties are not sized
  yet and start at their state's answer.

- **A defensive defendant weighs trial over admitting the charge in a plea** A
  person who protects their self-image when criticized now counts that habit as
  one reason to take a charge to trial instead of standing in court to admit it
  in a plea. It weighs beside the evidence, the sentence, their principles and
  everything else they carry, so the same defendant with the same facts and no
  such streak still takes the plea.

- **Conversations sit above the bar in a calmer green and brass panel** When you
  talk with someone, the panel now stays above the bar at the bottom of the
  screen. Before, at 1280×800 the bar covered its last lines and the replies
  under them, and a click on a reply could land on the bar instead. The panel
  itself is quieter: deep green that fades at its edges so the room shows
  through, one brass edge, and cream lettering. The faces of the people you are
  talking with are larger at its top left. Your replies read as lines you could
  say rather than a stack of boxes, and the Lie button stands beside them, where
  it swaps in the false answers in place of the true ones. Nothing in the panel
  moves on its own.

- **How a governor handles a disaster now wins or loses voters, and family and
  party react** A governor who asks for federal help when a disaster calls for
  it gains support in a race under way and starts later races slightly ahead.
  One who declines, or lets the request window close, loses support and starts
  behind. The played governor's family and party organizers read what happened
  and criticize, defend or praise it, and their relationship changes to match.
  The sizes are placeholders until they are researched.

- **A statewide race with an independent in it is decided by the voters** A
  governor's or the D.C. mayor's race with a candidate on neither major party's
  line, such as a sitting executive with no party on record, no longer falls
  back to a drawn count of a few thousand votes. That candidate now shares the
  part of the place's 2024 ballots that went to neither major party, and the
  major-party nominees split the rest by the place's own lean. In Washington,
  D.C. the Democratic nominee now wins about nine votes in ten, where a
  Republican used to be able to win on a drawn count.

- **The doors return more once people know your name** An afternoon on the doors
  used to be worth the same for everyone. A candidate nobody has heard of now
  gets about half as much from their first afternoons. Each afternoon makes the
  next one worth a little more, up to the full amount, and a candidate who has
  won races before starts ahead. These amounts are placeholders until the real
  ones are known.

- **Polling distance no longer contributes to turnout** Removes the unused
  polling-distance outcome link. Turnout uses recorded personal circumstances
  rather than polling-place geometry.

- **Local economic figures appear on the day they were really published** The
  "How the place is doing" panel used to show nothing for the first eight months
  of every life, because the game dated every figure by the day it had
  downloaded the files rather than by when the government published them.
  Figures now become visible on their real publication dates. A life that opens
  in January 2026 sees the local two-bedroom Fair Market Rent from the first
  day, because HUD published those rents in August 2024. County personal income
  for 2024 arrives on February 5, 2026, and state and metro price levels on
  February 19, 2026, the days those editions came out. A figure that describes
  an earlier year is not shown before the edition that holds it was released.
  Two things are still held back until their publication dates are established:
  HUD's income limits and the monthly unemployment series. In New England, where
  HUD sets rents town by town rather than county by county, rents do not reach a
  town yet.

- **The economy and every place's numbers move only for a reason you can point
  to** Before: the national economy and every state's measures (uninsured share,
  poverty, graduation, crime, air quality and the rest) were nudged a little
  every month by chance. The economy also took random jumps into a new era and
  random price shocks, and every place's numbers now and then rode a random
  society-wide wave. Two worlds with the same laws and the same history could
  end up far apart for no reason anyone could name. After: nothing is drawn. A
  place's numbers hold where they start until a law, a condition or a dated,
  recorded crisis moves them, and the record names the cause that did. The
  national economy moves only from its own causes: the credit cycle and the
  central bank's board, and recorded shocks such as an energy disruption, a
  disaster, a trade break or public money paid out. A price spike or a recession
  now has a date and a cause in the record; it is never just bad luck.

- **Automatic voter registration, home rule and council term limits now change
  the world** A state that adopts automatic voter registration now sees turnout
  rise by about one point a year later, and a state that repeals it loses that
  point. Whether a state gives its towns home rule now decides which local
  questions a town council may answer: under Dillon's rule a town may not pass
  ordinances on policing, zoning, rent, transit and the other powers the state
  has not granted, and adopting home rule opens them. A town that limits council
  terms bars a member with two consecutive full terms behind them from running
  again, and the seat opens.

- **Election losses show the result without generic consolation** The campaign
  result states who lost and by how much. It no longer adds a stock consolation
  sentence after the vote count.

- **Employers list work when it comes open and hire the best-placed applicant**
  A town employer no longer lists a job on a weekly chance, and no longer says
  yes or no to an application by chance. It lists work when somebody doing it
  has left, when the business is new, and otherwise as often as work that many
  people hold comes open nationwide: about 3.3% of jobs a month. A town
  government's staff is taken from the national average of about 20 for every
  1,000 residents. When more than one person applies, the employer offers the
  job to whoever has spent longer in the same line of work, then to whoever
  somebody who works there put forward, then to whoever applied first. The
  others are told the employer chose another applicant, which is now always
  true. A lone applicant is offered the job. An offer pays what the employer
  pays for the work now, a little more when its last listing went unfilled, for
  the hours the people doing it work. A missed start gets a call back when
  somebody vouched for the applicant or nobody else applied.

- **Enacted bills preserve their explicit coverage** Bills can save coverage
  from a declared list of modeled categories. Law readers use the version
  adopted at enactment, and an amendment that removes coverage does not revive
  an older list. Missing declarations remain unsupported. Save/Continue
  preserves the categories and their sources.

- **Enacted bills preserve their final numeric rules** Bills can preserve
  explicit numeric rules in their operative sections. Law readers use the
  version adopted at enactment. An amendment that removes a rule does not revive
  the earlier number, and Save/Continue preserves the recorded rules and their
  sources.

- **Judges have more ways to rule, reason and dissent** The judges' wording bank
  grows from 34 to 56 shapes taken from the Supreme Court's opinions, such as
  "We are not persuaded.", "We need not decide" and "I would reverse.", each
  found in at least two opinions.

- **Bills are written with more of Congress's own wording** The legislation
  wording bank grows from 35 to 89 shapes taken from bill text in the current
  Congress, such as "Severability.", "The term ... means" and "Now, therefore,
  be it", each found in at least two bills.

- **More ways to open and close a floor speech** The formal-meeting bank now has
  131 openings, procedural turns and closings, taken from twice as many days of
  the Congressional Record.

- **Container deposits, flood-zone limits and incentive caps change the world**
  Three laws that used to change nothing now do. A container deposit law raises
  the share of bottles and cans returned from about 28 to about 72 percent
  within six months of taking effect, and a repeal lowers it again. A law
  keeping new building out of flood zones means a flood damages fewer homes as
  the years pass, and repealing one means more. A cap on the tax breaks offered
  to attract employers lets a state collect more corporate income tax, and a
  county or city more property tax, from the day it takes effect; lifting a cap
  gives it back.

- **Clean-electricity standards, carbon prices and bottle deposits now change
  the world** A state that adopts a clean-electricity standard now sees its
  average price of electricity rise about 11% seven years later, and a repeal
  takes the rise back. A state that prices carbon lowers its carbon dioxide from
  energy per person about 3% a year later. A container deposit cuts litter about
  45% within a year and raises the state's consumer price level about 0.05%
  within three months, through the price of covered drinks. Each state's
  electricity price and emissions start at its real 2024 figures.

- **The illness going around weighs age and season by the day** How readily
  somebody catches the illness going around, how serious their case is, and how
  strongly the season brings it into town now slide by the day instead of
  jumping at a birthday or on the first of a month. A child the day before and
  the day after turning five, or somebody the day before and after turning
  eighty, gets nearly the same case: the same added risk, within a day the same
  time in bed, and within a day the same time until they are well. The levels in
  the middle of each old age band, and at the middle of each month, are what
  they were.

- **Every line in a bargaining conversation comes from the bill itself** When
  you bargain with another legislator, everything they say is now put together
  from what the bill and the room actually hold: its number, the section in
  play, who that section is written for, its cost and the next vote. Lines that
  used to claim things nothing recorded are gone. One member used to say "a
  dollar over that" and they would vote the way they always had, and another
  would demand a program "standing inside a year". A member who wants you to
  write a section in says so only after you have held out on them, and a member
  reminds you of what they said earlier only if they said it.

- **People's personalities come from their upbringing** Everyone in the world
  now has a personality drawn from one list of 97 traits, shaped by the family
  and circumstances they grew up in, with one to three traits they were born
  with.

- **Every town has its own local press, every state has a newsroom from the
  start, and Puerto Rico's press is its own.** A town used to get a local outlet
  only if the game had its city government on record, so San Juan, Ponce,
  Louisville and many small places had none at all, and Puerto Rico had no
  island newsroom either. Now every town a character lives in has local
  journalism, and their state's or territory's politics is covered from the
  first day, not only once they hold office there. Taking a legislative seat or
  an office in another state brings that state's newsroom too. Newsrooms now
  differ. A town might have a weekly paper, a small digital outlet, a community
  radio station or, less often, a daily; a state might have a public-radio
  capitol desk, a big daily's bureau or a lean politics newsletter. The kind of
  outlet decides its staff, how often it publishes and how many stories it can
  chase at once. Puerto Rico's outlets carry Spanish mastheads, and the District
  of Columbia's newsroom is named for the District.

- **Everyday and press replies use recorded facts in English parts** Meeting,
  chapter and invitation accounts use the existing English engine. Press
  questions use the reporter's own recorded account. Answers use facts from
  reviewed preparation, and a question alone does not become a fact.

- **Excitement can affect whether an officeholder seeks another term** An
  officeholder with a marked excitable tendency may lean toward seeking another
  term when weighing that opportunity.

- **Executive nominations are decided by each member's recorded vote** A
  nomination to an executive post now goes to the chamber and each member
  decides it from what they know and what they believe, so a confirmation vote
  reflects the actual members rather than a single tally.

- **An arbitrary person may make an unexpected career choice** A person's
  recorded arbitrary temperament now informs whether they seek another term in
  office. The choice and its reason are kept in the decision trace.

- **A fairness law now sets the pay of men partnered with men** A state fairness
  law, or a town's own ordinance, used to change nothing anyone was paid. Now a
  man whose partner is a man is hired at about 2.6% below his job's rate where
  no such law covers him, and at the full rate where one does: such laws raised
  these men's pay 2.7% in the research. The rate is set when he is hired, so a
  new law or a repeal reaches later hires. Women, and everyone else, are paid
  the same either way. In one opening, two partners in Charlotte, North Carolina
  were each paid 2.6% less a pay period than they would be under a fairness law.

- **The player is hired under the same fairness-law pay rule as the town** A
  fairness law set the pay of townspeople but never the player's. Now the player
  is hired under the same rule: a man partnered with a man who takes a job where
  no fairness law covers him is paid about 2.6% below the job's pay, never below
  the minimum wage, and the full pay where a law covers him. It applies to a job
  taken through the job listings, the job a grown-up new life starts with, and
  shift work. In one game in Charleston, South Carolina, the player's four-hour
  shop shift paid $70.11 instead of $72.00; under a fairness law, or single, it
  paid $72.00.

- **Every place now starts with its real fairness law, and two states bar city
  ordinances** The game did not know which places already bar discrimination by
  sexual orientation and gender identity, so a bill to add those grounds had
  nothing to compare against. Now all 56 places start with their 2026 law: 26
  cover both grounds in both jobs and housing, and 30 leave at least one out.
  Tennessee and Arkansas also forbid their cities from adding grounds of their
  own, so a city council there cannot pass a fairness ordinance until the state
  changes its law. In the other places a city may still go further than its
  state.

- **A grown-up's family is drawn from real American shares** A new life that
  starts as an adult no longer has one parent and nobody else. About seven in
  ten now grew up with two parents, the share the Census Bureau counts today,
  and the rest with one. Brothers and sisters come in the numbers real families
  have them, from none to four, older or younger and a few years apart.
  Grandparents are recorded on each parent's side, and the older ones may have
  died before the life began. Every place in the country follows the same rule,
  so families differ by draw, not by state. Saved games keep the family they
  already had.

- **Every place starts with transit riders paying fares** The fare-free transit
  question now starts from each place's real law. Transit agencies in all 50
  states, D.C. and the five territories collected fares in 2024, so every place
  starts with riders paying, and only a new law making transit free moves
  ridership.

- **Each state keeps how many transit rides its residents take, and a law making
  transit free raises them.** Every state, D.C. and Puerto Rico now keep how
  many transit rides their residents take a year, starting from the Federal
  Transit Administration's 2024 count: 22.5 rides per resident nationally, from
  under 1 in Idaho to 171 in New York. A law that makes local transit free to
  ride raises that about 42% a month after it takes effect, where it is law: a
  state law across the state, a city ordinance in that city. Before, a fare-free
  law changed nothing.

- **Paying to keep farmland from being developed changes how much a state
  loses** Paying to keep farmland from being developed changes how much farmland
  a state loses. Every state, D.C. and territory starts with its real answer: 30
  states and Puerto Rico run a program that buys farmland easements, and
  Tennessee's opened on 9/1/2026. The 48 contiguous states start at the farmland
  they lost to development each year from 2001 to 2016. A state that starts such
  a program loses 40% to 55% less farmland five years later, and one that ends
  its program loses that much more.

- **Recorded state provider payments feed federal matching claims at
  settlement** Successful state program installments bind their completed paid
  outcome to an existing federal appropriation's admitted provider-class claim
  rule. Providers come from the state's recorded organizations and current
  profiles; a missing or ineligible provider creates no substitute. The saved
  expenditure period, due date, and exact adopted matching term remain
  authoritative. The existing federal commitment and installment writer posts
  cash and protects replay. This adds no ordinary Medicaid rates, awards,
  eligible expenditure assumptions, or cash.

- **Laws play out a little differently in every game, and Congress's laws reach
  outcomes too.** How strongly a law or a change in the economy moves an outcome
  is now drawn, for each game and each state, from the range real studies
  report, so the same law can do a little more in one playthrough and a little
  less in another. Laws Congress passes now feed the same web: a federal minimum
  wage raise lowers poverty where a state's own minimum is well below $15 and
  leaves states already above it alone, and eleven more federal questions are
  ready to act as the world starts tracking what they change.

- **Some laws Congress passes now change life in every state** When Congress
  raises the Social Security retirement age, more older people in every state
  fall into poverty over the next five years, as benefits shrink for each new
  group of retirees. Other federal laws now have a known effect ready for the
  day the game tracks what they touch: negotiated Medicare drug prices lower
  what retirees pay at the pharmacy, a cap on loan interest cuts payday
  borrowing where a state has no cap of its own, housing vouchers for every
  family that qualifies cut homelessness, higher tariffs raise prices, and a
  later retirement age keeps more people in their sixties at work. Some federal
  laws that people expect to matter change nothing here, because careful studies
  found no effect: admitting more immigrants does not raise crime, shorter
  federal drug sentences do not raise crime, housing vouchers do not change
  whether children finish high school or get arrested, and neither a higher top
  income tax rate nor forgiving student loans changes the poverty rate.

- **A federal law to expand passenger rail brings more Amtrak riders** When
  Congress votes to pay for expanding passenger rail, Amtrak riders grow in
  every place it serves. The first added trains run two and a half years after
  the law, and riders keep growing for 15 years toward Amtrak's own plan of
  62.5% more. Rail plans rarely reach their forecasts, so each world takes
  between a fifth and three quarters of that rise. A later law that ends the
  expansion takes the added riders away.

- **Federal passenger rail bill wording now comes from data rows.** The existing
  passenger rail variant reads authored data through the existing bill-family
  renderer. Its wording, federal eligibility declaration, parameter bounds,
  service profile and saved keys remain the same; the inline wording path is
  removed.

- **When Congress repeals a law, what it changed ends that day** When Congress
  repeals a federal law, the change that law made to people's lives ends on the
  day the repeal takes effect. Before, an effect that builds slowly, such as the
  poverty a higher retirement age causes over five years, kept going until the
  same number of years had passed since the repeal. Now a law can be undone as
  well as passed, and the effect goes with it.

- **Federal laws on foreign aid, the debt limit, farm subsidies and defense now
  move the federal books** Four federal spending laws now change what the
  government spends each month. More foreign aid raises International Affairs
  spending by about 6.7%. Cuts that pay for a higher debt limit take $150
  billion a year from every program but defense and interest. A farm subsidy cut
  saves about $3.9 billion a year. A defense build-up grows defense spending
  each year it stays in force, for at most five years. The government borrows
  less, or more, to match.

- **Federal programs settle admitted state payment claims through existing
  public accounts** Saved federal appropriations can carry dated state payment
  claims referencing an exact final adopted amount or matching ratio. Matching
  claims reference actual posted state program expenditures. The existing
  public-program commitment and installment writer checks available authority
  and cash, posts the federal debit and state credit, and retains claim
  provenance and replay protection. The monthly budget handler admits due claims
  after closing the preceding month, preserving the actual posting month. This
  adds no default claims, legal rates, allocations, or estimated cash for
  programs whose payable source inputs are absent.

- **Raising the top federal income tax rate now changes top earners' paychecks**
  A federal law raising the top income tax rate now taxes pay in the top bracket
  at 39.6% instead of 37%, the top rate before 2018, from the first tax year
  that begins with the law in force. A later law lowering it puts 37% back the
  same way. Pay below the top bracket is withheld as before.

- **Campaigns apply support loss from public findings** Public adverse findings
  now reach campaign support through the existing campaign engine. The same
  support writer, active-contest checks, source evidence, and authored effect
  magnitudes apply. Press records the outcome; it no longer owns the
  support-loss implementation.

- **Fiscal bill families now read authored wording from data rows.** Three
  fiscal families retain their eight core variants, standing authorities,
  ordered terms and evidence through the existing bill-family materializer. The
  four separate leaf variants keep their registry order. Authority refusals,
  optional-authority wording and boundary-dependent terms remain data
  declarations; the old inline core bank and rendering path is removed.

- **Keep the ordinary day focused on choices that need the player** The room
  calls its situation “What’s happening.” The Calendar shows dated activities
  without skip boxes or commute rows. Date format lives in Options. Work earns
  saved pay without Calendar stops. The leisure plan, grocery route, and
  household-errand choices are gone. Candidate guidance no longer speaks unknown
  filing rules. A saved meeting plan takes the trip during Day or Week and opens
  the meeting at its start. Without a plan, Day or Week stops when the on-time
  trip must leave; Go spends the trip. A later choice still spends travel time
  and enters for the time left. Stay runs to the posted end.

- **Menus and screens are framed paper with brass trim instead of dark blue**
  The title menu, the new-game steps and every screen in play now sit on warm
  paper panels with a thin brass frame and dark ink type, in place of the dark
  blue glass. Headings use the serif face. The bottom bar's tiles are paper and
  turn oxblood when pressed. Buttons, fields, check boxes and lists share the
  same paper and brass. Party colors on the opening maps were darkened so each
  one still reads on the lighter ground.

- **What a law did to a friend or partner weighs by the tie** When someone heard
  what a law did to a friend, it used to move their view of the officials behind
  it by a fixed quarter, whoever the friend was. Now it moves them as much as
  they care about the one who told them: half from someone they are close to, a
  quarter from someone they like, and an eighth from anyone else. A law that hit
  a partner's paycheck used to count at a fixed half; now it counts in full
  against the couple's pooled pay, so it lands harder in a household that lives
  on one paycheck than in one with two.

- **The people around a life make friends where they already meet, not by
  chance** The parent, classmate or teacher written around a played life used to
  be given a couple of neighbors and a friend or two picked at random from their
  town. Now nothing is picked by chance. A friend is someone they already share
  a congregation, club, council, child's class or job with, closest in age
  first. A sociable person keeps two friends and anyone else one. A neighbor is
  someone their household already knows as a neighbor. When the record places
  them in no group and no job, their friend is the adult in town closest to
  their age, because friends are most often of an age.

- **Cash bail moves money and returns it when the case closes** Full cash bail
  reaches the saved court government's account before a defendant is released.
  An explicit resident-household payment can also end a hold. The saved case
  closure returns the deposit to its original payer once; missing accounts and
  insufficient funds do not claim payment. Commercial premiums and unsourced
  court deductions are not treated as court deposits.

- **People are named for the year they were born** Every generated person drew a
  name from one list made mostly of 1990s names, so someone born in the 1950s
  could come out named Aiden. Names now follow the year a person was born. Most
  people take a name from the Social Security Administration's top 100 for their
  own sex in the decades around their birth year, weighted by how many babies
  really got that name. A birth year between two decades draws from both, so
  there is no sudden change at 1950. The rest keep a name from the full list, so
  an uncommon name is still possible. In a Columbus, Ohio test world, the share
  of people born from 1935 to 1964 whose name was in their own decade's top 100
  went from 17% to 67%. How many people take a decade name is set by the game,
  not measured: the real figure is how many births each decade's top 100
  accounts for, which is not compiled yet. It applies to the people a new life
  starts with and to the members of Congress in a new world. Saved games keep
  their names.

- **Governor races follow the state's voters** A governor's race in a state
  nobody campaigns in is no longer settled by drawing a few thousand votes for
  each candidate. The state's own voters decide it: the race starts from how the
  state split between the two major parties in the 2024 presidential election,
  moved by this world's own political swing, and counts as many ballots as the
  state cast that year. The candidates run as the Democratic and Republican
  nominees, and the sitting governor, when running again, carries their own
  party's line. A state whose governor was elected shortly before the game
  opens, like Virginia's in November 2025, now has that governor-elect ready to
  take office when the outgoing term ends. Before, the office sat empty until
  the next regular election.

- **A governor signs or vetoes a bill for reasons, and writes them down** A
  governor the game plays now signs or vetoes each bill for reasons it records:
  their own principles on what the bill does, whether their party carried it and
  voted for it, how they get along with the sponsor, whether the legislature
  would override a veto anyway, and their chief of staff's advice. Before, a
  bill no strong principle bore on was signed or vetoed by a draw. The rest of a
  governor's desk follows the record too. A budget request is funded when the
  governor's party holds the legislature or the chief of staff knows how to work
  it; early work on a new law goes well on a careful plan, a steady chief of
  staff or money the legislature approved; and a governor takes each matter up
  the day before its deadline. Each of these was a draw before.

- **A governor decides what the budget does with money a law gained or lost the
  state** Before, when a law added money to a state or took money away, the next
  budget handled it the same way everywhere: extra money went to programs, and a
  loss was cut only where a law required a balanced budget. Now the governor
  decides when the next budget is adopted, from their own principles. A governor
  who favors fiscal restraint saves a gain in the rainy-day fund; one who favors
  public programs spends it. For a loss under a balanced-budget law, the first
  cuts programs and the second draws on the reserve above its required floor.
  Without that law, a governor who wants a balanced budget cuts, and one who
  does not keeps programs and runs a deficit. Each decision is recorded with the
  governor and the reason, and the money it moves reaches the state's programs
  and the jobs they pay for.

- **A governor decides whether a mid-year shortfall is met by cuts or the
  rainy-day fund first** Before, a state that fell short mid-year always cut
  every program by up to 3% first, then drew its rainy-day fund, then cut again,
  whatever its governor believed. Now the governor decides from their own
  principles. One who favors keeping a reserve cuts programs before touching it;
  one who puts public programs first draws the reserve before cutting. A
  governor with no view on it, and every county and city, draws the reserve
  first, since reserves are kept to protect services through a temporary revenue
  shortfall. Each cut and draw is recorded with who decided and why, in the
  order the money moved. When the reserve runs out first, the cut that follows
  says how much the reserve covered and how much was cut.

- **Metering and limiting groundwater changes how much farms pump** Metering and
  limiting groundwater withdrawals changes how much groundwater a state's farms
  pump. Every state, D.C. and territory starts with its real rule: 31 states,
  Puerto Rico and Guam require a permit that sets how much a large well may
  pump, and the rest only register wells or limit pumping in some areas. The 50
  states and Puerto Rico start at the groundwater they pumped for irrigation in 2015. A state that starts limiting withdrawals pumps 21% to 40% less for
  irrigation a year later, and one that stops pumps that much more.

- **A grown-up start holds a job** A grown-up New Game now opens with the job
  the person already holds, at a business in their own town, instead of between
  jobs. The employer is chosen from their own situation: where a relative or
  someone in their household works, else the kind of work they have done before,
  else the best-paying work they are fit for. The job pays each week and can be
  left like any other.

- **Keep current version labels out of the player screens** The title, setup,
  loading room, play screen, Options and current Patch Notes header no longer
  display the current version label. Historical patch notes and the route to
  them remain available.

- **Letting highway money pay for transit changes transit service** Before, a
  law letting highway money pay for transit changed nothing in the world, and no
  place had a starting answer on the question. Now every state, D.C. and
  territory starts with its real rule: 39 keep gas tax money on roads by
  constitution or statute, and 17 let it pay for transit. A state or city that
  changes its rule changes its transit service two years later, about 6% more
  service where the money is freed and 6% less where it is locked away, with
  each world drawing its own size between 1% and 18%. The size follows how much
  highway money states moved to transit when federal law let them.

- **Home quotes use sourced down payments and recorded mortgage rates** Opening
  down-payment estimates use the 2025 NAR median separately for first-time and
  repeat buyers. Completed purchases recorded in the game then supply the
  average for that buyer group; old placeholder purchases do not. The quoted
  monthly payment uses the recorded local or national game macro rate and the
  approved 30-year fixed term through the shared financing reader. When no rate
  is recorded, the quote stays unavailable. The purchase panel shows that gap,
  and households do not qualify for a mortgage using a guessed payment. Existing
  mortgage servicing remains a separate implementation.

- **The home screen shows the places of government, and your character in
  theirs** The home screen no longer opens on an apartment. It moves through
  every painted place of government in the game: the Oval Office, the U.S.
  Capitol, the House and Senate floors, each state's own capitol, legislative
  chambers, council and county chambers, courtrooms, city hall and campaign
  offices, with a new picture every 15 seconds. With a saved game, Continue now
  shows your character's role beside their name, and the first picture is where
  that role is done: your chamber, your courtroom, your campaign office, or your
  state's capitol. Your character stands in front of it, dressed for the place.
  Someone who holds no role starts at their town's city hall, never at home.
  Saved games, Options and Patch notes now open beside the menu, and the
  pictures keep changing behind them. The menu stays in place and never fades
  while a picture changes.

- **Home is a painted room that follows the time of day and the weather** Your
  home is now one of the new painted rooms, and it changes with the clock:
  morning light, midday, night, and rain on rainy days. The people who live with
  you stand in it, and the TV and the papers on the table are in the right
  places. Six kinds of home are painted (a small apartment, a rowhouse, a
  suburban house, a large house, a mobile home and a farmhouse), each sized for
  people from its own picture. Until new lives record what kind of building they
  live in, every home is the suburban house.

- **A hostile person no longer readily says yes when asked to meet** A person
  who often begins an exchange looking for a fight now counts that as one reason
  to turn down an invitation to meet. It weighs beside what is between the two
  of them, the day and everything else they carry, so the same person asked the
  same thing without that streak still says yes.

- **A home town in nine states answers from the U.S. House lines in use for 2026.** In Texas, California, North Carolina, Ohio, Utah, Florida, Tennessee,
  Louisiana and Alabama, a life started in 2026 or later now finds its U.S.
  House district, the House seats it can run for, and the "possible districts"
  for a split town from the lines those states use for the 2026 elections, not
  the older 119th Congress lines. Across those nine states, 2,472 towns have a
  different answer than before. Missouri stays on its 2022 lines, and every
  other state is unchanged. The map's district outlines for those nine states
  now draw the 2026 lines too, on every date of the history slider, since play
  starts in 2026 or later.

- **Each redrawn state's 2026 U.S. House lines start on the day that state's
  plan took effect.** In Texas, North Carolina, Ohio, California, Utah, Florida,
  Tennessee, Louisiana and Alabama, a home town now answers from the 2026 lines
  on the day that state's plan became law, not on one shared January 1, 2026.
  Texas, North Carolina, Ohio, California and Utah are in force before any 2026
  game date. A life on a date in early 2026 still reads the older lines in
  Florida until May 4, Tennessee until May 7, Louisiana until May 29 and Alabama
  until June 2. The dates are in
  `data/research/district-lines-2026/enactment-dates.json` with the event and a
  cited source for each state; California's row says its date is election day
  for Proposition 50 and Alabama's is the day the U.S. Supreme Court lifted the
  injunction.

- **Households in town move for their own reasons** A household in town used to
  buy, move or sell on a roll each season. Now it reconsiders its home when
  something in its life changed: its pay grew enough to carry the payments on a
  house, a child joined a family in an apartment, its rent reached half of what
  it earns, or an older owner was left alone in the house. Each move says why.
  Rent stabilization keeps renters in place because it holds their rent down,
  not by a fixed amount. A tenant with a lawyer who is more than four months
  behind now keeps the home only on a payment plan their pay can carry, so a
  debt no longer grows without end.

- **Rent stabilization and multifamily zoning move the share of homes rented,
  renters' moves and new apartment buildings** Every state, D.C. and Puerto Rico
  now keeps three housing figures from the Census Bureau's 2024 surveys: the
  share of homes that are rented, the share of renters who moved in the past
  year, and new apartments in buildings of five or more per 10,000 people. A
  rent stabilization law cuts the share of homes rented 15% a year after it
  takes effect and renters' moves 20% at once, as San Francisco's did. A law
  allowing small multifamily homes in single-family zones doubles new apartments
  in large buildings a year later. Each moves the other way when a law the place
  began with is repealed.

- **A law that lets more homes be built lowers the town's home prices and
  rents** A state or town that allows apartments in single-family zones, permits
  homes by right, or overrides local limits on housing now sees its home prices,
  and the rents that follow them, rise more slowly from a year after the law
  takes effect, ending about 11% lower five years on, as in Minneapolis.
  Repealing such a law the place began with moves prices the other way. The
  watched-world report now counts the laws that act through their own area's
  records, and says how many of the questions the law in force answers act in
  the world.

- **Incident response keeps its source on the response event** Incident response
  actions now keep their source on the recorded event instead of adding a
  separate causal-process receipt. Resource authorization still follows the same
  incident report, and older saved response receipts remain readable. This
  changes no hazard effects or resource amounts.

- **Estimated income-tax schedules use sourced peers** The existing missing-rate
  fallback uses comparable sourced tax schedules instead of a world-seed rate or
  deduction draw. It ranks peers by tax shape, region and household income,
  preserving known deductions and the existing tax-year reader. The result
  remains an estimate pending final enacted bracket-term admission.

- **An independent ward commission law takes a town's ward map from its
  council** A town, or a state over its towns, that adopts an independent ward
  commission now has its council wards redrawn by the commission at the town's
  next yearly review, at equal population and without regard to where members
  live, so two sitting members can land in one ward. A repeal hands the next
  census-year redraw back to the council.

- **Infrastructure bill families now read authored wording from data rows.**
  Four infrastructure families retain their eight variants, ordered clauses,
  evidence and parameter bounds through the shared bill-family renderer.
  Existing integer and year wording formatters are reused, including the
  original missing-duration wording. The old inline bank and rendering path is
  removed.

- **Privacy compliance is an initial business expense** Covered businesses pay
  one estimated initial privacy compliance expense based on their recorded
  employee count. Their books retain the law and source behind that expense.
  Later reviews do not add an unsupported yearly compliance charge.

- **Intro chapter text uses Kit 13 glass** The introduction's chapter text uses
  the shared dark glass panel and approved title, narrative and control fonts.
  Back, Next and Skip retain their existing navigation and Kit 13 letterpress
  actions.

- **Invitations and favors come from the other person's own life** Somebody you
  know asks you over for their birthday, in their own words, or asks for a hand
  at the place they just moved into, or, older and living alone, asks you to
  come by and help around the house. Each reason is true of that person, and
  whether they ask depends on their temperament. Letting weeks pass now stops on
  the morning a birthday invitation would go out. The reasonless Saturday
  invitation is gone, and twelve scenes whose events were never recorded are
  withheld until they are. The picnic favor and confidence are no longer
  written.

- **A jail term keeps a person from work** Somebody sentenced to jail no longer
  goes on working and drawing a paycheck. When the term begins, every job they
  hold goes on leave, so its pay and its hours stop. When the term ends, as
  handed down or cut short by clemency, the job resumes and pay starts again.
  The employer holds the job through the term for now; whether an employer lets
  a worker go while they are away is not yet anyone's decision in the game.
  Probation changes nothing about work.

- **Residents can be offered work elsewhere, and move for it** Once a year each
  adult in town weighs whether to look for work beyond it. Being out of work,
  short hours, low pay, being early in a career or in a profession, and a taste
  for risk push them to look. Steady full-time work, being settled in later life
  and the life they have in town hold them. Someone who looks applies to the
  state government where their closest relative outside town lives, or in their
