  own state: a professional in their own field, anyone else for entry-level
  office work. A professional who has done the work before gets an offer, as
  does anyone applying for the entry-level work. Whoever takes the offer moves
  there and starts work; whoever stays turns it down.

- **The Journal is written in your character's own voice** The Journal now reads
  as the character's own account, in the first person and the past tense: "I was
  born in Lincoln, Nebraska, on January 1, 1992." and "In January, I began
  working as a store assistant at Neighborhood Market." Quoted words, names and
  places stay exactly as recorded, and dates read as dates.

- **Judges wear their robes** Judges now wear a black judicial robe in the
  courtroom and in their own portraits, so you can tell who sits on the bench.
  At home and around town they wear their own clothes. Anyone holding a judge's
  seat counts, from a county court to the Chief Justice.

- **Shell chevrons appear only on activated static buttons** Shell static
  actions show their chevron or brass corner mark only while activated. Radial
  menu entries show neither at rest nor when opened, hovered, pressed or
  selected.

- **The seven main player sections now stay labeled along the bottom of the
  game** The seven main player sections now stay labeled along the bottom of the
  game. The player's portrait opens a smaller menu for Save, Guide, Options, and
  Return to title. On narrow screens the sections use two rows; the pin rail
  clears the measured bar height. The proposed Alt+Shift+1–7 shortcuts remain
  marked for overnight browser and desktop validation.

- **A rented home's landlord comes from the record** Who lets a rented home in
  town is no longer drawn at random. A home goes to the owner in town who lets
  the fewest homes, and among those to the one with the most recorded pay; a
  property firm is the one letting the fewest homes.

- **A privacy law or social-media age check now costs the state to enforce** A
  state that passed a consumer data privacy law or required social media sites
  to check ages used to spend nothing on it. Now the law puts enforcement staff
  on the state's budget from the day it takes effect, and a repeal takes them
  off. The cost comes from enacted bills: Colorado's and Virginia's privacy laws
  each cost about $330,000 a year, and Utah's age-check law $220,500, which the
  game spreads per resident, about 5 cents a person for privacy and 6 cents for
  age checks. A budget with a balanced-budget law makes room by trimming its
  other programs.

- **Law comparisons label the value without a law change as a model estimate.**
  Law comparisons now label the value without a law change as the game’s model
  estimate.

- **A worker whose pay a law raised now knows the law did it** When a minimum
  wage law raised town pay, the paycheck rose, but nothing recorded that the law
  had reached the worker, so no one formed a view of the lawmakers or told a
  friend. Now each raise a law made reaches the worker it raised, with the
  monthly difference, and their partner hears of it at home. In Abbeville,
  Mississippi, a federal raise lifted 14 workers' pay by $260 to $650 a month,
  and all 14 were reached, along with 5 partners.

- **Legislative rule packs declare which saved seats they read** Congress now
  declares its saved seat roll and institution-wide party lookup in its rule
  pack. The shared chamber reader preserves the members, vacancies, parties, and
  supplied ballots already recorded in the world. State packs without this
  declaration keep reading their existing candidacy openings. Malformed
  declarations are refused rather than supplying invented seats. The common bill
  agenda and executive desk select their federal or state route from the bill's
  actual jurisdiction. This changes how existing rules are selected, without
  changing term lengths, session limits, or vote requirements.

- **Legislators file bills and vote by their own principles** Sitting
  legislators and governors now hold political principles of their own. On each
  bill day, a member of the state's legislature files a bill on the question
  their principles press hardest, answering it the way they lean, or repealing a
  law in force that they oppose. Members vote for a bill that does what their
  principles call for and against one that cuts across them, even when their
  party's member carries it, and a governor whose principles bear on a bill
  signs or returns it on them. The forty-one states whose legislatures are not
  yet researched now send bills too. Their chambers have a standing committee,
  and are sized by the state's own Census legislative districts. Where the rules
  say pending bills die when the session adjourns, they now do.

- **Legislators vote for senators one by one, and old amendments come back only
  when something changes** Where an amendment has handed the choice of senators
  back to the state legislatures, each legislator now votes for a candidate on
  their own: for their party caucus's nominee, for a candidate they know and
  trust, against one they have clashed with, and for one who shares their
  principles. A sitting senator, including the player, is one candidate among
  the others, and a legislature that cannot give anyone a majority leaves the
  seat empty. Congress no longer proposes and rejects the same amendment every
  year: it tries again only when its members, or how any of them would vote,
  have changed. A state's application for a convention now stands until its
  legislature votes to rescind it. Empty Senate seats now follow the statutes
  read this week in 44 states.

- **Every state legislator now casts their own vote** Where your state's
  legislature has been seated with real people, a vote on a bill is no longer a
  count written in advance. Each member answers the question: a sponsor votes
  for their own bill, a member of the sponsor's party supports it, and a member
  of the other party has no reason to oppose an ordinary bill but lines up
  against the sponsor's party on an override of the governor's veto. A promise a
  member made about the question outweighs those cues. Each recorded vote says
  why the member voted as they did. An empty seat is a vacancy rather than a
  voter, and a legislator who is the player is never voted for. A legislative
  staffer now works for one of the members actually sitting in the chamber, and
  bills the legislature files on its own are carried by seated members, with the
  budget bill carried by the largest caucus. Older saves keep their recorded
  votes.

- **A legislative winner takes the seat when the term begins, in every state**
  Outside Kentucky, a player who won a seat in a state legislature was seated on
  election night. No state works that way, and a result grants no authority of
  its own. Now every legislative seat begins on the day its term begins.
  Kentucky keeps its researched rule. Every other state uses one general rule
  until its own is researched: the term begins on January 1 after the election,
  and it lasts two years in a lower chamber and four in a senate, or whatever
  term the game already knows for that office. Between the result and that day,
  Your office shows "Before you take office", and the campaign's closing line
  says when the term begins. When the day comes, the seat is taken up and the
  legislature's work opens. When the term ends, the seat ends with it.

- **A state legislature opens with the state's recorded party balance** Before,
  each seat in the home state's legislature took its party from the seat's
  generated lean, so a chamber could open with the wrong majority. A
  Pennsylvania world opened with Republicans holding the House 113 to 90 and
  Democrats holding the Senate 27 to 23. Now each chamber holds the party
  balance the state recorded (The Book of the States 2023, Table 3.3): the seats
  leaning furthest toward each party take that party's count, so Pennsylvania
  opens with Democrats holding the House 102 to 101 and Republicans holding the
  Senate 28 to 22. The members are still generated people. Seats the record
  gives to neither party keep their own lean, and Nebraska's nonpartisan
  legislature is unchanged.

- **A conversation keeps its lies behind a Lie button** When a conversation
  offers something you know to be false, its replies now start with the truthful
  ones. A Lie button beside them shows the false replies in place of the
  truthful answers they contradict, each still marked as a lie. The button is
  unavailable when nothing you could say is false. Replies that come with
  recorded words now show those words on the button.

- **Help that changed someone's life is still owed years later** A favor's felt
  debt used to fade to nothing within about three years, however much the help
  had meant. Now a life-changing favor never fades below a marked debt, and a
  great one never below a slight one, until it is returned. Someone who lets
  things slip holds half as much. In a new life in Billings, Montana, eight
  neighbors the player had helped through a hard year three years earlier agreed
  to meet on all 80 asks, against 50 of 80 without that help. A slight favor
  from three years earlier changed nothing.

- **News about people close to you now actually reaches you** When a friend
  moves away, a neighbor has a baby, or somebody in your household loses a job,
  the people tied to them by a household, family or close friendship now hear of
  it, and it shows under "Around you this past year". People with no tie to them
  hear nothing.

- **Who you are now says how you grew up and what you heard around town** The
  Who you are screen now has a short "How you grew up" section: what money was
  like at home, whether home stayed put, how the adults who raised you were, and
  what school was like, with whatever your temperament was left with. Below it,
  "Around you this past year" lists the births, moves and deaths you were told
  about, in the way you took them. Something you never heard of is not there.

- **Counties and cities balance their budgets instead of borrowing every
  deficit** Before, no county or city read a rule on whether its budget must
  balance, so every one of them borrowed whatever it fell short. Now a county or
  city whose own ordinances don't answer follows the most common real rule: it
  must pass a balanced budget. When collections fall short it cuts programs,
  draws its reserve, and cuts the next budget to fit, as a state under a
  balanced-budget law does. The rule is marked as estimated from what most local
  governments must do, until each state's rule for its local governments is
  read.

- **Local businesses pay what the job pays where you live** The staff of a
  town's shops and offices are now paid the published wage for their job in your
  area (Bureau of Labor Statistics, May 2025) instead of $2,800 a month
  everywhere. A job that started at one of these businesses before your life
  began pays the same.

- **Local fiscal agendas use the common member filer** City and county agenda
  intakes now use the same member filing mechanism as other legislatures.
  Recorded principles, actual seats, local authority, preemption and cooldowns
  govern proposals. Missing numeric references still allow a reasoned plain
  bill, without invented appropriations.

- **Local maintenance bill wording now comes from data rows.** The existing
  local maintenance variant reads authored data through the existing bill-family
  renderer. Its wording, ordered local eligibility declarations, bounds, service
  profile and saved keys remain the same; the inline wording path is removed.

- **Local position bills follow the member with the strongest recorded stake**
  Local council members use the shared member agenda to file position bills. The
  member whose recorded principles press hardest files the bill, and the filing
  event preserves those reasons. Existing local authority, preemption, numbering
  and council reading procedures still apply.

- **A lost right or eligibility now counts with the people who lose it** When a
  law takes away a right or an eligibility without costing money, the people it
  reaches now feel it. They weigh it like losing a tenth of a month's pay. That
  size is a placeholder until research measures it. They hold it against the
  officials who voted for the law, and six neighbors who lose it can band
  together against the law.

- **Louisiana's parishes are governed by their police juries and parish
  councils** A Louisiana parish's governing body now carries its own name: the
  police jury in the 38 parishes that keep one, whose members are police jurors,
  the parish council in the 21 home-rule parishes, and the parish commission in
  Caddo. Before, every parish board was called a board of commissioners, the
  national average name. The number of seats on each body is not read yet and
  stays the national average, marked as an estimate.

- **People now carry a private substance use health record at the survey's
  shares** Before: nothing on a person's health record said whether they live
  with a substance use disorder, so no law about harm reduction or treatment had
  anyone to reach. Now: when the model first exposes a person aged 12 or older,
  their private health record may hold a substance use disorder, at the 2023
  national survey's shares by age (8.5 percent of teens, 27.1 percent of 18 to
  25, 16.6 percent of older adults, sliding between bands). It changes no one's
  mortality yet, and nobody starts one later in life until onset from recorded
  causes is built.

- **The economy now lives through eras, recessions, depressions and inflation
  scares.** The economy no longer settles around one growth rate. Its long-run
  pace wanders and can shift into a new era; recessions arrive every few years
  with their own depth and length, and now and then a depression; long slumps
  leave unemployment higher for years; and a price shock can pull inflation up
  until it is brought back down. A long game can see booming decades, lost
  decades and a 1970s-style inflation, and no two games follow the same path.

- **Legislatures bring up bills backed by their majority** State legislatures
  and Congress consider their members' proposed bills and bring forward a bill
  only when a majority of the agenda caucus and the chamber supports it.

- **Towns whose charter is unread elect a mayor for four years** A town whose
  own charter the game has not read now follows the most common real rule: its
  voters elect the mayor directly, and the mayor serves four years. Before, each
  such town drew how its mayor is chosen and how long the term runs from
  national survey shares, so some towns had no mayoral race and others a
  one-year term for no reason of their own. Towns whose rules have been read
  keep them.

- **Gaining or losing Medicaid coverage names the person and the law that did
  it** When a state's Medicaid expansion or work requirement puts someone on
  coverage or takes it away, the change now shows up as something that law did
  to that person, and their partner hears of it at home. The record is a gain or
  a loss with no dollar amount, because the coverage record shows enrollment,
  not what it was worth to them. Officials behind the law can be seen in a
  better or worse light because of it, and a repeal that ends coverage records
  its own loss.

- **The people at the posted public meeting are your neighbors** The chair and
  the two residents who speak at the posted public meeting are now people who
  already live in your town. Before, where the town's council was not seated,
  the game made up a chair and two residents for the evening, with drawn names
  and ages. Now the neighbors who belong to the most groups, such as a party, a
  congregation, a club or a board, are the ones who come, and the one who
  belongs to the most chairs. Where the council is seated, a council member
  still chairs.

- **State and federal members can file their own bills** Seated state and
  federal lawmakers can file from their own recorded views, including
  minority-party members. A moving bill still keeps another bill on the same
  question from being filed, and committees decide what advances.

- **A legislator's formed view can shape a vote** When a bill says whether it
  supports or opposes a policy question, a legislator who has formed a view on
  that question can vote accordingly. The decision cites the legislator's
  recorded belief. A bill that only names the question gives no such voting
  reason, and an uncertain view does not become a yes or no vote by assumption.
  The importance assigned to a view is a marked game assumption pending
  calibration.

- **Missing new presidential views can read current recorded campaign support**
  When no other official has a saved support view, missing re-recorded
  presidential standing can now use the latest canonical support shares of
  current same-party candidates in active, pending contests. The result is
  labeled an estimate and names its actual candidates, campaign and
  support-state records, mean and spread. Duplicate references to one
  candidate's metric scope count once. Completed or canceled contests do not
  supply current standing. These contest shares are comparable estimates, not
  national adult-contact counts. Actual re-recorded presidential views still use
  the same recorded adult cohort and dated inauguration baseline. A wholly empty
  comparable-record pool remains a producer gap; this checkpoint does not create
  support records or wire estimate details into the player screen.

- **A midterm turns voters against the President's party in every seat** A
  congressional or state legislative count now carries the national mood. In a
  midterm year, every seat's vote shifts 3.6 points of the two-party vote
  against the President's party: the average loss over the 19 midterms from 1950
  to 2022 (Brookings Vital Statistics on Congress, Table 2-2, and the Clerk of
  the House). No draw decides it: the same shift reaches every seat, and a
  change of President changes which party pays it. A presidential year or an
  odd-year state election carries no mood.

- **A mischievous tendency affects public press responses** A person with a
  marked mischievous tendency may dispute an allegation when they are named in a
  press request.

- **Residents record monthly receipts from paid public services** Residents in a
  service area now record a monthly benefit when a public provider has actually
  received an installment under an active service law.

- **Menu reset - the title and saved-games screens now show record data only**
  Menu reset: Title now shows record data only.

- **Menu reset - the Campaign screen shows record data only** Menu reset:
  Campaign drops its helper sentences and empty-state lines in favor of short
  labels.

- **Menu reset - the Work screen shows record data only** Menu reset: Work drops
  its helper sentences on accepted offers, pending groups and the open-measure
  line.

- **Menu reset - the Journal shows recorded entries only** Menu reset: the
  Journal drops its empty-state sentences.

- **Menu reset - the Guide screen shows terms and entries only** Menu reset: the
  Guide drops its helper sentences.

- **Menu reset - the municipal screen shows record data only** Menu reset: the
  municipal screen drops its helper sentences, developer notes and empty-state
  lines.

- **Menu reset - Local government shows Home as a label and value** Menu reset:
  Local government shows "Home: {place}" and no unsupported-town line.

- **Menu reset - the measure paper shows record data only** Menu reset: the
  measure paper drops its helper sentences and empty-list lines.

- **Menu reset: the title menu's Watch the world control stands alone** Menu
  reset: the title menu's Watch the world control stands alone, with no sentence
  under it.

- **Menu reset - the character creator now shows record data only** Menu reset:
  Creator now shows record data only.

- **Close the completed Creator menu reset row** The assignment tracker now
  points to the existing Creator screen reset evidence.

- **Menu reset - Options and difficulty now show setting names and values only**
  Menu reset: Options now shows record data only.

- **Menu reset - the personal screens now show record data only** Menu reset:
  Personal now shows record data only.

- **Menu reset - the Calendar shows times and names, not written sentences**
  Menu reset: the Calendar detail shows the time an action ends, the minutes it
  waits and takes, and what blocks it, as data. The long explanation sentences
  are gone.

- **The calendar's entries show labeled values, not sentences** A calendar entry
  lists its kind, who is in charge, who it came through and where it is. A
  planned activity shows Starts and Length. The written lines are gone: "You
  might go.", "Starts Tuesday ... and takes 1 hour, 15 minutes.", the summary
  under each entry, and the empty-calendar notes.

- **Menu reset: the shell footer shows no status sentences** Menu reset: the
  shell footer no longer prints "Opened <view>." or the unsaved-life sentence;
  both stay as trace fields.

- **A person card shows who is here as a label and the room name** Before, a
  person card in the room said "Here in the room with you." Now the card shows a
  Present label with the name of the room beside it.

- **Desktop labels keep build identity without release numbers** The desktop
  header and Install update button omit release numbers. About still shows the
  build revision and composition, and Settings keeps the architecture and
  recorded build identities. Update checks and installation choices are
  unchanged.

- **A classmate or neighbor answers from who they are and how you stand** When
  the player asks a classmate to split the unfinished part of a project, or asks
  a neighbor to go to a posted public meeting, the answer now depends on the
  person's own temperament and on how they stand with the player from their
  side: warmth, trust, respect, what they feel bound to, and anything unsettled
  between them. Besides yes and no, a classmate can say they need to see what is
  left before agreeing, and a neighbor can say they have not decided, or suggest
  the player go instead. Those answers leave the question open, so the player
  can take it up again. The tone of every line in these two conversations also
  comes from that same reading rather than from a count of good and bad
  exchanges.

- **A household that arrives or is evicted finds a home by its own pay** A
  household with no home in town no longer gets its kind of home from a draw. It
  buys a house when it has work and its pay carries the payments, and rents
  otherwise: an apartment for one or two people, a rowhouse for more. A
  household evicted in the last season records that it was evicted from its last
  home. The town's opening homes still come from its housing mix.

- **Newsrooms now weigh how much a story matters, and big local news can go
  national.** Newsrooms still favor open matters and stories that name people.
  They now also weigh the scale an event records, such as how severe a flood
  was, whether it involves a public office, and whether it happened in the
  outlet's own area. Good news and bad news of the same size count the same.
  When a member of Congress or a senator dies in office, or a disaster is major
  or worse, the national outlets now cover it even though it happened in one
  state. Smaller local events stay local.

- **News outlets start with realistic owners** Who founds a news outlet now
  follows real ownership shares for newspapers and local digital outlets: about
  half of newspapers start independently owned, almost none are owned by a
  nonprofit, and nonprofits hold close to half of local digital outlets. The
  other owner numbers are still marked as placeholders awaiting research.

- **News stories have their own headlines** A story about a local proposal or an
  international development now has a short headline above its report, such as
  "City of Lincoln seeks comment on road repair schedule plan" above "City of
  Lincoln posted a proposal about the repair schedule for several local roads
  and opened a public comment period." Before, the same sentence was printed
  twice. Saved stories are unchanged.

- **The news starts with what happens in your life, newest first** A new life
  used to open with four local reports already in the archive, from a fixed
  template placed at set dates rather than anything that had happened in that
  town. New lives now start without them, and the news fills with what happens
  after the life starts. The front page also orders saved reports by date,
  newest first; before, every national story came ahead of every local one. A
  replay link made before this change still rebuilds the same world, archived
  reports included: new games record a new opening-data version.

- **News stories add what came before, name who died in office, and stop
  printing the game's own notes.** A story used to print its event's one-line
  record twice, once as the headline and again as the body. Now a story adds
  what the world actually holds about it: the earlier dated steps of the same
  development ("Earlier in this story"), the previous reading of an
  unemployment, inflation or growth figure and whether it rose or fell, the size
  and place of a flood or storm and how many that place has had this year, and a
  dateline for readers outside the place. A death or incapacity in office is now
  reported by name and office ("William Rogers, Governor of Kentucky, died on
  July 1."), with what happens to each seat. Those stories used to print the
  simulation's own ruling notes, including lines such as "the game has not
  compiled NH's rule", which no longer reach the page. Nothing is invented: no
  quotes, reactions or causes are written for a story.

- **A smaller newsroom covers less, and an owner's outlets run one another's
  stories** When an owner lays off reporters, the outlet now takes on fewer
  stories, in step with the reporters it kept. Once nobody is left, it covers
  nothing. An owner that orders its outlets to share now has each story run in
  its other outlets, credited to the newsroom that reported it. A sibling outlet
  carries a story only if it is relevant to that outlet's own readers. How often
  owners do this is still a placeholder awaiting research.

- **A refusal to stand for office now tells you the rule, not where we read it**
  **For the player.** Standing for office and being turned down used to hand you
  a legal citation. Columbus read "Ohio Const. art. XV, § 4 requires that the
  candidate is a qualified elector"; Nevada read "NRS 218A.200 requires 1 year".
  Twenty-six sentences across the qualification screens opened that way. They
  now say what the office asks and stop: "This office requires that the
  candidate is a qualified elector", "Not resident long enough: this office asks
  for 1 year, and this character has lived here 0 months." **What did not
  change.** Every verdict is the same. Nothing was loosened and nothing new is
  granted or refused. The citation, the authority, the URL and the retrieval are
  all still on the record each assessment carries — this takes them off the
  screen and out of nowhere else.

- **A governor is not asked about a disaster that damaged nothing** A played
  governor is no longer asked whether to request federal disaster help after a
  minor or moderate disaster that damaged no home or building and hurt no one.
  The governor's office records that no request was made, and it does not count
  for or against the governor. A major or catastrophic disaster still asks. This
  is a placeholder until research on when real governors ask comes back.

- **The evening "sit and talk" invitation is gone** Somebody you live with used
  to say they would be home this evening and ask whether you would like to sit
  and talk. Saying yes put two hours on your calendar and nothing else: no
  conversation ever came of it. It is no longer offered. An invitation already
  waiting in a saved game still reads and can still be answered.

- **No player screen offers a panel of sources** Four player screens offered a
  panel of sources. They no longer do. The career panel's "Occupation source
  context" showed an occupation code, a record id and a publisher attribution;
  what a player wanted from it — what the work pays nationally — stays, as a
  plain sentence. The constitutional workspace's procedure notes keep every word
  about how an amendment works and lose the three links to the authorities
  behind them. The nationwide candidacy screen no longer attributes government
  names to a listing, and no longer prints the statute citations behind a
  refusal; the refusal's own sentence is the whole of what a player is told. The
  party-life panel no longer lists the citations behind what a host said about
  running for office. Five more screens printed a citation without any panel at
  all, and they are gone too. The governor's calendar panel, "How this office's
  calendar works", listed statutes with quoted excerpts; it now says the term
  length, how often the election comes and when the winner takes office, and the
  governor's briefing says the same instead of explaining that a state's rules
  were "not compiled into the game yet". A constitutional measure no longer
  links its "Proposal rule source", the docket's session deadline and a city's
  manager election rule no longer end in a statute citation, and the Alaska tax
  filing no longer links the state constitution and calls its wording a game
  assumption. The same screen's county list printed Columbus's land as 98 + 2 +
  1 = 101 percent, because each county was rounded on its own. The shares are
  now rounded together and always total 100. The provenance itself is untouched
  and stays on the records, where a reviewer reads it.

- **Governors, legislators, judges and members of Congress are paid their real
  salaries** A governor is now paid the state's published salary, a state
  legislator the state's annual legislative salary where the state pays one, a
  judge the state's general trial court salary, and a member of Congress
  $174,000. Before, every office paid $60,000. Other offices still pay the same
  placeholder amount.

- **Missing office salaries use sourced peer estimates** An office with no
  published annual salary uses comparable sourced office salaries instead of a
  random point in a range. The estimate uses the state's region and recorded
  household-income comparison; where that income is unread, it uses the
  same-office average. Published salaries and enacted salary terms keep their
  existing readers.

- **Officeholder principles form from recorded life** New officeholder
  principles now form from recorded life instead of a seeded stance and
  conviction draw. Existing saved principles and the player's choices remain
  intact. A life with no supported pull receives no invented view. A public
  party label now supplies only a slight pull, below the threshold for forming a
  principle on its own, including for sitting officeholders.

- **Lawmakers' principles are not re-read from their whole lives several times a
  day** Before each bill step and agenda filing, every seated member's
  principles are formed again from their recorded life. On the same day, a
  member whose life records have not changed since an earlier reading that wrote
  nothing is now not read again. The check covers their own principle rows,
  those of the parents and confidants it read, the crimes against them and their
  seat roll. Anyone whose principles changed earlier in the same pass is read
  again, and a new day, a reloaded save or another world reads everyone afresh.
  The existing crime-victim index on the receiving main branch is preserved. The
  donor reported the same saved fingerprint in its watched year from South Fork,
  Pennsylvania; that year was not rerun for this receiving checkpoint. When
  sociability and conflict read the same person on the same immutable world
  snapshot, they now share the upbringing reading. A changed world snapshot, a
  new date or a reloaded save reads afresh. Focused checks compare these
  readings with fresh readings and cover a newly recorded childhood entry,
  separate people and the next day. This does not skip formation across days.

- **Officeholders decide for themselves whether to run again** Members of
  Congress, state lawmakers, governors and town officials no longer retire at a
  fixed age or on a random draw. Each one decides when the next election comes
  up, from the office they hold, their own health record, their own odds of
  living through the next term, the people they care for at home, and their
  temperament. A town official deciding whether to resign mid-term weighs the
  same things. The reason that weighed most is recorded with the choice, so a
  governor who steps down says why. A person the parties ask to run weighs their
  own health and age the same way, so nobody past a set age is left off the
  list. Sitting governors in a world nobody is playing now run again; before,
  only a game with a played character let them.

- **Whom you meet next, and when an old friend calls, come from the people, not
  chance** An old friend who gets back in touch no longer rings on a day picked
  by chance. They ring on a day they are not at work. How long they leave it
  follows how warm they are toward you, so a close friend calls sooner. How far
  ahead they suggest meeting follows how much of a planner they are. Whether
  they call at all is still their own decision, weighed from their temperament
  and their own goals. Meeting somebody new is no longer drawn either. The next
  person you meet is the one who turns up in the most of the places you share,
  such as the same job and the same congregation, and then the one nearest your
  age.

- **An employer on the older work list calls back for a reason** When someone
  accepts a job from the older work list and does not come in on the start date,
  the employer no longer tosses a coin over whether to call. It calls once with
  a new start date when somebody the person knows works there, or when nobody
  else is waiting to start whom it could call instead; otherwise it withdraws
  the offer. This is the same rule the job market's own employers follow. The
  new start date comes a few days later, or a week later when the person has a
  job to leave first, and an unanswered offer lapses after the short reply
  window the job market gives work paid by the shift.

- **Federal budgets settle once from actual account payments** The monthly
  public-budget pass uses the common government account settler for the nation.
  New worlds open only that budget. It preserves legacy federal forecast records
  without advancing them. A missing federal cash account does not produce
  forecast transactions or a fabricated loan.

- **An open-minded juror hears out the defense before voting to convict** A
  juror who gives unfamiliar explanations a hearing now counts that habit as one
  reason to vote not guilty. It weighs beside the evidence in the case, the
  burden of proof and what the rest of the room says, so the same juror on the
  same testimony without that streak still votes to convict.

- **A legislature's first bill number follows its own filing pace** Before, the
  bill number a legislature had reached when a new game opened was drawn at
  random between 12 and 639. Now it follows how many bills that legislature
  files in a year (The Book of the States 2023, Table 3.19) and how far into the
  year the game opens. A game that opens in early January meets bill 1 or close
  to it, and one that opens in July in a busy legislature meets a higher number.
  A legislature the table does not count takes the middle state's pace, marked
  as an estimate. A saved game continues from the highest number its legislature
  has already filed.

- **Opening budget estimates use researched bases without a random spread** A
  county's, city's or unsurveyed territory's opening budget now uses the
  existing researched average and population calculation directly. An extra
  random spread no longer changes revenue, spending or debt. Governments with
  their own read figures retain those values. Existing saved budgets are kept.

- **A new adult's opening savings follow what families at their pay really
  keep** A working adult's opening bank balance now comes from the Federal
  Reserve's Survey of Consumer Finances 2022: the median that families at their
  income keep in checking, savings and money market accounts. A person earning
  $33,600 a year opens with about $1,800; one earning $115,000 with about
  $15,600. Before, each adult drew a savings habit between 3% and 13% of pay by
  chance and multiplied it by their years of work.

- **Docket filing shows operative section choices** The office drafting table
  now offers only bill sections with a recorded enacted consumer in the current
  jurisdiction. Its preview and filing action use the same selected sections,
  and an appropriation cannot name a proposed measure as enacted spending
  authority.

- **Selected appropriation sections retain their stated effect** An
  appropriation filed with selected sections keeps those sections in order and
  preserves the selection through Save and Continue. The filing action refuses a
  selected duty the world cannot carry out. A state appropriation's
  available-through date follows its enacted clause. The existing Alaska transit
  service mandate remains a complete package because its service reader requires
  all of its sections together. The new ordinary filing entry requires an
  explicit selection; existing callers still use the legacy office function
  until the player control is connected.

- **Errands get done, rent is due, homes can be bought, officeholders are paid,
  and degrees can be applied for** "Get things done" now clears the week's
  errands, and new requests keep arriving while one is waiting. On the first of
  each month you pay $1,500 for rent, food and bills. Money and property offers
  a home for $250,000 with $50,000 down and $1,200 a month in place of rent. An
  officeholder you play is paid $60,000 a year, weekly. Associate's, bachelor's
  and master's degrees can be applied for and finished. Every dollar figure here
  is a placeholder until research on real costs, pay, home prices and admissions
  comes back.

- **Ordinary life no longer asks you to do chores, favors or shop shifts** Days
  no longer stop for household chores, grocery runs, favors for people you know,
  household evenings or shifts you had to start by hand. A job still pays on its
  own schedule as time passes, without clicking to work. Your own temperament is
  now written from the choices you make growing up, such as speaking up at
  school or putting money away, and the people you meet read it.

- **Keep ordinary days clear of routine chores** Ordinary lives no longer
  receive the grocery route, its old-save branches, a weekly leisure-planning
  prompt, or a generic good-day scene. Waiting through routine work shifts does
  not stop the clock, even with an older preference to stop for them. Day or
  Week stops at a posted public meeting's on-time departure unless the player
  already planned to attend; decisions and fixed commitments still stop time.

- **Ordinary replies follow recorded speaking habits** Ordinary conversations
  now build replies from the English engine's parts. Greetings can invite a
  question or leave room to listen, according to the speaker's recorded
  approach. The conversation keeps the words and parts for History and
  Save/Continue.

- **You say what became of your other parent** When a custom start puts a child
  in a home with one parent, the "At home" step now asks about the other parent:
  living, living somewhere else, or has died. The life records what you said. No
  parent is dead at the start of a life unless you say so, and an unanswered
  question leaves no other parent on the record rather than choosing one for
  you.

- **Effect inventory shows its recorded evidence and missing inputs** The effect
  inventory keeps research confidence separate from existing structural support.
  Missing sizes and missing readers carry explicit reasons; an inventory entry
  does not claim that a law has reached a person.

- **Laws and conditions can have their full effect on a place.** Monthly place
  outcomes now retain the full changes caused by laws and recorded conditions.
  Research ranges are checked afterward without cutting down those changes.

- **Crime now responds to hard times the way the research says it does.** When
  work dries up in a town, burglaries climb about 3% for each point of
  unemployment, while robberies and assaults rise much less, as studies of real
  American places find. This is the first part of a larger change: how schools,
  health, housing, safety, the environment, the economy and elections affect one
  another now comes from one shared, researched table, and more of it switches
  on as the world starts tracking each measure.

- **Contact dialog, ask-to-meet and ask-out controls removed** The Contact
  button, the Contact dialog and the ask-to-meet and ask-out controls with their
  date picker are gone from the screens. Couples and replies to an outstanding
  request are unchanged.

- **The player no longer starts a meeting or a date from a list** Asking
  somebody to meet or go out is removed from the people list. People still reach
  out on their own, and the player answers.

- **Missing paid-leave rates use sourced program peers** Unread paid-leave
  premium and replacement rates use comparable sourced program rates instead of
  a world-seed draw. The estimate ranks program peers by region and household
  income, preserving read rates, caps and the existing payment path. It remains
  labeled as an estimate until the law's own rate terms are admitted.

- **The papers waiting on you are on the table in the room** What was waiting on
  you could be acted on, but only after two presses: open the calendar, then
  find the day inside it. The room itself held nothing you could touch. A room
  you can only look at is a backdrop, not a place. The papers now sit on the
  table where the scene already has a place for them. Resting on them says how
  many things are waiting, and nothing about what they are; picking them up
  opens the list, and each line still leads to the record that answers it,
  exactly as it does on the day. Putting them down, or pressing escape, returns
  you to the room and to the paper you picked up. Reading them spends nothing.
  The clock on the shell does not move when the papers open, when a record opens
  from them, or when they go back down. When nothing is waiting there is no
  object to pick up, and the table is the painted one the scene has always
  shown.

- **Parties ask their past candidates first, and early filing deadlines are
  kept** When a seat in Congress or a state legislature comes up again, each
  party first asks the person it asked in that district last time, who decides
  again for themselves. The game makes up a new person only when nobody from
  before can run, so a long game no longer piles up unused candidates: a watched
  game in East Middlebury, Vermont, held 2,982 people by December 2030 instead
  of 4,062. A state whose real filing deadline falls before the game opens, such
  as Texas on December 8, 2025, now starts with its candidates already filed on
  that day, and later cycles file in the year before the election as the real
  calendar does.

- **Each government's pension grows and pays out at its own plans' rates** A
  government's pension now grows each year by the employer's normal cost its own
  plans report to the Public Plans Database, and pays out the benefits they
  report. Illinois' state plans owe 0.92% of their liability a year and pay out
  5.51%; Chicago's owe 0.51% and pay out 5.60%. A government whose plans do not
  report a rate, or whose reporting plans hold less than half its liability,
  takes the median of every plan: 0.83% and 5.59%, marked as estimated from the
  average. Before, every pension owed a hand-set 2% and paid out 8%: more than
  all but one state's plans owe, and more than any state's plans pay out.

- **Each government's pension opens as funded as its own plans report** A
  government's pension now opens holding the share of its liability that its own
  plans reported in the Public Plans Database, each plan's latest year:
  Illinois' state plans 54.8%, Chicago's 26.2%, Cook County's 65.9%. A
  government the database does not list opens at the median of every plan,
  76.7%, marked as estimated from the average. Before, every pension opened at
  the same hand-set 75%, so Chicago's plans opened as healthy as any other's and
  asked for a far smaller yearly contribution than their real gap does. A
  budget's record of a rainy-day deposit or a carried balance now names the
  reserve size its own law sets, such as Missouri's 7.5%. Before, both records
  still said every reserve was a hand-set 5%.

- **Each government pays the pension share its own plans reported** Each state,
  county and city now pays the share of its required pension contribution that
  its own plans reported paying in the Public Plans Database, instead of a
  random draw that drifted each year. A government with no plan listed pays the
  median of all plans, marked as estimated from the average, and the share holds
  until budgets pass as bills.

- **You see people at their jobs** The places people work are no longer empty.
  At the clerk's counter, the diner, the hospital hallway, the union hall, the
  church supper hall, the community room, the county party office, the campaign
  storefront, the store, the office and the classroom, the townspeople whose
  shift it is stand there, dressed for work, in their uniform if their job has
  one. The clerk stands behind the counter. Come back at night and the counter
  is empty. Click someone to see who they are.

- **The game knows about people sitting turned towards the room** Every seated
  figure the game had measured was drawn square to the camera, and the one
  number that placed a person on a cushion came from where their silhouette
  parts into two legs. That works on a figure facing you and means nothing on a
  figure turned towards the room, because the near leg covers the far one and
  the measurement runs on down to the shin. Ten bodies that had been drawn and
  banked could not be described well enough to seat, so they sat outside the
  catalog. Those bodies are now described. A turned figure's seat contact is
  read from the lowest point of its own hip mass on the side away from its
  knees, which is the part that rests on a cushion, and a figure the measurement
  cannot read is recorded as unread with the reason rather than given a
  plausible wrong number. The catalog of candidate bodies goes from twelve to
  twenty-two, nine of them seated, and a pose contract for a figure turned
  towards the room exists for the first time. None of this changes what a player
  sees yet. Every one of the twenty-two is a candidate awaiting the owner's look
  at it, and no candidate is ever released by a script. What changes is that the
  seated ones can now be looked at.

- **People dress for where they are** Every place now has a dress code, and
  people dress for it. At home and around the neighborhood they wear everyday
  clothes. In offices and campaign storefronts they wear work clothes, like a
  polo and khakis, a blouse and skirt or a suit. In chambers, courtrooms,
  hearings and the offices of governors and mayors they wear formal wear.
  Outdoors from November through March they wear their coats. Each person keeps
  their own clothes for each kind of place. Uniforms are saved for the jobs that
  wear them.

- **Everyone is drawn by the new people engine** Every adult in the game is now
  drawn by the new people engine: in rooms, in the conversation box, on person
  cards and in the character creator. Each person has one of six bodies
  (feminine or masculine, lean, average or heavier), one of seven skin tones, a
  whole face with no seams, and hair, and wears everyday clothes or, in
  government rooms, a formal suit. A person looks the same on every screen and
  after every load. In the creator and in Appearance and wardrobe you choose
  your body, build, skin tone and outfit with arrows, or press Randomize. Seated
  people and children still use the older pictures until the engine has seated
  and child bodies. More faces, hairstyles and clothes arrive as they are
  painted.

- **People look their age** Faces now age with the people who wear them. Someone
  in their twenties or thirties looks young. From their mid-forties they show
  lines at the eyes and mouth. From sixty-five they have deeper wrinkles and
  gray brows. Each person keeps the same face for life, so a neighbor you knew
  young is recognizably the same person years later. In the creator, the Face
  arrows choose among six faces, and the game ages the one you pick.

- **People have many more hairstyles and hair colors** People now wear 11
  hairstyles for women and 15 for men instead of one each, among them long
  straight hair, waves, curls, afros, box braids, locs, buns, ponytails, pixie
  cuts, bobs, fades, buzz cuts, side parts and man buns. Each comes in seven
  hair colors, from black to blonde, with gray and white growing more common
  with age. Hair is chosen for each person on its own, separate from skin and
  face. In the creator and in Appearance and wardrobe, new arrows choose your
  hair and hair color.

- **People know the council, congregation or club they belong to, and their
  children's classmates' parents** A person now knows the people they meet in
  the same place: the other members of a town council, a congregation, a club or
  another group they take part in, and the parents of their children's
  classmates. A class is the children at one school who started kindergarten the
  same fall. Nothing is drawn; both people have to be on record in the same
  place now. A party registration is not a place people meet, so it makes no
  acquaintance. Neighbors still come from the neighbor contacts already on
  record, because a home has no place on a street yet. Whom a person knows
  decides whom a mayor, governor or President can appoint, and whom a person
  tells about how a law touched them.

- **People have different faces** People no longer share one face. Women and men
  each have six faces now, chosen once per person, and the creator's Face arrows
  step through them. People sit only in seats that face you. Skin under collars
  and at the edge of hands now takes each person's skin tone.

- **Everyone's clothes come in their own colors** Each person's clothes now have
  their own colors: everyday tops in 14 colors and trousers in 8, suits in 6,
  shirts in 5 and ties in 5, chosen once per person so neighbors no longer dress
  alike. In the creator and in Appearance and wardrobe, arrows choose the color
  of each piece.

- **People wear many kinds of clothes** People now dress in many different
  styles, not just one everyday outfit and one suit. Women wear cardigans and
  jeans, hoodies, sweaters with slacks, winter coats with scarves, blouses with
  skirts, dresses with blazers and skirt suits. Men wear polos with khakis,
  hoodies, sweaters over collared shirts, work jackets, overcoats and
  double-breasted suits. Each person keeps their own everyday clothes and their
  own formal wear, and every piece comes in its own color. In the creator and in
  Appearance and wardrobe, the Outfit arrows step through every style, with a
  color choice for each piece.

- **Find somebody opens in place** The People search opens when selected and
  focuses its input. Clicking outside removes input focus and closes an empty
  search. Escape closes the search and returns focus to its label. Existing
  queries and People results are retained.

- **People sit in chairs** People now sit in the chairs of the rooms that have
  them: in offices, hearing rooms, committee rooms and the pavilion. Each person
  sits facing you, with their hips on the seat and their feet on the floor, in
  their own outfit, hair and skin tone. Every outfit has a seated version for
  every build.

- **Person cards omit redundant standalone labels** Person cards no longer show
  the standalone On the record label or a You badge on your own card. Facts,
  reported attribution, recorded relationships, and personal knowledge stay
  intact.

- **Personal separates current life from full history** Personal opens with a
  full figure beside current work and schooling, followed by identity, family,
  and personality. Full history opens on a separate page and keeps past jobs,
  schooling, upbringing, and remembered milestones. Contact uses the same figure
  layout while retaining its existing commands.

- **Personality changes at the pace of a life** How hard somebody is to change
  now depends on how strongly the trait is theirs, which is the owner's answer
  to what the pacing should turn on. A faint lean shifts sooner than a defining
  one, read from the strength already on the record rather than from anything
  new about the person. Two defects go with it. Repeating the same argument no
  longer counts as repeated evidence: what accumulates is separate experiences
  spread over time, capped by the declaring pack, so persistence alone cannot
  wear anybody down. And having already changed no longer makes somebody
  permanently harder to reach — a character who has lived through things stays
  reachable, which is what "every character can change" has to mean. For a
  player, an NPC who keeps being turned down still becomes a person who reaches
  out less. It now takes years of it rather than a fortnight.

- **The college admission timetable is marked as a designed game timetable**
  Before, the college admission decision time (45 days) and the fall term start
  (August 25) carried a placeholder label. Now they are marked as a designed
  game timetable, with a line naming what it reads and balances; no value
  changes.

- **The grown-up presentation age is marked as an estimate from most states'
  majority age** Before, the age from which a person is no longer described as
  somebody's dependent was labeled a placeholder. Now it is marked an estimate
  of 18, the age of majority in most states; nothing about when anyone legally
  becomes an adult changes.

- **The clemency timing windows are marked as estimates with a source** Before,
  how near the end of a term counts as leaving office, and how near an election
  counts as facing voters, were labeled hand-set placeholders. Now they are
  marked estimates tied to recorded end-of-term clemency waves; the values are
  unchanged.

- **Mark the council ordinance pace and degree-level notes as estimates**
  Before: the council ordinance pace and the degree-level admission note were
  labeled placeholders. Now: both are marked as estimated from the average, and
  the council pace carries its research key. No number changes.

- **Local crime starts from the national victimization and clearance rates**
  Before, every town ran on made-up crime rates. Now assault, robbery and
  burglary rates, the share reported and the share cleared start from the
  national Bureau of Justice Statistics and FBI figures, marked as estimates.

- **Game schedules with no real source are marked as designed** Before, the
  council ordinance pace, the local business fallback wage and the mogul offer
  schedule were marked as estimates from an average, but no average or source
  was named for any of them. Now each is marked as a designed game value, with a
  line naming what it reads and balances; no value changes.

- **Private-goal pacing is marked as an estimate with a source note** Before,
  how often people act on a private goal was labeled provisional. Now it is
  marked an estimate, with the working-age limit tied to the Social Security
  full retirement age; the values are unchanged.

- **A Supreme Court vacancy is now filled in about three weeks** Before, the
  President took 30 days to name a justice after a vacancy, a number with no
  source. Now it is 21 days, the median of the four most recent vacancies, and
  the court code says where its other weights come from.

- **Job market hiring timings are marked as estimates with a source** Before,
  how long employers take to answer, when a hire starts and what counts as full
  time were labeled placeholders. Now they are marked estimates tied to the
  national job openings survey and the 30-hour full-time line.

- **The fallback local business wage is marked as an estimate** Before, the
  monthly pay a local business gives a worker where no published wage applies
  was labeled a placeholder. Now it is marked an estimate of about 16 dollars an
  hour full time; the amount is unchanged.

- **Mogul offer rates are marked as estimates with a source note** Before, how
  often a mogul reconsiders, how long an offer stands and how much a gift is
  were labeled unresearched. Now they are marked estimates; no public record
  counts such approaches, and the values are unchanged.

- **Official-view weights are marked as estimates instead of placeholders**
  Before, the weights for blame, visibility and how hard a law landed on a voter
  carried the word placeholder. Now they are marked as game estimates; no value
  changes.

- **Official-view weights are marked as designed game weights** Before, nine
  weights for blame, visibility and how hard a law landed on a voter had been
  relabeled as estimates from an average, but no average or source was named for
  any of them. Now each is marked as a designed game weight, with a line naming
  the record it reads and the behavior it balances; no value changes.

- **The size at which a workplace stops meaning you know everyone is marked as
  an estimate** Before, the 20-person limit for knowing everyone in a workplace
  or group was labeled a placeholder. Now it is marked an estimate with a source
  note; the limit is unchanged.

- **Press finding and filing values are marked as estimates with a source**
  Before, the effect of an ethics finding on a candidate, the repeat-offense
  step and the monthly spending report were labeled unresearched. Now each is
  marked an estimate from published studies or the federal filing schedule.

- **Unrest anger and political-violence values are marked as estimates** Before,
  the anger a failed disaster response or an attack adds, and the lines that
  turn unrest into a threat, were labeled placeholders. Now each is marked an
  estimate with a source note; the values are unchanged.

- **State-to-state pressure sizes are marked as estimates with a source**
  Before, the pressure that hazards, taxes, crime and joblessness add, and the
  yearly share who leave a state, were labeled placeholders. Now each is marked
  an estimate, with the base outflow tied to the Census interstate mover share.

- **Prosecution timings and jail effects are marked as estimates with a source**
  Before, the fallback case timings and what a jail sentence does to an
  officeholder were labeled unresearched. Now they are marked estimates with a
  source note; the values are unchanged.

- **The school stage calendar is marked as an estimate of the common U.S.
  structure** Before, the stage calendar (kindergarten at five, middle school
  six years on, high school three after that) was labeled not researched. Now it
  is marked an estimate of the common U.S. grade structure; no date or
  enrollment changes.

- **Leaving-home weights are marked as estimates with their sources** Before,
  the weights behind a grown child leaving home were labeled unresearched. Now
  they are marked as estimated from the average and name the Census Bureau and
  HUD figures they rest on; the strengths themselves stay labeled as game
  values. The values are unchanged.

- **States' uninsured and poverty rates now drift over the years, and no two
  games end alike.** A state's share of uninsured people and share in poverty no
  longer hold near where they began. Each drifts month by month, partly with the
  whole country and partly on its own, and now and then a nationwide wave moves
  every state a long way. Laws and the economy still push them up or down on top
  of that, so over a long game each state's story is its own.

- **Plain words for trips, and no British English** The button for a trip now
  says where you are going, such as "Go to the public meeting", instead of "Make
  the journey", and other activities say "Start now". Trips on the calendar read
  "Trip to the public meeting". The game's wording check now catches British
  spellings and words (favour, neighbour, whilst, fortnight, queue, programme
  and others) and stiff phrases (make the journey, proceed to, commence), so new
  ones can't come back.

- **Player committee hearings read the shared session calendar.** The hearing
  action keeps an existing scheduled hearing first. Otherwise it reads the
  legislature's hearing task through the shared session-calendar query. Legacy
  state and federal packs retain their existing disclosed game-profile timing;
  unsupported bodies receive no invented hearing calendar. The existing hearing
  writer and ordinary-day advancement remain in charge.

- **Screens state what they know, without citing where it came from** The
  docket, character creation, national results, candidacy, the campaign office
  list, the education options, the constitutional and municipal screens, the
  press and public-information explanations and the patch notes no longer print
  citations, data-series labels, observation dates or research links beside the
  facts they show. Each screen simply tells you the thing. Refusals were the
  larger part of it. When the game will not let a character stand for an office,
  it now says which state, what is in the way and whether a different life could
  reach it, instead of quoting the provision it was reading. Several
  requirements blocked for the same reason used to repeat that reason once per
  requirement; you now read it once.

- **The presidential vote follows the record, not a dice roll** Each state's
  presidential vote now starts from its certified 2024 result and moves only
  with the national mood that every other race reads. It no longer draws a
  random national, regional and state swing, so two worlds with the same record
  give the same winner and the same margins. A state whose count ties exactly is
  no longer settled by a coin toss: its result is recorded without a winner, its
  electors are not appointed, and the count waits. Until the economy's effect on
  the vote is added, each state votes as it did in 2024.

- **Headlines are written for a reader, and three papers no longer run the same
  sentence** A story's headline used to be the simulation's own note to itself,
  printed as though a person had written it. A party founding read "2 organizers
  publicly decided to form The Commons Party." — a numeral where a newspaper
  writes a word, a count where the record holds names, and "publicly", which is
  the game telling you that what you are reading is public. Every paper rendered
  that same line, so three outlets covering one story printed it word for word.
  Headlines now spell a count out, name the people the record names when it
  names exactly them, and drop the words that only describe the record. And the
  outlets write differently because they already are different: a paper with the
  staff for it names people, a broadcaster keeps the count because a line read
  aloud has no room for both, and an outlet whose readers are not standing in
  the place says which state it was. No headline contains a word the record does
  not — the verb is always the one the game wrote down.

- **A law that lowers a rent charge names the renter whose rent it holds** When
  rent stabilization holds a renter's rent, the saved price change now also
  records that the law reached that person: the law, the monthly amount it moved
  (a saving when the price is held below what it would have been), and their
  partner hears of it at home. Officials behind the law can be seen in a better
  light by the people it helped, and a repeal stops the record from growing.

- **Party primaries follow each candidate's standing, not a dice roll** A party
  primary for Congress or a state legislature is now decided by each candidate's
  standing with the party's voters: a sitting member first, then someone the
  party asked to run, then anyone else. The roll that used to stretch or shrink
  each candidate's vote is gone, so the same field gives the same nominee in
  every world. A runoff now goes to whoever led in the primary. An exact tie is
  no longer settled by the order of names: the tied candidates are recorded as
  tied, nobody is nominated, and the state must settle it.

- **Members sign bills from their own recorded principles** Members of Congress
  who share a bill's direction strongly enough now cosponsor it regardless of
  party. Their signatures remain personal commitments; another party's signature
  does not change the sponsor-party cue heard by unsigned members.

- **People's principles form from their own lives** A person who meets a new law
  now forms their principles from what the game records about their life,
  instead of holding none. What their parents held, what the people they confide
  in hold, their party, a congregation or union, the years they grew up in,
  their town's size, their work and home, a job they lost and a crime against
  them each pull toward or away from a principle. They hold it as firmly as the
  pulls add up, and a person pulled both ways is torn. Reading a life again
  writes a new row only where the result changed, so principles move with
  experience. A combative temperament holds a view harder and a conflict-averse
  one gives way sooner, but temperament never picks the side. The player's own
  principles are still the player's. Sitting officeholders keep the principles
  drawn for them at the start until their own lives are recorded.

- **Privacy laws no longer create population-based invoices** Age-verification
  and consumer privacy laws no longer add a copied state's fiscal-note cost to
  another government's budget. A stated appropriation uses the existing
  authority path; actual staff costs use payroll payments. Historical budget
  attribution records remain readable.

- **Record citizenship privately for new people** New people have a private
  citizenship record. Citizenship can change through a recorded event, and
  eligibility can use a dated naturalization record without guessing from a
  person's name or appearance. Replaces: birthplace-only citizenship checks in
  compiled candidacy assessments.

- **Generated reports are no longer kept in the repository** Nothing changes in
  play. Behind the scenes, three files the tooling writes for itself are no
  longer kept in the repository, because the tooling rebuilds them from the
  source every time anyone asks: a prose coverage report, its readme, and a
  census of hardcoded content. They were mostly lists that changed whenever
  anybody added a file, so two pieces of work being brought together would
  collide on them even when neither had touched the same thing. The check the
  coverage report existed for is kept: the five numbers it actually reads are
  now written to a small file of their own, so adding prose without rebuilding
  still fails the build. The census guarded nothing — its checks rebuild it
  before reading it — and was verified to pass with no file present at all.

- **Prosecution stages record their own court deadlines** Saved referrals,
  charges and mistrials record the next case review on the existing clock. The
  court adapter reviews the actual named case and preserves its saved stage
  records. An actual saved local trial-bench appointment rechecks that court's
  already-due pending cases. The opening/load recovery adapter reviews overdue
  cases at the current date, without backdating their original stages or adding
  a polling deadline. The weekly fallback remains until both replacement routes
  are proved in the composed game. The existing world-time composer now
  registers the prosecution stage adapter after the tax handler, preserving
  every other handler.

- **Public account queries can read dated ownership evidence** Public account
  queries can accept a saved date and sequence cutoff. They read the
  organization and government profile visible then, while retaining the existing
  current-query return shape. Ownership evidence includes the actual
  organization and profile IDs. No account, legal power or recipient alias is
  created. Saved municipal identity validation uses the same cutoff as account
  ownership; account creation continues to validate the current identity.

- **Public services has a small-world play script with explicit missing steps.**
  The script checks funded payment, a parent's child-service request,
  enrollment, attendance, and Save/Continue. Family schedule and ordinary
  browser steps remain explicit TODOs. The child-service contract and script
  share one canonical fixture setup.

- **The radial menu is back in place of the labeled bar along the bottom** The
  bottom bar of seven labeled sections is gone. Clicking your portrait in the
  corner opens the radial menu again: Calendar, People, Politics, News, Journal,
  Personal, Travel, Guide and options, Save and Return to title fan out in rings
  above it, each a paper disc with a brass rim. A section with several screens
  opens its own ring, with Back to return. On a narrow or short window the same
  entries open as a framed list. Your portrait, name, the date and the place
  stay in the corner beside the day controls.

- **People you have drifted from get in touch on their own days** Before,
  everybody you had drifted from got in touch on the first day it was possible,
  always for an evening nine days away, so every life heard from somebody on the
  same schedule. Now each pair of people has its own days: how far ahead they
  ask, how long they wait before asking again, and which day they pick up the
  phone. The same two people keep the same days after a save and reload. The
  ranges are calibration, not research, and are filed as questions for the
  relationship research.

- **Campaign-money cases are heard by each state's real regulator** A case about
  a candidate's campaign money now goes before the body that actually oversees
  campaign finance in that state, D.C. or Puerto Rico, such as the Washington
  State Public Disclosure Commission or the Texas Ethics Commission, instead of
  an invented name. How long each step takes and what it fines are still
  simulated until researched.

- **States begin with their real voting, term-limit, district and rent laws**
  Every state, D.C. and territory now begins the game with the law it really had
  at the start of 2026 on five more questions: whether voters show a photo ID at
  the polls, whether people are registered to vote automatically, whether
  legislators have term limits, whether an independent commission draws the
  districts, and whether the state limits rent increases or bars its cities from
  doing so. Where the law in a place couldn't be confirmed, the game treats it
  as unknown rather than guessing.

- **Prices, drug costs and older workers start from real levels** Each state now
  starts at its real price level against the national average (California about
  11 percent above, Arkansas about 13 percent below), its real average yearly
  Medicare drug spending out of pocket (Alaska $233, Alabama $378), and its real
  share of people 62 to 66 who are working (Alabama about 39 percent, New York
  about 46 percent). A tariff, a drug price law or a higher retirement age moves
  these from where each place began, instead of from a flat 100 everywhere.

- **Receive the approved intro locality-card filter** The introduction omits the
  locality card using its already approved filter. Empty filtered views render
  safely; the family and remaining intro cards stay unchanged.

- **Opening preparation reports its actual stages** Opening names each existing
  preparation stage and reports counts only for counted government batches.
  Cancellation discards unfinished preparation.

- **Childhood profiles retain sentences without estimate banners** Profiles
  retain existing upbringing and family sentences without estimate prefixes or
  caregiver-capacity ratios. Saved fields and provenance remain intact.

- **Recession records keep their causes on the event** Recession onsets now cite
  their causes on the recorded onset event without adding a duplicate
  causal-process receipt. The recorded monthly drivers, recovery behavior and
  saved cycle event identity remain unchanged.

- **Recessions, interest rates and business closings now come from what is
  happening in the economy** A recession used to start on a roll of the dice:
  about one chance in 64 each month, whatever the economy was doing. Now none is
  drawn. Output follows new lending, the interest rate and lost jobs. When
  lending dries up, the rate is set too high or layoffs spread, output falls.
  When output shrinks for three months in a row, the game records a recession
  and names what pulled output down. When it grows for three months, the
  recession ends. Across a century of simulated years, recessions average about
  13 months, and long ones are rare, which is close to the historical record.
  The interest rate is set by a committee of 12 people, as federal law sets it:
  seven governors, the president of the New York reserve bank, and four other
  reserve bank presidents who take turns by year. Each member has a recorded
  view on inflation, and they vote at eight meetings a year. When a seat or the
  chair opens, the President names someone they know and trust, weighing favors
  and loyalty the way any appointer does, and passed-over allies remember it.
  The chair comes from the sitting governors. A town's businesses keep books.
  Each takes in its share of what the town spends on its kind of business. It
  pays its staff and its other costs, and borrows from the town's bank when cash
  runs short. It closes when its cash and credit are both gone, no longer at a
  flat rate a year. A new shop takes its sales from the ones already there. Town
  banks take the shape of real small banks in the same state. A bank whose
  losses use up its capital fails. Each closing or bank failure is recorded with
  its cause, and it slows the town's own economy.

- **Recorded schooling selects paid wage peers** A town worker's completed
  schooling can now select a wage offer from the recorded hourly average of
  already-paid peers in the same occupation and workplace. Missing eligible
  peers retain the existing tenure-based offer. The saved compensation note
  records the contributing records and labels the average as an estimate; no
  fixed degree premium is added.

- **Crisis advisers use recorded intelligence** International crises no longer
  invent intelligence confidence or foreign intent from the game's seed.
  Advisers reuse the latest recorded assessment and link their options to that
  evidence. Without an assessment, intelligence remains unrecorded and the
  existing tension rule guides the recommendation.

- **Legislators reflect on questions they actually meet** When a non-player
  sponsor encounters a policy question in a filed measure, their saved
  principles can now prompt a dated private reflection. The resulting view cites
  the exposure and those principles, survives Save and Continue, and can be
  reconsidered after another encounter. No view is assigned without a recorded
  bearing, and a player-controlled sponsor keeps authority over their own
  opinions.

- **Public budgets read recorded tax effects** Public budgets no longer apply
  Oklahoma's grocery fiscal note or California's business credit limitation to
  every government. Tax receipts continue to follow the shared engine's recorded
  bases, enacted terms, and completed collections. Automatic grocery and
  corporate-credit term bindings remain separate work.

- **Relationships fade with time apart, without a number** Before, a friend the
  player had not seen in thirty years read exactly like one they saw yesterday,
  and turning up again after a quarrel was enough to settle it. Now time apart
  changes how current a relationship is, never what happened in it. The pace
  belongs to each pair: a busy month away from a lifelong friend is nothing,
  while a single meeting a few years back has gone dormant. Warmth goes quiet
  rather than sour and comes back quickly when the two of them meet again. Trust
  keeps its record but stops being current until they have dealings again, so
  one evening together does not prove reliability. What is owed does not fade. A
  quarrel left alone goes quiet without ever being settled, and seeing each
  other again is not reconciliation. The person who asked to meet and never
  heard back feels the gap before the other does. People who share a home are
  never apart, and neither are people who work in the same place. Family takes
  far longer to drift than anyone else. Opening someone's card is not contact. A
  known reason for being apart, such as illness or travel, is not recorded
  anywhere yet, so every gap is read as unexplained. A quarrel that was followed
  only by the two of them seeing each other again now reads as unsettled, where
  before any friendly contact settled it. Talking to somebody now counts as
  seeing them. Before, a whole afternoon of conversation and a game left the
  person card saying the two of them last spoke months ago. Each day's
  conversation goes on the shared record once, however many turns it took, and
  half an hour spent together is recorded as time together. A childhood scene
  played with somebody counts as seeing them too. None of these changes how
  anyone feels about the player on its own, since saying hello is not a
  friendship. The person card says all of this in words, never as a meter.

- **A relationship is read along five lines now, not one** Before, every
  relationship the player had was one hidden sum of everything that had ever
  passed between the two of them, sorted into four words. A colleague the player
  worked beside for a decade and a friend they grew up with read the same. So
  did a friendship with a quarrel in it that was never made up, because the next
  pleasant afternoon canceled the quarrel out. Now the same log is read along
  five separate lines: whether the player is glad of that person's company,
  whether they would take them at their word, what they make of how that person
  goes about things, what is owed between them, and whether anything between
  them was left unsettled. A person can be rated at work and not wanted at home.
  An unsettled quarrel stays unsettled until the two of them do something about
  it, and being owed something survives falling out. Saying hello every day is
  not a friendship, and an honest disagreement is not a quarrel: routine contact
