  and a conflict that strained nothing stay on the record without moving where
  the two of them stand. The person card now says where the player stands in a
  sentence of plain words, where before it said only when they last spoke.
  Nothing fades with absence yet. The owner ruled on 2026-09-22 that it should,
  and that it must not read as a number; the shape of that fading is with
  ChatGPT and lands on top of these five lines rather than replacing them. Until
  then a friendship that has not been in view for thirty years reads exactly as
  it did, and what changes a relationship is what the two people do.

- **Parents and grandparents before the start die by the game's own life table**
  A grown-up life's parents and grandparents used to live to a set age between
  78 and 90. Each one now lives until the game's own mortality says they die,
  counted from the birth of their youngest child. The game uses this same rule
  for everyone once play starts. Across the 56 places, at 40 about nine in ten
  grandparents and one in five parents have died before the start.

- **The hand-written opening scenes and their scripted replies are gone** The
  game no longer offers the hand-written opening scenes, and the scripted
  replies that belonged only to them are removed. Conversations come from the
  English engine and the life records instead.

- **Remove the unused Morning note setting** Options no longer offers Daily
  notes or Show morning note. Other settings remain available, and existing
  saved preferences continue to load unchanged.

- **Rents in town follow the town's home prices, and an evicted household
  rents** A private landlord's renewal is no longer drawn at random. It follows
  the town's home prices over the year, worked out each month from the economy
  the world records, and rent stabilization holds it to the general price rise
  plus five points. A new lease's rent follows the same home prices. A household
  evicted within seven years rents its next home rather than buying one. A
  household named for one person reads "Ana Ruiz's household," not "The Ana
  Ruiz's household."

- **Rent stabilization raises the town's market rents a year after it comes in**
  A town or state that brings in rent stabilization now sees its landlords
  answer it: from a year after the law takes effect, every market rent in town
  is written 5.1% higher, the San Francisco measure, while a covered tenant's
  renewal is still held to the cap. Repealing a rent stabilization law the place
  began with lowers market rents the same way. The watched-world report's list
  of state laws now also says what else each law moves.

- **Taking campaign money after a finding, and lying to a reporter, now catch up
  with you** Campaign money a candidate takes for themselves after an ethics
  finding is now reviewed and charged in a second case, repaid, and fined more
  heavily than the first. Money taken in a campaign's last weeks still reaches a
  public report after the campaign ends. A candidate's hometown paper now covers
  an ethics case against them. A reporter who catches a candidate lying now
  takes the contradicting source's on-the-record account to their desk as a
  story and asks the candidate to respond before it runs. How much harder a
  repeat finding hits is a placeholder until it is researched.

- **Reporter assignment considers recorded press contacts** When multiple
  current reporters at an outlet can cover a story, prior recorded press
  contacts with its subjects now help decide who receives the assignment. The
  history records familiarity and does not promise favorable coverage.

- **New tax proposals require researched government authority** New worlds no
  longer generate fictional state tax and service profiles. Filing a new tax
  proposal requires researched authority for that government and instrument;
  unsupported authority is reported explicitly. Existing saved profile evidence
  remains readable.

- **Each state's reserve law fills its rainy-day fund to its own size** Under a
  minimum-reserve law, each state now fills its rainy-day fund to the size its
  own law sets, and moves in at most the yearly share its law sets, as NASBO's
  2021 survey of state budget laws reports them: Rhode Island 5% of revenue at
  3% a year, Nevada 20%, Missouri 7.5%. A state whose law sets no size, and
  every county and city, takes the median of the 36 states that set one, 10%,
  marked as estimated from the average. Before, every government used the same
  hand-set 5% floor and 1% a year.

- **Resilience bill families now read authored wording from data rows.** Three
  resilience families retain their six variants, ordered terms, evidence, bounds
  and amendment wording through the shared bill-family renderer. Existing
  statutory-date and integer formatting are reused. The old inline bank and
  clause-rendering path is removed.

- **A rival campaign acts from its own records, not dice** A rival candidate's
  way of campaigning now comes from their own temperament: a sociable, steady
  candidate works the doors, one who would rather not meet strangers or who
  weighs every word puts out paid messages, and one who avoids conflict works
  through the party and donors. A rival with no strong leaning simply answers
  the race in front of them. A rival's paid message now costs a set amount when
  the committee can afford it, and everything left when it cannot. Its messages
  and events now move voters exactly as the player's own do, with no luck added,
  and its weekly choices are no longer nudged at random.

- **A per-mile road charge and fix-it-first road spending change the world** A
  state's fuel tax now brings in a little less each year as cars burn less fuel
  per mile. A law replacing it with a per-mile road charge stops that loss from
  the first bill, two years after the law takes effect, so the state keeps money
  it would have lost; a repeal sends it back to the shrinking fuel tax. A
  fix-it-first law, which spends highway money on repair before new lanes,
  leaves fewer roads in poor condition: about a fifth fewer once the first
  five-year repaving cycle is done. Smoother roads cost drivers less in repairs,
  which lowers prices a little. Repealing the law undoes both.

- **Routine activities no longer interrupt the life story** Reading, drawing and
  resting no longer arrive as daily life-story scenes. The story can still
  continue a scene that was already open in an older save. No separate leisure
  button is added to the opening panel. The life story no longer offers separate
  time-pass buttons. Day, Week and Calendar remain the routes for moving time
  forward.

- **Talks about running for office keep English reply parts** Replies about
  running for office use the English engine and keep their parts with the
  conversation. Remembering a shared experience requires a record of that
  experience.

- **A manipulative person may dispute a live press request** The trait can now
  affect a subject's response at the live press desk.

- **Intimacy-guarded people may decline public press responses** People who
  guard their privacy may now decline to answer the press in public.

- **A child who moves now starts school in the new place** When a family moves,
  each child who was in grade school starts at a school in the new place, in the
  same grade. They start right away during the school year, or on the first day
  of the next one over the summer. If the game knows of no school there for that
  grade, the child's childhood record says so, and the child is not placed at a
  made-up school. Families with children in school are also slower to move in
  the middle of the year. The further a move would land from a school break, the
  more the school year weighs against it.

- **School and neighborhood replies keep English parts** School and neighborhood
  conversation replies use the English engine's parts and recorded relationship
  conditions. An opening names work or mentions a notice only when a record
  shows the speaker knows it.

- **School scenes name the school** The first morning of school, the lunch
  table, the teacher who keeps you back and the school changing a rule now
  happen at the school your character attends, by the same name the school
  screen shows. Older saves whose school was only "the town's public school"
  keep the plain wording.

- **Player controls share the main menu's palette** Player panels and controls
  share the main menu's glass, brass, and bundled typefaces. Creator lists keep
  their scrolling space and navigation footer; buttons, choices, checkboxes,
  sliders, and progress meters use the selected control artwork. Loading text
  uses the same palette while the app starts.

- **Senators weigh a Supreme Court nominee's views against their own** Before
  the Senate votes on a Supreme Court nominee, each senator now compares the
  principles the nominee holds with their own. A senator of the other party can
  vote for a nominee who shares their views, and a senator of the President's
  party can hesitate over one who does not. The game does not yet record court
  rulings, so a nominee's principles stand for their record.

- **Service bill families now read authored wording from data rows.** Seven
  service families retain their eleven variants, ordered wording, parameters and
  evidence through the shared bill-family renderer. Whole-program authorization
  ceilings retain their labels and create no appropriation effect. The old
  inline service-family and wording construction path is removed.

- **A child's funded pre-K, after-school or private-school day names the child
  and parent in the law's record** When a child completes a day of a service a
  law pays for (pre-K, an after-school program or a private-school program), the
  law now shows up as something that reached that child and each of the child's
  recorded parents. The parent's partner hears of it at home. Officials behind
  the law can be seen in a better or worse light because of it, and a repeal
  stops the record from growing.

- **Entitled organizers favor individualized campaign guidance** An organizer
  with a recorded entitled tendency favors individualized guidance for a
  candidate when that outreach option is available.

- **Imaginative organizers favor candidate guidance** An organizer with a
  recorded imaginative tendency favors candidate guidance when that outreach
  option is available.

- **Tactful organizers favor phone outreach** An organizer with a recorded
  tactful tendency favors a phone shift over door-to-door outreach.

- **Playful manner shapes campaign outreach choices** Campaign organizers with a
  playful manner favor a town hall, while those with a serious manner favor a
  phone shift. The recorded personality tendency is included in the ordinary
  outreach decision.

- **Law effect stamps use one registered kind list** Effect stamps now share one
  allowed kind list, and the legacy evaluator is separated from the saved effect
  records.

- **Devoted people give more weight to established commitments** A recorded
  devoted tendency favors staying in an established close commitment when the
  relationship's next stage is weighed.

- **Town households can move when recorded life changes call for it** Households
  can reconsider recorded changes in work, rent, and family size at their first
  scheduled town review.

- **Executive desks show recorded veto and override results** Executive desks
  distinguish expected override support from recorded roll calls and outcomes.
  Player item-veto selections use the existing signature and enacted-law
  writers, reject stale or ineligible choices before recording a decision, and
  retain the selected items in the saved decision. Council desks require their
  own recorded item-veto grant. Additive bill-scene data is available for the
  central scene consumer.

- **Council acts use the shared executive bill desk** Acts presented to a mayor
  now appear on the same governing desk as state and federal bills. Signing,
  returning, action deadlines and override forecasts use the shared executive
  functions, while council voting, publication and congressional review retain
  their existing procedure. Generated legislative profiles retain the day-count
  basis from their recorded veto-window table.

- **Council readings use the shared legislative floor driver** Council floor
  votes now use the shared institution driver, which checks the recorded roll
  call, quorum, and source-backed reading intervals.

- **Council members use the shared bill filer** Council sittings and local
  meetings file bills through the shared member agenda. Council members keep
  their recorded stakes, one bill per member and question, refile cooldown,
  preemption, numbering, and titles. Each new bill saves its sponsor's recorded
  reasons.

- **Completed work keeps its earned pay when a wage law changes** Completed
  shifts retain their recorded earnings and wage-law assessment. Paying those
  earnings uses the same saved assessment, including after saving and
  continuing. Existing jobs receive coverage records when a world opens, and new
  jobs receive them when employment begins.

- **A man or a woman can now be called Jordan, Taylor or another shared name**
  Names both sexes carried were reserved for non-binary characters, so the game
  could never produce a woman called Jordan or a man called Taylor, by any path.
  The SSA's own tables say otherwise: Willie in the 1920s, Terry in the 1950s,
  Jamie in the 1970s, Jordan and Taylor in the 1990s all appear on both the
  boys' and the girls' top 100 of the same decade, and the research answer is
  that a shared name is not reserved for non-binary people. A stated man or
  woman now draws from their own list and the 36 shared names together; a
  non-binary character draws from the shared names, as before. The rule is
  written as data, labeled as an authored game rule rather than a measured rate,
  so it can be read and changed by hand. A name is still never read back to
  decide anyone's gender, and a name the player types always wins. Every name
  reached is equally likely, which is the weighting the name list has always
  had, so for now about one generated man or woman in five carries a shared
  name. That is more than real life in most birth years. The real weights vary
  by name and by year and wait on the SSA's per-name, per-year counts, which
  have not been compiled yet. Saved characters keep the names they have.

- **Council bills use shared session numbering** New council bills and player
  ordinances use the shared measure counter and retain their numbering session.
  Existing saved labels remain unchanged.

- **Similar-state estimates preserve equal weights for equally close
  references** State tax, office-pay and paid-leave estimates use the same
  established ranking method. Existing sourced values, pay records and assessed
  taxes remain unchanged.

- **A skeptical juror asks what supports the charge before voting to convict** A
  juror who asks what supports a claim before relying on it now counts that
  habit as one reason to vote not guilty. It weighs beside the evidence in the
  case, the burden of proof and what the rest of the room says, so the same
  juror on the same testimony without that streak still votes to convict.

- **Slider fill follows its value** Bill-drafting sliders retain native range
  behavior while the occupied rail follows their actual value. Existing minimum,
  maximum, steps, values, and change handlers are retained.

- **A gradual social approach affects press responses** A person with a marked
  slow-to-warm-up tendency may decline an unfamiliar press request while they
  take time to open up to new contacts.

- **The person card is a small card** Selecting somebody opens a small card:
  their picture, their name, how you know them ("Your housemate", or where you
  work together) and up to three things you have seen of their temperament
  ("Confrontational, cautious and self-conscious, from what you've seen."), with
  when you last spoke. Only the ways of reaching them that work right now are
  drawn. Everything else, from the facts and connected people to why an action
  is unavailable, is under More details.

- **Each state keeps how many residents receive SNAP; a work requirement lowers
  it, which raises food insecurity.** Every state, D.C., Guam and the U.S.
  Virgin Islands now keep the share of their residents who receive SNAP,
  starting from the fiscal 2024 count the USDA publishes. A work requirement for
  assistance lowers it about 5% three months after it takes effect: studies find
  such requirements cut participation by about half among the adults they reach,
  with no gain in work. The 2025 federal law puts that requirement in force
  everywhere, so every place's share steps down from early 2026. Puerto Rico,
  American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands run a different food program
  and record no SNAP share. A law that takes residents off SNAP now raises the
  share of households that are food insecure. About half of households receiving
  SNAP are food insecure, and SNAP lowers that chance by about 30%, so each
  point of residents a law takes off SNAP raises food insecurity about 2%. The
  share's own month-to-month drift, which stands for changes in need, does not.

- **Territories start with their real juvenile court ages** The territories now
  begin with the ages at which a young person is handled by juvenile court,
  taken from their own laws, instead of borrowing a state's age or leaving it
  blank.

- **A special election for Congress follows the seat's own lean** When a seat in
  Congress is filled by a special election, the winner's party now follows how
  the seat's voters lean, as at a regular election. Before, the old party kept
  the seat three times in four by a draw.

- **Election-night speeches are spoken in full, and a no comes with a reason**
  After the votes are counted, a victory speech or a concession is now spoken in
  full. The winner thanks the room, names the other candidate and turns to the
  work ahead; the loser congratulates the winner by name. A speaker whose parent
  has died may say so, unless they are a private person. The result screen shows
  the opening line, and tapping it shows the whole speech. The people who live
  with the speaker, and the campaign's staff, are in the room. Each cheers,
  applauds or stays quiet in their own way, and the result screen says how the
  room took it. Each remembers the speech, more strongly if it moved them or
  they were young. Over the months they tell the people they live with, and
  family and close friends in town, who weren't there, and a memory nobody
  retells fades. Ask someone what they remember, and a person who heard your
  speech, or was told about it, may bring it up once: a clear memory quotes what
  you said, and someone who was told says who told them. When someone turns down
  a game, a quiet talk or a date, they now say why: they want time to
  themselves, they would rather try something new, or they would rather play a
  game you both know. Unless the two of you are at odds, they ease into it.
  Saying yes to a game is short.

- **A law that raises or lowers the juvenile court age now moves the state's
  corrections spending** Before, a state could raise the age at which teenagers
  go to juvenile court, or lower it again, and its budget spent exactly what it
  had planned. Now the state's corrections spending changes from the month the
  law takes effect and stays changed while the law is in force. Raising the age
  costs about $12.58 a resident each year, from New York's Raise the Age
  appropriation; lowering it saves the same. The figure is marked as estimated
  from New York's.

- **Sponsors save reference-backed numeric requests** Sponsors can save numeric
  requests from existing legislative references and their recorded principles.
  The request and its reasons survive Save/Continue. Automatic filing
  integration remains in progress.

- **States begin with their real tax, bail, energy, voting and privacy laws**
  Every state, D.C. and territory now begins the game with the law it really had
  at the start of 2026 on ten more questions: whether its income tax rises in
  steps, whether it has ended cash bail, whether its cities may run broadband or
  set their own minimum wage, whether it charges drivers by the mile, whether it
  bars cities from banning gas hookups, whether voting rights return
  automatically after a sentence, whether it limits abortion after a set point,
  whether bottles carry a deposit, and whether residents can see, correct and
  delete the data companies hold about them. Where a place's law couldn't be
  confirmed, the game treats it as unknown rather than guessing.

- **States begin with their real budget, labor, energy, housing and police
  laws** Every state, D.C. and territory now begins the game with the law it
  really had at the start of 2026 on twenty more questions. Where a place's law
  couldn't be confirmed, the game treats it as unknown rather than guessing. 1.
  Work and money: whether public workers may bargain as a union, whether small
  loans have an interest cap, and whether the state sets a minimum teacher
  salary. 2. State budgets: whether the budget must balance, whether groceries
  are free of the state sales tax, whether pensions must be paid in full,
  whether property tax bills can only grow so fast, and whether the state must
  keep a rainy-day reserve. 3. Energy: whether power companies must sell more
  clean electricity each year, and whether carbon has a price. 4. Housing:
  whether duplexes are allowed on every house lot, whether tenants facing
  eviction get a lawyer, and whether new buildings must include homes below
  market price. 5. Police and courts: whether 17-year-olds go to juvenile court,
  whether police use of facial recognition is limited, and whether civilian
  boards may investigate police. 6. Schools and families: whether public money
  can follow a student to a private school, and whether social media sites must
  check ages. 7. Government: whether cities may pass laws the state hasn't
  reserved, and whether former lawmakers must wait before lobbying. A law a
  place already had when its crime and school figures were measured is part of
  those figures, and repealing it later moves them. A law passed after that
  date, such as Maryland's 2024 limits on police facial recognition, counts as a
  change from those figures from the start. A new state law now takes effect
  when that state's own rule says, if the law names no date itself. In Alaska
  and Ohio that is 90 days after it passes, in Maryland the next June 1, in
  Minnesota the next August 1, and in Missouri August 28. Every other place
  still waits 90 days until its rule is looked up.

- **Every place starts with a known law on every researched question** At the
  start of a game, 412 of the 2,520 starting answers (45 questions in 56 places)
  were blank, so the law there read as unknown. Each has been read from the
  place's own law or a named source, or, for the 52 whose law could not be read,
  set to the most common answer among similar places and marked ESTIMATED FROM
  AVERAGE. A program already passed but not yet started, such as Maryland's paid
  family leave, now reads as not in force until its start date. A repeal passed
  in play also stops such a program before it begins.

- **State bill cooldowns follow each sponsor and question** A defeated state
  bill no longer prevents a different lawmaker from filing on the same question.

- **A state's opening budget is its real figures in every world** A state, and a
  territory the budget survey covers, now opens its budget at exactly the
  figures read for it, in every world: Illinois' income tax opens at its Census
  figure per resident in every new game. Only an estimate from an average, a
  county's or city's share of its state's local finances or an unsurveyed
  territory, still opens with a spread around the average, so two worlds differ
  where the game has no figure of its own. Before, every line of every budget, a
  state's included, was drawn by chance around its figure.

- **Every state has its own capitol** Each state now shows its own capitol
  building instead of one shared dome. Texas's is pink granite, Nebraska's and
  Louisiana's are towers, New Mexico's is round, Hawaii's stands on columns
  shaped like palm trunks, and New York's looks like a castle. D.C. shows the
  John A. Wilson Building, where its Council meets, and Puerto Rico, Guam, the
  U.S. Virgin Islands, American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands each show
  their own legislature. For now each is a daytime picture at every hour;
  morning, night and rainy versions follow.

- **New state laws take effect on their own state's date everywhere** A new law
  that names no date of its own now takes effect when its state's rule says, in
  all 56 places, up from 5. Georgia's take effect July 1 or January 1 by the
  half of the year they are signed, Virginia's and Vermont's the next July 1,
  Connecticut's and Nevada's the next October 1, New York's 20 days after they
  become law, and Colorado's, Delaware's, Guam's and the Virgin Islands' at
  once. North Dakota, Iowa and Rhode Island use a date in the same year, or a
  set number of days once that date has passed. Seventeen places count from the
  day their legislature's session ends. For 2026 that is the day each
  legislature adjourned, and after that the last day its constitution or statute
  allows. A 2026 Kentucky law takes effect July 15, 2026, a Washington law June
  11, and a Maine law July 29. Where a state sets no calendar limit and has not
  adjourned, the game uses the typical last day of the others. Illinois counts
  from the legislature's final vote, which each new law now records: January 1
  for a bill passed before June 1, and June 1 of the next year for the rest.
  California's take effect on the January 1 after 90 days, and first-year bills
  on the next January 1. Six places whose rule can't be dated, such as Kansas,
  where a law takes effect when it is published, use the rule most places like
  them follow. West Virginia cities now start unable to set rent limits: the
  state lists what its cities may do, and rent is not on the list.

- **State lawmakers can file bills from more of their recorded policy
  principles** The state legislature filing threshold now matches the saved
  principle-strength scale, so supported proposals can reach the existing
  legislative process.

- **Every state legislative race records its nominees and a vote count** A state
  legislature's regular seats were decided from each seat's lean plus a seeded
  swing, with no ballot and no count anyone could read. Now each seat whose
  nominees come from different parties gets a ballot 45 days before election
  day, counted on election day from the seat's own voters: its starting lean,
  the sitting member's usual edge, and a share of the state's ballots divided
  evenly among the chamber's seats. The count's winner takes the seat, and the
  seeded swing is gone. The world report sums each legislature's round in one
  line, names every sitting member who lost, and draws a random place from the
  seed when none is named.

- **State legislative races are decided on the state's real election day** A
  race for a state house or senate seat used to be decided 28 days after you
  filed. It is now decided on the state's regular November general election:
  even years in most states, odd years in New Jersey and Virginia, and every
  four years in Louisiana and Mississippi. A filing made after the field closes,
  sixty days before the election, stands in the next one. The campaign screen
  names the election date before you file.

- **State legislators stop running once their state's term limit is reached**
  The 16 states that limit their legislators' terms now enforce the limit: a
  member whose next term would pass it may not seek another, and the seat opens.
  A state that enacts a limit in play takes the most common real rule, eight
  years in each chamber, and a repeal lifts it. Opening legislatures in those
  states start with every member under the limit.

- **A state's legislators decide whether to propose a constitutional amendment**
  When a governor is barred from another term, or has served three in a row, a
  state's legislators now decide for themselves whether to amend the governor's
  term limit. Each weighs the governor's party, how they get along with the
  governor, and the higher bar of changing the constitution. The legislature
  proposes only when every chamber would carry it. Before, a yearly draw decided
  whether an amendment came up, and each chamber's vote was drawn. A state now
  writes a policy into its constitution, or takes one out, when its legislators'
  own principles would carry it, rather than on a drawn subject in a drawn year.
  A question the voters turned down at the last general election is a strong
  reason to leave it. The world no longer proposes changes to how towns recall
  their officials, since nothing gives a legislator a reason to. The voters' own
  share on a referred amendment is still drawn.

- **State legislature work runs on its recorded dates** The canonical clock
  reconciles saved state legislature obligations before advancing and dispatches
  them on their recorded dates. Ordinary date boundaries no longer repeat the
  state legislature turnover scan; other office, payroll and due-item writers
  remain in their existing clock order.

- **Every state's campaign-money regulator follows the same federal calendar** A
  complaint about a candidate's campaign money now moves on the same calendar in
  every state: the respondent is notified within 5 days and has 15 days to
  answer, as federal law sets for the Federal Election Commission. Before, each
  state was given a made-up calendar and fine of its own. The fine for each
  misused payment is now about $6,789, the Federal Election Commission's average
  penalty per closed case in fiscal 2025, and a regulator's own review can flag
  a filed payment after 30 days. A state's own rules replace these once they are
  researched.

- **Many more state laws now change life in the state, not just its budget.** A
  state that passes a right-to-work law sees fewer people vote in the years
  after. Capping how fast property taxes can grow costs schools ground in math.
  A mental health crisis team that answers calls instead of police trims crime,
  and so does giving a civilian board power to investigate police complaints,
  while limiting police use of facial recognition nudges it up. Raising
  teachers' minimum pay helps a few more students graduate, easing licensing
  rules pulls a few people out of poverty, and handing district lines to an
  independent commission lifts turnout slightly. Some laws that people expect to
  matter, such as term limits, raising the juvenile court age or exempting
  groceries from sales tax, change none of these measures, and the game now
  holds them to that. How big each effect is varies a little from one game to
  the next.

- **A state's income tax and paid leave laws now change what its workers take
  home.** When a state legislature repeals its income tax, its workers'
  paychecks stop withholding it from the next tax year. When a state without one
  adopts it, or a state switches between one flat rate and rates that rise with
  income, the new tax is withheld from every paycheck and sent to the state.
  Bills do not carry their own rates yet, so a new tax starts from the average
  of the states that already have that kind of tax, and the paycheck record says
  it was estimated from that average. Workers in the states and D.C. with a paid
  family and medical leave program now pay its premium from each paycheck, at
  the 2026 rate the program publishes, from the date it began collecting. A
  state that creates a program starts collecting a premium near the average of
  the others, and a state that repeals its program stops. Married workers, heads
  of household, and workers in the ten states whose standard deduction had not
  been read no longer have their state income tax left blank. Their schedules
  start from the most common state rule or the average of similar states, and
  the paycheck record says so. A head of household's federal income tax is now
  withheld at the 2026 IRS rates.

- **A stop says what stopped the clock** When the day or week stops early, the
  notice names the thing itself: "Stopped before Tuesday, January 6, 2026, 7:00
  AM. Journey to the public meeting comes first." It no longer says it stopped
  "for something protected that needs you". The note under each skip reads
  "Stops early if anything needs you first."

- **The strongest member pressure carries a majority-backed bill** When several
  member proposals have majority backing, the legislature selects the proposal
  from the member pressing hardest. Equal pressure resolves consistently by the
  members' recorded identities.

- **Student financing respects saved age, marriage and study progress** Supplied
  undergraduate financing is constrained by the student's recorded age, active
  marriage and completed study periods. Published representative annual and
  aggregate principal limits cap new borrowing through the existing tuition
  shortfall writer. Interest charges do not consume the aggregate principal cap.
  This does not create aid awards or infer military service or financial
  dependents.

- **Studs - the shell frame prints no written sentences** The shell frame
  (commitment button, Day and Week buttons, save-before-leaving prompt, pin
  rail, measure briefing and upcoming list) shows names, dates and codes instead
  of hand-written sentences.

- **The finances screen shows figures, not hand-written sentences** Before, the
  Money and property screen explained itself in fixed sentences: who may spend
  each balance, that the household has no shared money, that no new law has
  reached anyone's money, how many other people work at a business, and a
  paragraph on what a house costs and what you pay down and per month. Now those
  sentences are gone. Each balance shows its name and amount, the house shows
  its price, down payment and monthly mortgage payment, and an owned home shows
  the mortgage left. The graph captions and the loading line on the economic
  context are removed too. The buy-a-home button still turns off when you cannot
  afford the home.

- **A state income tax law changes the state's income tax revenue** When a state
  moves between a flat and a graduated income tax, its budget's income tax
  revenue now changes from the month the law takes effect, and each later budget
  expects the change once. The sizes come from real fiscal notes: Illinois' 2020
  graduated-rate estimate raises it 15.0%, and Iowa's 2024 move to a flat rate
  lowers it 12.2%, each as a share of the state's own 2022 collections. Before,
  a state that flattened its income tax kept booking the same rising revenue.

- **Adopting an income tax, exempting groceries and legalizing cannabis sales
  now change a state's budget** A state with no income tax that passed one
  withheld it from paychecks, but its budget collected nothing. It now collects
  the new tax from the next tax year, at the average the states with a wage tax
  collect per resident, moved by how much the state's workers earn. A later
  repeal ends it. A law exempting groceries from the sales tax, or taxing them
  again, now moves the state's sales tax from the day it takes effect, by the
  share Oklahoma's 2024 grocery tax repeal was estimated to cost. A law
  legalizing adult cannabis sales now adds cannabis taxes to the state's budget
  once the first stores open, 11 months after the law takes effect, at what the
  states with established stores collect per resident. A law ending legal sales
  removes that revenue the day it takes effect.

- **First-party local tax statute text for five more states** Before, the local
  income and payroll cells for Pennsylvania, Delaware, Kentucky and New Jersey
  had no first-party statute text behind them. Now the statute text is saved
  with its address, citation, retrieval time and checksum. Indiana, Colorado and
  Alabama stay unsourced, with the reason each fetch failed.

- **A state law on the teacher salary floor raises teachers' paychecks** When a
  state passes a law setting a minimum salary for teachers, public school
  teachers paid below it now get a raise from the first pay period of the next
  school year, and each raise names the law. Teachers hired while the floor is
  in force start at least at the floor. Private school teachers are not covered.
  A later law that ends the floor stops it for pay set from then on and cuts
  nobody's pay. In Appomattox, Virginia, a floor of $53,096 raised all three
  public school teachers, from about $1,892 to $2,042 a paycheck.

- **Legislators amend bills from views they have saved** Legislators who offer
  floor amendments now plan from views saved through the one belief pipeline. A
  member whose principles weigh on a question forms and keeps a view on it
  first, so the view behind an amendment is the same one the member's later
  votes and journal read.

- **A government with no recorded debt borrows at the measured national rate** A
  government whose Census figures show no debt or no interest no longer opens at
  a hand-set 4% interest rate. It opens at the measured national rate for its
  level: about 3.6% for a state and 3.9% for a local government. Its opening
  notes say whether the rate is its own or an estimate from the average.

- **Each government's pension debt opens at its state's measured size** A
  government's opening pension liability is no longer 1.2 times a year of its
  spending everywhere. It now uses its state's own measured ratio: every public
  plan's actuarial liability against the state's combined state and local
  spending. A place with no listed plan opens at the states' median, marked as
  an estimate.

- **People in the scene** Removed the separate Here people list from the scene.

- **Read your school years as part of your opening story** Your life so far
  connects the schools and years in your saved biography into a short paragraph,
  followed by your recorded work. It keeps each school's saved name and does not
  turn enrollment into graduation.

- **Show saved parents and guardians in their home region** The opening family
  card now illustrates the recorded parents and guardians, with their names and
  relationships, using the home-region picture selected for the state card.
  Missing coverage stays neutral; no family presence is invented.

- **Admit saved policy amendments to actual state chamber votes** The existing
  actual-state ratification recorder now accepts a saved policy amendment. It
  uses the same constitutional considerations, actual dated members, separate
  chamber requirements and sourced quorum rules. Unsupported rules or missing
  institutions still leave the action unrecorded. The Article V policy callback
  migration remains a separate pending ownership transfer.

- **Recorded Article V proposals use the shared chamber vote** Congressional
  Article V proposal votes and rejection-repeat comparisons use the same member
  decision path as ordinary chamber votes. The adapter requires the actual dated
  constitutional proposal and saved congressional seats. The existing
  constitutional rollcall writer still applies the proposal's legal threshold.

- **Keep legacy term-limit votes in parity tests only** Removes the retired
  term-limit ballot evaluator from production. Its unchanged before-and-after
  oracle now lives in a test fixture; live member votes continue through the
  shared chamber decision.

- **Congressional presidential term-limit proposals use the shared chamber
  vote** Congress saves a presidential term-limit amendment proposal before its
  members vote through the shared chamber decision path. A failed proposal keeps
  its actual rejection rollcall. Existing constitutional thresholds, amendment
  terms and state ratification timing remain unchanged. State term-limit callers
  retain their current decision path and reuse the shared rationale-weight
  table.

- **State budgets identify revenue lost when legal cannabis sales end** A state
  budget records the cannabis tax revenue its operative sales ban removes. The
  saved monthly loss identifies the governing law and remains available after
  saving and reopening the world. The existing tax calculation remains in place.

- **Tenant rent and eviction records identify their governing housing laws**
  Affordable leases, capped rent renewals and eviction cases with legal
  representation retain the housing law responsible for the recorded
  consequence. Rent amounts and case decisions follow the existing rules.

- **What people think of officials is a view they hold** When a law reaches
  someone, what they make of the officials behind it is now decided the way
  every other political view is: what the law did to them is weighed against the
  view of that official they already hold, and the result is kept as their own
  view. A second law that cuts the other way can leave them torn, rather than
  adding points to a tally. What they say about an official in conversation, how
  a town votes on a candidate, and how a legislator reads the people a law
  reached all read that same view. Saves made before this change still load and
  keep what their people had already decided.

- **Remove the day-in-review popup** The day-in-review popup no longer appears
  after passing time. Its saved events remain available in News and Journal. The
  optional morning note keeps its existing content.

- **Creator appearance follows the chosen gender presentation** The Creator uses
  the Character appearance heading. Male and female setup choices omit the
  repeated body-presentation selector; nonbinary setup retains it. Saved
  identity, appearance generation, manual home choice and the remaining
  appearance controls are unchanged.

- **Public meeting panels keep agenda and attendance without legacy chat**
  Public meeting panels no longer show placeholder roster speech or a separate
  comment-choice interface. Attendance, agenda, recorded roll call, brief visits
  and leaving remain available through their existing actions. Meeting buttons
  omit duration suffixes. Existing comments and history records are retained;
  conversation through the dialogue window is separate future work.

- **Telling somebody something is about something that happened** Before, asking
  whether you could tell somebody something ended with them asking "What do you
  want to tell me?" and no way to tell them anything. Now, once they say go
  ahead, the player can tell them about something from their own life: a moment
  with somebody at school, at home or in the neighborhood in the last fortnight
  that this person was not there for and has not heard about, or a plan the
  player has made. The answer depends on the person. Somebody who needs quiet
  asks whether it can wait. Somebody the player has an unsettled quarrel with
  says little. Somebody fond of the player asks how it went, and somebody who
  knows the other person in the story says their name. Once told, they know it,
  and it is not offered to them again. If there is nothing to tell, the player
  can say it can wait. How the person answers is interim for now. The answers
  are a small set of placeholder lines, and the real behavior is a question
  filed with ChatGPT.

- **The House elects its Speaker and the Senate its President pro tempore by
  vote** When the line of presidential succession reaches Congress, each chamber
  now votes for its presiding officer. Each party caucus first chooses a nominee
  from its longest-serving members, then the whole chamber votes among the
  nominees. Every member decides from their party, how they get along with each
  candidate and how close the candidate's principles are to their own, and every
  ballot is recorded with its reason. Before, the majority's longest-serving
  member was placed in the chair without a vote.

- **A new life opens on the year, your state's lawmakers, your town and your
  family** A new life now opens with the year: who is President, how Congress
  divides, how many people looking for work cannot find it, how fast prices are
  rising, and the day's headlines. The White House follows, set in the Oval
  Office. After your state comes its legislature, with each chamber by party and
  the two members who represent your home. Your town's screen names its mayor
  where one is in office, and the proposal people there are weighing. Before
  your life so far, a new screen names the parents or guardians who raised you
  and what they do for work. On the home screen, a saved character now stands
  posed for their role, and a judge wears the robe.

- **The parties screen says who settles a proposal, and what is public** Before:
  a new life opened the parties screen and read "No party proposals involve you
  yet." Party evolution is built and runs — parties are founded, they split,
  they join together, and their platforms drift — so the screen was closed
  rather than empty, and it said nothing to tell the two apart. After: it says
  that parties settle these among their own officers and committee people, that
  a proposal reaches you when you are one of them or have put one forward
  yourself, and that a change a party has already made is public and appears
  there either way. The last part is the screen describing what it already does:
  a decided change is shown to everybody, involved or not. A fresh world has
  simply not made one yet. How: the empty-state paragraph in the party
  initiatives panel. No behavior changed, and a browser test walks to the screen
  in a varied place and fails if the line stops saying who decides, how a player
  gets in, or that the decided half is public.

- **Time apart for a known reason no longer reads as drifting apart** A friend
  who was in jail, who moved to another state, who was grieving a death in the
  family, or who had just started a new school or job is no longer read as
  someone you neglected. Those stretches do not count toward the two of you
  drifting apart, and a friendship now fades a little more with each unexplained
  week rather than all at once.

- **Somebody is standing at the lectern on the title screen** The title screen
  has been able to draw a figure at the lectern for some time. The room, the
  lectern and the light were all there, and the component that draws them
  accepts a person to stand behind it. Nothing ever handed it one, so every
  player who opened the game saw an empty room. The title screen now resolves a
  figure from the most recent save and draws them at the lectern. A player with
  no saves sees the room as before, because there is nobody to draw and drawing
  a stranger would be worse than drawing no one. The candidate art preview modes
  pick up whatever library they are pointed at, so a preview build shows the
  candidate figure and a production build shows only released art. Nothing on
  this screen spends time and nothing here creates a fact: it is the menu, and
  it stays a picture.

- **The title screen shows your character** When you have a saved game, the
  title screen now shows your most recent character standing in a public hearing
  room, looking the way they did when you last saved and dressed for the room.
  People standing in rooms are now drawn at the right size for each room.
  Furniture only hides the people standing behind it. On the opening tour, the
  President and other officials wear suits.

- **Replies to personal news use recorded context** When you tell somebody about
  a plan or an experience, their reply uses the English engine. A shared plan
  comes from their own recorded goal; they name another person only when their
  relationship records show they know them.

- **Babies are born in your town about as often as in real life** Before, a town
  saw about one birth in five years, and only to a couple living together. Now
  any woman in town from 15 to 49 can have a baby, at the rate real women of her
  age do. Married women are the likeliest; a single mother is possible. A mother
  with a newborn, or with three children already, is less likely to have another
  soon, and nobody has two babies within about 15 months. Over five years,
  Columbus, Ohio, now sees several births instead of one.

- **A place inside a town or township gets its local law from that town's
  board** A census-designated place inside a New England or New York town, or
  inside a New Jersey, Pennsylvania or Midwest township, has no government of
  its own. Its local government is the town or township it lies in, and that
  board now makes its law. It is seated at the size its state's statute sets,
  read for 15 states, meets every two weeks, and votes on the ordinances its
  members file. The ordinances govern the place unless a state law on the same
  question outranks them. Before, these places took their county's board, or had
  no local body at all where the county has no government.

- **Town businesses open where customers go unserved, hire by their books and
  set their own prices** A new business used to open on a roll of the dice, and
  a resident was picked at random to run it. Now a resident opens one where the
  town spends more on its kind than the businesses already there can serve. The
  one who opens it is someone who has worked in that trade, then the oldest.
  Each business opens at its kind's real profit margin from the IRS's business
  tax records, and it is named for its founder's family, a family of the town, a
  street or its county. A business hires only while its sales cover one more
  worker, and it lets the newest go when they no longer do. Whoever runs a
  business never quits it. When the owner retires, dies or moves away and nobody
  is left, it closes, and its record says who left. Hiring draws nothing: a
  resident takes the work they have done before, then the kind of work the town
  is shortest of. Each worker at the start has held their job for the typical
  number of years for their age. Each business sets its prices from its costs,
  how busy it is and what its rivals charge, and customers choose among rivals
  by price. A city's sales tax now follows what its town's shops, restaurants,
  inns and other businesses sell, so a closing or a recession reaches the city's
  budget. A town bank now holds the deposits real banks hold in its county for
  each resident, from the FDIC's Summary of Deposits. Its losses come from its
  own borrowers: businesses that close owing it, and households whose
  breadwinner lost a job and stayed out of work past unemployment benefits and
  the day banks write a loan off. Every business borrows from the town's largest
  bank.

- **Couples in a new town are mostly a man and a woman** Before, a new game
  chose each partner in a town couple without regard to the other, so about half
  of the couples were two women or two men, and few couples could have a baby.
  Now most couples are a man and a woman, and about one in seventy is two women
  or two men.

- **A town's election rules no longer differ by a hidden draw** Before, a town
  nobody had researched got its council size, council terms, counting rule,
  election season and recall rules from a hidden draw keyed to its name, so two
  similar towns next door could have different councils for no reason. The
  candidate filing deadline for every town race was a flat four weeks. Now a
  town's rules come from its own records where the game has read them. Where it
  has not, every such town gets the most common rule in the country: a five-seat
  council on four-year terms, and the counting and recall rule the most states
  use. The candidate filing deadline is the national middle, 85 days before the
  first vote. A new campaign committee's first filing is due in 10 days, the
  federal rule, wherever a state's own rule is not read. The government screen
  for a town nobody has researched now lists the council members actually seated
  there, the player among them, instead of showing the council as empty.

- **Who in town quits, is laid off or is hired comes from their record** Each
  quarter, the town's jobs still turn over at the real national pace: 2.0% of
  jobs a month are quit and 1.1% end in a layoff. More are laid off and fewer
  quit when unemployment is high. Nobody is picked by chance anymore. The most
  recently hired are laid off first. The youngest quit first, then whoever is
  newest in the job. A quitter walks straight into another job when somebody
  they know from a congregation, club, council or a child's class works for
  another employer in town. Among the residents looking for work, the ones out
  of work the shortest time are hired first, and somebody who has never held a
  job comes last.

- **A town's newspaper follows the town's size** A town among the 1,000 largest
  places in the country now has a daily newspaper, and every other town has a
  weekly, the most common kind of local paper today. Before, which kind of
  newsroom a town had was picked at random when the game began, so a town of
  4,000 people could have a daily and a large city a newsletter.

- **Town council races fall on the November election day where state law puts
  them** A race for a town's own council used to be decided four weeks after
  filing, wherever the town was. In the states whose municipal election law
  holds town elections on the November general election day, the race is now
  held on that day, in the even or odd years the state sets. Ely, Minnesota, for
  example, votes on November 3, 2026. Where state law leaves the choice to each
  town, the town's choice is drawn from the options the law allows. Where the
  law read so far does not fix a day, as with town meeting day or a spring
  election, the race stays four weeks out. That remains a marked placeholder
  until the town's calendar has been researched.

- **Transit bill wording now comes from data rows.** Both existing transit
  variants retain their wording, parameter bounds, evidence, authority
  requirements and effect declarations through one typed data renderer. The
  inline variant and wording definitions are removed; saved variant keys and the
  existing bill compiler remain the same.

- **Completed transit service uses its recorded quantity** Completed transit
  service now records the paid vehicle-service hours directly from its
  completion record, preserving its original unit, scope and saved provenance.

- **More transit service brings more riders** When a law adds or cuts transit
  service, ridership follows a year later. Each 1% of service a law adds raises
  rides by 0.3% to 1.0%, and a cut lowers them the same way. A state that lets
  its highway money pay for transit now gains riders, not only buses and trains.
  Changes in service that no law caused are already part of how ridership moves
  on its own, so they are not counted twice.

- **A state tuition freeze lowers what the state's colleges collect** When a
  state passes a law freezing in-state tuition at public colleges, each school
  year that begins with the freeze in force holds tuition where the year before
  left it instead of letting it rise 3.1%. The state budget's charges and fees
  come in lower by the share of them that is college tuition in that state, and
  the next budget is set on the lower revenue. A later law ending the freeze
  lets tuition rise again from the next school year.

- **Two research questions ask for the sizes behind D.C. statehood funds and
  parks funding.** The game cannot yet move federal money when D.C. becomes a
  state, or a parks budget when a place dedicates revenue to parks, because no
  sized source exists for either. Two questions in the research queue now ask
  for them: the dollar changes to D.C.'s federal funds and budget, and which
  places dedicate revenue to parks, how much it yields and what it does to park
  access.

- **Unread state tax deductions use the most similar researched states** When a
  state's personal exemptions or credits have not been read, its modeled
  deduction now uses a weighted average of researched deductions. States with
  the same tax structure rank first, followed by the same Census region and the
  nearest median household income. Closer references receive more weight, and
  equally close references receive equal weight. The estimate stays the same
  across worlds. Researched deductions and tax brackets keep their values.

- **Unrest, threats and attacks on public figures now follow from how long anger
  lasts** Unrest in a state used to break out on a roll each quarter while its
  anger ran high, and a threat or an attack on a public figure came on further
  rolls. Now unrest starts whenever a state's anger is over the line and lasts
  until anger falls back. Once it has lasted, the state's most prominent
  political figure is threatened: its governor, then its U.S. senators, then its
  U.S. representatives, then its party organizers. An attack comes only when the
  anger that follows a threat has added up past a set line, and a threat lapses
  when the unrest calms. Each step is recorded with what it rested on.

- **The corner shows the version number instead of a build code** The title
  screen and every play screen now show the release version, such as v0.5.0, in
  the bottom-right corner in place of a seven-character build code. The desktop
  hub names the version beside the game you are playing, and its Install update
  button no longer shows a build code.

- **What is waiting on you now opens the thing that answers it** "Waiting on
  you" told the truth and stopped there. It said the posted meeting was waiting
  for a decision, and that an offer of work was waiting for an answer, and then
  left the player to go and find the screen that takes one. A list of things you
  cannot act on is a reminder, not a day. Each of those lines is now the way in
  to the record that answers it. The posted meeting opens the commitment on the
  calendar. An unanswered offer of work opens Work. A thing that is with
  somebody else opens that person. An errand you do with the time you have stays
  a plain sentence, because it is answered right below, with the time; and a
  line the record carries no route for also stays a plain sentence rather than
  becoming a control that guesses where to send you. Following one of these is
  still reading. It opens the record and the day does not move: no evening is
  spent by looking at what an evening would cost.

- **"Who are you?" asks five questions, or ten to twelve, and can open
  differently** The questions at the start of a new life are shorter now. The
  quick route asks five. The longer route asks about ten to twelve, and the
  setup screen says so, instead of aiming for thirty to fifty. The first
  question is no longer the same three scenes in the same order for every new
  life. It is chosen from the strongest questions for the character's age, so
  two starts can begin differently, and the same start always begins the same
  way. One new question asks you, the player, to imagine being mayor of a
  fictional American city. The council has paid for a license-plate-reader
  pilot, and it goes ahead only if you sign. You choose 90-day storage, 14-day
  storage, or not signing. If you choose 14 days, one follow-up asks what
  mattered most to you. Nothing in the question becomes part of your character's
  history. Questions about adult jobs no longer appear in a young child's route
  unless they are clearly framed as imagined situations. Lives started before
  this change keep the questions they were asked.

- **Who follows the news and who hears about a law comes from the person**
  Whether someone follows the news closely, knows how a legislator voted, and
  tells anyone what a law did to them was a roll of the dice. It now comes from
  who they are. A curious person follows the news, and so does someone of
  retirement age with no job to go to. A person knows a legislator's vote when
  they follow the news or know the legislator. When a law reaches someone, they
  tell up to three of the people closest to them, and someone who avoids
  conflict keeps politics to themselves.

- **After a break-up, who keeps the home follows the family, not a coin toss**
  When a couple in town breaks up while living together, the partner who parents
  more of the children living there keeps the home, and the other moves out.
  With no children to tell them apart, the one who lived there first stays, and
  after that the older of the two. When a couple moves in together from homes of
  the same size, the one who has lived in their home longer keeps it. Both
  choices used to be a coin toss.

- **Nurses and police officers wear their uniforms** People now wear their
  uniforms at work. Nurses, nursing assistants and medical assistants wear
  scrubs, police officers wear their uniform, and construction and quarry
  workers wear a safety vest. Their own portraits show the uniform too, so you
  can tell what someone does. At home and around the neighborhood they wear
  their own clothes, and a police officer on duty in a chamber or a courtroom
  stays in uniform.

- **The world report counts every law that acts in the world** The world
  report's law section now says how many laws in force act in the world today,
  whether through an outcome the world computes or through a paycheck, a budget,
  a rent or a seat. Each law also says what it moves.

- **The world report credits a law with the outcomes it moved through the
  outcome web** A watched-world report used to say a law "changed nothing
  outside its own passage" even when the outcome web moved homelessness or
  insurance through it. Each law now lists the outcomes it moved and in how many
  places, and the check on whether any law changed anything counts them.

- **The world report ends with the place's vital statistics, at the start and at
  the end** A watched-world report now closes with the same list of figures for
  every place, so two worlds can be set side by side. The list covers people and
  households, pay and unemployment, poverty, health insurance, graduation and
  college completion, crime, turnout, births and deaths, businesses opened and
  closed, and the governor's, legislature's and mayor's parties. Each figure is
  read from what the world recorded, at the opening and at the end, and says
  whether it belongs to the place or to its state. A figure the world does not
  record yet, such as household income or rent, says so and why instead of
  showing zero. This is a development report; nothing in play changes.

- **Campaign-finance answers say what the rule is instead of quoting the statute
  at you** When a campaign is told it cannot take a contribution yet, or that it
  may, the sentence now says what the rule is in ordinary words. It no longer
  opens with the statute's chapter and section number. Where the answer came
  from is still recorded against the ruling, so anyone who wants to check it
  can, but nothing on a screen reads like a citation any more.

- **When a crime happens to you, whether to call the police is your choice**
  When you are assaulted or robbed, or your home is broken into or vandalized,
  and nobody else it happened to has called the police, the game now asks you.
  You can report it, and it goes on the town's police log that day, where the
  paper, the police and the state's mood can see it, or keep it to yourself.
  Nothing decides it for you, and until you report it, it stays off the log. For
  everybody else, knowing the person who did it no longer makes a victim keep
  quiet: people hurt by family report about as often as people hurt by
  strangers, as they do in the national crime survey. A victim it has happened
  to before is now more likely to call.

- **People leave town for a reason, and go where it takes them** Nobody leaves
  town on a roll anymore. A resident moves away only when something in their own
  life pushes them past what keeps them there: a job lost and not replaced, a
  retirement, an eviction, rent that eats into what the household earns, or a
  brother, sister, parent or child who moved away. What keeps them is how rarely
  people their age in their state move, a home they own, children at home and
  their own appetite for risk. They go where the cause points: to the relative
  they follow, or to where their closest family lives. With no family elsewhere,
  they go to another part of their own state. Someone who moves leaves their
  club or party membership behind, though a leader stays held by the role. A
  person with nothing pushing them stays put. Newcomers still arrive at the rate
  the state's survey gives. They are counted the same way every time, so the
  town never gains or loses one by chance. Saves made before this change still
  load.

- **Who leaves after a disaster, where they go and who moves in follow their
  lives** A family whose home a flood or storm wrecks no longer leaves town on a
  chance. They weigh the wreck against what holds them: a renter living alone
  may go to a parent in another town, while an owner with children in school
  stays to rebuild. Anyone who leaves goes where their reason or their family
  is, never to a random state. Newcomers no longer arrive at a fixed rate. One
  comes when a job in town is left open by a worker who moved away, died or
  retired, nobody in town is looking for work, and the pay covers the rent.

- **Grown children decide for themselves when to leave home** A grown child
  living with a parent now decides whether to move into a place of their own,
  weighing their age, their own pay against the town's rent for one, a partner
  of their own and their taste for risk. One who moves out starts a new
  household in town, which then finds a home like any other. A household someone
  formed in the last year is now also a reason they might leave town: moving out
  of a parent's home, moving in with a partner or moving out after a breakup. It
  is a small push that tips only somebody already close to leaving.

- **Fewer residents leave town for a job offer** Residents now look for work out
  of town less often, and weigh an offer that pays no more than they earn now
  more lightly. About 2 in every 100 adults a year move away for a job offer, in
  line with how often Americans move for a new job. A resident whose pay is not
  on record is no longer treated as earning nothing, and someone working short
  hours is no longer counted twice as both short of hours and short of pay.

- **Job offers out of town come from real kinds of local businesses** A resident
  looking for work out of town is no longer offered the same office clerk job by
  the state government. They now apply to a business the place really has, such
  as a market, a diner or a builder, in the town where their family lives or the
  largest town in their state. The job fits what they have done before, and the
  pay is that town's going wage for the work.

- **A trait added by a content pack starts from a person's upbringing** Before,
  a trait a content pack added was picked for each person by lottery, so two
  people raised the same way could start on opposite ends of it. Now it starts
  where the person's upbringing leans it. A pack can name the built-in qualities
  its trait follows; the example patience pack follows patience, so someone
  raised with steady household limits starts patient. Someone whose upbringing
  leans neither way starts in the middle.

- **The next everyday moment follows what just happened in your life** Before,
  which everyday moment came next in a life was picked by a hash of the world,
  so the same day could open on a different moment in another world with the
  same history. Now a reply to the moment you just finished still comes first.
  After that comes the moment tied to the most recent thing that happened to
  you, either the same kind of moment or one with somebody who was part of it.
  When nothing ties, the moments come in their usual order.

- **Every made-up person gets their age and birthday the same way** Townspeople,
  the residents of a test world, your own character and the officials and
  colleagues an authored start names now all take their age from one table of
  age ranges by role and their birthday from one rule. Nobody's birthday
  changes: the same world gives the same people the same birthdays as before.

- **Young adults leave town more often than their parents, at the pace people
  really move in your state** How often a town's residents pack up and leave now
  depends on their age and their state. Across the nation a twenty-two-year-old
  weighs moving away about six times as often as someone past seventy, and each
  state keeps its own pace. Newcomers arrive at the state's real pace too, and
  the island territories follow the national pattern.

- **A town business borrows on a credit line sized for its trade, and shop sales
  catch up with the town's pay over years** Town businesses now borrow on credit
  lines sized for their trade: a restaurant's line covers about five weeks of
  its sales and a realty office's about four months, from what small firms
  really hold. Shop sales in town also follow a change in the town's pay more
  gently, catching up over a few years instead of all at once.

- **A federal limit on power plant carbon now lowers each state's carbon
  emissions** When Congress passes a limit on power plant carbon, it now lowers
  carbon dioxide per resident in every state, by about 2.6% two years after it
  takes effect. That size is the central research value, adjusted by each
  state's recorded conditions; the range of about 1.4% to 8% is only a check on
  it. Before, the law cleaned the air a little but left a state's carbon
  emissions where they were.

- **Universal pre-K now moves eighth-grade reading and teenage violent crime** A
  state that starts universal pre-K now sees two more effects years later.
  Eighth-grade reading moves a little, most likely slightly down, about nine and
  a half years on, because studies disagree, from no change in Boston to a loss
  in Tennessee. Violent crime falls about 1% fourteen years on, once the first
  enrolled children turn 18, and ending the program reverses it.

- **Universal pre-K's effect on math now arrives when its first children reach
  eighth grade** Universal pre-K's small effect on eighth-grade math now shows
  up about nine and a half years after a program starts, when its first
  four-year-olds take the eighth-grade test, instead of five years on. Four
  effects once set to exactly zero are now marked as unmeasured instead: housing
  vouchers on graduation and on arrests, a congressional stock-trading ban on
  members' returns, and local book removals on reading. They still move nothing.

- **A finished high school now counts when an offense points to someone** When
  the town works out who an offense points to, a resident's own school record
  now counts. Someone on record as finishing high school is a little less likely
  to be the one named, and someone who left without a diploma is more likely. A
  resident whose schooling is not on record is weighed like the average
  resident, with nothing guessed. How large the difference is remains a
  placeholder until the research is in.

## PRE-ALPHA 0.4.0

_Released September 19, 2026._

### Added

- **Read your life story and explore the calendar** Browse a month or week
  calendar, read a chronological life story, and inspect public news with its
  recorded sources. Municipal, constitutional and transit views explain which
  records are available and which actions are still unavailable. Household
  conversations refer to the errands recorded in your life.

- **Meet the world you start in, and catch up after time passes.** A new life
  now opens with a short look at who holds power: the White House, Congress,
  your state's governor and your local government, drawn from the same people
  you can later meet. When you skip ahead, a brief summary shows what changed
  that you could know about, and people you talk to can bring up those matters.
  Organizations and people you pin return you to them without replaying the
  introduction.

- **Party work, weekly campaign plans and rival campaigns that act on their
  own.** Local party organizers now come back with real work: an organization
  meeting, a canvass or a phone shift from home, a talk about running for
  office, a fundraiser, a request for the organization's support, or a town
  hall. Each one is on a real evening with a real host, travel is shown before
  you go, and you can go briefly without changing what happens. The people you
  meet stay in your life, and meeting the same volunteer again makes them a
  regular contact. Once you are a candidate you plan each week: field shifts,
  fundraising calls and advertising, with the channel, amount and area chosen by
  you. A plan the committee cannot afford is refused and nothing is spent.
  Without staff you plan alone; with staff you see their proposals. Sessions you
  cannot get to can be let go, so a busy week never stops time. Your opponents
  now campaign too, once a week, with their own money: field events, paid
  messages, fundraising and requests for party support. What they do in public
  reaches you.

- **Civilian job offers and recorded work** Compare civilian work, respond to an
  employer’s offer, carry out a scheduled responsibility and retain your work
  submission. Earned pay follows completed shifts. You can accept changed
  responsibilities, resign and pursue other work.

- **The economy now changes month by month** A new game's economy now keeps
  moving after you begin. At the start of each month, News carries the country's
  unemployment rate for the month before. Once a year has passed it also reports
  how much prices have risen, and each quarter how fast the economy grew. A
  shipping disruption abroad now shows up in growth and prices while it lasts,
  then eases gradually, and a flood or severe storm where you live slows the
  local economy until the repairs are finished. These figures belong to your
  world, not real government data, and games started before this update keep the
  history they already had.

- **Follow modeled public cash and a delivered-service response across the same
  life.** The Budget page now keeps a separate Modeled account history for cash
  actually collected and paid through the public receipts account. It shows the
  covered dates, settlement periods, exact transfer records and a balance only
  when the opening value and full modeled transfer history support one;
  government-wide budget figures and published reference series remain separate.
  When the controlled sponsor publishes a dated report for service that was both
  paid and delivered, one recorded supporting colleague who can read that
  publication may acknowledge it. The saved acknowledgment appears through
  People and the Journal once, without adding a vote, approval score, ridership
  claim or broad public reaction.

- **Fund public transit service with a tax you pass, and see what it
  delivered.** As an Alaska state legislator you can now carry a service
  decision all the way through. File an added transit service appropriation and
  a tax, move both through the legislature, and let the tax take effect and
  collect real public cash. Then request the service and watch each period come
  due. The Transit page shows which periods were delivered and paid, how many
  vehicle-service hours the money bought, and what is left in the public
  account. It also says plainly what the game does not model, such as ridership
  or travel times. If no collected cash is available, the page tells you before
  you ask for service, and a refused period pays nothing. Taxes and public
  receipts now has its own page under Politics, where you can follow a tax bill
  through its legislative steps. The legislative sittings and votes for these
  bills are fictional game content and are labeled as such.

- **State agency director Custom Start and personnel matters** Eligible adult
  Custom Start characters in Minnesota can direct a fictional state agency and
  review its personnel matters. Supported procedures become available when their
  sourced dates apply; notices use structured cause and known supporting
  records, and employee responses survive saving and reopening.

- **Floods and severe storms now follow a real recovery path.** When a flood or
  severe storm strikes, it damages the homes and workplaces the world actually
  knows about, and the people living there can be hurt. Local responders act
  first. The governor then decides whether to ask the President for a federal
  disaster declaration, and the President can approve or deny it. Rebuilding
  takes weeks and goes faster with federal help, but a declaration never repairs
  anything on the spot, and a denied request does not make the disaster
  disappear. If you hold one of those offices, the decision is yours.

- **International crises now unfold through advice, decisions and replies.**
  When tension rises abroad, the President's advisers now weigh talks, economic
  measures and moving forces, working from intelligence that can be wrong. The
  other government and allies answer on their own terms, and a crisis can ease,
  harden into a standoff, or come back for another round. Sending forces starts
  the War Powers clock: a report to Congress within two days and a sixty-day
  limit without authorization. If you are President, these calls are yours.

- **People can now fall ill, recover, and die in the course of ordinary life.**
  Everyone in the world now ages against published United States mortality
  rates, including officeholders and your own character. Nobody is protected by
  office or fame, and a death happens on its own day however you choose to pass
  time. When the cause is not known, the game says so instead of inventing one.
  People can also go through serious health episodes: they may be limited or
  unable to work for a while, most recover, and what they share stays private
  until they or the people around them disclose it. When a public official dies
  in office, it becomes public news, and when an official can no longer do the
  job and the people responsible know it, their office is told.

- **Illness, emergencies and the decisions only you can make now appear on your
  own screens.** Who you are now holds your own health: an illness or injury the
  record shows, who has been told about it, and the choice to tell someone you
  know, the people responsible, or everyone. Nothing is guessed — with no record
  it simply says there is none — and somebody else's private illness reaches you
  only when they or the people around them actually told you. The same screen
  lists the public emergencies any resident can read, in the words of the public
  record. When the office you actually hold is the one being asked, your office
  gains the decision itself: whether to ask for a federal disaster declaration,
  whether to declare one, and what to do about an international dispute, each
